### [1] [The Coin Flip Judge? Reliability and Bias in LLM-as-a-Judge Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13685)
*Abel Yagubyan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM-as-a-Judge exhibits significant run-to-run unreliability: pairwise preferences flip 13.6% on average, cross-judge agreement is only 76%, and 11–15 repeated trials are needed for reliable majority-vote verdicts. Single-trial judging is too noisy for high-stakes evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-Judge is widely deployed for ranking outputs, training reward models, and populating leaderboards, yet its run-to-run reliability remains poorly characterized. The authors aim to quantify the extent and sources of instability in LLM judging.

Method: The authors conduct repeated identical evaluations across 29 tasks in 10 categories using two OpenAI judge models (GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1-mini). For each question, they run 50 pairwise and 50 pointwise trials, supplemented by temperature and prompt-sensitivity ablations. They analyze flip rates, position bias, pairwise–pointwise gaps, cross-judge agreement, prompt-template sensitivity, and reliability curves for majority-vote aggregation.

Result: Key findings: (1) Pairwise preferences flip 13.6% on average, with 28% of questions exceeding 20% flip rate and one reaching 56%; (2) GPT-4o-mini shows significant first-position bias (72% A-majority, p=0.024); (3) Mean pointwise score gaps are small (0.19–0.36 on a 10-point scale) and not statistically significant, revealing a pairwise–pointwise gap; (4) Cross-judge agreement is 76% (κ=0.51); (5) Semantically equivalent prompt templates change majority outcomes in 25% of cases; (6) Deterministic decoding reduces but does not eliminate inconsistency; (7) 11 repeated trials are needed on average (15 for high-variance questions) for 95% probability of recovering the 50-trial reference verdict.

Conclusion: Single-trial LLM judging is too noisy for high-stakes evaluation. Multi-trial aggregation, position randomization, and explicit uncertainty reporting should become standard practice. Cross-provider replication remains a necessary next step, as both judges tested are from a single provider.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge is now widely used to rank model outputs, train reward models, and populate public leaderboards, but its run-to-run reliability remains under-characterized. We study repeated identical evaluations on 29 tasks spanning 10 categories using two OpenAI judge models (GPT-4o-mini and GPT-4.1-mini), with 50 pairwise trials and 50 pointwise trials per question, supplemented by temperature and prompt-sensitivity ablations. Across judges, pairwise preferences flip on average 13.6% of the time, with 28% of questions exceeding a 20% flip rate and one question reaching 56%. GPT-4o-mini also exhibits a significant first-position bias (72% A-majority, p = 0.024). At the same time, mean pointwise score gaps are small (0.19--0.36 on a 10-point scale) and not statistically significant in aggregate, producing a pairwise--pointwise gap: judges frequently choose a winner even when their own scalar scores provide little evidence of a meaningful quality difference. Beyond within-judge instability, cross-judge agreement is only 76% ($κ= 0.51$), semantically equivalent prompt templates change majority outcomes in 25% of tested cases, and deterministic decoding reduces but does not eliminate inconsistency. A reliability curve analysis shows that, in our dataset, 11 repeated trials are needed for a majority vote to recover the 50-trial reference verdict with 95% probability on average, rising to 15 for high-variance questions. These findings suggest that single-trial LLM judging is often too noisy for high-stakes evaluation, and that multi-trial aggregation, position randomization, and explicit uncertainty reporting should be standard practice. Because both judges are from a single provider, cross-provider replication remains an important next step.

</details>


### [2] [Benchmarking Web Agent Safety under E-commerce Deceptive Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13686)
*Zijing Shi,Meng Fang,Ling Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: WebDecept is a plugin framework for injecting deceptive interface patterns into web environments to evaluate the safety of autonomous web agents, revealing that current agents are highly susceptible to manipulation and that prompt-based safeguards are inadequate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As autonomous web agents are increasingly deployed for real-world tasks, understanding their vulnerability to deceptive interfaces is critical for safety. No prior work systematically evaluates agent behavior under controlled, realistic deceptive web patterns.

Method: The authors introduce WebDecept, a lightweight, configurable plugin framework that injects seven deceptive interface patterns (e.g., targeted ads, domain redirection, shopping manipulation) into existing web environments' frontends during task execution. Multiple multimodal web agents are then evaluated under these controlled conditions, with and without prompt-based safety constraints.

Result: Current multimodal web agents show high susceptibility across multiple classes of deceptive interfaces. Prompt-based constraints are insufficient to mitigate these failures. The design choices of deceptive patterns significantly influence manipulation success rates.

Conclusion: Significant safety challenges remain for web agents facing deceptive interfaces, highlighting the need for stronger defenses before real-world deployment at scale.

Abstract: As autonomous web agents are increasingly deployed to perform real-world tasks, ensuring their safety has become a critical concern. In this work, we study web agent behavior under realistic deceptive interfaces in the e-commerce domain. We introduce WebDecept, a lightweight and configurable plugin framework that enables controlled injection of deceptive interface patterns into existing web environments. Using WebDecept, we instantiate seven deceptive patterns commonly observed on the open web, including targeted advertisements, domain redirection, and shopping manipulation. By injecting these patterns into the frontend during task execution, we perform controlled evaluation of multiple multimodal web agents. Our results show that current web agents are highly susceptible to multiple classes of deceptive interfaces, and that prompt-based constraints are often insufficient to mitigate these failures. We further analyze how the design choices of deceptive patterns influence the success of such manipulations. These findings highlight safety challenges that should be addressed as web agents are scaled toward real-world deployment.

</details>


### [3] [Which Models Perform Better in Inheritance Reasoning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13751)
*Mohammed Amine Mouhoub,Chahinez Bouchekif*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Team PSL's QIAS 2026 Shared Task submission evaluates commercial vs. open-source LLMs on Arabic Islamic inheritance reasoning. Commercial models (best: Gemini 2.5 Flash, MRE 0.989) outperform open-source models in heir identification, exclusion rules, and consistency, while open-source models struggle with dependent decisions and fractional adjustments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess the effectiveness of large language models in solving Arabic Islamic inheritance cases requiring legal interpretation, multi-step reasoning, and precise numerical computation, and to compare commercial versus open-source models under a unified prompting strategy with minimal task-specific adaptation.

Method: A unified prompting strategy is applied to both commercial and open-source large language models on the QIAS 2026 Shared Task dataset. The evaluation focuses on the models' ability to identify eligible heirs, apply exclusion rules, perform fractional share adjustments, and maintain consistency across multi-step legal reasoning chains. Metrics include Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRE).

Result: Commercial models consistently outperform open-source models across all evaluated dimensions. Open-source models exhibit greater instability, particularly in cases with dependent legal decisions and fractional share adjustments. Gemini 2.5 Flash achieves the best overall performance with an MRE of 0.989.

Conclusion: There is a clear reliability gap between commercial and open-source LLMs for structured legal reasoning tasks. Commercial models are better suited for Islamic inheritance reasoning under minimal task-specific adaptation, though the high MRE of Gemini 2.5 Flash suggests the task is largely tractable with sufficiently capable models.

Abstract: This paper presents the participation of team PSL in the QIAS 2026 Shared Task on Arabic Islamic inheritance reasoning. The task evaluates the ability of large language models to solve inheritance cases that require legal interpretation, multi-step reasoning, and precise numerical computation. We compare \textit{commercial} and \textit{open-source} models under a unified prompting strategy to assess their effectiveness in structured legal reasoning with minimal task-specific adaptation. \\ Our results show a clear gap in reliability between the two model families. Commercial models demonstrate stronger performance in identifying eligible heirs, applying exclusion rules, and maintaining consistency across reasoning steps. In contrast, open-source models exhibit greater instability, particularly in cases involving dependent legal decisions and fractional share adjustments. The best performance is achieved by \textit{Gemini 2.5 Flash}, with an MRE of $0.989$.

</details>


### [4] [QIAS 2026: Overview of the Shared Task on Islamic Inheritance Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13756)
*Abdessalam Bouchekif,Somaya Eltanbouly,Samer Rashwani,Shahd Gaben,Mutaz Al-Khatib,Heba Sbahi,Emad Mohamed,Mohammed Ghaly*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: QIAS 2026 shared task evaluated LLMs on end-to-end Islamic inheritance reasoning using the MAWARITH benchmark (12,500 Arabic cases), with 16 teams exploring prompting, RAG, and fine-tuning approaches. Results show the domain remains highly challenging, especially for precise legal interpretation and numerical reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional QA benchmarks do not adequately test complex, multi-step reasoning in specialized domains. Islamic inheritance law requires identifying eligible heirs and calculating exact shares — a structured, rule-based process that challenges current LLMs' legal interpretation and numerical reasoning capabilities.

Method: The shared task uses the MAWARITH benchmark (12,500 annotated Arabic inheritance cases with intermediate reasoning steps) and evaluates systems via MIR-E, a multi-step metric covering all stages of inheritance reasoning. Sixteen teams employed prompting-based methods, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and fine-tuning strategies.

Result: Islamic inheritance remains a highly challenging benchmark for current language models, with particular difficulty in stages requiring precise legal interpretation and structured numerical reasoning.

Conclusion: The QIAS 2026 shared task demonstrates that while LLMs can be adapted to complex religious/legal reasoning via various techniques, substantial gaps remain in achieving reliable end-to-end inheritance calculation, highlighting the need for further advances in structured reasoning and domain-specific legal interpretation.

Abstract: This paper presents a comprehensive overview of the QIAS 2026 shared task, organized as part of the OSACT7 Workshop and co-located with LREC 2026. The shared task was designed to evaluate the ability of large language models to perform complex reasoning in the religious and legal domain of Islamic inheritance. Unlike conventional question-answering benchmarks, QIAS 2026 focuses on end-to-end reasoning from natural language cases, requiring systems to perform the full inheritance calculation process, from identifying the eligible heirs to assigning the correct share to each beneficiary. To support this evaluation, the task was based on the MAWARITH benchmark, a dataset of $12{,}500$ Arabic inheritance cases annotated with intermediate reasoning steps and final answers. System submissions were evaluated using MIR-E, a multi-step metric that measures performance across the main stages of inheritance reasoning. A total of $16$ teams participated in the shared task, investigating a range of approaches, including prompting-based methods, retrieval-augmented generation, and fine-tuning strategies. The results show that Islamic inheritance remains a highly challenging benchmark for current language models, especially in stages that require precise legal interpretation and structured numerical reasoning. This overview summarizes the task design, dataset, evaluation framework, participating systems, and main results.

</details>


### [5] [The Culture Funnel: You Can't Align What isn't in the Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13808)
*Ananya Sahu,Mehrnaz Mofakhami,Daniel D'Souza,Thomas Euyang,Julia Kreutzer,Marzieh Fadaee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper argues that modern LLM training pipelines suffer from a "cultural data funnel" where explicit cultural signals diminish during post-training stages, despite inference-time alignment approaches assuming sufficient cultural knowledge already exists. The authors introduce a multidimensional tagging framework to quantify this phenomenon across pretraining, fine-tuning, alignment, and reasoning datasets, and release a 5.6M-sample culturally tagged dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current cultural alignment methods operate at inference time under the assumption that models already possess adequate cultural knowledge. The authors challenge this assumption, arguing that the root problem lies earlier in the training pipeline — specifically, that cultural representation is systematically squeezed out during post-training stages, resulting in models with impoverished cultural understanding that no amount of inference-time intervention can fully remedy.

Method: The authors develop a multidimensional tagging framework that captures cultural signals across four stages of the LLM pipeline: pretraining, fine-tuning, alignment, and reasoning datasets. They apply these tags to analyze how cultural content evolves (or declines) through each stage, measuring both explicit cultural markers and geographic diversity. They also test whether multilinguality helps preserve cultural representation and evaluate whether their tags improve downstream cultural benchmark performance.

Result: Explicit cultural signals decline sharply during post-training stages. Post-training data is dominated by geographically concentrated, task-specialized content. Multilinguality improves geographic diversity of cultural knowledge but does not guarantee balanced representation across cultures. The authors' tagging framework demonstrably improves performance on downstream cultural benchmarks.

Conclusion: Meaningful advances in cultural alignment require shifting focus from inference-time interventions to the training data pipeline itself — specifically, ensuring cultural signals are preserved throughout post-training rather than filtered out. The released CultureMarkers dataset (5.6M samples) provides a foundation for future research in this direction.

Abstract: Current cultural alignment approaches focus on inference-time interventions, assuming models already contain sufficient cultural knowledge. We argue modern LLM pipelines suffer from a cultural data funnel. Using a multidimensional tagging framework across pretraining, fine-tuning, alignment, and reasoning datasets, we show explicit cultural signals decline sharply during post-training, while geographically concentrated, task-specialized data dominates. Multilinguality enhances geographic diversity of cultural knowledge but does not ensure balanced representation. Our tags improve downstream cultural benchmark performance, demonstrating that advances require shifting focus in training data pipelines. To facilitate future research, we release our culturally tagged dataset with 5.6M samples at https://huggingface.co/datasets/CohereLabs/CultureMarkers.

</details>


### [6] [When Plausible Is Not Realistic: Evaluating Human Mobility in LLM-Based Urban Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13835)
*Gustavo H. Santos,Aline Carneiro Viana,Thiago H. Silva*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a validation framework to assess whether LLM-based generative agents in urban simulators reproduce empirically realistic human mobility patterns. Evaluating AgentSociety and CitySim against real-world data from Paris and Shanghai, the authors find a significant gap between narrative plausibility and empirical mobility realism, particularly in spatial-temporal constraints. They also contribute open-source tools for reproducible evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based generative agents are increasingly deployed in urban simulators, but there is no rigorous framework to evaluate whether their generated mobility patterns match real-world empirical data, as opposed to merely producing plausible-sounding mobility narratives.

Method: The authors propose a multi-dimensional validation framework that evaluates generative agent mobility against real-world data using: (1) mobility laws (e.g., trip-length distributions), (2) temporal rhythms, (3) network motifs, (4) semantic activity transitions, and (5) behavioral mobility profiles. They apply this framework to AgentSociety and CitySim using datasets from Greater Paris and Shanghai, and contribute open-source infrastructure for map generation, simulation, metric computation, and traffic simulation.

Result: The analysis reveals a substantial gap between narrative plausibility and empirical mobility realism. While simulators capture high-level semantic activity distributions, they fail to reproduce realistic trip-length distributions, origin-destination flows, dwell times, and transition dynamics. Realistic mobility diversity is unstable under default prompting and may require explicit profile-aware initialization.

Conclusion: Rigorous empirical validation of LLM-based urban simulators is essential. The gap between narrative plausibility and empirical realism underscores the need for validation frameworks and tools like those contributed in this work to build more realistic, reproducible urban simulation systems.

Abstract: LLM-based generative agents are increasingly used in urban simulators, yet it remains unclear whether they reproduce empirically realistic human mobility patterns or merely generate plausible mobility narratives. We introduce a validation framework for evaluating the mobility of generative agents of LLM-based urban simulators against real-world mobility data. For this, we use mobility laws, temporal rhythms, network motifs, semantic activity transitions, and behavioral mobility profiles. Using datasets from the Greater Paris region and Shanghai, we evaluate AgentSociety and CitySim across multiple dimensions of mobility realism. Our analysis reveals a substantial gap between narrative plausibility and empirical mobility realism. Although the simulators capture some high-level semantic activity distributions, they struggle to reproduce core spatial and temporal constraints, including realistic trip-length distributions, origin-destination flows, dwell times, and transition dynamics. We further observe that realistic mobility diversity is unstable across default prompting configurations and may require explicit profile-aware initialization. To support reproducible evaluation, we also contribute scalable and open LLM-driven infrastructure for regional-scale map generation, observability-enhanced simulation, mobility-metric computation, and traffic simulation. Our findings highlight the need for rigorous empirical validation of LLM-based urban simulators and provide practical tools for building more realistic and reproducible urban simulation systems.

</details>


### [7] [Hybrid Classical-Quantum Variational Autoencoder for Neural Topic Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13852)
*Ivan Kankeu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A hybrid classical-quantum VAE for topic modeling is proposed, using parameterized quantum circuits in the inference network with a modified Gaussian Softmax posterior to operate on a 10-qubit device. The model achieves state-of-the-art coherence (C_v=0.71) and NPMI (0.20) on AgNews, demonstrating viability on NISQ-era hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural topic models (NTMs) are effective for semantic discovery, but their integration with quantum hardware is unexplored. The authors aim to demonstrate that hybrid classical-quantum architectures can be computationally viable for topic modeling even under current NISQ-era resource constraints.

Method: A hybrid classical-quantum variational autoencoder (VAE) is constructed: parameterized quantum circuits serve as the inference network (encoder), while a classical decoder handles topic-word distributions. A modified Gaussian Softmax posterior decouples latent dimensionality from the number of topics, enabling operation on a low-resource 10-qubit quantum device. A fully classical variant is also built for comparison.

Result: On the AgNews dataset, the hybrid VAE achieves a C_v coherence score of 0.71 and NPMI of 0.20, outperforming state-of-the-art NTMs while maintaining high topic diversity. The fully classical variant also surpasses SOTA models and exhibits clear class separation in latent space.

Conclusion: Hybrid classical-quantum VAEs are computationally viable on NISQ-era devices and represent a promising direction for quantum-enhanced topic modeling, with both quantum and classical variants outperforming existing approaches.

Abstract: Neural topic models enable scalable semantic discovery, but their integration with quantum hardware remains largely unexplored. We present a proof-of-concept hybrid classical-quantum variational autoencoder (VAE) for topic modeling, embedding parameterized quantum circuits within the VAE inference network while retaining a classical topic-word decoder. To address the resource constraints of quantum hardware, we propose a modified Gaussian Softmax posterior that decouples latent space dimensionality from the number of topics to be extracted, enabling the model to operate with a low-resource 10-qubit quantum device. On the AgNews dataset, the hybrid VAE outperforms state-of-the-art neural topic models (NTMs), reaching a $C_v$ coherence score of 0.71 and an NPMI score of 0.20 while preserving high topic diversity. For comparison, we also construct a fully classical variant, which also outperforms state-of-the-art models on AgNews and exhibits clear class separation in the latent space. These results demonstrate that hybrid VAEs are computationally viable even on NISQ-era devices and represent a promising direction for quantum-enhanced topic modeling.

</details>


### [8] [SANA: What Matters for QA Agents over Massive Data Lakes?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13904)
*Austin Senna Wijaya,Jiaxiang Liu,Haonan Wang,Eugene Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SANA is a diagnostic ablation framework that decomposes end-to-end exploratory QA performance into isolated components (search, planning, data analysis, and action policy), revealing that data analysis is a consistent bottleneck while search limitations depend on data-lake scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end accuracy alone cannot pinpoint where EQA agents fail — whether in search, planning, data analysis, or the agent's action policy (decisions about next steps and answer submission timing). A diagnostic framework is needed to isolate and quantify these failure modes.

Method: SANA transforms EQA tasks into runtime profiles containing gold source sequences, sanitized subquestions, and execution records. From these profiles, it constructs idealized (oracle) search, planning, and data-analysis tools. By selectively replacing agent components with these idealized versions (ablation), the residual accuracy gap isolates failures attributable to each component and to the action policy.

Result: Across LakeQA and KramaBench, data analysis emerged as a consistent bottleneck for both lightweight and mid-sized agents. Planning was less of a limitation. Search was a major limitation in LakeQA's large data-lake setting but less so in the smaller-scale KramaBench.

Conclusion: SANA deconstructs end-to-end task accuracies into granular diagnostics, enabling systematic comparison of progress across search, planning, data analysis, and agent design — and provides actionable evidence for where to focus improvements in data-lake QA agents.

Abstract: Exploratory question answering (EQA) over data lakes requires an LLM agent to discover relevant sources, analyze retrieved data, and adapt its actions based on intermediate results. End-to-end accuracy alone cannot distinguish failures in search, planning, data analysis, or the agent's Action Policy: its decisions about what to do next and when to submit an answer. We present SANA (Search Agent Navigation Ablation framework), a diagnostic ablation framework that transforms EQA tasks into runtime profiles containing gold source sequence, sanitized subquestions, and execution records. SANA uses these profiles to construct idealized search, planning, and data-analysis tools, allowing each component to be ablated; the residual gap is diagnostic evidence for policy failures.
  To illustrate SANA as a reusable evaluation framework, we adapted two recent EQA benchmarks, LakeQA and KramaBench, and evaluated lightweight and mid-sized agents under fixed prompts, budgets, data lakes, and runtimes. Across both benchmarks, data analysis is a consistent bottleneck while planning is less so. Search is a major limitation in LakeQA's large data-lake setting, but less so for the smaller-scale KramaBench. SANA thus deconstructs end-to-end task accuracies into a diagnosis of where data-lake agents fail, and allows for systematic comparisons of progress in search, planning, data analysis, and agent design.

</details>


### [9] [A Computational Audit of Demographic Association Encoding in ClinicalBERT Language Predictions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14460)
*Kehinde Temitayo Soetan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A computational audit of ClinicalBERT reveals that representational bias—especially against Black patients—operates predominantly through model-internal amplification rather than mere inheritance of training data statistical patterns, with 65.6% of significant findings contradicting observed corpus distributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer-based clinical language models are increasingly deployed in high-stakes clinical decision support, yet the precise computational mechanisms by which demographic associations in medical text propagate into biased model outputs remain empirically underspecified—specifically, whether bias reflects training data distributions or model-internal amplification.

Method: Two complementary probing methodologies are applied to ClinicalBERT (pretrained on MIMIC-III discharge summaries): (1) Log Probability Bias Analysis (LPBA), quantifying demographic descriptor-induced shifts in masked token probability distributions across behavioral and evaluative semantic categories; (2) Masked Language Model-based (MLM) probing of internal representational structure for demographic agency attribution encoding, using 98 real clinical sentence templates across eight intersectional race-gender combinations. Corpus frequency analysis benchmarks model outputs against empirical MIMIC-III term frequencies to operationalize the distinction between statistical disparity and bias amplification.

Result: Of 32 statistically significant findings, 65.6% contradict observed corpus distributions; this rises to 80% for Black patients and 87.5% for agency attribution under MLM probing—providing direct empirical evidence that representational bias operates predominantly through model-internal amplification rather than training data inheritance.

Conclusion: ClinicalBERT exhibits systematic representational bias that is not simply a reflection of training corpus statistics but arises from model-internal amplification mechanisms, underscoring the need for bias mitigation strategies that go beyond data debiasing to address architectural and optimization-induced distortions in clinical language models.

Abstract: Transformer-based clinical language models are increasingly integrated into high-stakes clinical decision support pipelines, yet the computational mechanisms through which demographic associations encoded in medical documentation propagate into model probability distributions remain empirically underspecified. We present a systematic computational audit of representational bias in ClinicalBERT (Alsentzer et al., 2019), a BERT-based model pretrained on MIMIC-III discharge summaries, employing two complementary probing methodologies: Log Probability Bias Analysis (LPBA), which quantifies demographic descriptor-induced shifts in masked token probability distributions across behavioral and evaluative semantic categories, and Masked Language Model-based analysis (MLM), which probes internal representational structure for demographic agency attribution encoding across 98 real clinical sentence templates and eight intersectional race-gender combinations. Corpus frequency analysis operationalizes the distinction between statistical disparity and bias amplification by benchmarking model outputs against empirical term frequencies in the MIMIC-III training corpus. Of 32 statistically significant findings, 65.6% contradict observed corpus distributions, rising to 80% for Black patients and 87.5% for agency attribution under MLM probing, providing direct empirical evidence that representational bias in ClinicalBERT operates predominantly through model-internal amplification rather than training data inheritance. Keywords: natural language processing, clinical documentation, algorithmic auditing, representational bias, health equity 1

</details>


### [10] [DLawBench: Evaluating LLMs Through Multi-Turn Legal Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13931)
*Li Zhang,Yuzhen Shi,Yiran Hu,Jingwen Zhang,Wenbo Lv,Yubo Ma,Wei Wang,Rongyao Shi,Yuanyang Qiu,Xinran Xu,Yuemeng Qi,Linlin Miao,Jaromir Savelka,Yun Liu,Kevin Ashley,Bing Zhao,Hu Wei,Lin Qu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DLawBench is a diagnostic benchmark evaluating LLMs on multi-turn lawyer-client consultations across four client personality types, revealing that even the best model (GPT-5.5) scores only 0.562, and exposing sycophancy and performance degradation when clients most need guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing legal benchmarks focus on static legal reasoning (e.g., bar exams, judgment prediction) but overlook the interactive, multi-turn consultation skills essential for real-world legal practice. LLMs must elicit sufficient and truthful facts from clients with diverse personalities — a capability gap that DLawBench aims to measure.

Method: DLawBench constructs 461 cases drawn from Chinese and U.S. law, with lawyer-client interactions categorized into four client types: Cooperative, Dependent, Withdrawn, and Adversarial. The benchmark contains 5,532 paired fact entries, 3,411 inquiry rubrics, and 3,348 issue-resolution rubrics. It evaluates 26 representative LLMs through multi-turn consultation dialogues grounded in realistic client behavior.

Result: The best-performing model, GPT-5.5, achieves only 0.562 on consultation-grounded legal reasoning, indicating substantial room for improvement. Two critical failure modes are identified: (1) sycophancy — models agree with clients rather than probing for missing or contradictory facts; (2) a paradox where models perform worse precisely when clients are most in need of guidance (e.g., Withdrawn or Adversarial types).

Conclusion: DLawBench demonstrates that current LLMs are ill-equipped for real-world legal consultation, lacking both the strategic fact-elicitation skills and the robustness to handle difficult client personalities. The benchmark serves as a diagnostic tool to drive progress toward interactive, client-centered legal AI systems.

Abstract: Lawyer-client consultation is a critical starting point for legal services. Effective legal assistance hinges on eliciting sufficient and truthful information from clients in order to devise strategies that best protect their interests. This task requires Large Language Models (LLMs) not only to perform robust legal reasoning, but also to strategically elicit material facts through multi-turn interactions and effectively guide clients with diverse personalities. Yet existing legal benchmarks overlook this interactive capability. To fill this gap, we introduce DLawBench, a diagnostic benchmark for real-world legal consultation. Drawing on realistic client behavior, we characterize lawyer-client interactions into four types: Cooperative, Dependent, Withdrawn, and Adversarial. Using dialogues grounded in real cases, DLawBench evaluates whether LLMs can effectively conduct legal consultation under realistic conditions. DLawBench comprises 461 cases from Chinese and U.S. law, 5,532 paired fact entries, 3,411 inquiry rubrics, and 3,348 issue-resolution rubrics, and evaluates 26 representative LLMs. Systematic experiments show substantial headroom: the best-performing model, GPT-5.5, achieves only 0.562 on consultation-grounded legal reasoning. More importantly, DLawBench exposes both sycophancy in legal consultation and a paradox: models perform worse when clients need guidance most.

</details>


### [11] [Can Post-Training Turn LLMs into Good Medical Coders? An Empirical Study of Generative ICD Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13940)
*Ziqing Wang,Weihao Li,Shijie Chen,Yuan Luo,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper challenges the prevailing view that LLMs are weak at ICD coding by showing that task-specific post-training (SFT, GRPO-based RL, and a novel PHI curriculum) dramatically improves performance over prompting-only evaluation, suggesting the bottleneck is optimization rather than the generative formulation itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are widely considered poor medical coders, but this conclusion is based almost exclusively on inference-time methods (prompting, retrieval, reranking). The role of task-specific post-training — particularly reinforcement learning — for generative ICD coding remains underexplored, motivating a controlled empirical comparison.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled study comparing discriminative baselines with generative LLM coders under three paradigms: (1) prompting, (2) supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and (3) reinforcement learning via GRPO. They introduce PHI, a diagnostic curriculum that extends GRPO by specifically targeting and refining missed-code cases to improve macro-level recall across the full ICD taxonomy.

Result: Prompting-only evaluation significantly underestimates LLM potential. SFT provides the largest capability jump, GRPO further improves code-set prediction beyond SFT, and PHI yields targeted gains on macro-level performance metrics, particularly addressing full-taxonomy recall.

Conclusion: The primary bottleneck in generative ICD coding is not the generative formulation itself, but rather how models are adapted and optimized for full-taxonomy recall. Proper post-training unlocks substantial performance, and the authors release all code, data splits, and checkpoints to support reproducibility.

Abstract: Automated International Classification of Diseases (ICD) coding is a core medical-coding task for billing, epidemiology, and clinical decision support. Generative large language models (LLMs) are often reported as weak medical coders, but this finding mainly comes from inference-time settings such as prompting, retrieval, reranking, or tool use, leaving the role of task-specific post-training underexplored. We present a controlled empirical study of post-training for generative ICD coding, comparing discriminative baselines with LLM coders across prompting, supervised fine-tuning, and reinforcement learning under a common protocol and metric set. To our knowledge, this is the first study to evaluate RL-based post-training for generative LLM coders in ICD coding. We further introduce PHI, a diagnostic curriculum that extends GRPO to refine missed-code cases. Our results show that prompting-only evaluation substantially underestimates the potential of LLMs for ICD coding. SFT provides the main capability jump, GRPO further improves code-set prediction beyond SFT, and PHI provides targeted gains on macro-level performance. These findings suggest that the main bottleneck is not the generative formulation alone, but how the model is adapted and optimized for full-taxonomy recall. We release our code, data splits, and checkpoints at https://github.com/AlexandreWANG915/LLM4ICD.

</details>


### [12] [LLMs Contain Multitudes: How Deployment Context Reshapes Model-Level Preferences and Values](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13944)
*Filip Trhlik,Aoife O'Flynn,Angela Yu,Arduin Findeis,Paula Buttery*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs' stated preferences and values shift dramatically when the deployment context (e.g., writing a Reddit post vs. a news article) changes, with effects far exceeding those of prompt paraphrasing or temperature. Reported model-level preferences are thus context-conditioned, not stable properties, undermining safety guarantees that assume consistency across framings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work characterizes LLMs as having stable, model-level preference and value systems, but robustness checks are limited to superficial prompt perturbations (syntax, option order). The authors question whether these measured properties survive changes in the surrounding task context — as occurs in real-world deployments — which remains untested.

Method: The authors systematically vary deployment context as a controlled variable across two pairwise paradigms: (1) ranking country preferences (15 countries) and (2) eliciting utility judgements (50 outcomes). Contexts include framings like writing a Reddit post or a news article. They test five LLMs, collecting over 1.2M pairwise decisions, and compare context-induced variation against prompt paraphrasing and temperature controls.

Result: Deployment context produces variation far larger than paraphrasing or temperature. In country rankings, context induces widespread, statistically significant rank shifts; Global North favouritism is itself context-dependent, with bias shifting systematically. In utility elicitation, broad cross-category ordering holds, but fine-grained rankings within domains vary substantially. Cardinal exchange rates between outcomes shift by a median factor of 2.47 across contexts.

Conclusion: Reported model-level preferences and utilities are better understood as context-conditioned measurements rather than fixed model-level properties. Safety guarantees obtained under one framing provide limited assurance in another, carrying significant implications for LLM safety evaluation and deployment.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly characterised in recent evaluation work as having stable, model-level preference and value systems. However, accompanying robustness checks are limited to incidental prompt perturbations such as syntax variation and option reordering. This leaves open whether the measured properties survive when the surrounding task context changes, as it does in most real deployments. We test this directly across two established pairwise paradigms: ranking country preferences and eliciting utility judgements. In both, we make the deployment context -- the high-level task the model is performing while making concrete value-dependent choices -- our controlled variable, varied across framings such as writing a Reddit post or a news article. Across five LLMs and over 1.2M pairwise decisions, deployment context produces variation far larger than prompt paraphrasing and temperature controls. In country preference rankings over 15 countries, context induces widespread, statistically significant rank shifts; the aggregate Global North favouritism reported in prior work is itself context-dependent, with each model's bias shifting systematically across contexts. In utility elicitation over 50 outcomes, broad cross-category ordering is preserved, but fine-grained rankings within domains vary substantially, and cardinal exchange rates between outcomes (e.g. how many lives in one region equal one in another) shift by a factor of 2.47 at the median. Reported model-level preferences and utilities are therefore better understood as context-conditioned measurements than fixed model-level properties: safety guarantees obtained under one framing provide limited assurance in another.

</details>


### [13] [MedLatentDx: Latent Multi-Agent Communication for Cross-Hospital Rare-Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13945)
*Ziqing Wang,Lili Zhao,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MedLatentDx introduces a privacy-preserving multi-agent framework for cross-hospital rare disease diagnosis, where hospitals share compact latent KV representations instead of raw clinical text, improving diagnostic accuracy while reducing information leakage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare diseases affect 300M+ patients across 7,000+ conditions, but individual hospitals lack sufficient cases for reliable diagnosis. Cross-hospital collaboration is needed, yet privacy regulations (e.g., HIPAA) prohibit sharing identifiable clinical text. Existing medical agent systems that exchange text or raw latent states (hidden states, KV caches) risk exposing sensitive clinical content.

Method: MedLatentDx is a latent multi-agent communication framework where hospital agents retain private clinical records locally and transmit only compact latent KV blocks to a host agent for diagnosis. It supports two deployment modes: (1) same-backbone hospitals use latent KV distillation, and (2) hospitals with different LLM backbones use cross-family latent alignment. Evaluation is conducted on CrossRare-Bench, a self-constructed large-scale rare-disease benchmark with hospital-level data partitions.

Result: MedLatentDx improves cross-hospital diagnostic performance on CrossRare-Bench compared to baselines, while simultaneously reducing the reconstructability of clinical content relative to raw-latent communication methods.

Conclusion: Latent-space communication via compact KV representations offers a viable privacy-preserving paradigm for multi-institutional clinical AI collaboration, balancing diagnostic utility with regulatory compliance for rare disease diagnosis.

Abstract: Rare diseases affect over $300$ million patients across more than $7{,}000$ conditions, yet no single hospital encounters enough cases of any one condition for reliable diagnosis. Cross-hospital collaboration could help by allowing a diagnosing institution to use distributed, case-specific diagnostic evidence, but privacy regulations restrict the transmission of identifiable clinical text across institutional boundaries. This setting raises two challenges: existing medical agent systems often rely on textual evidence exchange, while raw latent states such as hidden states and KV caches may still reveal prompt-derived clinical content. We introduce MedLatentDx, a latent multi-agent communication framework in which hospital agents keep private clinical records and retrieved cases local, and send compact latent KV blocks to a host agent for rare-disease diagnosis. MedLatentDx supports two deployment settings: same-backbone hospital agents use latent KV distillation, while hospitals with different LLM backbones use cross-family latent alignment. On CrossRare-Bench, a self-built large-scale rare-disease benchmark with hospital-level partitions, MedLatentDx improves cross-hospital diagnostic performance while reducing reconstructable clinical content relative to raw-latent communication baselines.

</details>


### [14] [Creative Integration: A Decidable Criterion of Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13977)
*Yoshinori Nomura*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a formal, decidable criterion for "creative integration" (CI): a resolution of a conflict between A and B genuinely counts as creative integration iff, under a fixed description language, the description length strictly shrinks (C = L_pre/L_post > 1), with the compression localized to the conflict itself. Four binary gates and a taxonomy of pseudo-integrations make the judgment operational, and the criterion passes four falsifiable tests (computational check, hard-negative discrimination, out-of-sample prediction, language robustness).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: "Integrative" solutions are widely praised but lack an operational definition — there is no way to distinguish genuine integration (which makes the world cheaper to describe) from mere tidy re-description. The authors aim to fill this gap by grounding creative integration in a falsifiable, compression-based criterion.

Method: The authors build on the tradition treating creativity and intelligence as compression. They operationalize CI as a strict reduction in description length (C > 1) localized to the conflict being resolved. Decidability is achieved through four binary, conjunctive gates. A taxonomy of pseudo-integration names and rejects look-alikes. Validation uses four falsifiable tests: an independent computational check, discrimination against hard negatives, out-of-sample prediction, and description-language robustness, all evaluated against a curated multi-domain corpus.

Result: All four falsifiable tests pass with margin, supporting the criterion's validity. The approach demonstrates decidability, strong discrimination against hard negatives, successful out-of-sample prediction, and robustness across description languages.

Conclusion: The contribution is not the idea that "creativity is compression" but its decidability, discrimination, and corpus. Genuinely creative moves compress a conflict, with novelty and value as downstream symptoms. Whether all creativity fits this mold is left as an explicit conjecture. The result provides a citable primitive for a broader research program, judging rather than generating creative output.

Abstract: "Integrative" solutions are widely praised but rarely defined: we lack an operational way to tell a genuine integration -- one that makes the world cheaper to describe -- from a tidy re-description. Building on the lineage that
  treats creativity and intelligence as compression, we give such a criterion for creative integration (CI): the resolution of a real conflict between A and B is CI if and only if, under a fixed description language, the description
  length strictly shrinks (C = L_pre/L_post > 1), with the reduction located in the conflict itself. We make the judgment decidable through four binary, conjunctive gates, and we fix its extension through a taxonomy of
  pseudo-integration that names and rejects the look-alikes. We back the criterion with a curated, multi-domain corpus and -- crucially -- validate it not by human inter-rater agreement but by four falsifiable tests it could fail: an
  independent computational check, discrimination against hard negatives, out-of-sample prediction, and description-language robustness; all pass with margin. The contribution is not "creativity is compression" but its decidability,
  discrimination, and corpus: on this account, what makes a move genuinely creative -- rather than merely novel -- is that it compresses a conflict, with novelty and value as downstream symptoms; whether all creativity is so
  constituted we state as an explicit conjecture. We claim only the sign of C-1; we judge, not generate. The result is a citable primitive for a broader program.

</details>


### [15] [Fusing Stylometric and Embedding Systems to Estimate Authorship Likelihood Ratios in Japanese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13991)
*Praju Ghatpande,Satoru Tsuge,Shunichi Ishihara,Wataru Zaitsu,Mitsuyuki Inaba*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study pioneers the application of the likelihood ratio (LR) framework for forensic text comparison to Japanese digital texts, demonstrating that fusing traditional stylometric features with embedding-based systems (via pre-trained LLMs) within the LR paradigm improves calibration, discriminability, and LR magnitude alignment with ground truth.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The likelihood ratio framework—the gold standard for forensic evidence evaluation—has been restricted to English-language texts in authorship analysis. Additionally, while fusion of stylometric and embedding-based approaches promises performance gains, no prior work has combined them within the LR paradigm. This study addresses both gaps by extending LR-based forensic text comparison to Japanese and evaluating heterogeneous system fusion.

Method: Using ~1,000-character Japanese blog excerpts, the study implements multiple systems: (1) stylometric-feature-based systems using traditional authorship markers (e.g., character n-grams, lexical features), (2) embedding-based systems leveraging pre-trained large language models for contextual embeddings, and (3) fused systems combining both. All systems operate within the likelihood ratio framework, with performance evaluated via calibration (log-likelihood-ratio cost, Cllr), discriminability, and LR magnitude analysis for both consistent-with-fact and contrary-to-fact conditions.

Result: The fused system maintained excellent calibration while achieving: (1) increased LR magnitudes for consistent-with-fact (same-author) comparisons, (2) decreased LR magnitudes for contrary-to-fact (different-author) comparisons, and (3) improved overall discriminability. The best fusion model attained a Cllr of 0.32484, validating both the LR framework's applicability to Japanese texts and the efficacy of fusing heterogeneous authorship analysis systems.

Conclusion: The likelihood ratio framework is viable and effective for forensic authorship analysis of Japanese digital texts. Fusing stylometric and embedding-based systems within this paradigm yields calibrated, discriminative, and practically useful LR outputs, encouraging broader cross-linguistic and multi-system adoption of the LR framework in forensic text comparison.

Abstract: The likelihood ratio framework is widely recognized as the logically and legally sound basis for evidential analysis across forensic sciences, and its importance is increasingly acknowledged in analyses of authorship in textual evidence. To date, however, its application has been confined to English-language texts. Meanwhile, authorship attribution has traditionally relied on a diverse array of stylometric features, even as the rise of pre-trained large language models enables new contextual-embedding approaches. Combining these diverse approaches through fusion promises enhanced performance, yet it has not been applied to integrate stylometric-feature systems with embedding-based systems within the likelihood ratio paradigm. This study is the first to apply likelihood ratio-based forensic text comparison to Japanese digital texts, using ~1,000-character excerpts from blogs, to 1) evaluate system performance and likelihood ratio magnitudes and 2) assess the impact of fusing stylometric-feature systems with embedding-based systems. The results demonstrate that the fused system maintains excellent calibration while 1) increasing consistent-with-fact likelihood ratio magnitudes; 2) decreasing contrary-to-fact likelihood ratio magnitudes and 3) improving overall discriminability. The best-performing fusion achieved a log-likelihood-ratio cost of 0.32484, illustrating both the feasibility of likelihood ratio framework for Japanese and the benefits of fusion across heterogeneous systems.

</details>


### [16] [The Holistic Storage of Verb+Up Phrases in Text-based and Audio-based Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13993)
*Zachary Nicholas Houghton,Yu Zhou,Dan Pluth,Vijay K. Gurbani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether language models (text-based LLMs and an ASR model) develop holistic internal representations for V+up phrasal verbs, finding that both frequency and predictability drive distinct stored representations, supporting usage-based theories of language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While prior work has explored abstract, rule-based knowledge in language models, the complementary phenomenon of holistic storage — where frequently co-occurring multi-word units are stored as whole chunks rather than composed productively — has been largely neglected. The authors aim to fill this gap by probing whether models represent phrasal verbs holistically.

Method: The authors probe internal representations of text-based LLMs and an automatic speech recognition (ASR) model, focusing on V+up phrasal verbs. They test whether these constructions develop distinct, holistic representations as a function of two factors: (1) frequency of co-occurrence and (2) semantic predictability (compositionality).

Result: All models examined show evidence of holistic storage for phrasal verbs. Both higher frequency and lower predictability (i.e., greater idiomaticity) are associated with more distinct, chunk-like representations, consistent across different model architectures.

Conclusion: The findings provide computational support for usage-based linguistic theories, which posit that language users store frequent and unpredictable multi-word expressions as holistic units rather than generating them compositionally each time.

Abstract: A crucial aspect of linguistic capability is the ability to trade off between stored representations and abstract knowledge: one must retrieve learned representations, but also generate novel ones by applying productive rules. While recent work has examined abstract knowledge in language models, holistic storage of multi-word units has received far less attention. We probe internal representations in text-based LLMs and an ASR model, testing whether V+up phrasal verbs develop distinct representations as a function of frequency and predictability. All models show evidence of holistic storage driven by frequency and predictability, further supporting usage-based theories of language.

</details>


### [17] [Dialogue SWE-Bench: A Benchmark for Dialogue-Driven Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13995)
*Brendan King,Jeffrey Flanigan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Dialogue SWE-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating coding agents' ability to solve software engineering tasks through interactive dialogue with users, featuring a persona-grounded user simulator and a schema-guided agent that improves dialogue performance by 3-14%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing coding agent benchmarks evaluate agents as fully-autonomous systems, ignoring the interactive, dialogue-driven nature of real-world coding assistants. There is a gap in assessing how well agents collaborate with users through conversation.

Method: The authors create Dialogue SWE-Bench, adapting SWE-Bench tasks into interactive dialogue scenarios. They develop a persona-grounded user simulator to emulate realistic user interactions. They propose a schema-guided agent architecture to enhance off-the-shelf coding agents' dialogue capabilities. Evaluation includes both task success metrics and automatic dialogue quality assessment.

Result: The schema-guided agent improves over strong baselines by 3-14% on task resolution through dialogue. Results also reveal that stronger coding models do not necessarily yield better dialogue performance, indicating dialogue capability is a distinct dimension of agent performance.

Conclusion: Dialogue capability is an understudied but critical dimension of coding agent performance, orthogonal to raw coding ability. The proposed benchmark and schema-guided agent provide foundations for advancing interactive, dialogue-based coding assistance.

Abstract: AI coding agents have rapidly transformed software engineering, powering widely used interactive coding assistants. Despite their interactive real-world use, existing benchmarks evaluate them as fully-autonomous systems. In this work, we introduce Dialogue SWE-Bench, an automatic benchmark dataset for evaluating the ability of coding agents to resolve real-world software engineering problems through dialogue with a user. We design a novel, persona-grounded user simulator to support our task evaluation, and augment our task evaluation with automatic evaluations of dialogue quality. We also propose a new schema-guided agent, aimed at improving the dialogue capabilities of off-the-shelf coding agents, which improves over strong baselines by 3-14%. Our results indicate that better coding models do not always correspond to better dialogue models, suggesting that dialogue capability is a distinct and currently understudied dimension of coding agent performance.

</details>


### [18] [Right or Wrong, Models Comply: Directional Blindness in LLM Moral Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14037)
*Jihye Kim,Jeffrey Flanigan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Compliance Asymmetry, a bidirectional metric comparing how LLMs follow helpful vs. harmful nudges. Key finding: models show selective compliance on factual questions (helpful > harmful) but near-identical compliance rates for both directions on moral questions — a "direction-blind" failure mode that persists across model families, scales, and prompting strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM alignment evaluations treat compliance as unidirectional (measuring general resistance to pressure). This misses whether models resist pressure *selectively* — following beneficial corrections while rejecting misleading ones. The authors argue that true alignment requires directionally calibrated updating, not merely lower overall compliance.

Method: The authors define Compliance Asymmetry as A = BCR/HCR, where BCR (Beneficial Compliance Rate) measures how often models update answers after helpful nudges and HCR (Harmful Compliance Rate) measures updating after misleading nudges. They evaluate 9 models across 972,000 nudge-condition responses on factual and moral questions, using multiple nudging types (suggestions, corrections, authority appeals). They also test chain-of-thought and identity-based prompting interventions.

Result: On factual questions, A = 1.58 (models follow helpful nudges ~58% more than harmful ones). On moral questions, A ≈ 1.04 (nearly identical compliance in both directions). This pattern holds across model families, capability levels, and nudge types. Chain-of-thought prompting amplifies both helpful and harmful compliance together, while identity-based prompting suppresses both by nearly equal margins — neither intervention improves asymmetry on moral questions.

Conclusion: Direction-blind moral compliance represents a distinct alignment failure mode: models lack the capacity to selectively resist misleading moral pressure while accepting valid moral correction. The authors recommend that alignment research target directionally calibrated updating rather than simply reducing overall compliance rates.

Abstract: As language models take integrated roles across many domains, the response of LLMs to user pushback becomes a critical alignment property. Yet many existing evaluations treat compliance as unidirectional, measuring whether models resist pressure but not whether they resist it selectively. We introduce Compliance Asymmetry (A = BCR/HCR), a bidirectional diagnostic that compares beneficial output change under helpful nudges with harmful change under misleading nudges. Across 9 models and 972,000 nudge-condition responses, we find that this selectivity differs in factual and moral judgments: models follow helpful nudges more than harmful ones on factual questions (A = 1.58), but follow both directions at nearly identical rates on moral questions (A = 1.04). This phenomenon persists across model families, capability levels, and nudging types. Interestingly, we also find that chain-of-thought prompting amplifies helpful and harmful compliance together, while identity-based prompting suppresses both by nearly identical margins. These results identify direction-blind moral compliance as a distinct failure mode in current LLMs and suggest that alignment should target directionally calibrated updating rather than lower compliance alone.

</details>


### [19] [Harsher on Male? Evaluating LLMs on Gender-Asymmetric Moral Framing Across Diverse Conflict Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14068)
*Guangzong Si,Dong Wang,Zhenhao Li,Yifan Yu,Panwang Pan,Wentao Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GAMA-Bench introduces a gender-mirrored benchmark of 1,298 scenarios to test whether LLMs apply consistent response standards to identical misconduct by male vs. female actors. Across 10 LLMs, results reveal a systematic male-disadvantaging asymmetry: male actors receive more punitive and blame-oriented responses, while female actors receive more empathy and therapeutic framing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on gender bias in LLMs has focused on stereotypes, occupational associations, or explicit toxicity. The authors identify a gap: no systematic study examines whether LLMs apply differential response standards (punishment, empathy, blame) to the same negative behavior based solely on the actor's gender.

Method: The authors introduce GAMA-Bench with 1,298 scenarios across intimate relationship and public social conflict domains. They construct gender-neutral misconduct templates using controlled grids and cross-model review, then create paired first-person prompts varying only actor gender and role references. A structured response-framing protocol measures five dimensions: punishment, empathy, escalation, instruction, and blame allocation by LLMs across 10 representative models.

Result: A consistent male-disadvantaging asymmetry emerges: male actors receive significantly more punitive, escalatory, and blame-centered framing, while female actors receive more therapeutic and empathy-oriented responses for identical misconduct. This pattern holds across model families, scenario tracks, model scale, and even when models employ explicit reasoning.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit a systematic double standard in responding to misconduct based on actor gender, disadvantaging male actors. This finding reveals a previously underexplored dimension of gender bias in LLMs beyond stereotype reproduction, with implications for fairness in AI-mediated judgment and content moderation.

Abstract: Existing studies on gender bias in LLMs have largely focused on stereotypes, occupational associations, or explicit harmful outputs. In this work, we ask whether LLMs apply consistent response standards to the same negative behavior under matched male-actor and female-actor conditions. We introduce GAMA-Bench, a gender-mirrored benchmark of 1,298 scenarios covering intimate relationship and public social conflicts. It constructs gender-neutral misconduct templates through controlled grids and cross-model review, then compiles them into paired first-person prompts with matched actor-gender and role-reference variations. We further design a structured response-framing protocol to measure how models allocate punishment, empathy, escalation, instruction, and blame. Experiments on 10 representative LLMs reveal a consistent male-disadvantaging asymmetry: male actors receive more punitive, escalatory, and blame-centered framing, whereas female actors receive more therapeutic and empathy-oriented framing for the same misconduct. Further analyses show that this pattern persists across model families, scenario tracks, model scale, and explicit thinking-style reasoning. The official code is available at https://github.com/xufeiqiong/GAMA-Bench.

</details>


### [20] [Beyond Perplexity: UTF-8 Validity in Byte-aware Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14122)
*Sangwhan Moon,Daisuke Oba,Youmi Ma,Tatsuya Hiraoka,Naoaki Okazaki*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A 355M-parameter byte-level language model trained on 80B multilingual tokens shows that reliable UTF-8 generation emerges ~2× slower than perplexity convergence (4.2B vs 2.1B tokens). Rare characters paradoxically achieve higher structural validity than common ones, suggesting over-specialization. UTF-8 validity is a distinct capability requiring separate evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Byte-level tokenization offers universal Unicode coverage but risks producing invalid UTF-8 byte sequences. The authors aim to understand how training scale affects the reliability of UTF-8 generation, and whether this capability can be disentangled from standard language modeling performance (perplexity).

Method: A 355M-parameter model is trained on 80B tokens from a balanced multilingual corpus (English, Japanese, Korean, Chinese). Multiple evaluation protocols are designed to isolate UTF-8 structural validity from language modeling quality. The authors measure perplexity and UTF-8 validity convergence rates, and compare context-free generation validity across character frequency bins (rare vs. common).

Result: Perplexity stabilizes after 2.1B tokens, but UTF-8 validity requires 4.2B tokens — roughly a 2× lag. In context-free generation, rare characters surprisingly exhibit higher structural validity than common characters, indicating over-specialization of frequent character representations.

Conclusion: Reliable UTF-8 generation is a distinct capability from language modeling proficiency and cannot be proxied by perplexity alone. It requires dedicated evaluation protocols, and its slower convergence suggests that scaling laws for byte-level models must account for structural validity separately.

Abstract: Byte-level tokenization enables language models to handle any Unicode input, but models can generate invalid UTF-8 sequences when encountering rare or unseen characters. We investigate the relationship between training scale and UTF-8 generation reliability with a 355M parameter model trained on 80B tokens from a balanced multilingual corpus of English, Japanese, Korean, and Chinese. We introduce multiple evaluation protocols that isolate UTF-8 structural validity from language modeling. UTF-8 validity convergence lags perplexity by a roughly a factor of two: perplexity stabilizes after 2.1B tokens, but UTF-8 validity requires 4.2B tokens. In context-free generation, rare characters achieve higher structural validity than common characters, suggesting over-specialization of frequent character representations. Through experiments, we observed that reliable UTF-8 generation is a distinct capability requiring evaluation beyond perplexity.

</details>


### [21] [Implicit Reasoning for Large Language Model-based Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14142)
*Yinhan He,Liam Collins,Bhuvesh Kumar,Jundong Li,Neil Shah,Donald Loveland*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PauseRec introduces an implicit reasoning paradigm for LLM-based generative recommendation that eliminates costly multi-stage explicit rationale training, outperforming explicit CoT methods by up to 6.22% while reducing training cost by 65% and inference time by 71.3%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based generative recommendation systems rely on Semantic IDs (SIDs) to represent items, but SIDs are unseen tokens during pretraining, disrupting the LLM's natural-language reasoning interface. Existing approaches use expensive multi-stage pipelines with explicit rationales and SID grounding, yet it remains unclear when each stage is necessary and why performance degrades. The paper aims to systematically decompose these pipelines to understand their limitations and propose a more efficient alternative.

Method: The authors first systematically decompose explicit reasoning training pipelines for LLM-based GR, identifying three failure modes: weakened world-knowledge verbalization, misalignment between SID and natural-language token embeddings, and sensitivity to rationale quality. They then propose PauseRec, a lightweight implicit reasoning paradigm that bypasses reasoning trace acquisition and reasoning alignment training entirely, instead relying on a more direct integration of recommendation signals within the LLM.

Result: PauseRec outperforms standard explicit Chain-of-Thought (CoT) methods by up to 6.22% in recommendation performance. It achieves up to 65% reduction in GPU hours during training and up to 71.3% faster inference compared to explicit reasoning baselines.

Conclusion: Implicit reasoning via PauseRec is a practical and effective alternative to explicit rationale generation for LLM-based generative recommendation, delivering superior performance alongside significant gains in training and inference efficiency.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly adopted as backbones for Generative Recommendation (GR), promising access to pretrained world knowledge. Yet reliably invoking this knowledge for GR remains poorly understood. A key obstacle is that LLM-based GR typically represents items with Semantic IDs (SIDs), disrupting LLMs' natural-language reasoning interface because these tokens are unseen by the LLM during pretraining. Existing approaches address this with expensive multi-stage pipelines that ground SIDs and elicit explicit rationales, but offer limited insight into when and why each stage is necessary. In this work, we systematically decompose explicit reasoning training pipelines for LLM-based GR, revealing three key limitations: weakened world-knowledge verbalization, misalignment between SID and natural-language token embedding spaces, and sensitivity to rationale quality, all of which hurt explicit reasoning performance. To circumvent these issues, we propose PauseRec, a lightweight implicit reasoning paradigm tailored for GR. PauseRec is exceptionally practical, avoiding costly reasoning trace acquisition and reasoning alignment training, leading to a multitude of benefits: (1) it outperforms standard explicit CoT methods by up to 6.22%, (2) it reduces training cost by up to 65% GPU hours, and (3) it speeds up inference by up to 71.3%. These results position PauseRec as a lightweight alternative to explicit rationale generation, enabling more effective and efficient LLM-based GR.

</details>


### [22] [Personal Care Utility: Health as Everyday Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14145)
*Mahyar Abbasian,Elahe Khatibi,Saba A. Farahani,Nitish Nagesh,Arshia Ilaty,Hooman Sajjadi,Amir Rahmani,Ramesh Jain*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes the Personal Care Utility (PCU), a layered event-driven architecture that serves as the missing infrastructure for everyday health management outside clinical settings, instantiated for Type 2 Diabetes management.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Healthcare is designed around brief clinical encounters (~1 hour/year), leaving the 8,759 hours of daily life — where health behaviors actually shape outcomes — without comparable infrastructure. The bottleneck for personalized health is not data or AI capability but the absence of an infrastructure layer for continuous, everyday health guidance.

Method: PCU organizes continuous personal signals (CGM, meals, activity, medication, sleep, stress) into semantically meaningful "life events" via a Personicle. It estimates dynamic health state against personal baselines, reasons about cause and context, and routes guidance through an orchestrator that separates: (1) clinical decision logic, (2) behavioral strategy selection, and (3) natural-language expression. This separation allows LLMs to handle reasoning/communication while keeping safety-critical clinical decisions grounded in validated evidence. Instantiated for Type 2 Diabetes.

Result: A day-in-the-life scenario demonstrates the same infrastructure producing context-appropriate outputs: real-time nudges, weekly summaries, medication check-ins, deliberate silence, or deterministic safety alerts, all modulated by context and risk level.

Conclusion: PCU generalizes to other chronic conditions and treats personalization as an architectural property rather than merely a messaging layer. The paper also addresses governance questions inherent to any always-on personal health utility.

Abstract: Healthcare is essential, expert, and episodic by design - built around the roughly one hour per year a person spends with a clinician. The 8,759 hours outside clinical settings, where eating, sleeping, movement, medication, and stress actually shape long-term health, have no comparable infrastructure. The bottleneck for personalized health is not raw data or reasoning capability; it is the absence of that infrastructure layer. This paper introduces the Personal Care Utility (PCU): a layered, event-driven architecture proposed as the missing utility for everyday health, in the way that payments, networks, and power are utilities for their domains. PCU organizes continuous personal signals into semantically meaningful life events through a Personicle, estimates dynamic health state against personal baselines, reasons about cause and context, and routes guidance through an orchestrator that separates clinical decision logic, behavioral strategy selection, and natural-language expression. This separation lets large language models support reasoning and communication while keeping safety-critical clinical decisions grounded in validated evidence. We instantiate PCU for Type 2 Diabetes - turning CGM, meal, activity, medication, sleep, stress, and clinical data into glycemic events, individualized state estimates, causal explanations, and knowledge-grounded interventions. A day-in-the-life scenario shows the same infrastructure producing real-time nudges, weekly summaries, medication check-ins, silence, or deterministic safety alerts depending on context and risk. We close with how PCU generalizes to other chronic conditions and the governance questions any always-on personal health utility must address. The result is a blueprint that treats personalization not as a final messaging layer, but as an architectural property of everyday health guidance.

</details>


### [23] [CacheRL:Multi-Turn Tool-Calling Agents via Cached Rollouts and Hybrid Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14179)
*Md Amirul Islam,Sumiran Thakur,Huancheng Chen,Su Min Park,Jiayun Wang,Gyuhak Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CacheRL trains small agent models to achieve near-GPT-5 performance on tool-calling tasks with 100x less compute, using LLM reasoning traces, a fuzzy cache to avoid live tool execution costs, and cache-aware rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical agent training faces three challenges: (1) transferring tool-calling knowledge from large models at scale, (2) enabling RL without costly live tool execution, and (3) learning robustly from noisy cached environments.

Method: CacheRL introduces three innovations: (1) a hybrid thinking trajectory pipeline that augments trajectories with LLM-generated reasoning traces; (2) the CacheAgentLoop, a three-tier fuzzy cache that eliminates live execution costs with token-level masking to preserve fidelity; and (3) cache-tier-aware rewards that dynamically adjust answer-quality weights. Training proceeds via iterative SFT and GRPO on Qwen3-4B-Thinking.

Result: CacheRL improves Qwen3-4B-Thinking's validation reward from 0.43 to 0.78, achieves 92% process accuracy (vs. GPT-5's 94%), and delivers competitive results on public agentic benchmarks. Ablations show removing knowledge transfer drops performance by 41%, while cache-aware rewards contribute a 17% improvement. RL improves stability but yields limited gains beyond strong SFT.

Conclusion: Data quality and reward design matter more than complex optimization methods for building practical small agent models. CacheRL demonstrates that small models can approach frontier performance with well-designed data pipelines and caching strategies.

Abstract: We present CacheRL, a system for training small agent foundation models that achieves 92 percent process accuracy on multi-step tool-calling tasks, approaching GPT-5's 94 percent while requiring 100 times less compute. Our approach addresses three challenges in practical agent training: transferring tool-calling knowledge from large models at scale, enabling reinforcement learning without costly live tool execution, and learning robustly from noisy cached environments. CacheRL introduces three key innovations. First, a hybrid thinking trajectory pipeline augments agent trajectories with LLM-generated reasoning traces, producing training examples that teach models not only what tools to call but also why. Second, the CacheAgentLoop eliminates live execution costs through a three-tier fuzzy cache while preserving trajectory fidelity using token-level masking. Third, a cache-tier-aware reward dynamically adjusts answer-quality weights to avoid penalizing models for cache-induced limitations. Through iterative supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), CacheRL improves Qwen3-4B-Thinking's validation reward from 0.43 to 0.78. On public agentic tool-calling benchmarks, our model achieves competitive performance against frontier models such as GPT-5. Ablation studies show that removing knowledge transfer reduces performance by 41 percent, while cache-aware rewards contribute a 17 percent improvement. Interestingly, reinforcement learning improves training stability but yields limited gains beyond strong supervised fine-tuning, suggesting that data quality and reward design play a more important role than complex optimization methods in building practical small agent models.

</details>


### [24] [OdysSim: Building Foundation Models for Human Behavior Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14199)
*Xuhui Zhou,Weiwei Sun,Weihua Du,Jiarui Liu,Haojia Sun,Qianou Ma,Tongshuang Wu,Yiming Yang,Maarten Sap*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: OdysSim presents the largest open investigation of training LLMs as behavioral simulators, identifying a "Sim2Real gap" from helpfulness tuning. They introduce the SOUL taxonomy covering 5 capability axes across 62 datasets, curate a 21.4M-interaction corpus, and train an 8B model (OSim) that outperforms frontier models on 8/23 benchmark tasks, especially in conversational and social simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs post-trained for helpfulness converge to a homogeneous, overly agreeable "assistant register," creating a behavioral gap between simulated and real human behavior (Sim2Real gap).
