This undermines their utility as human simulators for interactive evaluation and social simulation, motivating a systematic effort to build dedicated behavioral foundation models.

Method: The authors propose SOUL, a taxonomy with five axes (CONV, SS, COG, ROLE, EVAL) unifying 62 datasets and 23 tasks. They curate the OdysSim corpus (21.4M interactions, 10B tokens) with back-generated social contexts. Their training recipe combines midtraining, task-specific reinforcement learning, and expert distillation to produce OSim, an 8B open model. They also develop LLM-as-judge RL reward-hacking detectors.

Result: OSim ranks first or tied-first on 8 of 23 SOUL-Index tasks, surpassing any single frontier model by this count. Gains are strongest on conversational and social tasks. OSim outputs are more human-like in length, formatting, and word choice. Zero-shot transfer to τ-bench shows near-human reaction alignment (93.2 vs. 93.5). LLM-as-judge RL induces reward-hacking patterns, which their detectors mitigate.

Conclusion: Behavioral foundation models require fundamentally rethinking standard LLM training paradigms. Moving beyond helpfulness-driven post-training toward dedicated behavioral simulation training yields models that better approximate real human behavior across diverse interactive contexts.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed as human simulators for interactive evaluation and social simulation. Yet helpfulness-driven post-training pulls them toward a homogeneous, overly agreeable assistant register, creating a behavioral Sim2Real gap. We present OdysSim, the largest open systematic investigation of behavioral foundation models, i.e., models trained to simulate human behavior at scale. We propose SOUL, a taxonomy of five capability axes (CONV, SS, COG, ROLE, EVAL) that unifies 62 datasets and 23 benchmark tasks under one framework. Specifically, we curate the OdysSim corpus (21.4M interactions, 10B tokens, retrofitted with back-generated social contexts), construct the SOUL-Index benchmark, and develop an end-to-end training recipe combining midtraining, task-specific RL, and expert distillation. The resulting open 8B OSim model ranks first or tied-first on 8 of 23 tasks, outperforming any individual frontier model by this count, with the strongest gains on conversational and social tasks. Its outputs are also more human-like in length, formatting, and word choice, and it transfers zero-shot to out-of-distribution user simulation on $τ$-bench, nearly matching real users on reaction alignment (93.2 vs. 93.5). We further show that LLM-as-judge RL induces reward-hacking patterns, and that our detectors can mitigate them during post-training. Together, our findings suggest that behavioral foundation models require rethinking the LLM training paradigm. We release all artifacts to support future research.

</details>


### [25] [Detecting undisclosed LLM-generated content in parliamentary texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14209)
*Minerva Suvanto,Andrea McGlinchey,Peter J. Barclay,Mattias Wahde*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study quantifies undisclosed LLM-generated content in UK and Swedish parliamentary texts from 2022 onward, using a glass-box classifier trained on pre-LLM and LLM-generated parliamentary documents, and finds a steady increase in covert AI usage in both parliaments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While disclosure of AI use is required in domains like journalism and academia, parliamentary guidelines on AI disclosure remain vague. This ambiguity threatens transparency and public trust. The authors aim to empirically measure the extent to which parliamentarians are using LLMs without disclosure.

Method: An interpretable (glass-box) text classifier is trained on two corpora: (1) pre-LLM-era parliamentary texts and (2) LLM-generated versions of those same texts. This classifier is then applied to a test set of recent parliamentary texts from the UK and Swedish parliaments to detect undisclosed LLM use.

Result: The classifier reveals a steady increase in undisclosed LLM-generated content in parliamentary texts from both the United Kingdom and Sweden, beginning in 2022.

Conclusion: There is a growing trend of covert LLM use in parliamentary writing in both countries post-2022, highlighting a transparency gap that warrants clearer disclosure guidelines to safeguard public trust in legislative processes.

Abstract: In this paper, we evaluate the extent of undisclosed LLM-generated content in texts from the parliaments of the United Kingdom and Sweden. In many areas, such as in journalism or in academic writing, there are often requirements to clearly disclose whether AI tools, such as LLMs, have been used. In the case of parliamentary texts, the guidelines on disclosure of AI use are more vague. However, in order to maintain transparency and retain public trust, it is generally recommended that parliamentarians should state whether or not they have used AI when writing texts, such as parliamentary motions. Here, we train an interpretable (glass-box) text classifier using pre-LLM parliamentary texts and LLM-generated versions of such texts. We then apply the classifier to a test set containing recent parliamentary texts, finding a steady increase in undisclosed LLM use, in both parliaments, from 2022 onwards.

</details>


### [26] [Decoupled Mixture-of-Experts for Parametric Knowledge Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14243)
*Baoqing Yue,Weihang Su,Qingyao Ai,Yichen Tang,Changyue Wang,Jiacheng Kang,Jingtao Zhan,Yiqun Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DMoE proposes a modular approach to inject external knowledge into LLMs by decoupling expert modules and router from the base model, using lightweight uncertainty-aware routing attached only to the final FFN layer for efficient inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge injection methods face a trade-off: retrieval-augmented generation offers only prompt-level augmentation, while post-training (fine-tuning) methods suffer from catastrophic forgetting, knowledge conflicts, and expensive updates. A flexible, modular, and parameter-level injection method is needed.

Method: DMoE converts external knowledge corpora into independently updatable expert modules and employs a lightweight uncertainty-aware router that activates experts only when the base model lacks sufficient knowledge. Experts are attached solely to the final-layer feed-forward network (FFN), preserving KV-cache reuse. The architecture decouples both the experts and the router from the base model parameters.

Result: Experiments on knowledge-intensive benchmarks demonstrate that DMoE consistently improves answer quality compared to retrieval-based and adapter-based baselines.

Conclusion: DMoE provides an effective parametric knowledge injection framework that balances flexibility and integration, enabling modular, updatable expert knowledge without compromising base model performance or inference efficiency.

Abstract: Knowledge injection aims to equip large language models (LLMs) with external, domain-specific, or time-sensitive knowledge. Existing approaches typically face a trade-off between flexibility and integration: retrieval-augmented generation keeps knowledge outside the model but only provides prompt-level augmentation, whereas post-training based methods encode new knowledge into shared parameters but may introduce catastrophic forgetting, knowledge conflict, and costly updates. In this paper, we propose Decoupled Mixture-of-Experts (DMoE), a modular architecture for parametric knowledge injection that decouples both experts and the router from the base model. DMoE converts external knowledge corpora into independently updatable expert modules and uses a lightweight uncertainty-aware router to activate relevant experts only when the base model lacks sufficient knowledge during generation. To support efficient auto-regressive inference, DMoE attaches experts only to the final-layer feed-forward network, preserving KV-cache reuse while enabling parameter-level knowledge augmentation. Experiments on knowledge-intensive benchmarks show that DMoE consistently improves answer quality over retrieval and adapter-based baselines.

</details>


### [27] [The Linguistics Olympiads: Towards a New Corpus for Linguistics Research?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14257)
*Vlad A. Neacsu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper evaluates linguistics olympiad problems (LOPs) as a novel corpus for academic linguistics research, analyzing their strengths, limitations, and potential applications across linguistic subfields, while proposing criteria for their responsible scholarly use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite the global reach of linguistics olympiads (43 territories in IOL 2025) and recent interest in LOPs as LLM benchmarks, their scientific value and connections to academic linguistics remain unexplored. The authors aim to bridge this gap by establishing a theoretical framework for integrating LOPs into mainstream linguistics research.

Method: The study conducts a structured, critical evaluation of a corpus of over 1800 LOPs, examining their potential as linguistic data sources. It assesses their strengths and limitations as research tools and identifies specific linguistic subfields where these problems could be productively applied.

Result: LOPs demonstrate direct connections to multiple linguistic domains including linguistic typology, linguistic relativity, and fieldwork. Their recent adoption as benchmarks for large language models underscores their relevance in computational linguistics. The study proposes concrete criteria for the responsible integration of LOPs into academic research.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a foundational theoretical framework aimed at bridging the gap between linguistics olympiads and academic linguistics, opening new directions for incorporating LOPs as legitimate linguistic data sources in scholarly research.

Abstract: Linguistics olympiad problems (LOPs) are a category of self-sufficient puzzles consisting of a scaled-down corpus representative of certain linguistic phenomena, from which the solver must deduce a primitive set of rules of the language and then translate a new set of elements. The linguistics olympiads (LOs) have become a worldwide phenomenon with 43 different territories taking part in the International Linguistics Olympiad (IOL) 2025. While the typology and solving strategies of LOPs have been analysed, their scientific facet and connections to academic linguistics have yet to be explored. LOPs are directly connected to many linguistic fields, e.g., linguistic typology, linguistic relativity, and linguistics fieldwork. Recently, LOPs have become a research focus as benchmarks for large language models, thus highlighting their usefulness in computational linguistics. Nevertheless, they have not yet been integrated into mainstream linguistics research. This paper attempts to open new directions of including this particular type of puzzle in academic research by offering a structured evaluation of LOPs as linguistic data sources and proposes criteria for their responsible use in academic research. Starting from a set of over 1800 LOPs, this study critically examines the potential of LOPs as a novel corpus for linguistics research by discussing their strengths and limitations as tools, as well as the areas of linguistics into which these problems could fit. This work forms the foundation for a broader initiative aimed at bridging the gap between LOs and academic linguistics, by establishing a robust theoretical framework for LOPs.

</details>


### [28] [Does the Judge Prefer English? Evaluating Language-Switching Invariance in LLM-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14278)
*Shaojie Yin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Judge-LS is a meta-evaluation protocol that tests whether LLM-based judges change their preferences when evaluation items are translated into Chinese, English, or mixed-language variants. Across four API-accessible judges on the LLMBar benchmark, language switching induces 10.7–14.4% preference flips, with peak accuracy in English, though translation-equivalent tie probes show no systematic English bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as automatic judges for open-ended instruction-following tasks, but their reliability across languages is underexplored. The authors ask whether judges evaluate answer quality or react to the language of presentation — a critical validity concern for multilingual evaluation pipelines.

Method: Judge-LS transforms LLMBar pairwise comparison items into three language conditions (English, Chinese, Chinese-English code-switched) and introduces translation-equivalent tie probes where both answers are semantic equivalents. Four API-based judges are evaluated across 419 items, yielding 13,408 pairwise judgments. The protocol includes confidence intervals, paired significance tests, and a sensitivity analysis that flags and excludes mechanically high-risk transformations.

Result: Language switching induces 10.7–14.4% preference flips relative to English baselines; all judges achieve highest accuracy in English. Translation-equivalent tie probes do not reveal systematic English preference — most are judged as ties, and non-tie decisions more often favor Chinese. The protocol requires no model training and runs on modest hardware using only API calls.

Conclusion: LLM judges exhibit measurable language sensitivity, undermining their reliability as language-agnostic evaluators. While no systematic English preference emerges in tie-probe tests, the substantial preference flips under label-preserving transformations warrant caution when deploying automatic judges in multilingual settings.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are now widely used as automatic judges for open-ended instruction-following evaluation. This practice is convenient, scalable, and often more semantically aware than reference-based metrics, but it also introduces a new reliability question: does a judge evaluate the quality of an answer, or does it also react to the language in which the comparison is presented? We propose Judge-LS, a lightweight meta-evaluation protocol that transforms LLMBar response-pair items into English, Chinese, and Chinese-English language-switched variants. A reliable judge should preserve its preference under label-preserving language transformations and should not prefer a language when two answers are translation-equivalent. We evaluate four API-accessible judges on the full 419-item LLMBar benchmark, producing 13,408 successful pairwise judgments. Across models, Chinese and language-switched presentations induce 10.7--14.4% preference flips relative to English, and all judges achieve their highest accuracy in English. However, translation-equivalent tie probes do not reveal a systematic English preference: most probes are judged as ties, and non-tie decisions more often favor Chinese. We add confidence intervals, paired significance tests, and an automatic transformation audit with a sensitivity analysis that excludes mechanically flagged high-risk variants. The experiment requires no model training, uses only API calls, and is feasible on modest local hardware.

</details>


### [29] [Retrospective Progress-Aware Self-Refinement for LLM Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14302)
*Xinbei Ma,Congmin Zheng,Jiyang Qiu,Jiale Hong,Yao Yao,Xiangmou Qu,Jiaxin Yin,Xingyu Lou,Jun Wang,Weiwen Liu,Weinan Zhang,Zhuosheng Zhang,Hai Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RePro trains LLM agents to self-assess task progress retrospectively, using a forward-then-reflect rollout paradigm — improving long-horizon performance by up to 12% absolute success rate without continuous external supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents trained with RL optimize action prediction step-by-step but lack metacognitive awareness of task progress, creating a gap that limits long-horizon scaling. A pilot study shows that online progress prompting hurts performance while retrospective demonstrations help, but this capability cannot emerge from outcome-reward training alone.

Method: RePro introduces Retrospective Progress-Aware Training with two stages: (1) Retrospection Warmup — teaches the agent reflection format using minimal external demonstrations; (2) RePro-PO — further trains the agent using a composite reward that leverages self-generated progress signals. The core paradigm is "forward-then-reflect": the agent acts online, then retrospectively reassesses step-wise progress given the completed trajectory and known outcome.

Result: Experiments on WebShop, ALFWorld, and Sokoban demonstrate that RePro enhances the Qwen model family's performance, achieving up to 12% absolute success rate gains.

Conclusion: Retrospective progress-aware training effectively equips LLM agents with metacognitive progress assessment capabilities, enabling better long-horizon task scaling without requiring continuous external supervision signals.

Abstract: LLM-based agents trained with reinforcement learning optimize step-wise action prediction but lack metacognitive awareness of task progress, inducing a gap that hinders long-horizon scaling. A pilot study reveals that online progress prompting hurts performance while retrospective demonstrations help, yet this capability cannot emerge from outcome-reward training alone. We present RePro, Retrospective Progress-Aware Training, a framework that trains agents to self-generate progress signals via a forward-then-reflect rollout paradigm: the agent executes actions online, then retrospectively reassesses its step-wise progress given the completed trajectory and known outcome. RePro initializes with a Retrospection Warmup that teaches reflection format from minimal external demonstrations, then further trains through RePro-PO with a composite reward that produces self-generated signals without continuous external supervision. Experiments on WebShop, ALFWorld, and Sokoban show that RePro enhances the Qwen family's performance, with up to $12\%$ absolute success rate gains.

</details>


### [30] [Achieving Precise Text-To-Cypher Via Grounded Knowledge Graph Data Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14325)
*Francesco Cazzaro,Jessica Lennon,Ariadna Quattoni*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces an automatic synthetic data generation method for fine-tuning small LLMs on Text-to-Cypher tasks, demonstrating that these fine-tuned small models can match the performance of large proprietary models, enabling data-sovereign local deployment without manual annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Property Graphs are increasingly used for heterogeneous data representation, but accessing them requires conversational interfaces with Text-to-Cypher parsers. Existing large proprietary models are effective but cannot be locally deployed due to data sovereignty concerns, while small LLMs underperform on this task. Manual annotation campaigns are too costly, creating a need for automatic data generation methods.

Method: The authors propose an automatic synthetic data generation pipeline that creates training data for Text-to-Cypher tasks. This synthetic data is then used to fine-tune small language models, enabling them to translate natural language questions into Cypher queries for Property Graph databases.

Result: Experiments across all major Text-to-Cypher benchmarks show that small LLMs fine-tuned with synthetic data significantly improve in performance, reaching levels competitive with much larger proprietary models.

Conclusion: Synthetic data generation enables small LLMs to achieve competitive Text-to-Cypher performance, allowing organizations to deploy models locally while maintaining data sovereignty, accuracy, and avoiding expensive manual annotation efforts.

Abstract: Property Graphs are rapidly being adopted as database frameworks for representing heterogeneous data sources. To enable precise access to the information contained in them we need conversational interfaces based on Text-To-Cypher (Text2Cypher) parsers. This paper presents an automatic synthetic data generation method that can be leveraged to fine-tune small LLMs for this task. We conduct experiments on all the major Text-To-Cypher benchmarks, demonstrating that with our synthetic data generation approach we can significantly increase the performance of small LLMs, allowing them to compete with much larger proprietary models. This means that in settings in which models must be locally deployed we can ensure data-sovereignty without sacrificing accuracy and without costly annotation campaigns.

</details>


### [31] [Learning to Hear Hesitation: Continual Learning for Disfluency-Aware ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14391)
*Henri-Leon Kordt,Theresa Pekarek Rosin,Jae Hee Lee,Stefan Wermter*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper addresses disfluent speech recognition by introducing explicit disfluency tokens into pretrained ASR models and applying continual learning to adapt across diverse disfluency distributions, while analyzing the trade-off between marker learning and ASR performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art ASR systems are optimized to omit disfluencies, causing information loss and hallucinations. Prior verbatim transcription approaches risk catastrophic forgetting when fine-tuned on limited disfluency datasets. There is a need to preserve general-domain ASR knowledge while effectively modeling disfluencies.

Method: The authors introduce explicit disfluency tokens into a pretrained ASR model to establish stable token mechanisms. Then they apply continual learning (CL) methods to continue training on additional datasets with varying disfluency distributions. They also perform a detailed analysis of model dynamics, including cross-attention head mechanisms.

Result: The paper identifies a fundamental trade-off between disfluency marker learning and overall ASR performance. It also reveals a consistent cross-attention head mechanism that is shared across different continual learning methods.

Conclusion: Continual learning with explicit disfluency tokens is a viable strategy for adapting ASR models to disfluent speech without catastrophic forgetting. The identified cross-attention mechanism provides insights into how models represent and process disfluencies.

Abstract: Despite advances in large-scale Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR), disfluent speech remains challenging, as state-of-the-art systems are often optimized to omit disfluencies, leading to information loss and hallucinations. Prior work has focused on verbatim transcription and the integration of disfluency markers, but adapting models on limited datasets can lead to catastrophic forgetting of general-domain knowledge. We address this gap by leveraging continual learning (CL) with explicit disfluency tokens. We first introduce these tokens into a pretrained ASR model to establish stable token mechanisms, and then continue training on additional datasets with varying disfluency distributions. Through a detailed analysis of model dynamics during training, we identify a trade-off between marker learning and ASR performance, and a consistent cross-attention head mechanism shared across CL methods.

</details>


### [32] [Coping in Crisis: Computational Modeling of Coping Styles in Digital Crisis Discourse During the 2023 Turkiye Earthquake](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14420)
*Şevval Çakıcı*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Researchers developed a BERTurk-based multi-label classifier to detect three coping styles (problem-focused, emotion-focused, meaning-making) in over one million Turkish tweets following the 2023 Türkiye earthquake, finding distinct temporal patterns in how people coped and demonstrating that coping theory can be operationalized in real-time crisis data to inform humanitarian response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study is motivated by the need to understand how populations cope with large-scale disasters in real time through their digital expressions, and whether coping theory can be reliably operationalized at scale using social media data. The February 2023 Türkiye earthquake provided a natural test case situated in a politically polarized pre-election context, offering an opportunity to examine coping dynamics under compound stress.

Method: The authors collected over one million Turkish-language tweets posted after the February 6, 2023 earthquake. Drawing on Lazarus and Folkman's (1984) coping theory, they developed a multi-label BERTurk classifier to detect three coping styles — problem-focused, emotion-focused, and meaning-making — across four theoretically motivated crisis phases (urgency, emergency, recovery, and restoration). Performance was benchmarked against a zero-shot mDeBERTa baseline. The classifier was then applied to the full corpus, and temporal trajectories of coping styles were analyzed alongside emotion correlations (e.g., anger) using Spearman's rank correlation.

Result: BERTurk achieved a macro F1 of 0.693, substantially outperforming mDeBERTa (macro F1 = 0.324). The classifier revealed clear temporal patterns: problem-focused coping dominated the urgency phase then declined sharply; emotion-focused coping rose and stabilized; meaning-making increased monotonically over time. Anger correlated most strongly with meaning-making (Spearman r = 0.387), suggesting it serves as a mobilizing force toward blame attribution rather than practical action.

Conclusion: Coping theory can be reliably operationalized in real-world digital crisis data. The temporal divergence of coping styles has practical implications: humanitarian organizations can use such real-time signals to tailor interventions — for example, prioritizing logistical support when problem-focused coping dominates early, and shifting to psychosocial or informational support as emotion-focused and meaning-making coping rise.

Abstract: How do people cope when disaster strikes and can we detect it at scale, in real time, from what they write? This study addresses that question using over one million Turkish-language tweets posted in the aftermath of the February 6, 2023 earthquake in Turkiye, which unfolded in a deeply polarized political context just months before a national election. Drawing on Lazarus and Folkman's (1984) coping theory, we develop a multi-label BERTurk classifier to detect three coping styles (problem-focused, emotion-focused, and meaning-making) across four theoretically motivated crisis phases. BERTurk achieves a macro F1 of 0.693, substantially outperforming a zero-shot mDeBERTa baseline (macro F1 = 0.324). Applied to the full corpus, the classifier reveals a clear temporal trajectory: problem-focused coping dominates the urgency phase and declines sharply, emotion-focused coping rises and stabilizes, and meaning-making increases monotonically. Anger correlates most strongly with meaning-making (Spearman r = 0.387), suggesting it functions as a mobilizing force toward blame attribution rather than practical action. These findings demonstrate that coping theory can be reliably operationalized in real-world digital crisis data and that doing so can help humanitarian organizations tailor their responses to where a population actually is.

</details>


### [33] [MoDiCoL: A Modular Diagnostic Continual Learning Dataset for Robust Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14459)
*Theresa Pekarek Rosin,Matthias Kerzel,Stefan Wermter*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces MoDiCoL, a diagnostic dataset for studying ASR robustness under co-occurring distribution shifts (accent, noise, impairment), and proposes a continual learning curriculum to analyze how robustness is acquired, transferred, and forgotten across evolving real-world conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ASR systems perform well on clean benchmarks but degrade under real-world distribution shifts. Existing datasets isolate factors like noise, accent, or speech impairment, failing to capture their co-occurrence. The authors argue robustness should be treated as a dynamic, continually developing capability and that current evaluation paradigms cannot reveal how it evolves.

Method: The authors propose MoDiCoL (Modular Diagnostic Continual Learning dataset), which systematically combines linguistic content, speaker characteristics, and acoustic environments for controlled analysis. They also design a real-world-inspired continual learning curriculum with simulated incremental updates, evaluating three continual learning strategies to study robustness acquisition, transfer, and forgetting.

Result: The paper provides detailed empirical insights into how robustness develops under evolving conditions, comparing three continual learning strategies. Specific quantitative results are not detailed in the abstract but are promised as part of the analysis of robustness acquisition, transfer, and forgetting dynamics.

Conclusion: Treating ASR robustness as a dynamic, continually developing capability yields richer insights than static benchmarking. The MoDiCoL dataset and curriculum offer a principled framework for diagnosing how real-world distribution shifts—especially their co-occurrence—affect model performance over time.

Abstract: Modern Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR) systems have made remarkable progress on standard benchmarks, yet performance gaps have emerged under real-world distribution shifts, caused by recording conditions, accents, speech impairments, and noise. Existing datasets and benchmarks typically isolate these factors, which overlooks their co-occurrence in real-world applications. In this paper, we argue that model robustness can be treated as a dynamic capability that continually develops, and we introduce MoDiCoL, a Modular Diagnostic Continual Learning dataset designed for controlled analysis of linguistic content, speaker characteristics, and acoustic environments. Furthermore, we propose a real-world-inspired continual learning curriculum to simulate incremental updates and study how robustness is acquired, transferred, and forgotten. We evaluate three continual learning strategies and provide detailed insights into robustness under evolving conditions.

</details>


### [34] [Fodor and Pylyshyn's Systematicity Challenge Still Stands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14512)
*Michael Goodale,Salvador Mascarenhas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper challenges recent claims that neural networks have resolved Fodor and Pylyshyn's systematicity argument. By testing Lake and Baroni's meta-learning for compositionality model, the authors show it fails on both out-of-distribution and many within-distribution tasks, concluding the systematicity challenge remains unmet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent neural network successes in language generation have led some researchers to claim that classical cognitive science puzzles — particularly Fodor and Pylyshyn's systematicity argument — have been solved. The authors aim to rigorously test whether these claims, especially those by Lake and Baroni, actually hold up under scrutiny.

Method: The authors evaluate Lake and Baroni's meta-learning for compositionality (MLC) model by testing its ability to learn compositional rules both within and outside its training distribution, examining whether the model exhibits genuine systematic biconditional dependencies of the kind Fodor and Pylyshyn described.

Result: The model struggles to learn rules even slightly out of distribution relative to training data and behaves unsystematically on many within-distribution problems, indicating that the claimed resolution of the systematicity challenge is unfounded.

Conclusion: Fodor and Pylyshyn's challenge to neural networks remains unmet; claims that neural networks have resolved the systematicity puzzle are premature and not supported by rigorous empirical evaluation.

Abstract: The recent successes of neural networks producing human-like language have caused significant stir in cognitive science, with many researchers arguing that classical puzzles about human cognition and challenges to artificial intelligence are being solved by neural networks. A notable case is the argument from systematicity due to Jerry Fodor and Zenon Pylyshyn, argues that humans display systematic biconditional dependencies. For example, someone can understand the sentence "John saw Mary" just in case that they understand the sentence "Mary saw John." Symbolic systems explain this systematicity of language and thought, while neural networks offer no immediate explanation. Several recent articles argue that this challenge has now been met by neural networks. In particular, Brenden Lake and Marco Baroni argue that their meta-learning for compositionality protocol matches and perhaps explains human systematicity. We demonstrate that these conclusions are premature. Among other results, we found that their model struggles to learn rules that are even slightly out of distribution compared to their training data. Furthermore, the model behaves unsystematically even on many within-distribution problems. We conclude that Fodor and Pylyshyn's challenge to neural networks remains unmet.

</details>


### [35] [BayLing-Duplex: Native Full-Duplex Speech Dialogue with a Single Autoregressive LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14528)
*Qingkai Fang,Shoutao Guo,Yang Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: BayLing-Duplex is a native full-duplex SpeechLM that uses a single autoregressive LLM with special tokens to handle real-time, simultaneous listen-and-speak interaction without external VAD modules, achieving 92% turn-taking success and 100% interruption success while maintaining response quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SpeechLMs like LLaMA-Omni and GLM-4-Voice are turn-based and depend on external Voice Activity Detection (VAD) to mark turn boundaries, which fundamentally limits their ability to handle natural conversational phenomena such as overlap, hesitation, and barge-in. There is a need for a native full-duplex system that can autonomously decide when to listen, speak, and stop.

Method: BayLing-Duplex is built on a single autoregressive LLM with a minimal design: only a few special tokens (e.g., for listening, speaking, and stopping) are added to the standard vocabulary. This enables turn-taking decisions within the model itself, requires no architectural changes, and reuses existing training and serving infrastructure. Starting from the GLM-4-Voice checkpoint, the model is fine-tuned on only 400K full-duplex samples, followed by a lightweight Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) stage.

Result: On InstructS2S-Eval, BayLing-Duplex achieves 92% turn-taking success and 100% interruption success, and improves the speech-response score from 2.17 to 3.39 compared to Moshi. It also matches or surpasses its turn-based counterpart on Llama Questions, Web Questions, and Alpaca-Eval, demonstrating that full-duplex modeling does not degrade response quality.

Conclusion: A native full-duplex SpeechLM can be realized with a simple, transferable token-based design, achieving strong interactive performance without sacrificing response quality. This approach eliminates the bottleneck imposed by external VAD modules and enables more natural, simultaneous conversational speech interaction.

Abstract: Real-time, full-duplex speech interaction is a key feature of next-generation spoken chatbots, allowing the model to listen and speak at the same time and to handle natural phenomena such as overlap, hesitation, and barge-in. Existing speech language models (SpeechLMs) such as LLaMA-Omni and GLM-4-Voice are still turn-based and rely on an external Voice Activity Detection (VAD) module to mark the end of the user's turn, which fundamentally limits their interactive ability. In this paper, we introduce BayLing-Duplex, a native full-duplex SpeechLM where a single autoregressive LLM decides when to listen, when to speak, and when to stop, with no auxiliary turn-taking module. The design adds only a few special tokens to the standard vocabulary, so it transfers across LLMs and reuses existing training and serving stacks with no architectural adaptation. Starting from the public GLM-4-Voice checkpoint and using only 400K full-duplex samples for fine-tuning followed by a lightweight DPO stage, BayLing-Duplex reaches 92% turn-taking success and 100% interruption success on InstructS2S-Eval, while improving the speech-response score from 2.17 to 3.39 over Moshi. BayLing-Duplex also matches or surpasses its turn-based counterpart on Llama Questions, Web Questions, and Alpaca-Eval, showing that simultaneous listen-and-speak modeling does not sacrifice response quality.

</details>


### [36] [SIMMER: Benchmarking Latent Failures in LLM Executable Planning with a World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14574)
*Xiaoxin Lu,Ranran Haoran Zhang,Rui Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SIMMER is a benchmark for evaluating latent failures in LLM planning for household tasks. Using a kitchen-domain symbolic world model with 77 actions and ~46,800 interactions, it detects plans that silently fail rather than halting immediately. Even frontier LLMs achieve only 17% error-free plans, with up to 56% containing latent failures (mostly irreversible). Counterfactual foresight simulation reduces latent failures by up to 72%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM planning benchmarks only evaluate immediate execution failures, overlooking latent failures — errors that do not halt plan execution but silently compromise goal achievement, sometimes causing irreversible harm. This gap is critical for autonomous agents deployed in real-world environments like households.

Method: The authors introduce SIMMER, a human-curated symbolic world model grounded in the kitchen domain. It defines 77 actions, 262 unique objects, and ~46,800 semantically realistic interactions derived from real-world cooking scripts. A state machine executor validates plans against this world model, detecting immediate precondition violations, latent hazards, and irreversible failures. The paper also proposes counterfactual foresight simulation — explicit state reasoning that anticipates downstream consequences — as a mitigation strategy.

Result: Experiments across six LLMs show frontier models achieve at most 17% error-free plans. Up to 56% of plans contain latent failures, with the majority leading to irreversible consequences. Counterfactual foresight simulation reduces latent failures by up to 72% and irreversible cases by up to 75%.

Conclusion: Latent failures are a pervasive and under-evaluated problem in LLM-based planning. Current models perform poorly at avoiding them. Explicit state reasoning through foresight mechanisms offers a promising direction for building more robust LLM planners.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as planners for autonomous agents in household environments. While existing benchmarks evaluate whether LLM-generated plans execute successfully, they overlook a critical type of failure: latent failures. Unlike immediate failures that trigger instant feedback at execution time and enable timely correction, latent failures do not immediately halt plan execution but silently compromise goal achievement. In severe cases, they cause irreversible harm. To address this gap, we introduce SIMMER, a benchmark for evaluating latent failures in LLM planning through a human-curated symbolic world model grounded in the kitchen domain. SIMMER defines a world model comprising 77 actions, 262 unique objects, and approximately 46,800 possible interactions that are semantically realistic, derived from real-world cooking scripts. It then leverages a state machine executor that validates plans against the world model and detects immediate precondition violations, latent hazards, and irreversible failures. Experiments across six LLMs show that even frontier models achieve at most 17% error-free plans. Moreover, up to 56% of plans contain latent failures, the majority of which lead to irreversible consequences. We further demonstrate that explicit state reasoning via counterfactual foresight simulation can reduce latent failures by up to 72% and irreversible cases by up to 75%, suggesting a promising direction for more robust LLM planners.

</details>


### [37] [Persuasion Index: A Theory-Guided Framework for Persuasion Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14580)
*Liancheng Gong,Zhiyang Wang,Yiwei Xu,Julia Mendelsohn*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Persuasion Index (PI), a theory-grounded taxonomy of 15 rhetorical dimensions with 55 rule-based sub-features, validated across four datasets to show it provides interpretable, lightweight predictive signal for persuasion-related outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying persuasive rhetorical cues is critical for detecting information manipulation, improving AI safety, and advancing public health communication, yet existing approaches lack a shared, theoretically grounded, transparent, and modular feature space for analyzing persuasion across diverse contexts.

Method: The authors propose Persuasion Index (PI), grounded in persuasion theories from psychology and communication. PI comprises 15 dimensions implemented via 55 sub-features using lexicons and rule-based detectors. The taxonomy is modular, allowing individual detectors to be replaced. Evaluation uses linear models on four public datasets varying in domain, style, and outcome measures. Dimension-level and cross-dataset analyses are conducted to assess predictive signal, interpretability, and consistency.

Result: Linear models using PI features yield meaningful predictive signal while remaining computationally lightweight. Dimension-level analyses reveal recurring associations between PI dimensions and persuasion outcomes across datasets, along with topic- and stance-specific variation. PI provides a shared, interpretable feature space for rhetorical pattern analysis.

Conclusion: PI offers a principled, auditable, open-source framework for analyzing persuasion in both human and AI-mediated communication, with strong theoretical grounding and practical utility demonstrated across diverse domains.

Abstract: Identifying persuasive rhetorical cues is critical across domains, from detecting information manipulation and improving AI safety to advancing public health communication. We propose Persuasion Index (PI), a taxonomy of 15 dimensions grounded in persuasion theories from psychology and communication, and one transparent implementation using 55 sub-features built from lexicons and rule-based detectors. The taxonomy is modular: individual detectors can be replaced while preserving the theoretical structure. By evaluating PI on four public datasets varying in domain, style, and outcome measures, we show that PI provides a shared feature space for interpreting rhetorical patterns associated with persuasion-related outcomes. Linear models show that PI features carry meaningful predictive signal while remaining computationally lightweight. Dimension-level analyses reveal recurring associations between PI dimensions and persuasion outcomes across datasets, while also highlighting topic- and stance-specific variation. We release PI as an open-source package and web interface for principled and auditable analysis of human and AI-mediated communication.

</details>


### [38] [LoSoNA: A Benchmark for Local Social Norm Adaptation in Group Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14600)
*Mateusz Winiarek,Maksymilian Bilski,Mateusz Jacniacki*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LoSoNA is a benchmark testing whether LLMs can infer hidden local conversational norms from group-chat transcripts and apply them in a single response turn. Evaluating eight models under varied prompting conditions, most struggle with naive prompting; explicit norm-aware prompting helps unevenly, with Gemini 3.1 Pro (84.2%) and Claude Fable 5 (81.6%) leading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online group chats operate under implicit, unstated local conversational norms, yet LLM-based agents' capacity to recognize and adapt to such norms remains largely unexplored. This gap motivates a structured evaluation of social norm inference in multi-party settings.

Method: The authors introduce LoSoNA, a benchmark comprising curated group-chat transcripts where non-subject participants exhibit a hidden local norm. Each scenario concludes with an elicitor turn that forces the subject model to respond in a way that reveals whether it inferred the norm. Eight frontier and open-weight models are evaluated under four prompting conditions that vary how explicitly the model is instructed to treat prior conversation as normative evidence.

Result: Naive prompting yields limited performance for most models. Explicit norm-aware prompting produces uneven gains: Gemini 3.1 Pro achieves 84.2% and Claude Fable 5 reaches 81.6%, while several other models show only marginal improvements or even regressions.

Conclusion: LoSoNA answers calls for evaluating LLM social capabilities by demonstrating that inferring local conversational norms from precedent remains a challenging task, with performance highly sensitive to prompting strategy and model choice.

Abstract: Online group chats are social spaces with local conversational norms that are rarely stated explicitly. The ability and willingness of LLM-based agents to recognize and adapt to these norms remains mostly unexplored. We introduce LoSoNA, a benchmark for local social norm adaptation in multi-party chat. Each scenario gives a subject model a curated group-chat transcript in which non-subject participants demonstrate a hidden local norm, followed by a final elicitor turn that forces a response revealing whether the subject has inferred that norm. We evaluate eight frontier and open-weight models under four prompting conditions that vary how explicitly the model is told to treat the prior conversation as evidence for how it should answer. Naive prompting remains limited for most models; explicit norm-aware prompting helps unevenly, with Gemini 3.1 Pro reaching $84.2\%$ and Claude Fable 5 reaching $81.6\%$, while several other models show small gains or regressions. LoSoNA contributes to recent calls for evaluating LLM social capabilities by testing whether models can infer local conversational norms from precedent and use them in a one-turn group-chat response.

</details>


### [39] [Characterizing Cultural Localization in AI-Generated Stories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14626)
*Shaily Bhatt,Supriti Vijay,Jeremiah Milbauer,Fernando Diaz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to detect "templated localization" in AI-generated stories by identifying and removing culture-specific lexical markers, then measuring narrative similarity. Applied to stories from 5 models across 193 nationalities, results show only 9-17% of vocabulary drives cultural variation, with remaining narratives exhibiting repeated multi-word sequences indicative of a shared, culturally-agnostic template. The study further assesses stereotypicality and offensiveness of cultural markers, finding markers from 19 Global South countries are, on average, offensive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the global deployment of AI, there is growing interest in culturally localized content generation. The authors aim to distinguish between superficial "templated localization" (swapping cultural markers like names/places in a fixed narrative) and genuine "holistic localization" (varying plots, values, and themes). No existing method quantitatively measures the extent to which AI-generated stories rely on templated versus holistic localization.

Method: The method involves two steps: (1) Identifying lexical tokens that distinguish stories across nationalities, thereby isolating cultural markers; (2) Removing these tokens and measuring the similarity of the residual narratives using multi-word sequence overlap. This pipeline is applied to stories generated by 5 models on 125 topics for 193 nationalities. Cultural markers are further evaluated for stereotypicality and offensiveness using computational and human-judgment-based assessments.

Result: Only 9-17% of vocabulary accounts for cross-nationality variation. After removing these cultural markers, the remaining narratives contain repeated multi-word sequences, confirming the presence of a shared, culturally-agnostic template. Cultural markers from 19 countries — predominantly in the Global South — are flagged as offensive on average, raising ethical concerns about stereotyped localization.

Conclusion: Current AI models predominantly perform templated rather than holistic cultural localization, relying on shallow marker swaps within a generic narrative template. This approach risks producing stereotypical and offensive content, particularly for Global South countries. The proposed detection method offers a diagnostic tool for evaluating and improving cultural localization in generative AI.

Abstract: The global use of artificial intelligence has increased interest in assessing the ability to generate culturally localized content, including stories. Cultural localization in stories often occurs through either templated localization -- the use of cultural markers (e.g., names, locations) in a generic narrative -- or holistic localization -- the variation of plots, values, and themes, in addition to cultural markers. We propose a method to measure the degree to which content was generated through templated localization. Specifically, we identify the lexical tokens that distinguish stories across nationalities and measure the similarity of the narratives that remain after removing them. In stories generated by five models on 125 topics for 193 nationalities, our method is able to detect that only a small subset (9-17%) of the vocabulary accounts for the variation across nationalities and that the narratives that remain after removing them contain repeated multi-word sequences, suggesting the presence of a shared culturally-agnostic narrative template. Finally, we characterize the cultural markers for their stereotypicality and offensiveness, finding that markers from 19 countries, mostly located in the Global South, are on average offensive.

</details>


### [40] [AgentSpec: Understanding Embodied Agent Scaffolds Through Controlled Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14674)
*Jixuan Chen,Jianzhi Shen,Haoqiang Kang,Zhi Hong,Qingyi Jiang,Soham Bose,Yiming Zhang,Leon Leng,Amit Vyas,Lingjun Mao,Siru Ouyang,Kun Zhou,Lianhui Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AgentSpec is a modular framework that decomposes LLM-based embodied agents into typed, reusable components (perception, memory, reasoning, reflection, action, learning) with standardized interfaces, enabling controlled swapping and recombination to study scaffold interactions. Experiments across four benchmarks reveal that agent performance depends more on component compatibility and interaction effects than on individual module quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly built as complex scaffolded systems combining multiple modules, but these are typically embedded in tightly coupled pipelines. This makes it difficult to isolate individual component contributions, compare alternative designs, or understand how module interactions shape overall agent behavior.

Method: AgentSpec introduces a typed compositional framework with standardized interfaces for six component types: perception, memory, reasoning, reflection, action, and learning. Components are specified as typed policies that can be swapped and recombined under controlled conditions. The framework is instantiated across four embodied benchmarks (DeliveryBench, ALFRED, MiniGrid, RoboTHOR) to systematically analyze reasoning, memory, reflection, and reinforcement-learning modules across different model backbones.

Result: Key findings: (1) Structured multi-granularity memory improves long-horizon state tracking; (2) reasoning and memory interact non-uniformly across environments—their joint effect is environment-dependent; (3) reflection involves a trade-off between error correction and computational cost; (4) RL-trained policies compose best when optimized with the deployment-time scaffold structure rather than in isolation.

Conclusion: AgentSpec demonstrates that scaffold compatibility and interaction effects, not isolated module strength, govern agent performance. It provides a controlled foundation for studying, comparing, and designing composable LLM agents, with code and baselines publicly available.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly built not as single model calls, but as scaffolded systems that combine reasoning, memory, reflection, action execution, and learning. While such scaffolds often improve performance, they are often embedded in tightly coupled pipelines, making it difficult to isolate component contributions, compare alternative designs, or understand how module interactions shape agent behavior. We introduce AgentSpec, a modular specification framework that represents embodied agents as typed compositions of reusable policy components with standardized interfaces. AgentSpec standardizes the interfaces among perception, memory, reasoning, reflection, action, and optional learning, enabling components to be swapped and recombined under controlled conditions. We instantiate this framework across DeliveryBench, ALFRED, MiniGrid, and RoboTHOR, and analyze reasoning, memory, reflection, and reinforcement-learning modules across model backbones. Our results show that agent performance is governed by scaffold compatibility and interaction effects rather than isolated module strength. In particular, structured multi-granularity memory improves long-horizon state tracking, reasoning and memory interact non-uniformly across environments, reflection trades off correction and cost, and RL-trained policies compose best when optimized with deployment-time scaffold structure. AgentSpec provides a controlled foundation for studying, comparing, and designing composable LLM agents. Our code, baselines and interactive playground are publicly available at https://agentspec-embodied.github.io.

</details>


### [41] [CORA: Analyzing and bridging thinking-answer gap in Multimodal RLVR via Consistency-Oriented Reasoning Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14691)
*Jiayue Cao,Zhicong Lu,Xuehan Sun,Wei Jia,Hongling Zheng,Changyuan Tian,Zichuan Lin,Wenqian Lv,Nayu Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper addresses thinking-answer semantic inconsistency in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for large vision-language models (LVLMs), proposes CORA (Consistency-Oriented Reasoning Alignment) with a consistency reward model and HRAS to mitigate this issue, improving both task performance and reasoning faithfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RLVR methods for multimodal scenarios focus on visual coverage and hallucination reduction but overlook the semantic inconsistency between the reasoning trace and the final answer. The authors demonstrate this thinking-answer inconsistency persists throughout GRPO training and during inference, motivating a dedicated solution.

Method: CORA introduces: (1) a lightweight plug-and-play consistency reward model that injects thinking-answer semantic consistency into the RLVR pipeline, and (2) Hybrid Reward Advantage Splitting (HRAS), which stably coordinates task reward optimization with consistency reward optimization by splitting advantage estimation for each reward type.

Result: Extensive experiments on representative multimodal reasoning benchmarks with mainstream LVLMs show that CORA improves task performance while effectively mitigating thinking-answer inconsistency, yielding more faithful reasoning traces.

Conclusion: Thinking-answer inconsistency is a critical yet underexplored problem in RLVR for LVLMs. CORA successfully addresses this through a consistency reward model and HRAS-based stable coordination, enhancing both reasoning faithfulness and downstream task accuracy.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has successfully elicited the reasoning capabilities of large language models, motivating its extension to multimodal scenarios. Existing methods primarily focus on improving the visual coverage of reasoning traces and mitigating visual hallucinations, but underestimate the semantic inconsistency between the reasoning process and the final answer. In this paper, we delve into thinking-answer inconsistency in RLVR for large vision-language models (LVLMs), showing thorough analyses of rollouts collected throughout Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) training process and post-RLVR evaluation outputs that this issue persists during training and remains present during inference. Motivated by the analysis, we propose Consistency-Oriented Reasoning Alignment (CORA), which introduces thinking-answer semantic consistency into RLVR through a lightweight plug-and-play consistency reward model, and further incorporates Hybrid Reward Advantage Splitting (HRAS) to stably coordinate task and consistency optimization. Extensive experiments across representative multimodal reasoning benchmarks and mainstream LVLMs show that CORA improves task performance while effectively mitigating thinking-answer inconsistency, leading to more faithful reasoning traces.

</details>


### [42] [GAGPO: Generalized Advantage Grouped Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13217)
*Siyuan Zhu,Chao Yu,Rongxin Yang,Zongkai Liu,Jinjun Hu,Qiwen Chen,Yibo Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GAGPO is a critic-free RL method that addresses temporal credit assignment in multi-turn LLM agents by constructing non-parametric grouped value proxies from sampled rollouts, enabling TD/GAE-style advantage computation without auxiliary value models. It outperforms baselines on ALFWorld and WebShop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-turn LLM agents typically receive only sparse, trajectory-level rewards at episode end, making it difficult to attribute credit to individual intermediate actions. Existing solutions rely on costly auxiliary value models (critics), and propagating delayed outcomes back to specific decision steps without such models remains an open problem.

Method: GAGPO constructs a non-parametric grouped value proxy directly from sampled rollouts. It computes TD(λ)/GAE-style temporal advantages by recursively propagating outcome supervision backward through time. This is combined with group-wise advantage normalization and an action-level importance ratio to extract stable, step-localized optimization signals from multi-turn trajectories — all without training a separate critic network.

Result: GAGPO outperforms strong RL baselines on ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks. Ablation analyses show faster early-stage learning, improved interaction efficiency, and smoother optimization dynamics compared to existing methods.

Conclusion: GAGPO provides a simple yet effective critic-free framework for multi-turn agentic RL, demonstrating that precise step-level credit assignment can be achieved without auxiliary value models by leveraging grouped rollouts and recursive temporal advantage computation.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has become a powerful paradigm for post-training large language model agents, yet credit assignment in multi-turn environments remains a challenge. Agents often receive sparse, trajectory-level rewards only at the end of an episode, making it difficult to determine which intermediate actions contributed to success or failure. As a result, propagating delayed outcomes back to individual decision steps without relying on costly auxiliary value models remains an open problem. We propose Generalized Advantage Grouped Policy Optimization (GAGPO), a critic-free reinforcement learning method for precise, step-aligned temporal credit assignment. GAGPO constructs a non-parametric grouped value proxy from sampled rollouts and uses it to compute TD/GAE-style temporal advantages, recursively propagating outcome supervision backward through time. Combined with group-wise advantage normalization and an action-level importance ratio, GAGPO extracts stable, localized optimization signals directly from multi-turn trajectories. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop show that GAGPO outperforms strong reinforcement learning baselines. Further analyses demonstrate faster early-stage learning, improved interaction efficiency, and smoother optimization dynamics, suggesting that GAGPO offers a simple yet effective framework for multi-turn agentic reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [43] [AdaSR: Adaptive Streaming Reasoning with Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14694)
*Junlong Tong,Wenqi Xu,Yingqi Fan,Anhao Zhao,Xuan Lu,Yang Tan,Xiaoyu Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: AdaSR is an adaptive streaming reasoning framework that lets models reason during input streaming and perform final deliberation after the stream ends. It uses Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization (HRPO) to learn when and how much to think at different stages, balancing accuracy, efficiency, and latency better than supervised fine-tuning baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing large reasoning models follow a read-then-think paradigm unsuitable for dynamic streaming scenarios (audio, video). Current streaming reasoning methods rely on supervised imitation of pre-constructed trajectories, limiting flexibility. There is a need for models that can autonomously decide when to think and how much computation to allocate during streaming input.

Method: The authors propose AdaSR, an adaptive streaming reasoning framework with two phases: streaming reasoning (thinking while reading) and final deliberation (after stream completion). They introduce Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization (HRPO), which decomposes policy optimization into these two phases with fine-grained, phase-specific advantage assignment, replacing uniform sequence-level advantages. HRPO incorporates three reward types: format rewards (enforcing reasoning protocols), accuracy rewards (preserving task performance), and adaptive thinking rewards (encouraging latency-aware computation allocation).

Result: Experiments show that AdaSR achieves a better balance among reasoning accuracy, computational efficiency, and streaming latency compared with supervised fine-tuning baselines.

Conclusion: AdaSR with HRPO provides an effective approach for adaptive streaming reasoning, enabling models to flexibly allocate computation across streaming and deliberation phases, yielding improved trade-offs between accuracy, efficiency, and latency in dynamic input scenarios.

Abstract: Large reasoning models typically follow a read-then-think paradigm: they observe the complete input, reason over a static context, and then produce the answer. Yet many real-world scenarios are inherently dynamic, such as audio and video stream, where information arrives as a continuous stream and models must reason, update, and respond under partial observations. Recent streaming reasoning methods allow models to think while reading, but they largely rely on supervised imitation of pre-constructed trajectories, which limits their flexibility. In this paper, we propose AdaSR, an adaptive streaming reasoning framework that enables models to reason during input streaming and perform final deliberation once the stream is complete, learning when to think, and how much computation to allocate across different stages. To optimize this hierarchical reasoning process, we introduce Hierarchical Relative Policy Optimization (HRPO), which decomposes policy optimization into streaming reasoning and deep reasoning phases, providing more fine-grained advantage assignment instead of uniformly distributing a single sequence-level advantage over all tokens. HRPO integrates format, accuracy, and adaptive thinking rewards to enforce valid reasoning protocols, preserve final task performance, and encourage latency-aware computation allocation. Experiments show that AdaSR achieves a better balance among reasoning accuracy, computational efficiency, and streaming latency compared with supervised fine-tuning baseline. We release our code at https://github.com/EIT-NLP/StreamingLLM/tree/main/AdaSR.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

### [44] [FreoStream:Enhancing Stream Guardrails via Future-Aware Reasoning and Safety-Aligned Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13737)
*Jianwei Wang,Guoyang Shen,Yanhong Wu,Haoran Li,Hao Peng,Huiping Zhuang,Cen Chen,Ziqian Zeng*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: FreoStream is a streaming guardrail framework that uses Future-Aware Reasoning (predicting future content before judging) and Safety-Aligned Optimization to reduce over-refusal and improve jailbreak detection in token-level safety systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming guardrails suffer from two key problems: (1) over-refusal — blocking safe but sensitive tokens due to overly conservative judgments, and (2) failure to detect implicitly harmful jailbreak content due to lack of full context. These limitations motivate the need for a more context-aware streaming safety mechanism.

Method: FreoStream employs two main components: (1) A LoRA fine-tuned module that performs Future-Aware Reasoning following a Future-Reason-Judge paradigm — when the base guardrail flags an unsafe token, the system predicts future content, reasons about the full context, and then delivers a final judgment to reduce over-refusal. (2) A Safety-Aligned Optimization module that extracts safety-aligned components from reasoning gradients to update the base guardrail, enhancing its detection capability.

Result: Extensive experiments across multiple safety benchmarks show that FreoStream achieves lower over-refusal rates and stronger jailbreak defense compared to existing streaming guardrail methods.

Conclusion: FreoStream effectively addresses the dual challenges of over-refusal and inadequate jailbreak detection in streaming guardrails by incorporating future-aware reasoning and safety-aligned gradient optimization, advancing the state of token-level safety detection.

Abstract: Stream guardrails enable token-level safety detection before full responses are generated. However, they often make overly conservative judgements and block those sensitive but safe tokens, which is known as over-refusal. Due to lack of full context, they also fail to detect implicitly harmful content from jailbreaking. To address these challenges, we propose FreoStream, a novel streaming guardrail framework. Specifically, FreoStream fine-tunes a LoRA module to perform Future-Aware Reasoning when the base guardrail detects unsafe tokens. The reasoning process follows a Future-Reason-Judge paradigm: predict the future, reason about the full context and give the final judgement. This design can effectively reduce over-refusal by incorporating the future information. Moreover, we introduce the Safety-Aligned Optimization module that extracts the safety-aligned component from the reasoning gradients to update the base guardrail model, thereby enhancing streaming safety detection. Extensive experiments on various safety benchmarks demonstrate that FreoStream achieves lower over-refusal rates and better jailbreak defense compared to existing streaming guardrails.

</details>


### [45] [SEVRA-BENCH: Social Engineering of Vulnerabilities in Review Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13757)
*Rui Melo,Riccardo Fogliato,Sean Zhou,Pratiksha Thaker,Zhiwei Steven Wu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: SEVRA-BENCH is a benchmark measuring how often LLM-based code reviewers approve malicious pull requests where both the code change and accompanying text are adversarially crafted. It contains 1,062 malicious PRs derived from real CVE fixes across the top 10 CWE categories, wrapped in 15 social-engineering framings. Evaluation of 8 LLMs reveals a significant security gap between closed- and open-source models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM benchmarks focus on static vulnerability detection or code generation but fail to address a realistic threat: can an LLM reviewer detect and reject a malicious PR when the attacker controls both the code diff and the accompanying natural-language PR narrative? With automated code review becoming common in real-world PR workflows, this adversarial scenario poses a concrete supply-chain security risk that remains unmeasured.

Method: The authors construct SEVRA-BENCH by (1) sourcing real CVE-linked fixes from the top 10 CWE categories of the 2025 CWE Top 25; (2) automatically inverting each fix to restore the original vulnerable code; (3) wrapping each malicious change in one of 15 social-engineering framings that manipulate claims, evidence, urgency, prior-approval signals, and authority appeals; (4) evaluating 8 LLMs as review agents in a realistic PR-review setting, measuring approval rates on the resulting 1,062 adversarial pull requests.

Result: Evaluation reveals a sharp security gap: closed-source LLMs substantially outperform open-source models in rejecting malicious PRs under social-engineering pressure. Specific approval rates and model-level comparisons are not detailed in the abstract, but the gap is characterized as pronounced and concerning for open-source deployment scenarios.

Conclusion: SEVRA-BENCH exposes a critical weakness in LLM-based code review: adversarial PR framing can induce unsafe approvals, and open-source models lag significantly behind closed-source counterparts. The benchmark is positioned as a resource to drive improvement in open-source review agents and narrow this security gap.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) reviewers are increasingly used in pull-request (PR) workflows, where their approvals help decide which code is merged into a repository. This raises a question that benchmarks for static vulnerability detection or code generation do not address: can an automated reviewer reject a malicious contribution when the attacker controls both the code change and the accompanying PR text? We introduce SEVRA-BENCH (Social Engineering of Vulnerabilities in Review Agents), a benchmark that measures how often an automated reviewer approves such adversarial pull requests. Each malicious PR in SEVRA-BENCH is built from a real project commit that previously fixed a vulnerability listed in the Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) database. We automatically invert that fix to restore the original vulnerable code and submit it as a pull request wrapped in one of 15 social-engineering framings, which vary the claims made, the supporting evidence, the urgency conveyed, signals of prior approval, and appeals to authority. SEVRA-BENCH contains 1,062 malicious PRs drawn from Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE)-linked fixes across the top 10 entries of the 2025 Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) Top 25. In a realistic setting, we evaluate 8 current LLMs as code review agents on PRs that introduce vulnerabilities previously reported in public disclosures. Our results reveal a sharp gap in security capabilities between closed- and open-source models. We hope SEVRA-BENCH will serve as a valuable resource for advancing open-source models and narrowing this gap.

</details>


### [46] [Smart Blockchain-Based Access Control for the Internet of Things](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13798)
*Mahdi Manavi,Yunpeng Zhang,Guoning Chen*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a risk-adaptive access control layer for Hyperledger Fabric that uses an off-chain LSTM oracle to classify requests into risk tiers (Low, Moderate, High) and enforces tier-conditioned state-based endorsement (SBE) policies on-chain, achieving stronger validation for high-risk requests without increasing latency for benign traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing permissioned blockchains like Hyperledger Fabric use static endorsement policies that apply uniform validation depth to all access requests, regardless of varying risk levels. This one-size-fits-all approach is inadequate for large-scale IoT deployments that demand both strong security for anomalous requests and low latency for normal operations.

Method: The authors design a dual-layer architecture: (1) an off-chain LSTM-based risk oracle that classifies each access request into Low, Moderate, or High risk tiers and issues a signed attestation binding client identity to the target key/version; (2) on-chain chaincode executed by endorsing peers that verifies the attestation's authenticity and applies tier-conditioned state-based endorsement (SBE) policies — e.g., requiring additional endorsements for higher-risk tiers — without altering Hyperledger Fabric's ordering service or consensus mechanism.

Result: Experimental evaluation on a Fabric testbed demonstrates that tier-conditioned endorsement effectively intensifies validation scrutiny for higher-risk requests while maintaining low confirmation latency for benign (low-risk) workloads.

Conclusion: The proposed risk-adaptive enforcement layer successfully reconciles the trade-off between security and performance in IoT access control by dynamically scaling endorsement requirements based on real-time risk assessment, all without invasive changes to the underlying blockchain consensus.

Abstract: Securing access control in large-scale Internet of Things (IoT) deployments requires mechanisms that adapt to risk while preserving low latency for benign traffic. Permissioned blockchains such as Hyperledger Fabric offer auditability through smart contracts, but static endorsement policies impose the same validation depth on all requests, regardless of security posture. We propose a risk-adaptive enforcement layer for Hyperledger Fabric that couples an off-chain LSTM-based risk oracle with deterministic on-chain checks. The oracle assigns each request to a tier (Low, Moderate, High) and issues a signed attestation bound to the client identity and target key/version. Endorsing peers verify the attestation in chaincode and enforce tier-conditioned SBE policies without modifying the ordering service or consensus. Experiments on a Fabric testbed show that tier-conditioned endorsement strengthens validation for higher-risk requests while retaining low confirmation latency for benign workloads.

</details>


### [47] [Information Flow Paths from RTL Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13860)
*Calvin Deutschbein,Owyn Wyatt*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper presents a novel method for automatically constructing complete information flow paths from RTL trace data, addressing limitations of prior work that could only detect information flow between registers without capturing full propagation paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Security validation in hardware design is manually intensive and error-prone, involving threat modeling, property specification, violation detection, and consequence assessment. Prior work combining information flow tracking and specification mining was limited to detecting flows between registers without reconstructing complete propagation paths, leaving a gap in understanding how sensitive information propagates through a design.

Method: The authors develop a novel approach for constructing information flow paths from RTL trace data, leveraging new technologies that accelerate hardware analysis to go beyond simple register-to-register flow detection and instead reproduce full information propagation paths through the design.

Result: The paper proposes a technique capable of reconstructing complete information flow paths from RTL traces, overcoming the prior limitation of only identifying pairwise register flows.

Conclusion: Automated construction of full information flow paths from RTL trace data can significantly reduce the manual burden in hardware security validation and provide deeper insight into how sensitive information propagates through a design.

Abstract: Security validation is an important yet challenging part of the hardware design process, yet, by convention, validation engineers are tasked with defining the threat model, specifying the relevant security properties, detecting any violations of those properties, and assessing the consequences to system security, each of which is manually intensive and may introduce errors. The combined technologies of information flow tracking and specification mining represent an automated approach to property generation and validation, but prior work on information flow tracking on RTL trace data was limited to find cases under which information flowed between registers, without reproducing full paths to capture how sensitive information propagates through a design. With the introduction of new technologies accelerating hardware analysis, we develop a novel approach for constructing information flow paths from register transfer level (RTL) trace data.

</details>


### [48] [RTL-Arrow: Hardware-to-Cloud Bridge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13865)
*Calvin Deutschbein,Jimmy Ostler*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper proposes RTL-Arrow, a framework that compiles hardware simulation outputs (value change dumps) into cloud-ready dataframes, bridging the gap between hardware execution traces and modern data science workflows for security verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers in hardware security spend excessive development time manually parsing traces-of-execution from hardware designs to identify vulnerabilities at the hardware, software, or interface levels. This labor-intensive process lacks standardization and interoperability with modern data science tooling.

Method: The RTL-Arrow framework translates simulated hardware execution outputs, specifically Value Change Dump (VCD) files, into dataframe formats compatible with cloud-based data science workflows. This enables standardized program verification across hardware and software boundaries.

Result: The framework produces performant binaries that streamline the hardware/data integration pipeline, reducing manual parsing overhead and enabling researchers to leverage modern data science ecosystems for security analysis.

Conclusion: RTL-Arrow successfully bridges the hardware/data divide, and the paper shares practical lessons learned from packaging and distributing these libraries within an academic security research program.

Abstract: Hardware Security at Willamette is a Willamette University affiliated research group studying the hardware-software interface of security critical services. Within our program, we noticed many researchers spent considerable development time learning to understand and manually parse traces-of-execution of hardware designs which are used to identifying whether vulnerabilities or weaknesses arise at the hardware, software, or interface level.
