We propose the "RTL-Arrow" framework, a framework to compile performant binaries which bridge the hardware/data divide. We translate the outputs of simulated hardware execution, as "value change dumps" into modern data science workflows as cloud-ready "dataframes", to standardize program verification across the hardware and software levels. We describe our approach, its benefits, and lessons learned from the process of packaging and distributing these libraries for our security research program.

</details>


### [49] [Crypto x AI, AI x Crypto: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13892)
*Sarah Allen,Pranay Anchuri,James Austgen,Maryam Bahrani,Samuel Breckenridge,Aaron Buchwald,Christian Cachin,Andrés Fábrega,Jared Fernandez,James Hsin-yu Chiang,Marwa Mouallem,Roi Bar-Zur,Neil DeSilva,Ittay Eyal,Giulia Fanti,Ari Juels,Andrew Miller,Christian Sillaber,Dani Vilardell,Pramod Viswanath,Wenhao Wang,Matt Weinberg,Sen Yang,Jianzhu Yao,Fan Zhang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A survey paper mapping the bidirectional intersection of AI and crypto, systematizing existing work, identifying open research questions, and arguing that meaningful integration between the two fields remains nascent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing hype around "crypto x AI" obscures what has actually been accomplished and what remains unsolved. The authors aim to cut through the noise to provide a clear, structured overview of real opportunities, challenges, and open questions.

Method: Systematic literature survey. The paper categorizes work into two directions: (1) what AI can do for blockchain/crypto, and (2) what blockchain/crypto can do for AI. It synthesizes key takeaways, highlights research gaps, and critiques pervasive industry misconceptions.

Result: The survey organizes a fragmented landscape into a coherent taxonomy, identifies specific open research questions in each sub-area, and surfaces common misunderstandings that distort expectations about what crypto+AI can currently deliver.

Conclusion: AI and crypto remain in very early stages of meaningful integration despite considerable hype. Both directions — AI for crypto and crypto for AI — present genuine but distinct opportunities and challenges that require focused research rather than speculative narratives.

Abstract: The intersection of crypto x AI is spawning papers, products, online posts, and companies. All the surrounding buzz, though, obscures what exactly has been done, what the opportunities and challenges are, and what open questions deserve attention. This survey paper asks what AI can do for blockchain-based technologies (broadly construed as "crypto") (crypto x AI), and vice versa (AI x crypto). We systematize existing work, summarize key takeaways, highlight open research questions, and offer a perspective on pervasive industry misconceptions, concluding that AI and crypto are still in the very early stages of meaningful integration.

</details>


### [50] [Side-Channel Attacks Bypass Protection in 3D Printers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13952)
*Eric Yocam,Varghese Vaidyan,Micah Flack,Gurcan Comert,Judith L. Mwakalonge*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Empirical evaluation of Active Motor Noise Cancellation (AMNC) in Bambu Lab FDM 3D printers shows it fully neutralizes acoustic side-channel attacks, but vibration side-channel leakage persists (up to ~61% classification accuracy with sequential modeling), though not at reconstruction-grade fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AMNC is deployed commercially as a hardware countermeasure against acoustic side-channel attacks that steal intellectual property from 3D printers, yet no empirical evaluation of its effectiveness existed. The authors seek to rigorously assess whether AMNC actually prevents information leakage and whether alternative side channels (vibration, magnetic, power) remain exploitable.

Method: The authors use a public dataset of synchronized acoustic and vibration recordings from two AMNC-equipped Bambu Lab FDM printers across 12 object classes. They evaluate classification accuracy on both channels using: (1) summary statistics (amplitude-driven), (2) frequency-only features (waveform shape), (3) full-sequence temporal models that ingest ordered print evolution, and (4) an order-shuffling control to isolate sequential contributions. Cross-printer transfer learning is also tested.

Result: AMNC renders acoustic classification at chance (8.33%). Vibration leaks: summary statistics yield ~31% pooled accuracy (36-47% within-printer); frequency-only features perform at chance, indicating waveform shape carries negligible information. Full-sequence temporal models reach ~61% accuracy, with shuffled controls at ~33%, confirming genuine sequential leakage tied to print progression. Cross-printer transfer is near chance, indicating device-specific leakage.

Conclusion: AMNC is an effective but narrow acoustic-only defense. Vibration remains a partial, geometry-correlated side channel insufficient for full geometric reconstruction on this dataset. Reconstruction-grade attacks would likely require magnetic or power side channels, which AMNC also leaves unaddressed.

Abstract: Active Motor Noise Cancellation (AMNC) ships in commercial fused deposition modeling (FDM) 3D printers as a hardware countermeasure against acoustic side-channel attacks that target intellectual property (IP). We present the first empirical evaluation of a deployed AMNC countermeasure, using a public dataset of synchronized acoustic and vibration recordings from two AMNC-equipped Bambu Lab printers across 12 object classes. AMNC fully neutralizes the acoustic channel: classification accuracy is indistinguishable from the 8.33% random baseline. The vibration channel, which AMNC does not target, still leaks. With summary statistics the leak is coarse and amplitude-driven (vibration accuracy approximately 31% pooled, 36-47% within-printer), while the waveform shape carries essentially nothing (frequency-only features at chance). A full-sequence temporal model that ingests the ordered evolution of the print raises accuracy to approximately 61%, and an order-shuffling control (approximately 33%) shows that a substantial component is genuinely sequential and tied to print progression. The leak is device-specific: a classifier trained on one printer transfers near chance to the other. We conclude that AMNC is an acoustic-only defense: vibration remains a partial, geometry-correlated side channel it does not address, but one that does not, on this dataset, support full geometric reconstruction; reconstruction-grade attacks would require the magnetic or power channels AMNC also leaves untouched. We release all code.

</details>


### [51] [Software Dark Matter: Gazing at Uncharted Files to Navigate SBOM Integrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13966)
*Abhishek Reddypalle,Dennis Roellke,Santiago Torres-Arias*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Software supply chains suffer from a "Software Dark Matter" (SDM) problem — security-critical files present on disk but missing from package metadata/SBOMs. Using their DARKFILES tool across four ecosystems, the authors quantify SDM, demonstrate it conceals vulnerable software and secrets, and discover three confirmed high-severity CVEs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current SBOM practices rely on package manager metadata to describe software components, but this assumption is flawed: filesystem-level content often diverges from metadata declarations. This creates a systemic visibility gap where security-critical files remain invisible to SBOM-driven security pipelines, leaving organizations blind to real risks.

Method: The authors implement DARKFILES, a reference tool that compares an artifact's filesystem contents against its metadata declarations to identify untracked files (SDM). They define a fidelity metric (SDM ratio = untracked files / total files) and introduce the concept of "Packaging Lag." Analysis spans four ecosystems: DockerHub, Maven Central, plugin marketplaces (Jenkins/OpenVSX), and a real-world enterprise environment. SDM is cross-referenced against CVE databases and scanned for secrets.

Result: SDM is pervasive across all four ecosystems. Packaging Lag shows metadata remains stale across multiple versions before catching up. Untracked files contain known-vulnerable packages invisible to SBOM pipelines. The study directly discovers three confirmed high-severity CVEs and finds strong correlation between SDM and sensitive information (secrets, cryptographic keys).

Conclusion: The metadata-centric SBOM paradigm is fundamentally inadequate for supply chain transparency. SDM represents a systemic, previously unquantified risk. Security tooling must incorporate filesystem-level analysis alongside metadata declarations to achieve genuine artifact fidelity and close the visibility gap.

Abstract: Modern software supply chains have evolved into vast, heterogeneous networks where transparency - the granular understanding of all software components - is now a critical security requirement. While Software Bills of Materials (SBOMs) have emerged as the primary mechanism for this transparency, current industry practices rely on a metadata-centric paradigm that assumes an artifact is defined solely by its package manager declarations. We posit that this assumption is fundamentally flawed, creating a systemic visibility gap we define as Software Dark Matter (SDM). SDM represents the set of security-critical files present in an artifact's filesystem that are unaccounted for by its associated metadata. We implement a reference tool, DARKFILES, and use it to analyze four ecosystems of disjoint nature: DockerHub, Maven Central, plugin/extension marketplaces (Jenkins plugins and OpenVSX), and a real-world enterprise environment.
  Our research makes the following contributions: we introduce a general-purpose metric for artifact fidelity calculating SDM as the ratio of untracked files per total file count. We introduce Packaging Lag, a phenomenon where official metadata remains out-of-date across multiple versions before catching up to an artifact's actual content. We demonstrate that SDM exposes vulnerable software invisible to SBOM-driven pipelines both by cross-referencing untracked packages against known CVE databases and through the direct discovery of three confirmed high-severity CVEs, showing that SDM is highly correlated with sensitive information including secrets and cryptographic keys.

</details>


### [52] [Choric Masking in Ambient Release Systems: A Finite Certificate Calculus for Trace Indistinguishability under Bounded Audiences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13967)
*Faruk Alpay,Taylan Alpay*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A finite certificate calculus for ambient release systems that quantifies "choric masking" — a security notion ensuring trace laws of protected coordinates remain within the convex hull of admissible cover traces. The framework uses polytope geometry, separating hyperplanes, and information-theoretic bounds to produce verifiable certificates for privacy, unlinkability, and side-channel leakage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public-facing release systems expose protected coordinates indirectly through observable side channels (timing, roles, movement patterns, attention, linked rooms). Existing approaches lack a finite, checkable formal language for auditing whether such residual observability compromises the protected coordinate. The paper addresses this gap by developing a calculus that can certify when masking holds or produce exposure certificates when it fails.

Method: The authors model trace laws as polytopes over finite horizons, with audiences inducing measurement operators that project these polytopes. Choric masking is defined as intersection containment in the projected measurement space. Certification leverages: (1) separating hyperplanes for exposure detection, (2) kernel obstructions, (3) hypothesis-testing and Fano-type localization lower bounds, (4) dual separation certificates, (5) data-processing laws for attention lenses, (6) aperture identities for gaze-thinned observation, and (7) composition rules for linked releases. A "repeated-room debt theorem" captures how unresolved pressure propagates across rooms.

Result: The calculus yields measurement-polytope equivalence for exact and approximate masks, dual separation certificates, data-processing inequalities for attention lenses, aperture identities, lower bounds for mandatory unique gestures, composition rules for linked releases, and the repeated-room debt theorem. These collectively form a finite, checkable language for auditing privacy, unlinkability, side-channel leakage, and accountability.

Conclusion: The proposed finite certificate calculus provides a principled, verifiable framework for analyzing privacy and exposure in ambient release systems. By reducing masking to polytope intersection and exposure to separating certificates, it enables rigorous auditing of side-channel vulnerability in settings where protected information leaks through structured, repeated observations across linked environments.

Abstract: This paper develops a finite certificate calculus for ambient release systems, staged probabilistic environments in which a protected coordinate is not observed directly but can remain statistically readable through visible roles, timing, repeated movement, bounded attention, linked rooms, and post-release state. The security notion, choric masking, requires the trace law induced by a protected locus to lie inside or near the convex hull of admissible cover traces under the tests available to a specified audience. For finite horizons, trace laws form polytopes, audiences induce measurement operators, and masking becomes intersection in the projected measurement space. Exposure is certified by separating hyperplanes, kernel obstructions, hypothesis-testing bounds, Fano-type localization lower bounds, and support separation in downstream rooms. The calculus distinguishes trace residue from carrier localization, full-trace exposure from attention-filtered exposure, first-room masking from delayed post-release exposure, and unresolved system pressure from carrier hazard. It proves measurement-polytope equivalence for exact and approximate masks, dual separation certificates, data-processing laws for attention lenses, aperture identities for gaze-thinned observation, lower bounds for mandatory unique gestures, composition rules for linked releases, and a repeated-room debt theorem showing how unresolved pressure can broaden selection and shift cost onto cover populations without localizing the source. The result is a finite, checkable language for auditing privacy, unlinkability, side-channel leakage, and accountability in public-facing release systems.

</details>


### [53] [Hidden in Plain Sight: Benchmarking Agent Safety Against Decomposition Attacks with DECOMPBENCH](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13994)
*Vikhyath Kothamasu,Virginia Smith,Chhavi Yadav*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: DeCompBench is a benchmark designed to evaluate LLM-based agent safety against decomposition attacks, where harmful tasks are broken into benign subtasks that evade safety mechanisms. Experiments show SOTA agents have high refusal rates on monolithic harmful tasks but much lower rates on decomposed variants, often inadvertently fulfilling adversarial objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent safety benchmarks evaluate multi-turn and multi-tool-use settings but fail to explicitly capture decomposition attacks — a realistic adversarial misuse pattern where harmful tasks are split into individually benign subtasks that cumulatively achieve malicious intent. There is a need for targeted evaluation of agentic safety under this threat model.

Method: DeCompBench is constructed using a decomposition-by-design principle via a graphical framework. A custom decomposer breaks harmful tasks into individually benign, executable subtasks with realistic workflows. The benchmark is then used to evaluate SOTA agents by comparing refusal rates on monolithic vs. decomposed harmful tasks.

Result: State-of-the-art LLM-based agents exhibit high refusal rates when presented with monolithic harmful tasks, but show significantly lower refusal rates on their decomposed variants. The agents often inadvertently fulfill adversarial objectives when processing the decomposed subtasks.

Conclusion: Current safety mechanisms are inadequate against decomposition attacks, revealing a critical blind spot in agentic safety. The findings underscore the urgent need for safety evaluations that target decompositional misuse and the development of corresponding defenses.

Abstract: LLM-based Agents are becoming increasingly capable and widely deployed, creating growing incentives for adversarial misuse in the real-world. A key emerging threat is Decomposition Attacks \cite{glukhov2024breach, jones2024adversaries} in which a harmful task is broken into simpler, benign subtasks that evade safety mechanisms when executed separately but cumulatively fulfill the malicious intent. Although recent benchmarks assess agent safety in multi-turn and multi-tool-use settings, they do not explicitly capture this form of decompositional misuse and may not represent realistic adversarial execution flows. To this end, we introduce DeCompBench, a benchmark designed specifically to evaluate agentic safety under decomposition attacks. DeCompBench is created with a decomposition-by-design principle using a graphical framework and enables harmful task decomposition into individually benign and executable subtasks with realistic workflows. Our experiments using a custom decomposer show that state-of-the-art agents exhibit high refusal rates on monolithic harmful tasks, but significantly lower refusal rates on their decomposed variants, while often inadvertently fulfilling the adversarial objectives. These findings underscore the need for safety evaluations against decomposition attacks and corresponding defenses. Our dataset is publicly available and can be found at https://huggingface.co/datasets/decompositionbench/DeCompBench.

</details>


### [54] [Pseudonym Scheme Based on Hybrid Certificates for Security Credential Management System in Vehicular Communications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14008)
*Abel C. H. Chen,F. J. Hwang,Yu-Chih Wei,Chin-Chen Chang,Bon-Yeh Lin*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: To address the quantum vulnerability of ECC-based vehicular communication standards (IEEE/ETSI SCMS, BKE), this paper proposes a hybrid ECC+PQC certificate and a generalized pseudonym scheme that unlinks pseudonym and enrollment certificates, with performance benchmarking of RSA, ECC, and NIST-standardized PQC algorithms to recommend suitable configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current vehicular communication security standards (IEEE 1609.2, ETSI ITS) rely on ECC for credential management and pseudonymity. With the advent of quantum computing, ECC's security is threatened. A proactive migration path combining classical and post-quantum cryptography is needed to ensure long-term privacy and authentication in V2X ecosystems.

Method: (1) Design of a hybrid certificate structure that binds an ECC public key with one or more PQC public keys under a single credential. (2) A generalized pseudonym certificate generation scheme that is algorithm-agnostic and cryptographically ensures unlinkability between enrollment certificates and derived pseudonym certificates. (3) Comprehensive performance evaluation comparing RSA, ECC (ECDSA, ECDH), and NIST PQC candidates (e.g., CRYSTALS-Kyber, CRYSTALS-Dilithium, Falcon) across message size and computation time metrics.

Result: The hybrid certificate design is shown to be feasible for vehicular PKI. The generalized pseudonym scheme eliminates public key correlation attacks. Performance benchmarks reveal trade-offs: PQC algorithms generally incur larger message sizes (notably signature and key encapsulation sizes) and higher computation times than ECC, but certain lattice-based schemes offer acceptable overhead for V2X latency constraints. Specific PQC algorithm recommendations are made based on the trade-off analysis.

Conclusion: Hybrid ECC+PQC certificates combined with a generalized, algorithm-agnostic pseudonym scheme provide a practical migration path toward quantum-resistant vehicular communication systems. Lattice-based NIST standards strike a reasonable balance between security, message overhead, and computational efficiency for resource-constrained V2X environments.

Abstract: In recent years, the Institute of Electrical and Electronics Engineers (IEEE) and the European Telecommunications Standards Institute (ETSI) have developed a series of security communication standards for vehicular communications. These standards include mechanisms such as the Security Credential Management System (SCMS) and Butterfly Key Expansion (BKE) to protect vehicle privacy. However, these standards are mainly based on the Elliptic-Curve Cryptography (ECC), which may be vulnerable to attacks from quantum computing in the future. In response to this potential risk, this study proposes a hybrid certificate that combines the ECC with Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC). This approach enables infrastructure systems to be built on cryptographic foundations that are more resilient to quantum-based attacks. Furthermore, this study presents a generalized pseudonym scheme that is compatible with various cryptographic algorithms for generating pseudonym certificates. This design aims to eliminate the possibility of inferring any correlation between the public key in a pseudonym certificate and that in an enrollment certificate. This study also conducts a comprehensive performance evaluation of the RSA, ECC, and PQC algorithms, particularly those standardized by the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). The comparison considers factors such as message length and computation time. Based on the findings, this study recommends suitable pseudonym schemes that adopt hybrid certificates for secure and efficient use in vehicular communications.

</details>


### [55] [Same-Origin Policy for Agentic Browsers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14027)
*Xilong Wang,Xiaoxing Chen,Patrick Li,Dawn Song,Neil Gong*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether the Same-Origin Policy (SOP) remains effective in agentic browsers, finds that current implementations frequently violate SOP, proposes a benchmark (SOPBench) and a mitigation mechanism (SOPGuard), and demonstrates SOPGuard's effectiveness with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic browsers allow AI agents to automate web tasks via natural language, but the security implications of this paradigm shift are underexplored. Specifically, whether the Same-Origin Policy—a cornerstone browser security mechanism preventing unauthorized cross-origin data flows—still functions correctly in agentic browsers has never been systematically studied, leaving a critical security gap.

Method: The authors first identify that agentic browsers can act as automated channels for cross-origin data flows, bypassing SOP. They construct SOPBench, a benchmark to systematically evaluate SOP violations. They then design SOPGuard, a tailored SOP enforcement mechanism for agentic browsers, and implement it in BrowserOS, an open-source agentic browser, followed by extensive evaluation.

Result: Evaluation with SOPBench reveals that existing agentic browsers frequently violate SOP in both benign scenarios and under adversarial attacks. SOPGuard, when integrated into BrowserOS, effectively enforces SOP, preserves the utility of the agentic browser, and incurs only a small runtime overhead.

Conclusion: SOP is systematically violated in current agentic browsers, representing a significant security vulnerability. SOPGuard provides an effective, low-overhead solution that enforces SOP without compromising the functionality of agentic browsers. The authors release their code and data to support further research.

Abstract: Agentic browsers integrate autonomous AI agents into web browsers, enabling users to accomplish web tasks through natural-language instructions. The same-origin policy (SOP) is a fundamental browser security mechanism that prevents unauthorized automated cross-origin data flows induced by scripts. However, whether SOP remains effective in agentic browsers is an open question that has not been systematically studied. In this work, we bridge this gap. We first observe that an agentic browser can itself serve as an automated channel for cross-origin data flows, potentially leading to SOP violations. To investigate this phenomenon, we construct SOPBench, a benchmark for evaluating SOP violations in agentic browsers. Our evaluation shows that existing agentic browsers frequently violate SOP, both in benign settings and under attacks. To address this problem, we propose SOPGuard, an SOP enforcement mechanism tailored to agentic browsers. We implement SOPGuard in BrowserOS, an open-source agentic browser. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that SOPGuard effectively enforces SOP while preserving utility and incurring only a small runtime overhead. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/wxl-lxw/BrowserOS-SOPGuard.

</details>


### [56] [Defending the Core: A Centrality-Based Protection Strategy for Supply Chain Security in npm Dependency Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14036)
*Zixin Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The npm dependency network exhibits a scale-free topology, making it highly vulnerable to targeted attacks on hub nodes. A dual defense strategy — hardening the top 1% of central nodes and pruning 30% of trivial edges — effectively prevents catastrophic network collapse and cascading malware spread.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source shared dependencies in the npm ecosystem enable rapid development but introduce systemic security vulnerabilities. The authors aim to characterize the structural risks of the npm dependency network and design practical, topology-aware mitigation strategies against targeted attacks and malware propagation.

Method: The authors modeled the npm dependency network as a graph with 53,481 packages (nodes) and 78,520 dependency edges. They classified its topology as scale-free and analyzed vulnerability to targeted attacks on high-degree hubs. A dual-pronged defense was proposed: (1) Centrality-Based Node-Hardening, applying strict security protocols to the most central nodes, and (2) a Dependency Weight Warning system to identify and prune low-importance edges. Effectiveness was evaluated through network simulations under various attack scenarios.

Result: Hardening just the top 1% of nodes by centrality, combined with pruning 30% of structurally trivial dependency edges, was sufficient to prevent catastrophic network collapse and neutralize cascading malware infections in simulated attack scenarios.

Conclusion: The scale-free nature of the npm dependency network makes it susceptible to hub-targeted attacks, but a cost-effective, topology-informed defense — concentrating security efforts on a small fraction of critical nodes and trimming non-essential edges — can substantially harden the software supply chain against systemic threats.

Abstract: The modern software supply chain, taking Node Package Manager (npm) dependency network for example, relies heavily on shared open-source dependencies. While this promotes rapid development, it introduces systemic vulnerabilities as well. Concerning this potential risk, we analyze the npm dependency network by modeling 53,481 packages and 78,520 dependency edges, and classify the network as a scale-free topology. Thus, we demonstrate its inherent vulnerability to targeted attacks on high-degree hubs. To mitigate this, we propose and evaluate a dual-pronged defense strategy consisting of Centrality-Based Node-Hardening and Dependency Weight Warning system. Moreover, by simulating the network under various attack scenarios, we prove that applying strict security protocols to just the top 1% of nodes, combined with pruning 30% of structurally trivial edges, prevents catastrophic network collapse and neutralizes cascading malware infections. The source code can be found at https://github.com/5tarWhee1/Centrality-Based-Protection-Strategy-for-Supply-Chain-Security-in-npm-Dependency-Network.

</details>


### [57] [Hierarchical Identity-Based Signature with Designated Aggregator from Lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14090)
*Stuti Kumari,Kunal Dey,Vikas Srivastava,Sumit Kumar Debnath*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Hierarchical Identity-Based Signature with Designated Aggregator (HIBS-DA) framework and presents the first lattice-based construction, enabling users across hierarchical levels to generate signatures that aggregate into a single compact signature, reducing communication and verification overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In hierarchical organizations (e.g., universities, corporations, government agencies), authenticating data from multiple users at different levels is complex and resource-intensive. Existing solutions lack an efficient, post-quantum secure mechanism for aggregating signatures across hierarchical structures.

Method: The authors formally define the HIBS-DA framework and propose the first lattice-based construction, leveraging the hardness of lattice problems (e.g., SIS/LWE) to achieve post-quantum security. The scheme allows individual signatures from users at different hierarchy levels to be combined by a designated aggregator into a single compact signature.

Result: The proposed HIBS-DA scheme is proven secure, correct, and resistant to forgery under lattice-based hardness assumptions. It achieves signature aggregation across hierarchical levels, yielding a compact aggregated signature that reduces both communication and verification costs.

Conclusion: The first lattice-based HIBS-DA construction provides an efficient, post-quantum secure solution for hierarchical multi-user authentication, well-suited for large-scale organizational deployments.

Abstract: In hierarchical organizations, authenticating data from multiple users can be complex and resource-intensive. Hierarchical Identity-Based Signature with Designated Aggregator (HIBS-DA) provides an efficient solution by allowing users at different levels to generate signatures that can be combined into a single, compact signature. We first introduce the HIBS-DA framework and present the {\em{first}} lattice-based construction of HIBS-DA. Our scheme allows users at different hierarchical levels to generate individual signatures that can be aggregated into a single, compact signature, reducing communication and verification costs. The proposed construction is secure, correct, and resistant to forgery, making it suitable for large-scale environments such as universities, corporations, and government agencies.

</details>


### [58] [SkillMutator: Benchmarking and Defending Language-and-Code Cross-modal Attacks on LLM Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14154)
*Youngduk Kim,Minkyoo Song,Seungwon Shin*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: LLM agents loading "Agent Skills" face a new cross-modal attack surface where attackers embed malicious directives in natural-language specs (SKILL.md) that appear benign but steer agents to exfiltrate data. Existing scanners detect only 2-17% of such attacks. The authors introduce SkillMutator, a benchmark generating adversarial cross-modal attacks across 13 categories, and propose a reasoning-trajectory distillation framework that trains a small open-weight model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B) achieving 88.2% detection, surpassing GPT-4o and rivaling frontier models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly load "Agent Skills" that pair natural-language specs with executable code. Assessing safety requires cross-modal reasoning across language and code, but prior work treats skills solely as prompt-injection vectors or static code artifacts. Attacks exploiting cross-modal interactions — where SKILL.md appears benign while implicitly steering agent behavior toward malicious actions — remain understudied and largely undetected by existing scanners.

Method: The authors introduce SkillMutator, a benchmark that emulates an adversarial mutation process across 13 attack categories, iteratively refining malicious skills using scanner feedback to make injected behaviors indistinguishable from legitimate workflows. They also propose a four-phase reasoning-trajectory distillation framework that distills reasoning traces from frontier teacher models into smaller open-weight models (e.g., Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct), producing a locally deployable scanner.

Result: Existing open-source and commercial scanners detect only 2-8% and 9-17% of cross-modal attacks, respectively. On the strongest SkillMutator subset (n=76), the distilled Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct achieves 88.2% detection, surpassing GPT-4o-mini (23.7%) and GPT-5.4-mini (79.0%), and approaching GPT-5.4 (86.8%).

Conclusion: Practical defense against language-and-code cross-modal attacks on Agent Skills is feasible without relying on costly frontier models. Distillation of reasoning trajectories into smaller open-weight models yields effective, locally deployable scanners that avoid third-party data exposure and high API costs.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly extend their capabilities at runtime by loading Agent Skills, which pair natural-language specifications (SKILL.md) with executable scripts and resources. Because a skill's behavior relies on both natural-language instructions and executable code, assessing its safety requires cross-modal reasoning, creating a new language-and-code attack surface. Attackers can present a benign workflow in SKILL.md while embedding implicit directives that steer the agent to exfiltrate sensitive files, even if the scripts appear harmless. This attack surface remains understudied; prior work treats skills merely as prompt-injection vectors or static code artifacts, leaving attacks emerging from cross-modal interactions largely unmeasured. In our evaluation, open-source and commercial skill scanners detect only 2%-8% and 9%-17% of such attacks, respectively. To address this gap, we introduce SkillMutator, the first benchmark for install-time detection of language-and-code cross-modal attacks on Agent Skills. It emulates an adversarial mutation process across 13 attack categories, iteratively refining malicious skills using scanner feedback to make injected behaviors indistinguishable from legitimate workflows. We further propose a four-phase reasoning-trajectory distillation framework to distill frontier-teacher traces into smaller open-weight models. This produces a locally deployable scanner avoiding third-party data exposure and excessive API costs. On the strongest SkillMutator subset (n=76), our distilled model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) improves detection from 17.1% to 88.2%, surpassing GPT-4o-mini (23.7%) and GPT-5.4-mini (79.0%), and reaching frontier-level GPT-5.4 (86.8%). These results show practical defense against cross-modal attacks is feasible without relying on costly frontier models.

</details>


### [59] [Security Evaluation of Mobile Banking Applications in Sudan](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14165)
*Abdelmonim Naway*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A security audit of four major Sudanese mobile banking apps (Bankak, Fawry, Okash, Sahil) with 1.6M+ users reveals critical vulnerabilities: absent SSL pinning, unsafe TrustManager, weak random number generation, deprecated cryptography, and debugging symbols in production—highlighting systemic security gaps in the national fintech ecosystem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid digitalization of Sudan's financial sector has led to widespread adoption of mobile banking applications without commensurate security auditing. This study aims to rigorously assess the security posture of the most popular MBAs in Sudan, identify systemic vulnerabilities, and provide a roadmap for security-by-design adoption to protect over 1.6 million users from cyber threats.

Method: Static Application Security Testing (SAST) was performed using two tools: Mobile Security Framework (MobSF) and Quixxi. The four applications—Bankak, Fawry, Okash, and Sahil—were evaluated against the OWASP Mobile Application Security Verification Standard (MASVS). Identified vulnerabilities were then mapped to Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) identifiers for standardized classification and comparative analysis.

Result: Bankak exhibited the highest risk with 12 vulnerabilities, including critical absence of SSL certificate pinning and unsafe TrustManager implementations (high MitM susceptibility). Fawry was relatively more mature with 7 vulnerabilities. A universal weakness across all four apps was insecure random number generation (CWE-330), threatening session token integrity. Bankak and Okash used deprecated cryptographic algorithms (MD5/SHA-1). All apps properly disabled ADB backups, but 100% retained verbose debugging symbols in production APKs, facilitating reverse engineering.

Conclusion: The audit reveals systemic security deficiencies across Sudan's leading MBAs, with the market leader paradoxically posing the greatest risk. The findings underscore an urgent need for industry-wide adoption of security-by-design principles, including mandatory SSL pinning, secure random number generation, modern cryptographic standards, and production build hardening. The study provides actionable remediation guidance for developers and a strategic framework for regulatory improvement.

Abstract: The rapid digitalization of the Sudanese financial sector has precipitated a surge in Mobile Banking Applications (MBAs); however, this growth has frequently outpaced rigorous security auditing. This study provides a comprehensive technical audit of the four most widely used Sudanese MBAs( Bankak, Fawry, Okash, and Sahil )collectively serving a user base of over 1.6 million. Utilizing Static Application Security Testing (SAST) via the Mobile Security Framework (MobSF) and Quixxi, the applications were evaluated against the OWASP Mobile Application Security Verification Standard (MASVS). Findings were mapped to Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) identifiers to identify systemic vulnerabilities. Analysis revealed critical disparities in security posture. Bankak, the market leader, exhibited the highest risk profile (12 vulnerabilities), including a critical absence of SSL certificate pinning and unsafe TrustManager implementations, rendering it highly susceptible to Man-in-the-Middle (MitM) attacks. While Fawry demonstrated relative maturity (7 vulnerabilities), a universal failure was observed across all four applications regarding secure random number generation (CWE-330), potentially compromising session token integrity. Additionally, Bankak and Okash were found to utilize deprecated cryptographic algorithms (MD5/SHA-1). Notably, all applications successfully disabled ADB backups, yet 100% retained verbose debugging symbols in production APKs, significantly lowering the barrier for reverse engineering. This research addresses a critical gap in the national fintech ecosystem by providing actionable technical recommendations for developers and a strategic roadmap for implementing "security-by-design" principles across the sector.

</details>


### [60] [From Prompts to Responses: Dual-Sided Data Leakage and Defense in Split Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14210)
*Zixuan Gu,Xiaojun Ye,Yang Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper exposes new privacy vulnerabilities in Split-LLM architectures by introducing PIDI, a two-stage attack that reconstructs both private input prompts and output responses. To defend against such bidirectional leakage, the authors propose ADMI, which combines adapter-based local warmup with mutual information regularization to protect privacy while preserving task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Split learning for LLMs mitigates the risk of exposing raw data to external APIs and reduces local computational burden, but prior privacy research has focused narrowly on input prompt leakage via inversion attacks. The potential for sensitive information leakage through generated output responses in Split-LLM settings remains largely unexplored, creating a critical blind spot in the privacy landscape.

Method: The authors propose PIDI (Patched Model Inversion with Dual-Sided Initialization), a two-stage attack that simultaneously reconstructs both private input prompts and output responses. It employs dual-sided initialization and a patched inversion strategy to handle long sequences effectively. For defense, ADMI (Adapter-based DualGuard with Mutual Information Defense) integrates a lightweight adapter-based local warmup strategy on the client side with mutual information regularization to bound information leakage while maintaining task utility.

Result: Extensive experiments across diverse tasks and model configurations show that PIDI substantially outperforms prior inversion methods in reconstructing both inputs and outputs. ADMI effectively defends against PIDI and other state-of-the-art inversion attacks with minimal degradation in task performance.

Conclusion: Split-LLM architectures suffer from bidirectional privacy leakage — both input prompts and output responses can be reconstructed. PIDI demonstrates the severity of this threat, while ADMI offers a practical, empirically grounded defense that balances privacy protection with model utility. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in privacy-sensitive domains, where users must balance the risk of data exposure through external APIs against the high computational cost of local deployment. Split learning has therefore emerged as a promising paradigm for LLM fine-tuning and inference under limited local resources. However, it introduces new privacy risks. Prior work primarily studies leakage of private input prompts, typically via inversion attacks on intermediate representations, while the potential for sensitive information leakage through generative response outputs remains largely unexplored.
  In this work, we unveil novel vulnerabilities of Split-LLM by presenting Patched Model Inversion with Dual-Sided Initialization (PIDI), a two-stage attack that simultaneously targets both private input prompts and output responses in Split-LLM settings. It combines dual-sided initialization with a patched inversion strategy to tackle long sequences, substantially outperforming prior inversion methods. To counter threats from both sides, we further propose the Adapter-based DualGuard with Mutual Information Defense (ADMI), which integrates an adapter-based local warmup strategy and mutual information regularization to provide a strong empirical privacy protection with minimal impact on task performance. Extensive experiments across diverse tasks and models demonstrate that ADMI effectively defends against PIDI and other state-of-the-art inversion attacks. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/FLAIR-THU/VFLAIR-LLM.

</details>


### [61] [Security in a Workflow: Exploring Role-Based Agentic Architectures for Vulnerability Handling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14261)
*Srijita Basu,Miroslaw Staron*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a role-based multi-agent workflow (Planner, Analyzer, Fixer, Verifier) for vulnerability analysis and remediation, evaluates it on 25 real-world C/C++ vulnerabilities using multiple LLMs, and reports 44% detection accuracy and 19% fix accuracy while exploring CodeQL integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based software security approaches focus on isolated tasks (detection or patch generation) rather than reflecting the full industrial multi-stage workflow. This creates a gap between academic methods and real-world secure software engineering practices, which involve vulnerability analysis, remediation, and fix verification in sequence.

Method: The authors design a role-based agentic workflow with four distinct roles: Planner (coordinates overall strategy), Analyzer (performs vulnerability detection, optionally integrated with CodeQL static analysis), Fixer (generates patches), and Verifier (validates fixes). Three LLMs are tested: nemotron-cascade-2:30b, qwen3-coder-next, and gpt-oss:120b. Evaluation is conducted on 25 real-world C/C++ vulnerabilities.

Result: The workflow achieves 44% vulnerability detection accuracy (comparable to GPT 5.5 baseline) and 19% fix accuracy across the 25 C/C++ vulnerabilities tested.

Conclusion: The role-based agentic architecture shows promise for bridging the gap between LLM-based security methods and industrial practice, but fix generation remains challenging. The paper also derives practical implications for software security practitioners regarding LLM-assisted vulnerability management workflows.

Abstract: Secure software engineering in practice is a multi-stage workflow involving vulnerability analysis, remediation, and fix verification. However, current LLM-based software security approaches often focus on isolated tasks such as detection or patch generation, with limited attention to agentic architectures reflecting industrial workflow. This creates a gap between existing LLM-based vulnerability-handling methods and real-world practices. In this paper, we study a role-based agentic workflow for vulnerability analysis and mitigation consisting of Planner, Analyzer, Fixer, and Verifier roles. To explore the effect of static analysis tool, the analyzer agent was integrated with the CodeQL in one of the workflows. The models used include nemotron-cascade-2:30b, qwen3-coder-next, and gpt-oss:120b. Our evaluation uses 25 real-world C/C++ vulnerabilities. The study reports 44% vulnerability detection accuracy comparable to GPT 5.5 and 19% fix accuracy. We also list implications from this study in context of software security practitioners.

</details>


### [62] [AgentCyberRange: Benchmarking Frontier AI Systems in Realistic Cyber Ranges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14295)
*Fengyu Liu,Jiarun Dai,Yihe Fan,Wuyuao Mai,Ziao Li,Bofei Chen,Jie Zhang,Zheng Lou,Bocheng Xiang,Qiyi Zhang,Xudong Pan,Geng Hong,Yuan Zhang,Min Yang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: AgentCyberRange is the first open, multi-range benchmark for evaluating autonomous AI cyber attack capabilities across realistic scenarios. Testing six frontier AI models, GPT-5.5 with Codex achieved 16.1% on web exploitation and 31.7% on post-exploitation tasks, with scores rising to 33.0% and 46.3% when given concrete hints. The authors also discovered unknown vulnerabilities and novel payload mutations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing public benchmarks (CTFs, vulnerability reproduction, exploit generation) abstract away realistic intrusion workflows — service discovery, foothold establishment, internal reconnaissance, and lateral movement. Frontier AI systems are rarely evaluated under realistic multi-host attack conditions, making it difficult to observe emerging offensive risks early.

Method: The authors built AgentCyberRange, combining 110 vulnerabilities across 15 real web applications and 8 enterprise-like cyber ranges with 156 internal hosts. They also developed Cage, a toolchain for execution, orchestration, result collection, and verification. The benchmark covers two stages: web exploitation (exploring exposed apps, validating vulnerabilities) and post-exploitation (expanding an initial foothold into broader internal compromise). Six frontier AI systems were evaluated under matched prompts and budgets.

Result: GPT-5.5 with Codex performed best: 16.1% on web exploitation and 31.7% on post-exploitation tasks. With more concrete hints, these rates rose to 33.0% and 46.3%. Out-of-benchmark findings included previously unknown vulnerabilities in popular projects and payload mutations that bypass host defenses.

Conclusion: Open cyber-range evaluation under realistic, reproducible conditions is necessary for observing emerging offensive AI capabilities. Current benchmarks are insufficient, and AgentCyberRange provides a meaningful step toward systematically measuring and monitoring autonomous cyber attack capability.

Abstract: Frontier AI systems are increasingly capable of cybersecurity tasks, including codebase inspection, vulnerability detection, and exploitation. However, evaluating their offensive capabilities remains constrained by limited access to open, reproducible, multi-host cyber ranges. Existing public benchmarks capture isolated skills such as CTF solving, vulnerability reproduction, and exploit generation, but often abstract away realistic intrusion workflows: discovering exposed services, gaining a foothold, collecting internal information, and expanding compromise across hosts. This gap makes it difficult to observe emerging risks early, because frontier AI systems are rarely evaluated under realistic attack conditions.
  We introduce AgentCyberRange, the first open, multi-range infrastructure for measuring autonomous cyber attack capability in realistic cyber ranges. It combines 110 vulnerabilities across 15 real web applications and 8 enterprise-like cyber ranges with 156 internal hosts, plus Cage, a toolchain for execution, orchestration, result collection, and verification. The benchmark covers two core stages: web exploitation, where agents explore exposed applications and validate vulnerabilities, and post exploitation, where agents turn an initial foothold into broader internal compromise. We evaluate six frontier AI systems under matched prompts and budgets. GPT-5.5 with Codex performs best, solving 16.1% of web exploitation tasks and 31.7% of post-exploitation tasks; with more concrete hints, these rates increase to 33.0% and 46.3%. We also observe out-of-benchmark findings, including unknown vulnerabilities in popular projects, and payload mutation that bypasses host defenses. These results show that open cyber-range evaluation is necessary for observing emerging offensive capabilities under realistic and reproducible conditions.

</details>


### [63] [REPOSE: Quantifying the Price of Security in Weakly-Hard Real-Time Cyber-Physical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14395)
*Vijay Banerjee,Monowar Hasan*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: REPOSE is a runtime analytical framework that enables weakly-hard real-time control systems to dynamically balance safety and security, achieving negligible feasibility overhead (~0.06%) compared to traditional hard-constraint approaches (~29%) by applying proactive/reactive security based on task behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RT-CPS security approaches fall into an undesirable "all-or-nothing" scenario: design-time opportunistic security may skip checks under high load, while per-task security imposes prohibitive performance penalties. This motivates a framework that enables a bounded, analyzable trade-off between safety and security.

Method: REPOSE is an analytical framework targeting weakly-hard real-time control systems (tolerating bounded deadline misses). It applies security operations in both proactive and reactive modes based on runtime task behavior, rather than imposing fixed design-time overhead. The framework analytically calculates the safety-security trade-off at runtime.

Result: At 80% system utilization, REPOSE incurs only 0.06% feasibility overhead versus 29% for hard-constraint systems. A case study on a classic control system demonstrates REPOSE's ability to robustly analyze and calculate the safety-security trade-off.

Conclusion: REPOSE demonstrates that weakly-hard real-time semantics provide an effective foundation for integrating security into RT-CPS without the severe performance penalties of prior approaches, enabling a principled, quantifiable safety-security trade-off.

Abstract: In contemporary IoT edge devices with real-time requirements, security is primarily enforced through design-time parameters associated with security tasks, leading to mechanisms that operate in an \emph{opportunistic} manner. As a result, security checks are often performed as secondary operations. This approach can result in systems where no security tasks are executed due to high utilization by other tasks. An alternative approach taken in prior work is to add security mechanisms to every task in the system, resulting in substantially lower performance than that of a system with no security. These approaches have resulted in an \emph{all-or-nothing} scenario for edge device security, motivating numerous studies on the safety-security trade-off in real-time cyber-physical systems (RT-CPS). This study introduces an analytical framework -- REPOSE -- for evaluating the security feasibility of real-time control systems at runtime. REPOSE is developed for \textit{weakly-hard} real-time control systems that facilitate a ``bounded trade-off'' between safety and security. In contrast to imposing additional (pessimistic) design-time overhead as considered in some real-time security literature, REPOSE performs security operations in both \textit{proactive} and \textit{reactive} manners based on the task's current behavior. Our evaluations show that REPOSE can effectively add security operations to RT-CPS with a feasibility overhead of $0.06\%$ at $80\%$ utilization, compared to a $ 29\%$ overhead observed in systems with hard constraints. Through a case study of a classic control system, we also demonstrate that REPOSE provides a robust framework to \textit{analyze and calculate} the safety-security tradeoff.

</details>


### [64] [Breaking TinyML: Why Quantized Neural Networks Need Domain-Specific Security Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14427)
*Jacob Huckelberry,Andrea Mattia Garavagno,Yuke Zhang,Peter A. Beerel,James Mickens,Vijay Janapa Reddi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper highlights a security gap in TinyML hardware: while most accelerators support quantized neural networks (QNNs), no tailored attack strategies exist for them. The authors propose a two-step attack pipeline that outperforms current state-of-the-art methods in the QNN context, demonstrating the urgent need for hardware-aware security research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TinyML hardware accelerators predominantly support QNNs to meet power and size constraints, yet the security implications of quantization in this hardware context remain unexplored. Although QNNs show comparable or better robustness than full-precision DNNs against standard evasion attacks, no attack has been specifically designed for TinyML hardware. This gap motivates the work.

Method: The authors propose a two-step attack pipeline specifically tailored for QNNs deployed on TinyML hardware. This pipeline is designed to surpass existing attack strategies that were not optimized for the quantized, resource-constrained setting.

Result: The proposed two-step attack pipeline surpasses the current state-of-the-art in the QNN context, demonstrating that existing assumptions about QNN robustness are insufficient when hardware-aware attack strategies are employed.

Conclusion: There is a demonstrated need for more hardware-aware security research in TinyML. The success of the tailored attack pipeline reveals that quantization alone does not guarantee security, and the TinyML community must develop defenses that account for hardware-specific attack surfaces.

Abstract: Most TinyML hardware accelerators focus on supporting Quantized Neural Networks (QNNs) to meet stringent constraints on power consumption and size. Despite this, the security aspects of quantization within TinyML hardware remain largely unexplored. Although previous studies indicate that QNNs demonstrate similar or enhanced robustness when compared to full-precision Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) against typical evasion attacks, no attack strategies tailored specifically for TinyML hardware have been proposed yet. This paper addresses this shortfall by demonstrating how a two-step attack pipeline can surpass the current state-of-the-art in the QNN context and shows the need for more hardware-aware security research.

</details>


### [65] [Securing the Future of IoMT in the Post-Quantum Era: An Edge-Native Federated Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14515)
*Taym Alshoghri,Deemah H. Tashman,Mohammad Reza Gerami,Soumaya Cherkaoui*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents a Kubernetes-based framework integrating Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) into Federated Learning (FL)-enabled Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) systems, validated on a Raspberry Pi testbed, demonstrating that distributed cryptographic processing reduces latency versus sequential designs while maintaining feasible resource overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IoMT devices face strict resource constraints yet handle sensitive health data, making security paramount. FL introduces privacy risks through model updates that may leak medical information. Simultaneously, emerging quantum computing threatens conventional lightweight cryptographic mechanisms, necessitating the integration of PQC into IoMT systems.

Method: The authors propose a scalable Kubernetes-based orchestration framework that integrates post-quantum key establishment, lightweight encryption, and edge-native orchestration into FL-enabled IoMT environments. The framework is validated on a Raspberry Pi testbed, comparing distributed versus sequential cryptographic processing designs.

Result: Distributed cryptographic processing achieves significantly reduced latency compared to sequential designs while maintaining feasible resource overhead on resource-constrained hardware.

Conclusion: The primary contribution is the design and validation of a secure orchestration and communication framework for FL-enabled IoMT systems. Future directions include energy-aware architectures, intelligent security optimization, and resilient next-generation Intelligent Internet of Medical Things (IIoMT) ecosystems.

Abstract: Internet of Medical Things (IoMT) devices operate under strict resource constraints while handling highly sensitive health data, making security and privacy critical concerns. Federated learning (FL) further complicates this landscape, as model updates exchanged during training may unintentionally expose private medical information. Emerging quantum computing capabilities threaten the long-term viability of conventional lightweight cryptographic mechanisms, motivating the integration of Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) into IoMT systems. This article discusses key enabling technologies for quantum-resilient IoMT, including post-quantum key establishment, lightweight encryption, and edge-native orchestration. We propose a scalable Kubernetes-based framework that integrates PQC into FL-enabled IoMT environments and validate it on a Raspberry Pi testbed. Results demonstrate that distributed cryptographic processing significantly reduces latency compared to sequential designs while maintaining feasible resource overhead. The primary contribution of this work lies in the design and validation of a secure orchestration and communication framework for FL-enabled IoMT systems. We conclude by outlining future directions toward energy-aware architectures, intelligent security optimization, and resilient next-generation Intelligent Internet of Medical Things (IIoMT) ecosystems.

</details>


### [66] [From Shield to Target: Denial-of-Service Attacks on LLM-Based Agent Guardrails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14517)
*Yuguang Zhou,Xunguang Wang,Pingchuan Ma,Zhantong Xue,Zhaoyu Wang,Shuai Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: LLM guardrails are vulnerable to DoS attacks where crafted inputs trap them in extended reasoning loops. A beam-search optimization framework generates payloads that amplify token consumption by 13–63× across major models, with up to 148× latency amplification in real-world deployments, potentially paralyzing shared guardrail infrastructures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM guardrails are widely deployed to defend against prompt injection and jailbreak attacks, but their reasoning capabilities create an unexplored attack surface. The authors aim to expose a systematic denial-of-service vulnerability where adversaries can exhaust guardrail resources through crafted inputs that trigger excessive reasoning loops.

Method: Two attack frameworks are proposed: (1) A beam-search optimization framework that uses an LLM proposer with a strategy bank to generate natural-language payloads maximizing guardrail reasoning length. (2) A mechanism-aware structural mutation framework that exploits the guardrail's schema-following nature with lower computational cost. Evaluation spans standalone tests across diverse guardrail architectures, safety templates, and benchmarks, plus end-to-end real-world deployments on web, desktop, code, and multi-agent systems.

Result: The beam-search attack achieves 13–63× token amplification when transferred from a single open-source surrogate to eight leading models (Claude, GPT, Gemini, DeepSeek, Qwen). In real-world agent deployments, latency amplification reaches up to 148×. A single poisoned document can saturate shared guardrail infrastructure, starving co-located agents and paralyzing entire systems.

Conclusion: The work reveals a critical availability flaw in reasoning-based LLM guardrails, demonstrating that their protective reasoning capabilities can be weaponized against them. The authors call for developing cost-bounded, reasoning-robust guardrail designs to mitigate this class of DoS attacks.

Abstract: LLM-based guardrails have emerged as a highly effective defense against prompt injection and jailbreak attacks in autonomous agents. However, we reveal that the very reasoning and task-following capabilities enabling this protection introduce a novel vulnerability: attackers can inject crafted data to trap the guardrail in extended reasoning loops, effectuating a systematic denial-of-service (DoS) attack. To systematically expose this threat, we design a beam-search optimization framework that crafts natural-language payloads to maximize guardrail reasoning length, utilizing an LLM proposer guided by a strategy bank. Based on the observation of guardrail's schema-following nature, we also provide another attack framework driven by mechanism-aware structural mutations with less computational load. The attack efficacy is systematically evaluated in two parts. First, in standalone evaluations, the attack generalizes across diverse guardrail architectures, safety templates, and agent benchmarks. Payloads optimized on a single open-source surrogate successfully transfer to eight leading model backbones (e.g., Claude, GPT, Gemini, DeepSeek, and Qwen), achieving a 13--63$\times$ token amplification. Second, in end-to-end real-world agent deployments (web, desktop, code, and multi-agent systems), the attack reveals up to a 148$\times$ latency amplification. We show that a single poisoned document can saturate shared guardrail infrastructures, effectively starving co-located agents and paralyzing the entire system. By uncovering this availability flaw, our work underscores the urgent need to develop cost-bounded, reasoning-robust guardrails.

</details>


### [67] [Detecting Bot Detection: Prevalence, Techniques, and Implications for Web Measurement Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14525)
*Ralf Gundelach,Michael Mühlhauser,Dominik Herrmann*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A measurement study of 10,000 websites reveals that bot detection causes significant sample loss in browser automation, with 15% of Chromium headless visits blocked versus 7% for other configs. Despite this, 83% of prior web measurement papers fail to discuss bot detection blocking, threatening measurement validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Browser automation frameworks are critical for web security/privacy research, but bot detection increasingly blocks or alters content served to automated browsers, threatening measurement validity. Prior work focuses on detecting deployment of bot detection scripts, but none quantify the actual sample loss caused by blocking. A literature survey reveals 83% of top-tier papers ignore this issue entirely.

Method: The authors conduct a measurement study visiting 10,000 websites across four browser configurations (40K total page visits): Chromium headless, Chromium headed, Firefox, and a instrumented configuration. They develop custom instrumentation to detect when sites probe for automation artifacts, build a taxonomy of bot detection techniques, and measure blocking rates. A header spoofing experiment isolates header-level vs. JavaScript-based detection signals.

Result: Chromium headless experiences a 15% soft block rate vs. 7% for other configurations. 82% of blocks are attributable to bot detection (59% vendor-confirmed, 23% inferred). Cloudflare (37%) and Akamai (26%) dominate blocking. Header spoofing shows 75% of headless-only blocks are triggered by header-level signals alone; however, JavaScript environment probing is more extensive than current blocking rates suggest.

Conclusion: Bot detection creates systematic, provider-correlated sample loss that the web measurement community neither measures nor reports. The downstream impact on specific measurement outcomes remains future work, but the findings urge researchers to incorporate blocking analysis into their methodologies.

Abstract: Browser automation frameworks are essential tools for security and privacy research on the web, yet bot detection scripts increasingly probe their artifacts, threatening measurement validity as automated browsers may be blocked or served different content. Prior work measures detection deployment, while we measure blocking-induced sample loss. Through a literature survey of top-tier security, privacy, and web measurement venues, we find that 83% of papers omit any discussion of bot detection blocking. To address this gap, we conduct a measurement study of 10,000 websites across four browser configurations (40K page visits in total) to quantify detection prevalence and employed techniques. Using custom instrumentation to detect when sites probe for automation, we develop a taxonomy of bot detection techniques and measure how often they appear in practice. Chromium headless encounters a 15% soft block rate compared to 7% for other configurations. Across all conditions, 82% of blocks are attributable to bot detection (59% vendor-confirmed, 23% inferred from condition-dependent blocking), predominantly by providers with integrated bot detection such as Cloudflare (37% block rate) and Akamai (26%). A header spoofing experiment establishes that 75% of Chromium-headless-only blocks are caused by header-level signals alone, yet JavaScript-based environment probing is more extensive than current blocking rates suggest. These findings demonstrate that bot detection creates systematic, provider-correlated sample loss that the web measurement community neither measures nor reports. The downstream effect on specific measurement outcomes remains future work.

</details>


### [68] [When Good Verifiers Go Bad: Self-Improving VLMs Can Regress on New Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14629)
*Jianzhe Lin*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Verifier-driven self-DPO can harm visual-language models when the verifier is task-mismatched: verifiers that improve performance on one task can silently regress performance on another, despite decreasing DPO loss. The authors provide a mechanistic explanation and operational guidelines for safe deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The prevailing assumption in self-improving production VLMs is monotonic: a stronger verifier should produce a stronger student. The authors challenge this by investigating whether verifier quality is task-specific and whether below-threshold verifiers can silently damage student performance.

Method: The authors construct a four-rung open-source verifier ladder and test across three benchmarks (MathVista, MMMU, BLINK) using two student models (Qwen-3-VL-2B and Qwen-2.5-VL-3B). They analyze the relationship between verifier task-rubric accuracy, DPO training loss, and downstream student performance, and provide a mechanistic explanation via a variance theorem for progress-gated replay.

Result: Verifiers above-threshold on MathVista become sub-threshold on MMMU (8-23% rubric accuracy). In this failure regime, every verifier silently regresses the student (3.4-10.9 pp drops below frozen baseline) while DPO loss continues decreasing. Damage is confidence-inverted: more accurate-but-wrong verifiers cause larger regression than near-random ones. The regression replicates across both student models.

Conclusion: Teams should measure target-task rubric accuracy before running verifier-driven loops, rank verifiers by task-specific rubric quality rather than parameter count, and treat diminishing returns in above-threshold regimes as a verifier-side compute budget cap. The variance theorem explains that progress-gated replay amplifies confidently wrong preference pairs when verifier accuracy is insufficient.

Abstract: Verifier-driven self-DPO is a common recipe for self-improving production visual-language models. In this setup, a frozen verifier scores candidate generations, the top- and bottom-scoring candidates form a preference example, and DPO updates the learner. The deployment-time assumption is monotone: a stronger verifier should yield a stronger student. We show that this assumption can fail because verifier quality is highly task-specific. On a four-rung open-source verifier ladder across MathVista, MMMU, and BLINK, the same verifiers that are above-threshold and improve a Qwen-3-VL-2B student on MathVista become sub-threshold on MMMU, where their task-rubric accuracy drops to 8% to 23%.
  In this regime, every verifier we tested silently regresses the student, producing drops of 3.4 to 10.9 percentage points below the frozen baseline while the DPO training loss continues to decrease. The regression replicates on a second student, Qwen-2.5-VL-3B. Moreover, within the failure regime, damage is confidence-inverted: the more accurate-but-still-wrong verifier causes larger regression than a near-random verifier, suggesting that progress-gated replay amplifies confidently wrong preference pairs. We give a compact mechanistic explanation via a variance theorem for progress-gated replay and its direction-mismatch failure mode.
  The deployment message is operational rather than purely diagnostic: before running any verifier-driven loop, teams should measure target-task rubric accuracy, rank verifiers by target-task rubric quality rather than parameter count, and treat diminishing returns in above-threshold regimes as a verifier-side compute budget cap.

</details>

### [69] [The Frustrometer: Detecting User Frustration in Data Visualization Tasks using Biomarkers and Interaction Patterns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13687)
*Johannes Ellemose,Sophia Wanner,Djordje Slijepčević,Laura Cesar,Vanessa Leung,Wolfgang Aigner,Niklas Elmqvist*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The Frustrometer paper investigates real-time detection of user frustration during visualization tasks by fusing physiological and interaction signals, finding that mouse movements and gaze data carry the strongest predictive signal, while physiological signals like heart rate and galvanic skin response offer limited insight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While visualization research has addressed *how* to help stuck users (via onboarding, contextual help, guidance), the critical unsolved problem is *when* to intervene — too early disrupts workflow, too late means the user is already lost. The paper aims to solve this timing problem through real-time frustration prediction.

Method: A controlled study with 14 participants performing analytical tasks on two interactive visualization dashboards. Multiple signals were captured: eye movement, pupil dilation, galvanic skin response (GSR), heart rate, head orientation, mouse dynamics, and keyboard events. Participants self-assessed performance while researchers annotated stuck episodes. A convolutional neural network classifier (the Frustrometer) was trained to predict stuckness in real-time from these multimodal signals.

Result: Autonomous physiological responses (heart rate, GSR) and head orientation provided limited predictive value. Mouse movements and gaze data carried the majority of predictive signal, with mouse movements alone showing strong correlation for some participants — suggesting lightweight instrumentation may suffice for real-time frustration detection.

Conclusion: The findings inform the design of adaptive guidance systems for complex visualization tasks, advocating a multimodal approach to frustration detection that can rely on relatively lightweight, non-invasive interaction signals rather than requiring extensive physiological sensing.

Abstract: Visualization research has largely solved \textit{how} to help a stuck or frustrated user -- through interactive onboarding, contextual help, and active guidance. The unsolved problem is \textit{when}: trigger help too eagerly and you break the user's train of thought; wait too long and they have already gone astray. We present the \textsc{Frustrometer}, a series of experiments to predict user stuckness and frustration by fusing physiological and interaction signals. The Frustrometer consists of a convolutional neural network classifier, that in real-time estimates whether user are stuck in their task or not. We collected data from a controlled study where 14 participants performed analytical tasks on two interactive visualization dashboards while we captured eye movement, pupil dilation, galvanic skin response, heart-rate, head orientation, mouse dynamics, and keyboard events. In addition participants assessed their own performance, while we annotated when during the tasks the participants were stuck. Our results reveal that autonomous physiological responses such as heart-rate and galvanic skin response provide limited insights into the frustration level of the user. Similarly, head orientations are not easily correlated with the frustrations felt by the user during visual analysis tasks. Mouse movements and gaze data conversely carry the majority of predictive signal, with mouse movements alone having a strong correlation for some participants, suggesting that lightweight instrumentation may suffice for real-time frustration detection. We end the paper by discussing how these findings can inform the design of adaptive guidance systems for complex visualization tasks that takes a multimodal approach to frustration and stuckness detection.

</details>


### [70] [Rethinking the UI of GenUI: A Tale of Two Designs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13843)
*Xiang `Anthony' Chen,Savvas Dimitrios Petridis,Tian Deng,Humad Bari,Ruofei Du,Yang Li*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper rethinks GenUI (AI-generated UI mock-ups) design by contrasting the conventional unstructured, depth-first, high-fidelity approach with a structured, breadth-first, low-fidelity alternative. A study with 24 UX professionals reveals trade-offs: structured input surfaces key facets but adds friction; breadth-first exploration reveals more ideas but makes multi-screen previewing difficult; and while low-fidelity has value, professionals prefer high-fidelity due to practice norms and heightened GenAI expectations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GenUI tools inherit conversational AI conventions (unstructured prompts, depth-first design, high-fidelity output), which may not optimally support early-stage, 0-to-1 design exploration. The paper investigates whether this dominant paradigm is well-suited for the divergent, exploratory nature of early UX design.

Method: The authors designed a contrastive GenUI tool featuring structured input, breadth-first exploration, and low-fidelity generation. They conducted a comparative user study with 24 UX designers and product managers who performed mini design exploration exercises using both an existing GenUI tool and the contrastive prototype, gathering qualitative and quantitative data on perceived benefits and trade-offs.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Structured input surfaces critical design facets but increases workload and raises entry barriers. (2) Breadth-first workflow reveals more design possibilities, but previewing UX ideas across many screens remains challenging. (3) Professionals favor high-fidelity output because it aligns with existing workflows, and generative AI amplifies fidelity expectations, though low-fidelity has recognized value for early exploration.

Conclusion: The paper concludes with design implications for GenUI and AI-powered creativity support tools, advocating for a nuanced balance between structure and flexibility, breadth and depth, and fidelity levels tailored to different stages of the design process.

Abstract: GenUI is an emergent class of AI tools that use large models to generate UI mock-ups based on users' high-level descriptions, promising to democratize UX design exploration for a broader audience. Most GenUI designs to date tend to inherit the conventions of conversational large models, such as ChatGPT and Gemini, where a user describes their design needs primarily via an unstructured prompt, and the tool then takes a depth-first approach, delving into the design right away and producing a high-fidelity prototype. In this research, we rethink how well this unstructured, depth-first, and high-fidelity GenUI design can support early-stage, 0-to-1 design exploration. To probe this question, we propose a contrastive design with structured input, breadth-first exploration, and low-fidelity generation. We then conducted a comparison study with 24 UX designers and product managers who conducted mini design exploration exercises using an existing GenUI tool and our contrastive GenUI tool. Findings reveal participants' perceived benefits and trade-offs of the two GenUI designs: structured input surfaces key facets but requires more work, raising entry barriers to start exploration; breadth-first workflow reveals more possibilities, but previewing UX ideas spanning many screens remains hard; and though low fidelity has value, professionals favor high fidelity because it fits practice and GenAI heightens fidelity expectations. We conclude with design implications for GenUI and similar AI-powered creativity support tools.

</details>


### [71] [SpheriCity: Designing Trustworthy Conversational AI for Sustainability Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13854)
*Ahmed Qayyum,Madison Werner,Kathryn Youngblood,Jenna R. Jambeck,Tahiya Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: SpheriCity is a provenance-first conversational agent for synthesizing city-level circularity reports, evaluated by six sustainability experts.
