Results show that transparent sourcing, contextual explanation, and workflow alignment are critical to expert trust in AI-assisted sustainability sensemaking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: City circularity assessment reports are long, heterogeneous, and hard to compare across documents. While LLMs could accelerate synthesis, their opacity, hallucinations, and lack of source transparency undermine trust in high-stakes sustainability contexts, creating a need for verifiable, traceable AI support.

Method: The authors built SpheriCity, a conversational prototype emphasizing provenance, structured synthesis, and interaction scaffolds. They conducted a formative expert review with six sustainability experts using representative query types (cross-city comparison, policy summarization, recommendation tasks), collecting quantitative evaluations and qualitative reflections on dimensions of trust and usefulness.

Result: Experts rated responses across multiple dimensions. Qualitative findings revealed that transparent sourcing, contextual explanations, interpretability, and alignment with expert workflows strongly shaped both trust and perceived usefulness of the system for sustainability knowledge work.

Conclusion: The paper contributes: (1) a conversational prototype for sustainability knowledge sensemaking, (2) an expert-grounded evaluation framework for AI in high-stakes domains, and (3) design insights on how provenance, uncertainty communication, and workflow integration influence expert trust in AI-driven decision support.

Abstract: We present SpheriCity, an expert-grounded conversational prototype designed to support trustworthy knowledge sensemaking from sustainability reports. City-level circularity assessment reports contain rich information about materials, infrastructure, and policy interventions, yet their length and heterogeneous structure make cross-document synthesis and comparison difficult for practitioners and researchers working on circular economy initiatives. While large language models (LLM) promise faster knowledge access and synthesis, their opaque reasoning, hallucinations, and lack of source transparency introduce risks for trust and interpretability, and require verification in high-stakes sustainability contexts. SpheriCity addresses these challenges through a provenance-first conversational agent that foregrounds evidence traceability, structured synthesis, and interaction scaffolds to support exploratory querying and cross-document synthesis across sustainability reports. We conducted a formative expert review with six sustainability experts using representative queries spanning cross-city comparison, policy summarization, and recommendation-oriented tasks. Experts evaluated responses across dimensions and provided qualitative reflections on the system's usefulness for sustainability knowledge work. Our results reveal that transparent sourcing, contextual explanation, interpretability, and alignment with expert workflow strongly shape expert trust and judgments of system usefulness. This work contributes (1) a conversational prototype for sustainability knowledge sensemaking, (2) an expert-grounded evaluation framework for assessing AI responses in high-stakes knowledge domains, and (3) design insights into how provenance, uncertainty communication, and integration in workflow influence expert users' trust in AI assistance for sustainability decision support.

</details>


### [72] [The Silent Cost of Artificial Intelligence Assistance: A Theory of Autonomy Surrender, the Recovery Mechanism, and the Restoration of Human Agency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13962)
*Ancuta Margondai,Julie Rader,Emma Rader,Sara Willox,Mustapha Mouloua*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper models autonomy surrender as a measurable, cumulative process driven by cognitive bandwidth depletion when humans integrate AI into decision-making. It introduces three mechanisms — silent cost, surrender threshold, and recovery mechanisms — and warns of a terminal state ("preference inversion") where AI dependence is experienced as a preference rather than a deficit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The integration of AI into human decision-making has an undertheorized cost: the gradual, often unnoticed surrender of human autonomy in exchange for computational assistance and information access. The paper seeks to formalize this phenomenon theoretically.

Method: Building on the Human Identity and Autonomy Gap (HIAG) framework, the paper advances a theoretical model with three interacting mechanisms: (1) the silent cost of AI assistance (incremental, unaware autonomy transfer), (2) the surrender threshold (a point beyond which reclaiming autonomy becomes cognitively and psychologically difficult), and (3) recovery mechanisms (structured re-entry pathways enabling deliberate human re-assumption of control).

Result: The model predicts that human re-entry into decision loops is an active cognitive event requiring intentional bandwidth restoration, not a passive option. It further predicts a terminal state called "preference inversion," where functional AI dependence is experienced as a preference rather than a deficit, shifting the problem from design to cultural and political domains.

Conclusion: AI systems must incorporate structured recovery mechanisms that preserve human agency and appropriately distribute responsibility. Implications extend to AI system design, governance frameworks, and human factors research, with preference inversion posing a distinct cultural and political challenge beyond design solutions.

Abstract: The integration of artificial intelligence into human decision-making environments has introduced a previously undertheorized cost: the gradual surrender of human autonomy in exchange for access to information and computational assistance. Building on the Human Identity and Autonomy Gap (HIAG) framework, this paper advances a theoretical model of autonomy surrender as a measurable, cumulative process driven by cognitive bandwidth depletion. The model proposes three interacting mechanisms: the silent cost of AI assistance, in which autonomy is transferred incrementally and without awareness; the surrender threshold, beyond which reclaiming autonomous function becomes cognitively and psychologically difficult; and the recovery mechanism, which establishes the design obligation and the ethical responsibility accompanying deliberate human re-assumption of control. The paper argues that human re-entry into the decision loop is not a passive option but an active cognitive event requiring intentional bandwidth restoration. The design of AI systems must incorporate structured re-entry pathways, here termed recovery mechanisms, that preserve human agency while appropriately distributing responsibility. The model further predicts a terminal state, here termed preference inversion, in which functional dependence on AI assistance is experienced not as a deficit but as a preference, transforming the restoration of autonomy from a design problem into a cultural and political one. Implications are drawn for AI system design, governance frameworks, and human factors research.

</details>


### [73] [Visible Adoption, Untracked Contribution: GitHub Evidence of the Accountability Gap Across Three Cohorts of an HCI Prototyping Course](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14054)
*Maria Teresa Parreira,Pranav Prabhat Sinha,Hauke Sandhaus,Wendy Ju*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A longitudinal study across three cohorts (2022–2025) of a graduate HCI course analyzed 203 GitHub repos and 23K commits, revealing that while AI tool disclosure rose from 0% to 66%, explicit contribution attribution remains rare. The gap between disclosure and attribution exposes a failure of disclosure-only accountability norms under continuous, ambient AI co-creation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing accountability frameworks for GenAI use in education rely on disclosure-based norms (stating that AI was used). However, with the shift toward pervasive, ambient AI co-creation ("vibe-coding"), disclosure alone may be insufficient to capture genuine accountability for AI contributions. The authors seek empirical grounding to understand how student AI accountability practices are evolving and where existing norms break down.

Method: Longitudinal observational case study across three cohorts (Fall 2022, 2023, 2025) of the same graduate-level HCI prototyping course. Computational analysis of 203 GitHub repositories containing student activity, encompassing 23,065 student commits. The study distinguishes two levels of AI accountability trace: disclosure (naming that AI was used) and attribution (crediting a specific artifact or task to AI). Builds on prior qualitative work from the 2023 cohort.

Result: Tool disclosure grew from 0% (2022) to 66% (2025) of repositories. However, explicit contribution attribution remains a minority practice across all cohorts. By 2025, AI is embedded as infrastructure in course templates and student-built devices; students increasingly name tools used but rarely specify what those tools contributed. The gap between disclosure and attribution rates reveals where accountability is missing even among students who do disclose.

Conclusion: Disclosure-based accountability frameworks are insufficient for the "vibe-coding" era. The core failure is not student concealment of AI use, but that norms built for episodic, identifiable acts cannot capture continuous, ambient co-creation. The paper offers this case study as grounding for workshop discussions on what genuine co-thinking accountability should entail.

Abstract: This paper presents a longitudinal, observational case study of how student GenAI adoption shifted across three cohorts (Fall 2022, 2023, and 2025) of the same graduate-level HCI prototyping course, using computational analysis of 203 GitHub repositories with student activity and 23,065 student commits. Building on a prior qualitative study of the 2023 cohort, we distinguish two levels of AI accountability trace: disclosure (naming that an AI tool was used) and attribution (crediting a specific artifact or task to an AI tool). We find that tool disclosure grew from 0% to 66% of repositories across the three cohorts, while explicit contribution attribution remains a minority practice, and the gap between the two reveals where accountability is missing even among students who disclose. By 2025, AI is infrastructure embedded in course templates and student-built devices: students increasingly name the tools they used, but rarely specify what those tools contributed. We argue that disclosure-based frameworks are insufficient for the vibe-coding era. The failure is not that students conceal AI use; it is that a norm built for episodic, identifiable acts cannot capture continuous, ambient co-creation. We offer this case study as grounding for the workshop's conversation about what genuine co-thinking accountability looks like.

</details>


### [74] [Thinking Outside the [Chat]Box: Bridging Computer Science and Industrial Design for Cognitive-Inclusive Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14306)
*Virginia Francisco,Daniel Guasch,Raquel Hervás*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper reports on a co-design challenge exploring GenAI interfaces beyond chat for cognitive accessibility. CS and Industrial Design students developed proposals from shared requirements, revealing two complementary scaffolding layers: structural (predictability, trust, navigation) from CS and experiential (pacing, multimodality, agency) from ID. The authors synthesize these into a dual-layer scaffolding framework to expand the design space for cognitively accessible GenAI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GenAI interfaces are predominantly chat-based, imposing high cognitive demands on users with intellectual disabilities (ID) — including difficulties in prompt formulation, response overload, and inability to assess information reliability. The work is motivated by the need to explore alternative, more cognitively accessible interaction models.

Method: A cross-disciplinary co-design challenge was conducted with two student cohorts: Computer Science and Industrial Design. Both cohorts received identical functional requirements (prompt scaffolding, structured output, GUI-based refinement, transparency, personalization) and independently developed interface concepts. The resulting proposals were systematically compared to identify convergence and complementary contributions.

Result: Both cohorts converged on foundational requirements: initial calibration, proactive prompting, and direct manipulation of response fragments. CS teams contributed structural scaffolding (reliability indicators, explicit sources, context management). ID teams contributed experiential scaffolding (pacing, attention guidance, multimodality, step-by-step flows, focus modes, assistant-like integration).

Conclusion: The findings are synthesized into a dual-layer scaffolding framework combining structural and experiential supports, expanding the design space for cognitively accessible GenAI interaction beyond chat-centric models. The authors call for future work on expert refinement, technical feasibility, and empirical validation with users with ID.

Abstract: Current Generative AI (GenAI) interfaces remain largely constrained to chatbox interaction, which can impose high cognitive demands on users and create substantial barriers for people with intellectual disabilities (ID), including prompt formulation difficulties, response overload, and limited mechanisms to assess information reliability. To explore alternative interaction models for cognitive accessibility, we conducted a cross-disciplinary co-design challenge in which two student cohorts (Computer Science and Industrial Design) developed interface concepts from the same set of functional requirements (e.g., prompt scaffolding, structured output, GUI-based refinement, transparency, and personalization). Comparing the resulting proposals reveals both convergence on foundational requirements (notably initial calibration, proactive prompting, and direct manipulation of response fragments) and complementary contributions that outline a multi-layered support system. Computer Science teams primarily produced structural scaffolding, emphasizing predictability, navigability, and trust through mechanisms such as reliability indicators, explicit sources, and context management for long conversations. Industrial Design teams emphasized experiential scaffolding, focusing on pacing, attention guidance, multimodality, and proactive agency, including step-by-step response flows, focus modes, and assistant-like integrations. We synthesize these findings into a dual-layer scaffolding framework that expands the design space for cognitively accessible GenAI interaction beyond chat-centric models and motivates future work on expert refinement, technical feasibility, and empirical validation with users with ID.

</details>


### [75] [Friction in AI-Assisted Clinical Decision-Making: A Case Study on The Role of Questions and 'What-if' Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14406)
*Simon WS Fischer,Hanna Schraffenberger,Miranda L. van Hooff,Serge Thill,Pim Haselager*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: This paper investigates how medical experts perceive two forms of friction—data-driven questions and what-if analysis—designed to reduce AI overreliance in clinical decision-support systems. Through in-situ interviews with 7 clinicians using a replicated real-world DSS, the study found that questions were seen as reminders rather than reflection prompts, while what-if analysis was valued for improving patient care. The prototype was viewed as a promising training tool for novices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To counter overreliance on clinical decision-support systems (DSSs), the authors explore friction mechanisms that promote cognitive engagement and reflective thinking, and investigate how such interventions are perceived by medical experts in practice.

Method: The authors replicated a real-world DSS used in clinical practice and developed a prototype incorporating two friction mechanisms: data-driven questions and what-if analysis. They gathered qualitative feedback through in-situ interviews with 7 clinicians, focusing on their perceptions of these friction designs in an authentic decision-making context.

Result: Data-driven questions were perceived as unhelpful for stimulating reflective thinking but were seen as useful reminders to attend to relevant information. What-if hypotheticals were found useful for potentially improving patient care. Overall, clinicians regarded the prototype as a promising training tool for novice clinicians rather than for experienced practitioners.

Conclusion: The paper contributes design recommendations for incorporating friction into clinical work practices and advances human-AI interaction research aimed at encouraging reflection to mitigate overreliance on AI-driven decision support.

Abstract: Clinical decision-making is augmented by decision-support systems (DSSs). To counter overreliance on DSSs, several methods have been proposed that create friction in order to promote cognitive engagement and reflection. In this paper, we investigate how two such forms of friction, namely data-driven questions and `what-if' analysis, are perceived by medical experts. For a real-world decision task, we replicated a DSS used in clinical practice and gathered clinicians' feedback on a prototype through in-situ interviews (n=7). Our findings suggest that while the questions were perceived as unhelpful for reflective thinking, they could serve as reminders to consider relevant information. Furthermore, inspecting `what-if' hypotheticals was found useful for potentially improving patient care. Clinicians saw our prototype as a promising training tool for novice clinicians. From the clinicians' feedback, we make recommendations for designing friction in work practices. Our work contributes to human-AI interaction research, which aims to encourage reflection to mitigate AI overreliance.

</details>


### [76] [Fabula: Building a Narrative Storytelling Sidekick with the Writers' Community](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14411)
*Piotr Mirowski,Ben Wedin,Reinald Kim Amplayo,Rich Galt,Duncan Williams,Rida Qadri,Jaume Sanchez-Elias,Erin Drake-Kajioka,Sian Gooding,Lucia Lopez-Rivilla,Joao G. M. Araujo,Lion Schulz,Satinder Baveja,Shakir Mohamed,Edward Grefenstette,Laura Rimell,Richard Evans*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Fabula is an interactive AI-assisted fiction writing app that structures stories hierarchically into scenes and beats using narratological theory. Evaluated with 42 expert writers, the paper interrogates design choices around AI auto-evaluation, narrative plan visibility, cross-cultural applicability, and convergent iteration, while also serving as a cultural probe for adversarial design insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore how detailed, narratologically-informed narrative plans can be integrated into AI-assisted creative writing tools, and to critically evaluate whether such structuring supports or constrains writers' creativity across different storytelling traditions and media (screenplays, plays).

Method: The authors built Fabula using a hierarchical story model (scenes and beats) grounded in narratological theory, with participatory AI design. They conducted design interviews and writing sessions with 42 expert writers (both casual and published), large-scale internal and external testing, and employed an LM-based auto-evaluator optimized on human expert preferences. The system was also used as a cultural probe for adversarial design analysis.

Result: Four design interrogations are reported: (1) the LM auto-evaluator optimized on expert preferences can improve story quality; (2) users showed mixed preferences regarding UI exposure of the narrative plan alongside the script; (3) narratological assumptions did not universally fit localized storytelling traditions, revealing limitations for screenwriters and playwrights; (4) convergent iteration over story plans showed both creative support and potential constraints, with critical feedback informing adversarial design insights and future directions for writing feedback and interactive storytelling.

Conclusion: Fabula demonstrates both promise and tension in AI-assisted structured narrative writing. While hierarchical planning and expert-aligned auto-evaluation can enhance story quality, cultural and medium-specific mismatches highlight the need for flexible, pluralistic narratological models. Adversarial design probing reveals broader potentials for writing feedback systems and interactive storytelling platforms.

Abstract: We design and evaluate Fabula, an interactive app for fiction writers. Fabula uses detailed narrative plans informed by general narratological theory. Stories are structured hierarchically into scenes and beats that can be (re)generated and revised at script and story plan level. Using participatory AI, we critically evaluate and improve Fabula with casual and published writers, via design interviews and writing sessions with 42 experts, and large-scale internal and external testing. We interrogate our design choices: (1) whether a language model-based auto-evaluator, optimized on human experts' preferences, can improve story quality, (2) whether users want UI that exposes the detailed narrative plan alongside the story script, (3) to what extent our narratology assumptions fit localised storytelling traditions and serve screenwriters or playwrights, and (4) whether convergent iteration over the story plan supports writers' creativity. Building on critical feedback and concerns, we use Fabula as a cultural probe in adversarial design, and identify potentials for writing feedback and for interactive storytelling.

</details>


### [77] [The Self-Aware Body: A User-Centered Framework for Designing Therapeutic Sonic Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14664)
*Prithvi Ravi Kantan,Sofia Dahl,Erika G. Spaich*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: A design framework for movement sonification technologies in motor rehabilitation that combines perceptual calibration, professional audio mixing workflows, and user-centered healthcare methodology, exemplified by the HearWalk gait biofeedback system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Movement sonification technologies show evidence of effectiveness but lack systematic clinical adoption due to absent standardized development methodologies and insufficient integration of clinical stakeholder perspectives into interaction design.

Method: The framework comprises three components: (1) conceptual reframing of design as calibration of sonic variability to perceptual affordances and clinical demands; (2) a practical design platform using professional audio mixing signal-flow architecture for structured, rapid iterative exploration; (3) a user-centered development methodology adapted from healthcare intervention science grounding design in clinician and patient engagement. Illustrated via the HearWalk biofeedback system for hemiparetic gait rehabilitation.

Result: The framework provides a structured, learnable, and clinically-grounded approach to designing movement sonification technologies, with identification of where AI/LLMs can assist versus where human clinical and perceptual expertise remains irreplaceable.

Conclusion: Systematic adoption of movement sonification in clinical practice requires standardized design methodologies that bridge perceptual science, professional audio workflows, and healthcare intervention design, with AI offering selective augmentation rather than replacement of human expertise.

Abstract: This chapter presents a framework for designing therapeutic sonic interaction technologies, with a focus on movement sonification: the real-time conversion of bodily motion into sound that serves as feedback during motor rehabilitation. Despite growing evidence for their effectiveness, technologies implementing movement sonification are yet to be systematically adopted as part of clinical practice, potentially due to a lack of standardized development methodologies as well as inadequate integration of clinical stakeholder perspectives into interaction design. The framework addresses these barriers through three interconnected contributions. The first is a conceptual reframing of the design task as the calibration of sonic variability to the perceptual affordances of the listener and the demands of the clinical context. The second is a practical design platform inspired by professional audio mixing workflows, which imposes a structured and learnable signal-flow architecture on the interaction design process and enables rapid iterative exploration. The third is a user-centered development methodology adapted from healthcare intervention science, which grounds design decisions in engagement with the clinicians and patients who will use the resulting systems. The HearWalk biofeedback system for hemiparetic gait rehabilitation illustrates the framework, and the chapter concludes by examining where large language models and AI tools can meaningfully assist each stage of this design process, as well as where human clinical and perceptual expertise remains irreplaceable.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [78] [Personalization and Evaluation of Conversational Information Access](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13717)
*Hideaki Joko*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This thesis addresses three core challenges in building reliable Conversational Information Access (CIA) systems: extracting personal context via conversational entity linking (ConEL and CREL), generating personalized responses using large-scale datasets (LAPS), and evaluating systems effectively with a reference-free metric (FACE) that aligns with human judgments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As users increasingly prefer direct, conversational answers over traditional search result lists, CIA systems must account for personal context to be reliable. However, existing methods lack robust solutions for extracting personal context from conversations, generating personalized responses at scale, and evaluating such systems in an interpretable, human-aligned manner.

Method: The thesis proposes three contributions: (1) CREL, a novel Entity Linking method tailored for conversational settings, supported by a new benchmark dataset (ConEL); (2) LAPS, an efficient pipeline for constructing large-scale, human-written, personalized conversational datasets to study preference-driven response generation; and (3) FACE, an automatic, reference-free evaluation metric that assesses full conversations and correlates closely with human judgments.

Result: The work introduces ConEL as a dataset for conversational entity linking, proposes CREL for improved EL in dialogues, demonstrates LAPS for scalable personalized dataset construction and personalized response generation, and presents FACE as an effective automatic evaluation framework aligned with human assessments.

Conclusion: Addressing personal context extraction, personalized generation, and interpretable evaluation collectively advances the development of reliable CIA systems. The proposed methods — CREL, LAPS, and FACE — provide foundational tools for building conversational systems that are both personalized and trustworthy.

Abstract: Conversational interactions have reshaped information retrieval systems, as users increasingly favour direct answers over traditional hyperlinks. To build reliable Conversational Information Access (CIA) systems that account for personal context, this thesis addresses challenges: (1) personal context extraction, (2) personalized response generation, and (3) effective and interpretable system evaluation. First, we tackle personal context extraction by studying what Entity Linking (EL) in conversations entails, introducing a dataset for conversational entity linking (ConEL), and proposing CREL, a novel EL method tailored for conversational settings. Second, we focus on personalized response generation by proposing LAPS, a method for efficiently constructing large-scale, human-written, personalized conversational datasets, and using them to study how users' preferences can be utilized to generate personalized responses. Finally, we address the need for effective and interpretable system evaluation by introducing FACE, an automatic, reference-free method that assesses entire conversations and aligns closely with human judgments.

</details>


### [79] [Nomenclature Ontology for Medical And Disease names (NOMAD): taxonomy of types and origins of disease names](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13719)
*Spiros Denaxas,Cai Ytsma,Giannos Louloudis,Jackie MacArthur,Harry Hemingway*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: NOMAD is a meta-taxonomy classifying disease names by naming convention, applied to 22,548 ICD-10-CM entries via a multi-label ML pipeline. 99.1% of terms were classified (mean 2.12 labels), revealing anatomical names dominate (63.8%), eponymous (9.7%) and geographical (1.9%) names are rarer than perceived, only 2.6% of eponymous diseases are named after women, and naming profiles vary substantially by medical specialty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disease nomenclature has developed organically and heterogeneously across centuries, yet no systematic framework exists to characterize naming conventions across all diseases. The authors seek to fill this gap by creating a structured taxonomy for classifying how diseases are named.

Method: The authors developed NOMAD, a two-level meta-taxonomy with 9 top-level categories and 20 subcategories. They applied it to 22,548 index entries from ICD-10-CM 2026 Alphabetical Index using a scalable three-stage machine learning-driven classification pipeline with multi-label classification to reflect compositional naming. Manual review of a 10% random sample (n=2,255) was conducted for validation, yielding macro-averaged Cohen's Kappa of 0.832.

Result: 99.1% of terms were classified (mean 2.12 labels/entry). Anatomical categories were most prevalent (63.8%), followed by Descriptive (48.4%) and Pathophysiological (40.2%). Eponymous (9.7%) and Geographical (1.9%) labels were less common than cultural prominence suggests. Only 57 of all eponymous diseases (2.6%) were named after a female person. Naming profiles varied substantially across ICD-10-CM chapters: infectious diseases dominated by etiological/geographical labels, circulatory by anatomical/pathophysiological, and mental/behavioral disorders by socio-behavioral labels.

Conclusion: NOMAD provides the first systematic framework for characterizing disease naming conventions at scale, revealing quantifiable patterns in medical nomenclature and exposing biases such as stark gender disparity in eponymous disease names. The taxonomy and classification pipeline offer a foundation for studying epistemological traditions across medical specialties.

Abstract: The nomenclature of human disease has developed organically over the past centuries using Greek, Latin, and Arabic terminology and reflects the idiosyncrasies of different eras of medical discovery. Despite evident heterogeneity in naming practices, no systematic framework exists for characterising these conventions across all diseases. In this paper, we describe the Nomenclature Ontology for Medical And Disease names (NOMAD), a meta-taxonomy that classifies disease names according to their naming conventions. We developed a two-level taxonomy comprising 9 top-level categories and 20 subcategories and applied it to 22,548 index entries from the ICD-10-CM 2026 Alphabetical Index in a scalable three-stage machine learning-driven classification pipeline. Classification was multi-label, reflecting the compositional nature of medical nomenclature. We classified 99.1% of terms with a mean of 2.12 labels per entry. Anatomical categories were the most prevalent (63.8% of entries), followed by Descriptive (48.4%) and Pathophysiological (40.2%), while Eponymous and Geographical labels were less common than their cultural prominence might suggest (9.7% and 1.9% respectively). Among all Eponymous diseases, we identified only 57 (2.6%) of diseases named after a female person. We manually reviewed a random sample of n=2,255 entries (10%) for accuracy and calculated a full agreement rate of 70% and partial agreement rate of 26% (macro-averaged Cohen's Kappa score 0.832). Naming convention profiles varied substantially across ICD-10-CM chapters, reflecting specialty-specific epistemological traditions: infectious disease chapters were dominated by etiological labels and showed the highest proportion of geographical region related labels, the circulatory chapter by anatomical and pathophysiological labels, and mental and behavioural disorders showed the highest prevalence of socio-behavioral labels.

</details>


### [80] [TASR: Training-Free Adaptive Stopping for Iterative Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13814)
*Adrian Kieback,Uyiosa Philip Amadasun,Aman Chadha,Aaron Elkins*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: TASR is a simple, training-free stopping rule for iterative RAG agents that triggers when the model repeats its previous answer and the calibrated logit margin exceeds 0.25, achieving near-optimal performance at substantially reduced retrieval calls across 24 diverse configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Iterative RAG agents waste compute by continuing retrieval after convergence. Existing stopping policies require learned components trained on labeled trajectories, making them model- and task-specific. A training-free, universally applicable stopping rule is needed.

Method: TASR is a one-line predicate: stop when the normalized answer matches the previous round AND the isotonically calibrated logit margin exceeds a fixed threshold of 0.25. The rule was selected via exhaustive enumeration of 381 candidate rules. No classifier or value head is trained; the threshold is fixed across all configurations. Signal-quality analysis compares verbalized confidence scores against logit margins.

Result: On a 3-model × 2-dataset distractor grid, TASR retains 94.8% of fixed-k=5 macro F1 at 62.6% of its calls and exceeds fixed-k=3 by +3.42 F1. On 9 open-domain BM25 cells: 55.01 F1 at 2.98 calls vs. 54.33 at 3.00 for fixed-k=3. On 9 dense-retrieval cells across two retriever families: zero significant regressions. No alternative among 381 candidates Pareto-dominates TASR. Verbalized confidence collapses (96.5% = 5, entropy 0.182 nats); logit margin achieves 44× better class-conditional separation.

Conclusion: TASR provides an auditable, training-free Pareto baseline for iterative RAG stopping. Its design is grounded in the measurable pathology of collapsed verbalized confidence in RLHF-tuned models. The fixed 0.25 threshold generalizes across models, retrievers, and corpora without retraining.

Abstract: Iterative retrieval-augmented generation agents commonly overspend by continuing to retrieve after the model has converged on an answer, incurring calls that change neither the prediction nor the supporting evidence. Existing remedies learn a stopping policy from labeled trajectories, tying the decision to a trained component that requires retraining for each new model or task. We propose TASR (Training-Free Adaptive Stopping Rule), a one-line predicate that fires when the model repeats its previous-round normalized answer and the isotonically calibrated logit margin exceeds 0.25. No classifier or value head is learned; the threshold is fixed across all twenty-four (model, retriever, corpus) configurations we evaluate. On a 3-model x 2-dataset distractor grid, TASR retains 94.8% of fixed-k=5's macro F1 at 62.6% of its calls and exceeds fixed-k=3 by +3.42 F1. The pattern holds on nine open-domain BM25 cells (55.01 F1 at 2.98 calls vs. 54.33 at 3.00 for fixed-k=3) and, with calibration locked from the distractor split, on nine dense-retrieval cells across two retriever families, with zero significant regressions in either extension. The rule was selected from an exhaustive enumeration of 381 candidate stopping rules; no alternative Pareto-dominates it on any evaluated configuration. A signal-quality analysis shows that verbalized 1-5 confidence collapses on RLHF-tuned models (96.5% of values equal 5, entropy 0.182 nats), while the logit margin achieves 44x better class-conditional separation, grounding the design in a measurable model pathology. TASR is an auditable, training-free Pareto baseline against which learned stopping controllers can be compared. Code is publicly available.

</details>


### [81] [Hybrid Neural Retrieval with Generative Query Refinement for Quranic Passage Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13837)
*Mohamed G. Salman,Mohammad E. Moftah,Ali Hamdi*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A four-phase neural architecture combining dense and sparse retrieval, cross-encoder reranking, confidence gating, and T5-based refinement is proposed to improve multi-verse Quranic passage retrieval, achieving Recall@10 of 0.7024 and MAP@10 of 0.4947 on an expanded Quran QA 2022 dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quranic Passage Retrieval is hindered by the linguistic complexity and semantic gap between Modern Standard Arabic (MSA) queries and Classical Arabic (CA) of the Quran, causing conventional retrieval methods to struggle with multi-verse retrieval and zero-answer queries.

Method: A four-phase neural architecture: (1) hybrid candidate retrieval using AraColBERT dense indexing and BM25 sparse retrieval, (2) semantic reranking via a CAMeLBERTmix cross-encoder, (3) confidence gating to filter zero-answer queries, and (4) an AraT5-based refinement module for multi-verse aggregation.

Result: Recall@10 = 0.7024, MAP@10 = 0.4947, MRR = 0.5807. The system outperforms baselines in comprehensive and reliable retrieval, though shows a marginal tradeoff in absolute top-rank precision (MRR) compared to heavily optimized single models.

Conclusion: The proposed architecture provides a substantially more comprehensive, reliable, and context-aware solution for multi-verse Quranic passage retrieval, effectively addressing the MSA-CA semantic gap and zero-answer query filtering despite a slight MRR tradeoff.

Abstract: Quranic Passage Retrieval (PR) could be a challenging task due to the linguistic complexity and the semantic gap between the Modern Standard Arabic (MSA) used in daily queries and the Classical Arabic (CA) of the Holy Quran. These factors hinder conventional retrieval methods. To handle these limitations and improve multi-verse retrieval and filter the zero-answer queries, this paper proposes a four-phase neural architecture designed to enhance retrieval accuracy and contextual understanding. The methodology combines hybrid candidate retrieval using AraColBERT dense indexing and BM25 sparse retrieval, followed by semantic reranking with a CAMeLBERTmix cross-encoder. A confidence gating mechanism is then applied to filter zero-answer queries, and an AraT5-based refinement module for multi-verse aggregation. The system is evaluated on an expanded version of the Quran QA 2022 dataset. Results show improved performance compared to the baseline models, achieving a Recall@10 of 0.7024 and a Mean Average Precision (MAP@10) of 0.4947. While the system exhibits a marginal tradeoff in absolute top-rank precision (MRR = 0.5807) compared to heavily optimised single models, the proposed architecture provides a substantially more comprehensive, reliable, and context aware solution for multi-verse Quranic passage retrieval.

</details>


### [82] [Mood-Aware Music Recommendation: Integrating User Affective Signals into Ranking Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13858)
*Terence Zeng,Abhishek K. Umrawal*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper proposes a mood-conditioned ranking framework for music recommendation that integrates user affective signals (energy-valence space) via softmax-based sampling. Single-blind experiments show improved perceived recommendation quality compared to a baseline, offering preliminary evidence for emotion-aware music personalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Collaborative filtering performs poorly in sparse interaction domains like music streaming. Content-based filtering has explored genre, instrumentation, and lyrics, but emotion recognition — despite the strong influence of mood on music choice — has received relatively little attention. The authors aim to fill this gap by leveraging emotional signals for personalization.

Method: A mood-conditioned ranking framework that integrates user affective signals into the recommendation process. Specifically, it employs softmax-based sampling within the energy-valence (circumplex) emotional space to condition recommendations on mood. Evaluation is conducted via single-blind experiments where participants compare the proposed system's recommendations against a baseline.

Result: The proposed mood-conditioned approach yields improved perceived recommendation quality over the baseline, as measured by participant judgments in single-blind comparisons.

Conclusion: Incorporating mood-based inputs into music recommendation systems shows preliminary effectiveness, suggesting that emotion recognition is a promising and underexplored direction for improving personalization in music streaming platforms.

Abstract: Recommendation systems are essential in modern music streaming platforms due to the vast amount of available content. While collaborative filtering is widely used to suggest items based on the preferences of others with similar patterns, it performs poorly in domains where user-item interactions are sparse, such as music. Content-based filtering is an alternative approach that examines the qualities of the items themselves. Genre, instrumentation, and lyrics have been explored; however, relatively little attention has been given to emotion recognition. Since a user's emotional state strongly influences their music choice, incorporating mood signals offers a promising direction for personalization. In this work, we propose a mood-conditioned ranking framework that integrates user affective signals into the recommendation process via softmax-based sampling in the energy-valence space. We evaluate the approach via single-blind experiments in which participants compare recommendations from the proposed system against a baseline. The results indicate improved perceived recommendation quality, providing preliminary evidence for the effectiveness of incorporating mood-based inputs into music recommendations.

</details>


### [83] [ADORE: Iterative Query Expansion with Retrieval-Grounded Relevance Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13905)
*Amin Bigdeli,Negar Arabzadeh,Radin Hamidi Rad,Sajad Ebrahimi,Charles L. A. Clarke,Ebrahim Bagheri*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ADORE is an iterative query expansion framework that uses retrieval-grounded feedback — generating pseudo-passages, observing corpus response, and evaluating relevance — to refine expansions. It outperforms baselines by 24.5% over BM25 and 3.6% over prior expansion methods on BEIR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM-based query expansion methods are generation-driven, producing plausible expansions without verifying how the target corpus responds. This causes retrieval drift, reinforces misleading vocabulary, and misses discriminative terms. The authors argue that effective expansion requires retrieval-grounded feedback rather than single-pass generation.

Method: ADORE (ADapt, Observe, Relevance Evaluate) is an iterative framework with three per-round steps: (1) an LLM generates pseudo-passages as expansions, (2) a retriever queries the target corpus to expose the corpus response, and (3) a relevance assessor evaluates retrieved documents against the original query. These judgments identify what terms to reinforce, what remains undercovered, and what to suppress in subsequent rounds.

Result: On BEIR, ADORE improves average nDCG@10 by 24.5% over BM25 and 3.6% over the strongest prior query expansion method. On BRIGHT, gains reach 122.9% over BM25 and 9.2% over the best expansion baseline. Consistent improvements are reported across TREC Deep Learning, BEIR, and BRIGHT benchmarks.

Conclusion: Retrieval-grounded feedback, rather than generation-only expansion, is key to effective query expansion. ADORE's observe-and-adapt loop significantly mitigates retrieval drift and consistently outperforms strong baselines. Code and data are publicly released.

Abstract: LLM-based query expansion improves retrieval by enriching the original query with additional context. Yet most methods remain generation-driven, producing plausible pseudo-documents or expansions without checking how the target corpus responds. This can introduce retrieval drift, amplify misleading vocabulary, or miss terms that distinguish relevant from non-relevant documents. We argue that effective expansion requires retrieval-grounded feedback, not just single-pass generation or unverified iteration. We introduce ADORE (ADapt, Observe, Relevance Evaluate), an iterative framework that turns retrieval outcomes into feedback for the next expansion. At each round, an LLM generates pseudo-passages, a retriever exposes the corpus response, and a relevance assessor evaluates retrieved documents against the original query. These judgments identify what to reinforce, what remains undercovered, and what to suppress. Across TREC Deep Learning, BEIR, and BRIGHT, ADORE consistently outperforms strong query expansion baselines with notable improvements across nearly all evaluation settings, improving average nDCG@10 by 24.5% over BM25 and 3.6% over the strongest prior query expansion method on BEIR, and by 122.9% over BM25 and 9.2% over the best query expansion baseline on BRIGHT. Our code and data are publicly available.

</details>


### [84] [When Recommendation Denoising Meets Popularity Bias: Understanding and Mitigating Their Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14046)
*Guohang Zeng,Jie Lu,Guangquan Zhang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper revisits the small-loss heuristic for denoising recommender systems, showing that popularity bias causes tail-item positives to receive larger losses even when genuine, leading loss-based denoising to wrongly suppress clean long-tail signals. The authors propose Popularity-Aware Denoising (PAD), a plug-in framework that adjusts denoising strength based on item popularity, preserving tail signals while aggressively denoising head items.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Implicit feedback (clicks, views) contains false-positive noise from mis-clicks, exposure bias, and UI effects. Existing denoising methods use the small-loss heuristic—treating high-loss interactions as noisy—but this conflates genuinely hard-to-fit tail-item positives with noise. Since tail items are sparsely observed, they naturally incur higher losses, making small-loss reweighting systematically suppress clean long-tail signals and exacerbate the head-tail imbalance in supervision.

Method: The authors formalize the problem through an effective head-tail signal ratio metric that quantifies how denoising weights alter supervision across item popularity groups. They derive a conditional reallocation result: when tail-positive loss distributions are right-shifted relative to head positives, monotone loss-based reweighting necessarily increases the effective head-tail ratio versus ERM. PAD addresses this by modulating denoising strength inversely with item popularity—applying aggressive denoising to head items and conservative denoising to tail items. PAD is lightweight and pluggable into existing denoising recommenders.

Result: Experiments on three datasets (including ML-1M, Yelp, and a large-scale industrial dataset) with three backbone denoising methods show PAD consistently improves recommendation accuracy over representative baselines. It also yields favorable accuracy-diversity tradeoffs. Gains are especially pronounced on MF-style recommenders, which are more susceptible to the head-tail imbalance issue.

Conclusion: The small-loss heuristic in denoising recommendation introduces a systematic popularity bias that suppresses tail-item supervision. PAD corrects this by making denoising strength popularity-dependent, offering a simple, effective, and model-agnostic solution that improves both accuracy and long-tail coverage.

Abstract: Implicit feedback is the dominant data source for recommender systems, but behavioral logs are often contaminated by false-positive interactions caused by mis-clicks, biased exposure, and interface effects. Denoising recommendation methods improve robustness by down-weighting or filtering interactions suspected to be noisy, often relying on the small-loss heuristic. We revisit this heuristic through the lens of popularity bias. Tail-item positives can be harder to fit because they are sparsely observed, and thus may receive larger losses even when they reflect genuine user preference. Under such popularity-dependent loss patterns, monotone loss-based reweighting can suppress clean-but-hard tail signals and increase the head-tail imbalance in effective supervision.
  We formalize this interaction through the effective head-tail signal ratio induced by denoising weights and derive a conditional reallocation result: when the loss distribution of tail positives is right-shifted relative to that of head positives, small-loss reweighting increases the effective head-tail signal ratio compared with ERM. Motivated by this analysis, we propose Popularity-Aware Denoising (PAD), a lightweight plug-in framework that modulates denoising strength by item popularity. PAD applies stronger denoising to highly exposed items while being more conservative on tail items, preserving more clean-but-hard long-tail signals. Experiments on three datasets and three backbones show that PAD generally improves over representative denoising baselines and provides favorable accuracy-diversity tradeoffs, especially on MF-style recommenders.

</details>


### [85] [Knowledge Graph Enhanced Memory-Augmented Retrieval for Long Context Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14047)
*Ghadir Alselwi,Basem Suleiman,Hao Xue,Shoaib Jameel,Hakim Hacid,Flora D. Salim,Imran Razzak*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: KGERMAR constructs dynamic, context-specific knowledge graphs from input text during inference, integrating graph-structural embeddings with textual semantics via a multi-component memory architecture to improve long-context language modeling, achieving up to 8.5% lower perplexity and 2–2.5x better memory efficiency compared to memory-augmented baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context language modeling requires maintaining coherent understanding of entity states and relationships across thousands of tokens, which semantic similarity alone cannot address. Existing approaches fail to capture explicit entity relationships and domain-specific knowledge that adapts to input contexts rather than relying on fixed knowledge bases.

Method: KGERMAR performs real-time entity and relation extraction to build contextual knowledge graphs during inference. It maintains three memory banks — contextual, semantic, and structural — with retrieval signals fused via learned weights. The framework integrates graph-structural embeddings with textual semantics through a multi-component memory architecture, enabling domain-adaptive retrieval that leverages both semantic similarity and explicit entity relationships.

Result: Evaluated on SlimPajama (84.7K examples), WikiText-103 (4,358 examples), PG-19 (100 examples), and Proof-pile (46.3K examples), KGERMAR achieves up to 8.5% lower perplexity and 2–2.5x better memory efficiency than memory-augmented baselines across context lengths from 1K to 32K tokens, with superior in-context learning performance across five NLU tasks.

Conclusion: The dynamic knowledge graph construction approach advances memory-augmented language modeling by enabling domain-specific knowledge representation that adapts to input contexts rather than relying on fixed knowledge bases.

Abstract: Long-context language modeling requires not only extending context windows but maintaining coherent understanding of entity states and relationships across thousands of tokens -- a challenge that semantic similarity alone cannot address. KGERMAR addresses this by constructing dynamic, context-specific knowledge graphs from input text during inference, enabling domain-adaptive retrieval that leverages both semantic similarity and explicit entity relationships. The framework performs real-time entity and relation extraction to build contextual knowledge graphs, then integrates graph-structural embeddings with textual semantics through a multi-component memory architecture. Three memory banks -- contextual, semantic, and structural -- are maintained with retrieval signals fused via learned weights to capture both surface-level semantics and deeper relational patterns. Evaluated on SlimPajama (84.7K training examples), WikiText-103 (4,358 examples), PG-19 (100 examples), and Proof-pile (46.3K examples), KGERMAR achieves up to 8.5\% lower perplexity and 2--2.5x better memory efficiency than memory-augmented baselines across context lengths from 1K to 32K tokens, with superior in-context learning performance across five NLU tasks. The dynamic knowledge graph construction approach advances memory-augmented language modeling by enabling domain-specific knowledge representation that adapts to input contexts rather than relying on fixed knowledge bases.

</details>


### [86] [CoRe: A Continuously Reward-Finetuned LLM Query Rewriter for Multi-Stage Context-Aware Relevance in Web-Scale Video Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14127)
*Yilin Wen,Rong Yang,Xiaojia Chang,Hong Sun,Gefu Tang,Chunhui Liu,Jeffrey Chen,Zeyu Ma,Lisong Qiu,Xiaochuan Fan,Congjia Yu,Quan Zhou,Yuheng Chen,Zian Wang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: CoRe is a production LLM-based query rewriting system that uses a multiplicative ratio reward derived from the deployed multimodal relevance model and a semi-online Mixed Preference Optimization loop to enable cost-effective weekly redeployment. It has been successfully deployed in a major short-video search engine, reducing change-query rates while maintaining or improving relevance and engagement metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Production LLM-based query rewriters face a fundamental tension: training rewards must faithfully reflect how the production ranker consumes rewrites to avoid simulation gaps, but training must be cheap enough to support continuous redeployment as data drifts. Existing offline reward proxies fail to close this gap, and the computational cost of using the actual deployed relevance model as a reward signal at scale is prohibitive.

Method: CoRe introduces three key components: (1) A reward function that uses the deployed multimodal relevance model directly, structured as a multiplicative ratio that mirrors the production fusion algebra, closing the simulation-production gap. (2) A semi-online Mixed Preference Optimization (MPO) loop where a DPO-style pairwise objective restricts gradient flow to only a small top-k/bottom-k subset of sampled trajectories, and a phase structure reduces trainer-inference server parameter synchronization from per-step to per-phase, making multi-million-instance weekly training affordable. (3) An automated promotion gate over reward-like and stability metrics that detects and recovers from reward hacking. The rewriter output serves as parallel relevance signals at recall, rawrank, and finerank stages without displacing original signals.

Result: Deployed weekly for over five months in a major short-video search engine. Two sequential online A/B tests — first deploying at finerank, then extending to recall and rawrank — yielded statistically significant reductions in change-query rate on rewrite-impacted queries, with all headline relevance and engagement metrics moving in the expected positive direction. The automated promotion gate successfully detected and recovered from a real reward-hacking incident.

Conclusion: CoRe demonstrates that it is feasible to deploy an LLM-based query rewriter in a large-scale production search system with continuous weekly retraining, using the deployed relevance model directly as a reward without prohibitive computational cost. The multiplicative ratio reward form and semi-online MPO loop are critical enablers, and the phased deployment strategy (finerank first, then recall/rawrank) provides a safe rollout path with bounded blast radius.

Abstract: LLM-based query rewriters in production face a tension: the training reward must reflect how the rewrite is consumed by the production ranker, yet the training procedure must be cheap enough to support continuous redeployment as data drifts. We present CoRe (Context Relevance), such a system, redeployed weekly for over five months in a major short-video search engine. Our reward uses the deployed multimodal relevance model as its source and a multiplicative ratio form mirroring the production fusion algebra, closing the simulation-production gap that offline reward proxies leave open. A semi-online Mixed Preference Optimization loop makes this reward affordable at multi-million-instance weekly scale: a DPO-style pairwise objective restricts the gradient pass to a small top-k/bottom-k subset of sampled trajectories, and a phase structure reduces trainer/inference-server parameter syncs from per-step to per-phase. An automated promotion gate over reward-like and stability metrics detected and recovered from a real reward-hacking incident in production. Rewriter output is consumed as parallel relevance signals at recall, rawrank, and finerank without displacing the original signals, bounding rewriter-failure blast radius. Online A/B from two sequential production launches, first deploying the rewriter at finerank, then extending consumption to recall and rawrank, delivers statistically significant reductions in change-query rate on rewrite-impacted queries, with all headline relevance and engagement metrics moving in the expected direction.

</details>


### [87] [Verifiable User Simulation for Search and Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14474)
*Chenglong Ma,Xinye Wanyan,Danula Hettiachchi,Ziqi Xu,Yongli Ren,Jeffrey Chan*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This tutorial proposes a design-and-audit framework for LLM-based user simulators, decomposing them into seven auditable components (Persona, Contract, Execution, Trace, Verification, Feedback, Refinement) to address opacity, fidelity, and demographic bias concerns in simulation-based evaluation of search, recommendation, and RAG systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based user simulators are increasingly used to evaluate IR and recommender systems, but they suffer from two critical problems: (1) opacity — it is unclear why simulated users make specific choices or whether those choices align with intended user profiles; and (2) fairness — LLMs can produce biased or discriminatory responses based on user attributes such as language, education, and culture, threatening equitable treatment of minority and disadvantaged groups.

Method: The tutorial introduces a design-and-audit framework that treats user simulators as verifiable engineering artefacts with seven auditable components: (1) structured Persona, (2) task-aware Contract, (3) matched human-vs-agent Execution, (4) auditable Trace, (5) persona-aligned Verification, (6) structured Feedback, and (7) a Refinement loop for updating personas and contracts. Two hands-on mini-labs cover recommendation-list evaluation and search-query formulation, teaching participants to perform end-to-end inspection, distinguish diagnostic discrepancy analysis from statistical validation, and apply fidelity, credibility, and demographic bias checks.

Result: The abstract describes the tutorial's design rather than empirical results. The tutorial delivers practical skills: participants learn to inspect simulator behavior end-to-end, differentiate diagnostic discrepancy analysis from statistical validation, and systematically audit for fidelity, credibility, and demographic bias in LLM-based user simulations.

Conclusion: A systematic audit framework with seven interconnected components can make LLM-based user simulators more transparent, verifiable, and fair. The tutorial targets IR and recommender systems researchers and practitioners seeking responsible approaches to user behavior simulation and evaluation.

Abstract: Large-language-model (LLM) based user simulation is increasingly adopted for evaluating search engines, recommender systems, and retrieval-augmented generation pipelines, yet most simulators remain opaque: it is difficult to determine why a simulated user made a particular choice or whether that choice is consistent with the intended user profile. Compounding this, recent research shows that LLMs can produce biased or discriminatory responses depending on user background characteristics such as language, education level, and cultural context, raising concerns about the equitable treatment of minority and disadvantaged groups. This half-day, in-person tutorial introduces a proposed design-and-audit framework that treats a user simulator as a verifiable engineering artefact composed of seven auditable components - structured Persona, task-aware Contract, matched human-vs-agent Execution, auditable Trace, persona-aligned Verification, structured Feedback, and a Refinement loop that updates personas and contracts. Through two hands-on mini-labs on recommendation-list evaluation and search-query formulation, participants will inspect simulator behaviour end-to-end, distinguish diagnostic discrepancy analysis from statistical validation, and apply checks for fidelity, credibility, and demographic bias. The tutorial targets information retrieval and recommender systems researchers and practitioners interested in user behaviour simulation and responsible AI.

</details>


### [88] [ChronoID: Infusing Explicit Temporal Signals into Semantic IDs for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14260)
*Dongdong Nian,Dongqi Fu,Chenliang Xu,Yinglong Xia,Hong Li,Hong Yan,Jian Kang*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper proposes ChronoID, a framework that explicitly incorporates temporal information into semantic ID learning for generative recommendation, addressing the limitation that existing semantic IDs are time-agnostic despite temporal dynamics being central to real-world recommendation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing semantic ID learning for generative recommendation is entirely time-agnostic: it conflates interactions from different temporal contexts into identical representations, implicitly assuming stationary item semantics and user intent. This contradicts real-world scenarios where temporal rhythms and evolving user interests are critical.

Method: The authors systematically characterize the design space along three orthogonal dimensions of temporal signals and propose ChronoID, a unified framework for time-aware semantic ID learning. They also construct a new time-explicit generative recommendation benchmark to evaluate where and how time should be infused, how to design the architecture, and where performance gains originate.

Result: The paper provides empirical answers via the benchmark: it identifies effective strategies for infusing temporal signals into semantic IDs, proposes architectural designs for time-aware learning, and analyzes the sources of performance improvement.

Conclusion: Explicitly incorporating temporal information into semantic IDs yields meaningful gains for generative recommendation. The ChronoID framework and benchmark serve as a foundation for future research on time-aware semantic representations in recommendation systems.

Abstract: Semantic IDs are crucial in generative recommendation, but with a fundamental limitation: temporal information is not well incorporated into semantic IDs. Instead, time influences recommendation only implicitly (e.g., through session construction heuristics, preference alignment, or sequence order), while existing semantic ID learning remains entirely time-agnostic. This design conflates interactions occurring under distinct temporal contexts into identical semantic representations, implicitly assuming that item semantics and user intent are temporally stationary. Such an assumption is misaligned with real-world recommendation scenarios, where evolving interaction rhythms play a central role. In this work, we investigate where and how the explicit time should be incorporated into semantic ID for generative recommendation. First, we systematically characterize the design space along three orthogonal dimensions of temporal signals and present a unified framework, ChronoID, for time-aware semantic ID learning. Then, by contributing a new time-explicit generation recommendation benchmark, ChronoID answers the questions: what is the effective way of infusing time, how to design the architecture, and where does the gain come from.

</details>


### [89] [ScoreGate: Adaptive Chunk Selection for Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Dual-Score Statistical Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14269)
*Karamvir Singh,Arvind Jain*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: ScoreGate is a lightweight inference-time mechanism that uses existing bi-encoder and cross-encoder scores to adaptively control how many chunks are passed to the generator, reducing over-retrieval and under-retrieval without additional model calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed top-K retrieval ignores query complexity: simple queries waste computation on irrelevant chunks (over-retrieval), while complex compositional queries miss needed chunks (under-retrieval). Existing single-score thresholding fails when bi-encoders rank semantically relevant chunks poorly due to vocabulary mismatch — a problem cross-encoders can correct.

Method: ScoreGate uses a decision rule operating on two existing pipeline scores: bi-encoder similarity (s_i) and cross-encoder reranker score (r_i). It gates chunk inclusion based on the cross-encoder's ability to "rescue" chunks that the bi-encoder ranked poorly. No additional inference calls are needed, adding only 31ms latency.

Result: On MS MARCO (200 dev queries), ScoreGate achieves MRR@10=0.401 with 35% fewer retained chunks vs. Standard Top-K. On an internal benchmark (n=300, Fleiss' κ=0.87), it achieves zero false positives (95% CI [96.4%, 100%]), 97.77–99.34% recall, 34.8% fewer tokens/query, and only 31ms added latency.

Conclusion: Adaptive retrieval cardinality via ScoreGate improves retrieval efficiency without degrading quality, validated on both public (MS MARCO) and production benchmarks.

Abstract: Fixed-cardinality retrieval injects a constant top-K chunks into the generator regardless of query complexity, causing over-retrieval for narrow queries and under-retrieval for compositional ones. We describe ScoreGate, a lightweight score-space decision mechanism that controls retrieval cardinality at inference time using two scores already produced by the standard pipeline: bi-encoder similarity s_i and cross-encoder reranker score r_i, with no additional model inference calls required. Its core insight is that cross-encoder affirmation can rescue semantically relevant chunks that bi-encoder retrieval ranks poorly due to vocabulary mismatch -- a failure mode unaddressed by fixed-K or single-score thresholding. On MS MARCO (200 dev queries), ScoreGate achieves MRR@10 = 0.401 with 35% fewer retained chunks than Standard Top-K. On an internal benchmark (n=300, Fleiss' kappa=0.87), ScoreGate observed zero false positives (95% CI [96.4%, 100%]) at 97.77-99.34% recall, with 34.8% fewer tokens per query and only 31ms added latency. Results on both MS MARCO and real-world production traffic suggest that adaptive retrieval cardinality can improve retrieval efficiency without degrading retrieval quality.

</details>


### [90] [Private Information Retrieval for Large-Scale DNA-Based Data Storage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14557)
*Gökberk Erdoğan,Daniella Bar-Lev,Rawad Bitar,Antonia Wachter-Zeh,Zohar Yakhini*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: The paper explores adapting two-server Private Information Retrieval (PIR) protocols to synthetic DNA-based data storage, addressing the unique biochemical constraints of querying DNA databases and analyzing the resulting privacy-efficiency-feasibility trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PIR is well-established for digital databases, but DNA-based storage introduces fundamentally different query mechanisms due to biochemical constraints (e.g., hybridization, amplification, sequencing). Bridging PIR to this domain is non-trivial and requires rethinking how privacy guarantees can be maintained under these physical constraints.

Method: The authors propose two approaches for adapting two-server PIR protocols to synthetic DNA-based storage systems. These approaches navigate the tension between information-theoretic privacy guarantees, query efficiency, and biochemical feasibility, explicitly characterizing the privacy trade-offs that arise from the physical nature of DNA queries.

Result: The two proposed approaches demonstrate different points on the privacy-efficiency-feasibility spectrum, revealing how information-theoretic privacy trade-offs concretely manifest when PIR is instantiated in a DNA storage context rather than a purely digital one.

Conclusion: Adapting PIR to DNA-based storage is feasible but requires careful balancing of privacy, efficiency, and biochemical practicality. The work lays a foundation for privacy-preserving query mechanisms in emerging DNA data storage systems.

Abstract: We investigate Private Information Retrieval (PIR) in the context of synthetic DNA-based data storage. While PIR is a well-studied primitive for digital databases, extending it to DNA-based databases presents unique challenges arising from biochemical query mechanisms and their complexity. We propose two approaches for adapting two-server PIR protocols to DNA-based storage, balancing privacy, efficiency, and feasibility. These approaches illustrate how information-theoretic privacy trade-offs manifest in DNA-based storage systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [91] [Can Editing 1 Neuron Fix Repetition Loops in LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13705)
*Aristotelis Lazaridis,Aman Sharma,Dylan Bates,Brian King,Vincent Lu,Jack FitzGerald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gemma 4 models exhibit high-rate repetitive "loop" failures on long factual enumeration tasks. The authors localize this behavior to a small set of MLP neurons or routed experts, suppress them via static weight edits ("surgery"), and eliminate the loops while preserving benchmark performance. However, under longer thinking budgets, a residual "doom looping" (non-convergent self-correction) persists, which they argue is a knowledge-precision problem unsolvable by weight edits alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gemma 4 instruction-tuned models reliably collapse into repetitive loops (verbatim or decaying lists) on long factual enumeration prompts at rates up to 95%, resistant to prompt rewording, inference-engine changes, and sampling adjustments. The authors aim to determine whether this pathology is sufficiently localized to be removed via targeted weight edits, and to delineate the limits of such an approach.

Method: The authors employ per-layer ablation and per-neuron attribution to localize the looping behavior to specific MLP neurons (or routed experts in the 26B-A4B MoE model). Candidate neurons are validated through full-generation sweeps. Static weight edits — as minimal as a single sign-inverted neuron in the E2B model — are then applied to suppress the identified circuitry. The approach is tested across model scales (E2B, 9B, 26B-A4B) under normal generation budgets, and further analyzed under longer thinking budgets where doom looping emerges.

Result: The looping behavior reliably localizes to a small set of MLP neurons/experts. Weight-edit "surgeries" successfully eliminate loop patterns across all model sizes at normal generation budgets while preserving general-purpose benchmark scores. Edit size grows with model scale. However, under longer thinking budgets, the two larger models exhibit residual doom looping — non-convergent self-correction over unrecallable facts. The same edits reduce but do not eliminate this failure, which the authors attribute to a fundamental knowledge-precision gap rather than a removable circuit.

Conclusion: Targeted weight surgery can effectively excise a concrete, localized generation pathology (factual enumeration loops) with minimal side effects, demonstrating feasibility. However, when the underlying issue is missing knowledge rather than a dysfunctional circuit — as in doom looping under extended thinking budgets — weight edits cannot compensate. The work thus both validates the weight-edit paradigm for certain failures and clearly delineates its boundary: it can delete a loop but cannot supply a missing fact.

Abstract: Yes. Can it cure doom loops? Probably not.
  The Gemma 4 instruction-tuned models share a reproducible failure: on long factual enumeration prompts, such as listing every episode of a TV series, the 88 IAU constellations, or the 151 original Pokemon, they collapse into repetition, either a tight verbatim loop or a list whose entries decay onto a single answer. These loops occur at rates as high as 95% and survive prompt rewording, inference-engine changes, and most sampling adjustments. In this paper we explore whether this behavior is localized enough to remove by weight edits. To localize the cause, we use per-layer ablation and per-neuron attribution, then confirm the strongest candidates with full-generation sweeps. The loops trace to a small set of MLP neurons (or, in the 26B-A4B Mixture-of-Experts model, a few routed experts) which we suppress with static weight edits. These "surgeries" can be as small as a single sign-inverted neuron (in the E2B model). The size of the effective edits grows with model scale, but in all cases, the loop patterns can be addressed at normal generation budgets while preserving general-purpose benchmark scores. However, the edits do not solve everything: we also study longer thinking budgets, where the two larger models most visibly enter doom looping, i.e. a non-convergent regime in which the model self-corrects in circles over a fact it cannot recall, exhausting the budget without committing to a final answer. We show this residual failure is reduced but not eliminated by the same edits, and argue it is fundamentally a knowledge-precision problem rather than a removable circuit; weight surgery can delete a loop, but it cannot supply a missing fact. Our results are both a feasibility demonstration, that is, evidence that a concrete generation pathology can be localized to a few parameters and edited out, and a delineation of where that approach stops.

</details>


### [92] [Efficient On-Device Diffusion LLM Inference with Mobile NPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13740)
*Tuowei Wang,Yanfan Sun,Ju Ren*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: llada.cpp is the first NPU-aware inference framework for diffusion LLMs on smartphones, using Multi-Block Speculative Decoding, Dual-Path Progressive Revision, and Swap-Optimized Memory Runtime to achieve 17x–42x latency reduction over CPU baselines while preserving generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion LLMs enable fast parallel token generation but suffer from high computational cost on smartphones. Mobile NPUs offer dense matrix throughput but are difficult to exploit due to three challenges: (1) token commitment shrinks per-block effective workloads, (2) token revision complicates KV cache reuse, and (3) limited NPU-visible address space causes expensive remapping and data transfer overheads.

Method: The paper proposes three techniques: (1) Multi-Block Speculative Decoding, which fills shrinking late-stage workloads with speculative future-block tokens; (2) Dual-Path Progressive Revision, which keeps committed tokens revisable via a CPU-side path without stalling NPU execution; and (3) Swap-Optimized Memory Runtime, which compacts NPU-visible address layouts and overlaps data staging with NPU computation.

Result: llada.cpp reduces LLaDA-8B generation latency by 17x–42x over the CPU baseline with prefix KV cache reuse across diverse hardware platforms and dLLM workloads, while preserving generation quality.

Conclusion: llada.cpp demonstrates that careful alignment of dLLM inference with mobile NPU execution characteristics can unlock substantial latency improvements, making diffusion LLMs practical for latency-sensitive on-device deployment.

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) accelerate generation by denoising multiple tokens in parallel, making them attractive for latency-sensitive mobile inference. However, repeated denoising introduces substantial computation on smartphones. Mobile neural processing units (NPUs) offer high-throughput dense matrix computation, but efficiently exploiting them remains challenging: token commitment shrinks per-block effective workloads, token revision complicates KV cache reuse, and limited NPU-visible address space incurs costly remapping and data transfer overheads.
  In this paper, we propose llada.cpp, the first NPU-aware inference framework for accelerating dLLMs on smartphones. llada.cpp aligns block-wise dLLM inference with the execution characteristics of mobile NPUs through three techniques. (1) Multi-Block Speculative Decoding fills the shrinking workload in late-stage current-block decoding with speculative future-block tokens. (2) Dual-Path Progressive Revision keeps committed tokens revisable until stable and refreshes unstable tokens through a CPU-side path without stalling dense NPU execution. (3) Swap-Optimized Memory Runtime compacts NPU-visible address layouts and overlaps data staging with NPU computation to reduce remapping and transfer overheads. We implement llada.cpp as an end-to-end framework and evaluate it across diverse hardware platforms and dLLM workloads. llada.cpp reduces LLaDA-8B generation latency by 17x-42x over the CPU baseline with prefix KV cache reuse, while preserving generation quality.

</details>


### [93] [High-Frequency Pricing at Scale for E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13741)
*Stefan Birr,Tobias Huelden,Mones Raslan,Adele Gouttes,Andreas Schmitt,Mateusz Koren,Johannes Stephan,Robert Streek,Manuel Kunz,Tim Januschowski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zalando developed a forecast-then-optimize algorithmic pricing tool for fashion e-commerce sales campaigns, combining gradient-boosted tree demand forecasting with multi-objective optimization over 5M+ articles.
