Validated via 23 A/B tests across 12 markets, it achieves ~6% higher profit while maintaining sales/revenue, reduces decision time from hours to minutes, and is now deployed in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pricing systems at Zalando operated at weekly granularity with a manual-algorithmic hybrid approach, which was too slow (hours of decision time) and insufficiently responsive for the volatile demand patterns and rapid-fire pricing decisions characteristic of sales campaigns. There was a need to balance short-term revenue with long-term profitability at scale across millions of fashion articles.

Method: A forecast-then-optimize architecture: (1) Daily-resolution demand forecasting using gradient-boosted trees to predict demand at the article level. (2) A multi-objective optimization framework that jointly maximizes long-term profit and net merchandise value (NMV) across more than 5 million articles, generating pricing decisions in minutes rather than hours.

Result: Validated through 23 A/B tests across 12 markets during 2023-2024 sales campaigns. The new pricing system achieved approximately 6% higher profit while maintaining equivalent performance on sales volume and revenue compared to the previous manual-algorithmic hybrid approach.

Conclusion: The forecast-then-optimize approach is effective for sales campaign pricing at scale. Based on the successful experimental results, the algorithm was deployed to production and now handles the majority of algorithmic pricing decisions for sales campaigns at Zalando.

Abstract: This paper presents the design, development, and implementation of a specialized forecast-then-optimize algorithmic pricing tool for sales campaigns in fashion e-commerce. Sales events present unique challenges for pricing including volatile demand patterns, rapid pricing decisions, and the need to balance short-term revenue with long-term profitability. We describe our approach combining daily-resolution demand forecasting using gradient-boosted trees with a multi-objective optimization framework that maximizes both long-term profit and net merchandise value for more than 5 million articles. Our solution addresses key limitations of existing weekly-granularity systems by implementing a forecast-then-optimize architecture that reduces pricing decision time from hours to minutes. We validate our approach through 23 A/B tests across 12 markets during 2023-2024 sales campaigns at Zalando, one of Europe's leading online fashion retailers. Experimental results demonstrate that the new pricing system achieves approximately 6% higher profit while maintaining equivalent performance on sales and revenue compared to the previous manual-algorithmic hybrid approach. Based on these results, the algorithm was successfully deployed to production and now handles the majority of algorithmic pricing decisions for sales campaigns at the company.

</details>


### [94] [A fully GPU-based workflow for building physics emulators of hypersonic flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13742)
*Fabian Paischer,Dylan Rubini,Deniz A. Bezgin,Aaron B. Buhendwa,David Hauser,Florian Sestak,Johannes Brandstetter,Sebastian Kaltenbach,Nikolaus A. Adams*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fully GPU-based workflow integrating differentiable high-fidelity CFD (JAX-Fluids) with neural emulators, augmented by uncertainty quantification and residual-based physics refinement, to accurately predict hypersonic flow topologies — particularly shock waves — at low computational cost while remaining reliable beyond the training distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supersonic and hypersonic flows pose a major challenge for traditional reduced-order models and neural emulators, which struggle to capture steep gradients (shock waves) with physical consistency in industrially relevant settings. There is a pressing need for surrogates that are both computationally cheap and reliably accurate beyond their training distribution for real-world engineering design loops.

Method: The authors develop a fully GPU-based workflow combining: (1) JAX-Fluids, a differentiable high-fidelity solver, used for rapid dataset generation; (2) a suite of neural emulator architectures with analyzed scaling behavior; (3) residual-based refinement that leverages PDE residuals to improve physical consistency, enabling training even when only mesh and input parameters are available (no ground-truth solution); and (4) uncertainty quantification to assess prediction reliability.

Result: Residual-based refinement substantially reduces PDE residuals and improves physical consistency of the neural emulators. The combined differentiable simulation and refinement framework yields physics emulators that remain reliable outside their training distribution — a critical requirement for engineering deployment.

Conclusion: Differentiable high-fidelity solvers paired with residual-based physics refinement represent a promising path toward neural emulators that are both accurate and generalizable, overcoming the long-standing failure of surrogate models in capturing complex shock-dominated flows for industrial applications.

Abstract: The ability to resolve complex physical phenomena with high fidelity and at low computational cost is central to addressing key challenges in modern engineering. A prime example lies in hypersonic flows, where the precise prediction of the full flowfield topology, in particular with respect to shock wave location and intensity, is critical. Yet supersonic and hypersonic flows continue to be a stumbling block for traditional reduced-order models and neural emulators that struggle to capture steep gradients in flow states with physical consistency in applications of industrial relevance. To that end, we introduce a fully GPU based workflow that integrates accelerated data generation with the training of neural emulators augmented by uncertainty quantification and physics-aware refinement. Our workflow is enabled by a differentiable high-fidelity solver (JAX-Fluids) which we employ for rapid dataset creation and residual-based improvement of the neural emulator to enhance physical consistency. Building on this framework, we first present a suite of model architectures and analyze their scaling behavior to expose their strengths and shortcomings. We then show that residual-based refinement enables training on cases where only mesh and input parameters are available, substantially reducing residuals and improving physical consistency. Together, differentiable simulation and residual-based refinement yield physics emulators that remain reliable beyond their training distribution, a key requirement for deploying surrogates in real-world engineering design loops.

</details>


### [95] [FedSPC: Shared Parameter Correction for Personalized Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13748)
*Kannanthodath Induchoodan Ajay Menon,Christian Prehofer,Yunfei Xu,Toru Hirano*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedSPC is a modular correction method for personalized federated learning that applies control-variate correction solely to shared parameters to resolve inconsistent update issues, improving performance across multiple PFL settings and backbone architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In PFL methods that split models into shared and personalized parameters, clients optimize different local objectives. This causes inconsistent shared parameter updates, weakening the shared representation. A targeted correction mechanism is needed that addresses this without disrupting personalized training.

Method: FedSPC applies control-variate correction exclusively to the shared parameters of existing PFL methods, leaving personalized parameters unchanged. It is a plug-and-play module compatible with three common PFL settings: shared feature extractors, shared classifiers, and fully shared models with local regularization.

Result: Experiments on CIFAR-100 and Tiny-ImageNet using ViT, ResNet-34, and VGG-11 demonstrate that FedSPC consistently improves performance across representative PFL methods including FedPer, FedRep, FedBABU, LG-FedAvg, and Ditto.

Conclusion: FedSPC effectively mitigates the shared parameter inconsistency problem in PFL through a lightweight, modular control-variate correction, offering a general-purpose enhancement that can be integrated into diverse PFL frameworks and architectures.

Abstract: Personalized federated learning (PFL) is one of the important approaches in federated learning for addressing statistical heterogeneity while enabling client-specific adaptation. Many PFL methods split the model into shared and personalized parameters, which are jointly trained on each client. However, this creates an optimization issue: shared parameters are updated by clients optimizing different local objectives, which can lead to inconsistent shared updates and weaken the shared representation. To address this problem, we propose Federated Shared Parameter Correction (FedSPC), a modular correction method for PFL. FedSPC applies control-variate correction only to the shared parameters of a given PFL method, while leaving personalized parameters unchanged. It can be integrated into three common PFL settings: shared feature extractors, shared classifiers, and fully shared models with local regularization. Experiments on CIFAR-100 and Tiny-ImageNet with ViT, ResNet-34, and VGG-11 show that FedSPC improves performance across representative PFL methods, including FedPer, FedRep, FedBABU, LG-FedAvg, and Ditto.

</details>


### [96] [The Weight Norm Sets the Grokking Timescale: A Causal Delay Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13753)
*Truong Xuan Khanh,Doan Hoang Viet,Luu Duc Trung,Phan Thanh Duc*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether weight norm causes the delayed generalization (grokking) in neural networks. By intervening on the norm during training, the authors find that grokking occurs when weight norm reaches a critical value Wc. When the norm is clamped at a multiple of Wc, the grokking delay follows an exponential law, T_grok ∝ exp(α·ρ).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The delayed onset of generalization (grokking) is a well-known phenomenon, but whether weight norm is causal remains disputed — some studies find a critical norm at the transition, while others report grokking without any fixed norm. This paper aims to resolve the dispute by directly intervening on the norm rather than merely observing it.

Method: The authors interven on weight norm during training via clamping. Under free training with weight decay, they measure the critical weight norm Wc at the grokking transition. They then clamp the weight norm to a fixed multiple ρ of Wc and measure grokking delay T_grok across four different modular arithmetic bases. They also analyze the effect of LayerNorm, which decouples weight scale from network function.

Result: Under free training with weight decay, networks grok when weight norm reaches Wc, which varies little across seeds and learning rates (CV 1–2%) and grows with the modular base as a power law. When norm is clamped at ρ·Wc, grokking still occurs but delay follows T_grok ∝ exp(α·ρ) with α ≈ 7.5, fitting across four moduli (R² = 0.996). The held norm shifts delay by ~19× while learning rate shifts it by only ~2×. Clamping above Wc slows grokking rather than preventing it. Adding LayerNorm removes the dependence; without it, the exponential law returns.

Conclusion: Weight norm is causal for grokking delay: the pinned-norm delay follows an exponential law, which is the counterpart to the logarithmic delay predicted for a freely contracting norm. The results unify conflicting prior observations by showing that norm determines delay when coupled to network function, and LayerNorm breaks this coupling.

Abstract: Grokking is the delayed onset of generalization in neural networks, arising long after they fit the training data. Whether the weight norm causes this delay is disputed: some studies report a critical norm at the transition, others observe grokking with no fixed norm at all. We settle this by intervening on the norm during training rather than only observing it. Under free training with weight decay, networks grok when the weight norm reaches a value Wc that varies little across seeds and learning rates (CV 1 to 2 percent) and grows with the modular base as a power law. When we instead clamp the norm to a fixed multiple rho of Wc and hold it there, the network still groks, but the delay follows T_grok proportional to exp(alpha rho). One exponent, alpha near 7.5, fits this delay across four moduli (R^2 = 0.996). Over the swept ranges the held norm moves the delay by about 19x and the learning rate by only about 2x, and holding the norm above Wc slows grokking rather than preventing it. A final LayerNorm removes the dependence by decoupling weight scale from the network function; without it the exponential law returns. This pinned-norm delay is the exponential counterpart to the logarithmic delay predicted for a freely contracting norm.

</details>


### [97] [D2H-AD: A Hybrid Model Utilizing Hyperdimensional Computing for Advanced Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13754)
*Ghazal Ghajari,Elaheh Ghajari,Ashutosh Ghimire,Saeid Ataei,Faris Alsulami,Fathi Amsaad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: D2H-AD is a hyperdimensional computing framework for anomaly detection that integrates distance-based similarity and density-aware encoding. It outperforms five established baselines (HDAD, ODHD, One-Class SVM, Isolation Forest, Autoencoders) across all tested datasets, offering superior F1 and ROC-AUC scores with low computational cost, small memory footprint, and robustness to class imbalance and noise — making it well-suited for TinyML and edge AI deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional ML/DL anomaly detection methods require large labeled datasets, incur high computational costs, and struggle with scalability in edge and high-dimensional settings. Existing HDC-based anomaly detection approaches lack a unified framework combining distance-based similarity with density-aware encoding, leaving room for improved anomaly representation and detection performance in resource-constrained environments.

Method: D2H-AD proposes a novel HDC-based anomaly detection framework that combines distance-based similarity metrics with density-aware encoding in a unified architecture. It encodes data into high-dimensional distributed binary vectors using brain-inspired hyperdimensional computing, then applies density-distance scoring within this hyperdimensional space. The framework leverages binary computations for compact design, low latency, and interpretability.

Result: Ablation studies show hyperdimensional encoding alone yields up to 5.4% higher ROC-AUC than applying the same density-distance scoring in the original feature space. D2H-AD consistently outperforms five baselines (HDAD, ODHD, One-Class SVM, Isolation Forest, Autoencoders) across all evaluated datasets. The framework demonstrates superior F1-score and ROC-AUC, robustness to class imbalance, noise, and data complexity, along with scalability and low-latency operation.

Conclusion: D2H-AD highlights the strong potential of hyperdimensional computing for accurate, interpretable, and energy-efficient anomaly detection in dynamic environments. Its lightweight, scalable, and computationally efficient design makes it particularly attractive for TinyML and edge AI deployments, where resource constraints and real-time requirements are critical.

Abstract: Anomaly detection is a fundamental component of intelligent systems with applications in healthcare, cybersecurity, smart grids, and IoT environments. Although conventional machine learning and deep learning methods have demonstrated effectiveness in identifying anomalies, they often rely on large labeled datasets, incur high computational costs, and face scalability challenges in edge and high-dimensional settings. This paper presents D2H-AD, a novel anomaly detection framework based on Hyperdimensional Computing (HDC), a brain-inspired paradigm that represents information using high-dimensional distributed vectors. Unlike existing HDC-based methods, D2H-AD integrates distance-based similarity and density-aware encoding within a unified framework, improving anomaly representation and detection performance. Ablation studies show that hyperdimensional encoding alone yields up to 5.4% higher ROC-AUC than applying the same density-distance scoring directly in the original feature space. Furthermore, D2H-AD consistently outperforms five established baselines, namely HDAD, ODHD, One-Class SVM, Isolation Forest, and Autoencoders, across all evaluated datasets. The framework is lightweight, interpretable, and computationally efficient, making it suitable for resource-constrained and real-time applications. We validate D2H-AD on five benchmark datasets and demonstrate superior F1-score and ROC-AUC performance, together with robustness to class imbalance, noise, and data complexity. In addition to improved accuracy, D2H-AD offers scalability, a small memory footprint, and low-latency operation enabled by binary computations and a compact design. These properties make it particularly attractive for TinyML and edge AI deployments. The proposed framework highlights the potential of HDC for accurate, interpretable, and energy-efficient anomaly detection in dynamic environments.

</details>


### [98] [Beyond LoRA: Is Sparsity-Induced Adaptation Better?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13767)
*Elijah Cadenhead,Cristian McGee,Xin Li,El Houcine Bergou,Aritra Dutta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Cheap LoRA (cLA) and c³LA, sparse and structured extensions of LoRA that restrict adaptation to a column-subspace, providing information-theoretic generalization bounds. Across 11 methods, 10 models, and 14 datasets, these sparse variants remain competitive with parameter-matched baselines while reducing training time (~10%) and GPU memory (~15%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LoRA and its variants offer parameter-efficient fine-tuning, their comparative generalization properties and the impact of structural restrictions on low-rank updates remain poorly understood. The authors aim to develop cheaper, simpler extensions with principled theoretical generalization guarantees.

Method: The authors introduce Cheap LoRA (cLA), which trains only one low-rank factor while keeping the other fixed (deterministically or stochastically via randomized cLA), and c³LA, a chained circulant variant. They frame cLA as structured asymmetric LoRA — a controlled column-subspace restriction of full fine-tuning — and derive information-theoretic generalization error bounds. Empirical evaluation spans 11 PEFT methods, 10 pre-trained models, and 14 datasets, using loss landscapes and spectral analysis.

Result: Sparse, structured column-space LoRA variants remain competitive with parameter-matched baselines across tasks. Even with a naïve sparse implementation, they reduce training time by up to 10% and peak GPU memory by up to 15%. The proposed theoretical and empirical generalization measures offer more consistent, principled evaluation than commonly used analytical tools.

Conclusion: Restricting LoRA-based PEFT adaptation to a sparse, structured column space is a cost-effective strategy that maintains competitive performance. The information-theoretic generalization bounds provide a principled framework for analyzing these methods, and the proposed measures are more reliable than standard analytical tools for cost-effective adaptation.

Abstract: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) and its variants provide a memory- and compute-efficient alternative to full fine-tuning of pre-trained models. However, questions remain about the comparative generalizability of these approaches and how the structural restrictions on low-rank updates preserve effective adaptation performance. We present a historical framing, covering the past (full fine-tuning and original LoRA), the present (different variants of LoRA), and propose simpler, cheaper, parameter-efficient extensions by inducing sparsity within existing LoRA variants: Cheap LoRA (cLA), training a single low-rank factor with the other fixed (deterministically or, in its randomized variant, stochastically), and the chained circulant variant, ${c}^3$LA. We frame cLA as a structured instance of asymmetric LoRA, serving as a controlled column-subspace restriction of full fine-tuning. We derive information-theoretic generalization error bounds for these variants, marking one of the first endeavors in this area. Empirically, we evaluate 11 fine-tuning methods across 10 pre-trained models and 14 datasets, analyzing the fine-tuned models' performance and generalization using tools such as loss landscapes and spectral analysis. Despite the sensitivity of fine-tuned models to the pre-trained model, datasets, and other factors, our study suggests that restricting LoRA-based PEFT methods' adaptation to a sparse, structured column space remains competitive across tasks with their parameter-matched baselines while reducing up to 10% training time and peak GPU memory up to 15%, even with a naïve, non-optimized, sparse implementation. Our theoretical and empirical generalization measures provide a more consistent and principled approach to their cost-effective adaptation than commonly used analytical tools. Overview and code are available at: https://elicaden.github.io/Beyond_LoRA/.

</details>


### [99] [Contract-Based Compositional Shielding for Safe Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14130)
*Omar Adalat,Edwin Hamel-De le Court,Francesco Belardinelli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses safe coordination in multi-agent reinforcement learning by introducing a framework where agents use a shared global LTL safety specification, decomposed into local obligations whose conjunction implies the global spec. A non-stationary multi-armed bandit selects among obligation tuples at training time to optimize team reward while maintaining deterministic safety guarantees under fully decentralized execution—no central runtime controller needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-agent RL, global safety often cannot be enforced unilaterally—one agent's safe action may depend on others' dynamics. Existing decentralized shields using purely factorized permissions can be overly conservative, excluding team-optimal behaviors that are safe only through coordinated action. The authors aim to recover team-optimal safe behavior without resorting to centralized runtime control.

Method: The approach uses a shared global specification φ in the safety fragment of Linear Temporal Logic (LTL_safe). Agents maintain a library of local LTL_safe obligations. The key mechanism is that a tuple of local obligations is certified simultaneously such that their conjunction implies φ, and each agent can treat others' obligations as assumptions, enabling projection into local action masks. At training time, a non-stationary multi-armed bandit algorithm selects among obligation tuples to maximize team reward, while end-to-end safety is preserved throughout.

Result: The approach was evaluated across 6 environments and 15 algorithmic variants, though specific quantitative results are not detailed in the abstract.

Conclusion: Deterministic safety guarantees can be achieved for decentralized multi-agent RL by leveraging assumption-guarantee reasoning with LTL_safe obligations and a bandit-based selection mechanism, enabling team-optimal behavior without centralized runtime control.

Abstract: Safe coordination problems surface in multi-agent reinforcement learning when global safety cannot be enforced by any agent unilaterally: the admissibility of one agent's action may depend on the dynamics of other agents. Decentralised shields can enforce safety at runtime, but purely factorised permissions often exclude optimal team behaviour that is safe only through coordination. We study deterministic safety guarantees for agents trained and deployed under decentralised execution, recovering team-optimal safe behaviour without centralised runtime control. Agents have a shared global specification $φ$ in the safety fragment of Linear Temporal Logic ($\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{safe}}$ ), and select among tuples of local $\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{safe}}$ obligations whose conjunction implies the global specification $φ$. Each agent may rely on the other agents' local obligations as assumptions because the whole contract tuple is certified simultaneously and allows projection into local action masks. At learning time, a non-stationary multi-armed bandit chooses among a library of local $\mathsf{LTL}_{\mathsf{safe}}$ obligations to select the tuple that optimises team reward, all without forgoing end-to-end safety. We evaluate the approach across 6 environments and 15 algorithmic variants.

</details>


### [100] [Diffusion Policy Optimization without Drifting Apart](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13795)
*Haozhe Jiang,Haiwen Feng,Pieter Abbeel,Jiantao Jiao,Angjoo Kanazawa,Nika Haghtalab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Existing diffusion policy-gradient methods suffer from instability due to a "double-drift" phenomenon where the ELBO separates from the true log-likelihood, misaligning proxy and true policy gradients. The authors propose DiPOD, which interleaves self-distillation with policy-gradient updates and augments each update with an on-policy ELBO regularizer, achieving more stable training and higher rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL post-training for diffusion policies is promising but existing policy-gradient methods are unstable and fail to reliably improve policies. The authors diagnose the root cause as the double-drift phenomenon: optimizing a variational surrogate objective causes the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) to drift away from the true log-likelihood, which in turn causes the proxy policy gradient to misalign with the true policy gradient of expected return, undermining reliable policy optimization.

Method: DiPOD (Diffusion Policy Optimization with Distillation) maintains tight-bound behavior between the ELBO and true log-likelihood throughout training. It does so by interleaving self-distillation with policy-improving gradient updates. The practical algorithm is straightforward: each diffusion policy-gradient update is augmented with an on-policy ELBO regularizer, which prevents the ELBO from drifting and keeps the proxy gradient aligned with the true gradient.

Result: DiPOD substantially stabilizes training and achieves higher rewards compared to previous methods, as demonstrated across both diffusion language model post-training and continuous-control diffusion policy tasks.

Conclusion: DiPOD addresses the fundamental double-drift instability in diffusion policy-gradient methods through a simple on-policy ELBO regularization approach, enabling reliable policy improvement and superior performance across diverse diffusion policy domains.

Abstract: RL post-training has become increasingly pivotal for improving diffusion policies, but existing diffusion policy-gradient methods are often unstable and cannot achieve reliable policy improvement. We identify the cause as the double-drift phenomenon: optimizing a variational surrogate can let the ELBO separate from the true log-likelihood, which then makes the resulting proxy policy gradient misaligned with the true policy gradient of expected return. We propose \textbf{DiPOD}, a diffusion policy optimization framework that maintains tight-bound behavior throughout training by interleaving self-distillation with policy-improving gradient updates. This leads to a simple and practical algorithm: augmenting each diffusion policy-gradient update with an on-policy ELBO regularizer. Across diffusion language model post-training and continuous-control diffusion policies, DiPOD substantially stabilizes training and reaches higher rewards than previous methods.

</details>


### [101] [Neural Variability Enhances Artificial Network Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13801)
*Robin Preble,Praveen Venkatesh,Stefan Mihalas,Kameron Decker Harris*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether correlated (structured) noise in neural activations improves artificial neural network robustness to adversarial attacks and naturalistic image modifications, finding that structured noise generally enhances robustness, with adversarial noise structure generalizing across attack types while naturalistic noise structure transfers poorly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biological cortical neurons exhibit high trial-to-trial variability (noise) with correlations, unlike consistent peripheral neurons. While prior work suggests noise and signal correlations may optimize discrimination in animals, and ANN studies show noise benefits, most ANN work neglects the effects of correlated/structured noise. The authors aim to bridge this gap by studying whether correlated noise improves ANN robustness.

Method: The authors analyze the covariance of ANN activations under modified (adversarially perturbed or naturalistically altered) versus clean inputs. They introduce structured noise into network activations based on these covariance patterns and evaluate how this affects robustness against both adversarial attacks and naturalistic image modifications across different modification types.

Result: Structured noise significantly improves network robustness. Robustness to naturalistic image modifications benefits most from its own noise structure, but this structure transfers poorly across different modification types. Conversely, noise structure derived from one type of adversarial attack generalizes well to other attack types.

Conclusion: Correlated/structured noise in ANN activations generally improves robustness, and the findings establish a biologically plausible strategy for building robust ANNs that relies only on local information, paralleling potential benefits of stochasticity in biological cortex.

Abstract: Neural responses in cortex exhibit substantial trial-to-trial variability in response to repeated stimuli, while peripheral sensory neurons respond far more consistently, leading many to wonder whether stochasticity may carry meaning. Existing work has argued that noise and signal correlations may be optimized for discrimination in animals, whereas artificial neural network (ANN) studies have shown similar benefits of noise in machine learning tasks, although most ANN work has neglected the effects of correlations. Here we investigate whether correlated noise improves the robustness of artificial neural networks to adversarial attacks and naturalistic image modifications. Using the covariance of activations under modified versus clean inputs, we find that structured noise may significantly improve network robustness. Robustness to naturalistic image modifications benefits most from structure, but this structure transfers poorly across modification types. In contrast, noise structure from adversarial attacks can generalize to other kinds of attacks. These results suggest that structured noise in ANN activations generally improves robustness, establishing a biologically plausible strategy for creating robust artificial neural networks that only relies on local information.

</details>


### [102] [Neural Slack Variables for Shape Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13803)
*Ruben Wiedemann,Antoine Jacquier,Lukas Gonon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural slack variables reformulate functional inequality constraint enforcement in neural networks as a regression problem using an auxiliary network, achieving zero measured violations on monotonicity/convexity tasks where penalty and primal-dual methods fail, and solving arbitrage-free volatility surface learning in quantitative finance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for enforcing functional inequality constraints (e.g., monotonicity, convexity) in neural networks suffer from two key limitations: (1) classical penalty and primal-dual methods only provide constraint gradients at violated locations, leading to fragile and incomplete satisfaction; (2) architecture-guaranteed feasibility methods are restricted to elementary cases and impose unwanted inductive biases. There is a need for a general, robust method that ensures constraint satisfaction without these drawbacks.

Method: The authors propose neural slack variables, a primal-side approach that couples the primary network with a jointly learned auxiliary network. The auxiliary network acts as a valid, feasible target for the constraint quantities of the primary network. Constraint enforcement is converted into a regression problem: the primary network is trained to match the auxiliary network's outputs, which encode the desired monotonicity/convexity properties, thereby inducing feasibility and regularity without requiring constraint gradients only at violation points.

Result: Neural slack variables achieve zero measured violations on dense-grid test cases for both monotonicity and convexity constraints, whereas penalty methods and primal-dual baselines leave residual violations. The method also successfully enables arbitrage-free learning of volatility surfaces, an open industrial challenge in quantitative finance.

Conclusion: Neural slack variables provide a general, robust, deep learning native framework for enforcing functional inequality constraints, outperforming existing penalty and primal-dual methods in terms of constraint satisfaction while avoiding the limitations of architecture-based guarantees. The approach is practically validated on a challenging real-world quantitative finance problem.

Abstract: Enforcing functional inequality constraints such as monotonicity and convexity in neural networks is a fundamental challenge in many industrial and scientific applications. Classical one-sided penalty methods, along with primal-dual methods gated by complementary slackness, provide constraint gradients only at violated locations, resulting in fragile satisfaction. Architectures that guarantee feasibility by construction, on the other hand, remain largely limited to elementary cases and impose additional inductive biases. We introduce neural slack variables, a deep learning native primal-side approach that converts constraint enforcement into a regression problem by coupling the primary network with a jointly learned auxiliary network. The auxiliary network serves as a valid target for the primary network's constraint quantities, inducing feasibility and regularity. Neural slack variables achieve zero measured violations on dense-grid monotonicity and convexity test cases, where penalty and primal-dual baselines leave residual violations, and enable arbitrage-free learning of volatility surfaces, an open industrial challenge in quantitative finance.

</details>


### [103] [Uncertainty Estimation and Generalization Bounds for Modern Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13818)
*Luis A. Ortega*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This thesis unifies Bayesian inference, function-space modeling, and large-deviation theory to address uncertainty quantification and generalization in deep learning. It proposes three methodological contributions (DVIP, VaLLA, FMGP) for scalable Bayesian deep learning and post-hoc uncertainty calibration, while developing a PAC-Bayesian/large-deviation framework explaining how diversity, smoothness, and stochasticity drive generalization in over-parameterized networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks deliver strong predictive performance but suffer from two critical gaps: poorly understood generalization mechanisms (especially in over-parameterized regimes) and unreliable uncertainty quantification. The thesis aims to bridge these gaps by leveraging Bayesian principles to provide both practical tools and theoretical insights.

Method: The thesis combines three methodological innovations with a unified theoretical framework. Methodologically: (1) Deep Variational Implicit Process (DVIP) — extends implicit processes to deep architectures for scalable Bayesian inference; (2) Variational Linearized Laplace Approximation (VaLLA) and Fixed-Mean Gaussian Process (FMGP) — post-hoc methods that equip pretrained deterministic networks with calibrated uncertainty estimates without retraining. Theoretically: develops a PAC-Bayesian and large-deviation-theoretic framework that formalizes how diversity (ensemble disagreement), smoothness (function regularity), and stochasticity (noise injection) interact to explain generalization in over-parameterized networks.

Result: The thesis contributes (a) DVIP as a scalable Bayesian deep learning method bridging implicit processes and deep architectures; (b) VaLLA and FMGP as practical post-hoc uncertainty calibration tools for pretrained models; and (c) a unified probabilistic account showing that diversity, smoothness, and stochasticity are jointly captured within PAC-Bayesian and large-deviation bounds, offering a principled explanation for generalization in large neural networks.

Conclusion: Bayesian principles provide a fertile lens for both advancing practical deep learning methodology and resolving foundational theoretical puzzles. The thesis demonstrates that function-space Bayesian modeling, post-hoc uncertainty calibration, and PAC-Bayesian generalization theory can be coherently integrated, pointing toward more trustworthy and theoretically grounded deep learning systems.

Abstract: This thesis investigates how Bayesian principles can deepen our understanding of modern deep learning systems. While neural networks achieve remarkable predictive performance, their ability to generalize and to quantify uncertainty remains only partly understood. This thesis approaches this challenge from both methodological and theoretical angles: unifying Bayesian inference, function-space modeling, and large-deviation theory under a common probabilistic perspective.
  On the methodological side, the thesis introduces the Deep Variational Implicit Process (DVIP), a scalable Bayesian framework that extends implicit processes to deep architectures. Complementing this, two post-hoc methods -- the Variational Linearized Laplace Approximation (VaLLA) and the Fixed-Mean Gaussian Process (FMGP) -- are proposed to equip pretrained deterministic networks with calibrated uncertainty estimates.
  The theoretical contributions focus on one of the central open questions in modern machine learning: why do large, over-parameterized neural networks generalize so well? To address this, the thesis develops a unified probabilistic framework that connects three key mechanisms -- diversity, smoothness, and stochasticity -- within the language of PAC-Bayesian and large-deviation theory.

</details>


### [104] [Attention-Based Estimation of the Individual Treatment Benefit Probability under Dose Variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13821)
*Lev V. Utkin,Andrei V. Konstantinov,Stanislav K. Kogan,Natalya M. Verbova,Maksim I. Goriunov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Dose-AIPTB, a framework for estimating Individual Probability of Treatment Benefit (IPTB) with ordinal outcomes under discrete dose assignments, extending beyond binary treatment settings. Uses covariate-similar pairwise comparisons with pseudo-labels aggregated via attention mechanisms or kernel regression, with attention-based aggregation proving superior in experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IPTB estimation methods are limited to binary treatment settings (treated vs. control), yet clinical practice frequently involves dose-varying interventions with multiple discrete dose levels. There is a need for a general framework that can estimate individual-level treatment benefit probabilities across multiple dose assignments for personalized dose selection.

Method: The Dose-AIPTB framework reformulates IPTB estimation as binary classification over the unobserved sign of the individual treatment effect. It constructs pseudo-labels by comparing covariate-similar individuals receiving different dose levels, then aggregates these pairwise comparisons using either attention mechanisms or Nadaraya-Watson kernel regression. This formulation naturally handles multiple discrete dose levels and ordinal outcomes.

Result: Numerical experiments on both real-world and synthetic data — covering covariate shift, varying sample sizes, and heterogeneous outcomes — show that attention-based aggregation consistently outperforms kernel-based alternatives (Nadaraya-Watson) for IPTB estimation across multiple dose levels.

Conclusion: The Dose-AIPTB framework provides a clinically intuitive foundation for personalized dose selection grounded in individual-level benefit probabilities, successfully extending IPTB estimation beyond binary treatments to discrete dose-varying interventions. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Estimating the probability that a treatment outperforms a control for an individual patient, called the Individual Probability of Treatment Benefit (IPTB), offers a clinically intuitive alternative to population-average metrics. However, existing methods for IPTB estimation are largely confined to binary treatment settings, despite the prevalence of dose-varying interventions in clinical practice. We propose a general framework for IPTB estimation with ordinal outcomes under discrete dose assignments, called Dose-AIPTB (Dose Attention-based IPTB). Our approach recasts the problem as binary classification over the unobserved sign of the individual treatment effect, constructing pseudo-labels from covariate-similar pairwise comparisons and aggregating them via attention mechanisms or Nadaraya-Watson kernel regression. This formulation naturally accommodates multiple discrete dose levels, extending beyond the binary treatment paradigm. Through numerical experiments on real-world and synthetic data under covariate shift, varying sample sizes, and heterogeneous outcomes, we demonstrate that attention-based aggregation consistently outperforms kernel alternatives. The framework provides a foundation for personalized dose selection grounded in individual-level benefit probabilities. Codes implementing the model are publicly available at https://github.com/NTAILab/AIPTBDose.

</details>


### [105] [A Stationarity-and-Coupling Criterion for Training-Free Time-Lagged Spectral Embeddings of Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13823)
*Siddharth Pal,Viktoria Rojkova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a training-free multivariate time series descriptor D(τ) based on time-lagged correlation matrices truncated at the Marchenko-Pastur edge, along with a pre-flight test (ADF stationarity check + power-baseline saturation check) that predicts when the descriptor will work before any training occurs. It succeeds on four paradigms meeting the criterion and fails on three that violate it, with the failures being the most informative result.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing time series descriptors rarely provide falsifiable criteria for their own applicability. The authors seek not just a new descriptor but a principled way to know in advance when a training-free approach based on stationary cross-channel temporal coupling will succeed or fail, addressing the "when does it work at all?" question rather than merely demonstrating performance.

Method: The descriptor D(τ) is constructed from a time-lagged correlation matrix, truncated at the Marchenko-Pastur edge to retain only signal-bearing eigenvalues. Classification uses cosine similarity to class centroids with zero learned parameters. The core theoretical contribution is a semi-formal analysis based on a stationary Gaussian VAR(1) model, yielding three consequences: a distinguishability condition, why the static (τ=0) covariance fails, and why power-discriminated paradigms defeat the descriptor. The operational criterion is a two-part pre-flight test combining an augmented Dickey-Fuller stationarity check and a power-baseline saturation check.

Result: On four paradigms satisfying the criterion (Sleep-EDF, BCI-IV-2a, MIT-BIH, ESC-50), D(τ) is competitive with strong baselines at a fraction of their cost, achieving 88.5±4.5% under 20-subject leave-one-subject-out on Sleep-EDF on a single CPU thread. On three violating paradigms (non-stationary ERPs, financial-volatility, wearable-stress regimes that are power-discriminated), the descriptor fails exactly as the pre-flight test predicts.

Conclusion: D(τ) is explicitly not the most accurate representation; its value is a compact, training-free embedding with a known domain of validity. The negative results on paradigms that violate the criterion are the more informative half, demonstrating that the pre-flight test reliably predicts failure. The work contributes a methodology for stating when a descriptor should work rather than merely claiming that it does.

Abstract: We study training-free fixed-length descriptors for multivariate time series and ask not merely whether such a descriptor performs well, but when it can be expected to work at all. Our object of study is $D(τ)$, built from a time-lagged correlation matrix truncated at the Marchenko-Pastur edge so that only signal-bearing eigenvalues survive and classified by cosine similarity to class centroids with zero learned parameters. The central contribution is not the descriptor but a falsifiable applicability criterion for it. Working from a stationary Gaussian VAR(1) model, we argue that $D(τ)$ separates two classes when the signals are approximately stationary and the class information lives in their cross-channel temporal coupling rather than in marginal per-channel power. We derive, semi-formally, three consequences: a distinguishability condition, why the static ($τ=0$) covariance collapses to chance, and why a stationary but power-discriminated paradigm defeats the descriptor. The criterion is operational: a two-part pre-flight test -- an augmented Dickey-Fuller stationarity check and a power-baseline saturation check -- predicts applicability before any training. We validate both halves on a mixed assortment. On four paradigms that satisfy the criterion (Sleep-EDF, BCI-IV-2a, MIT-BIH, ESC-50) the descriptor is competitive with strong baselines at a fraction of their cost, reaching $88.5\pm4.5\%$ under 20-subject leave-one-subject-out on Sleep-EDF on a single CPU thread. On three that violate it -- non-stationary ERPs, and financial-volatility and wearable-stress regimes that are power-discriminated -- it fails exactly as the pre-flight predicts, and these negatives are the more informative half. We are explicit that $D(τ)$ is not the most accurate representation; its value is a compact, training-free embedding whose domain of validity is known in advance.

</details>


### [106] [SuperThoughts: Reasoning Tokens in Superposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13862)
*Zheyang Xiong,Shivam Garg,Max Yu,Vaishnavi Shrivastava,Haoyu Zhao,Anastasios Kyrillidis,Dimitris Papailiopoulos*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SuperThoughts compresses consecutive Chain-of-Thought token pairs into single latent representations and decodes two tokens per step, doubling inference throughput while preserving discrete supervision. A confidence-based fallback mechanism achieves ~20–30% CoT length reduction with minimal accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long Chain-of-Thought reasoning improves LLM problem-solving but is computationally expensive due to sequential, autoregressive token generation. Prior work on continuous latent-space reasoning eliminates discrete token generation but suffers from training instability and fails on complex, long-horizon tasks due to absent supervision signals in latent space.

Method: SuperThoughts compresses pairs of consecutive CoT tokens into single latent representations. A lightweight Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) module decodes two tokens per reasoning step, doubling throughput at inference. Discrete token supervision is preserved at training time. A confidence-based adaptive mechanism detects uncertain steps and falls back to standard autoregressive decoding to safeguard accuracy.

Result: Evaluated on MATH500, AMC, OlympiadBench, and GPQA-Diamond using Qwen2.5-Math models (1.5B, 7B, 14B). SuperThoughts achieves approximately 20–30% CoT length reduction while maintaining accuracy with only 1–2 point degradation on most benchmarks.

Conclusion: SuperThoughts offers a practical middle ground between full discrete CoT reasoning and pure latent-space approaches, enabling faster inference with negligible accuracy trade-offs via supervised latent compression and an adaptive fallback strategy.

Abstract: Long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning improves LLM problem-solving but is computationally expensive due to sequential token generation. While recent works explore reasoning in continuous latent spaces to bypass discrete token generation, they often struggle with training stability and fail to scale to complex, long-horizon tasks due to lack of supervision signal. We propose SuperThoughts, which compresses pairs of consecutive CoT tokens into single latent representations and decodes two tokens per step via a lightweight Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) module. This preserves discrete token supervision at training time while doubling throughput at inference time. We finetune Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-Math-7B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-Math-14B-Instruct, and evaluate on MATH500, AMC, OlympiadBench, and GPQA-Diamond. With a confidence-based adaptive mechanism that falls back to standard decoding when uncertain, SuperThoughts achieves $\sim$20--30\% CoT length reduction while maintaining accuracy with minimal degradation (1-2 points accuracy drop on most tasks).

</details>


### [107] [Muon$^p$: Muon with Fractional Spectral Powers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13867)
*Yihe Dong,Will Sawin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Muon^p generalizes the Muon optimizer by using fractional spectral-power updates US^pV^T instead of full polar factorization UV^T, interpolating between Muon (p=0) and gradient descent (p=1). The authors prove fractional powers require non-polynomial computation, derive efficient bivariate polynomial approximations preserving matrix-multiplication-only structure, and demonstrate gains in billion-scale finetuning while analyzing when spectral preservation matters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon's full spectral flattening (replacing all singular values with 1) discards potentially useful singular-value information. The authors seek a principled middle ground that partially preserves the singular spectrum while retaining Muon's computational efficiency and matrix-multiplication-only design.

Method: The authors propose Muon^p, which applies fractional spectral-power updates US^pV^T for p in (0,1). They prove no fixed univariate polynomial can compute arbitrary fractional spectral powers, then derive low-degree odd bivariate recurrences that approximate US^pV^T using only matrix multiplications. They also establish a theoretical connection: Muon^p maximizes linear loss improvement under the Schatten q-norm for q = 1 + 1/p.

Result: Empirically, Muon^p excels at finetuning billion-scale models, improving validation perplexity and downstream task performance over baselines. The authors also characterize regimes where Muon^p is less suitable via spectral geometry analysis.

Conclusion: Partially preserving the singular spectrum via fractional power updates yields significant practical gains in optimization, particularly for finetuning. Muon^p provides a theoretically grounded, computationally efficient method that interpolates between gradient descent and Muon, offering insights into when and why spectral information matters for training dynamics.

Abstract: Muon is an increasingly widely used optimizer that replaces a gradient $G=USV^\top$ with its polar factor $UV^\top$, thereby flattening the singular spectrum. However, full flattening discards singular-value information that may matter for adaptation. We introduce Muon$^p$, a Muon-style optimizer that instead uses fractional spectral-power updates $US^pV^\top$ for rational $p\in(0,1)$, interpolating between Muon and gradient descent. To make it practical, we prove that fractional spectral powers cannot be computed by any fixed univariate polynomial iteration, and furthermore derive low-degree odd bivariate recurrences that approximate $US^pV^\top$ using only matrix multiplications, preserving Muon's matrix-multiplication-only structure and compute complexity. We show that Muon$^p$ maximizes the linear improvement in loss under the Schatten $q$-norm for $q=1+\frac{1}{p}$. Empirically, Muon$^p$ is especially effective for finetuning: on billion-scale models, Muon$^p$ improves validation perplexity and downstream task performance. We further analyze when Muon$^p$ is less suitable, through the lens of spectral geometry. Our results reveal important insights on when preserving the singular spectrum can bring significant gains, and introduce a principled way to achieve them.

</details>


### [108] [Natively Unlearnable Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13873)
*Gaurav R. Ghosal,Pratyush Maini,Aditi Raghunathan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NULLs (Natively Unlearnable LLMs) introduce a training architecture with shared backbone neurons and sparsely activated source-specific sinks, enabling isolated unlearning of individual data sources at deployment by simply disabling sinks, without gradient updates or access to retained data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unlearning methods struggle because different data sources' contributions are entangled in model parameters. Isolating contributions makes unlearning easier but obstructs joint learning. The challenge is achieving both goals simultaneously: source-specific isolation for easy removal and shared representation learning across sources.

Method: NULLs train a set of shared backbone neurons alongside a pool of sparsely activated sinks. During training, source-specific information naturally concentrates in each source's assigned sinks, while shared information accumulates in the backbone. A source is unlearned at deployment by simply disabling its corresponding sinks—no gradient updates, no access to retained data. Scaling is demonstrated on Wikipedia (~6M articles, each as an independent source).

Result: Unlearning a single Wikipedia article removes source-specific knowledge while preserving facts shared with semantically related articles, closely matching retraining from scratch. In a Harry Potter case study, NULLs resist both adversarial extraction and relearning attacks. NULLs also match standard transformer performance on downstream benchmarks.

Conclusion: Source-level unlearning can be built natively into LLM training rather than applied post-hoc, retaining the benefits of shared representation learning while enabling clean, robust, and efficient source removal at deployment.

Abstract: Unlearning aims to remove the influence of specific training data sources, but this has proved challenging because the contributions of different sources are entangled within the model. Isolating source contributions to disjoint parameters makes removal easier, though it obstructs joint learning across sources. We propose NULLs (Natively Unlearnable LLMs), a model class that satisfies the two opposing goals of isolating source-specific contributions and learning jointly across sources, by training a set of shared backbone neurons alongside a pool of sparsely activated sinks. During training, information specific to a source naturally concentrates in its sinks while information shared across sources accumulates in the backbone. A source is then unlearned at deployment by disabling its corresponding sinks, with no gradient updates and no access to the retained data. We show that NULLs scales to Wikipedia's ~6M articles, isolating each as an independent source. Unlearning a single article removes knowledge specific to it while preserving facts shared with semantically related articles, closely matching retraining from scratch. We note that unlearning with NULLs is also robust: in a case study of unlearning the Harry Potter books, NULLs resists both adversarial extraction and relearning that reverses post-hoc unlearning. Finally, NULLs preserves general language capabilities, matching a standard transformer on downstream benchmarks. Together, these results suggest that source-level unlearning need not be an afterthought. It can be built natively into LLM training while retaining the benefits of shared representation learning.

</details>


### [109] [A Longitudinal Attribute-Conditioned Neural Network for Modeling Health-State Transition Probabilities in Temporally Irregular Data: The LANTERN Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13880)
*Bright Kwaku Manu,Beckett Sterner,Petar Jevtic*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a calibrated machine learning estimator for multi-state transition probabilities in long-term care/disability insurance, designed for irregular longitudinal health data. The model predicts transitions among four states (healthy, mild disability, severe disability, death) using individual health histories, inter-observation gaps, and demographic covariates. Evaluated on HRS data, it outperforms logistic regression, gradient-boosted trees, RNNs, and a last-state benchmark in transition matrix error while improving severe disability discrimination and maintaining strong calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical actuarial multi-state models (Markov, semi-Markov, proportional hazard) are often too restrictive for irregular longitudinal health data exhibiting nonlinear aging patterns and heterogeneous covariate histories. There is a need for estimators that are both well-calibrated at the individual level and aggregate accurately into cohort transition matrices for pricing and reserving.

Method: The authors develop a structured machine learning estimator that conditions transition probabilities on individual health history, time elapsed between observations, and demographic/socioeconomic covariates. It outputs a valid probability distribution over four states (healthy, mild disability, severe disability, death). Individual probabilities are aggregated by age group and origin state into transition matrices. The method is benchmarked against logistic regression, gradient-boosted trees, a recurrent neural network, and a last-state persistence model using Health and Retirement Study data.

Result: The proposed estimator achieves improved severe disability discrimination over logistic regression and gradient-boosted trees, maintains strong calibration, and yields the lowest transition matrix error among all evaluated models in the held-out test set.

Conclusion: A structured machine learning approach can effectively support long-term care transition modeling when evaluated on both discrimination and projection fidelity (calibration), making it suitable for actuarial applications such as disability insurance pricing and solvency assessment.

Abstract: Accurate estimation of long-term care transition probabilities is central to disability insurance pricing, reserving, and solvency assessment. Classical actuarial multi-state models commonly rely on Markov, semi-Markov, or proportional-hazard specifications, which provide a direct connection to cohort projection but may be restrictive for irregular longitudinal health data with nonlinear aging patterns and heterogeneous covariate histories. This paper develops a well-calibrated estimator of multi-state transition probabilities for irregular longitudinal health data. The model learns from individual health history, incorporates the time elapsed between observations, and conditions transition probabilities on demographic and socioeconomic attributes. It produces a valid probability distribution over the next observed health state, with four possible states: healthy, mild disability, severe disability, and death. Individual probabilities are aggregated by age group and origin state to form transition matrices compatible with actuarial cohort projection. Using longitudinal data from the Health and Retirement Study, we compare the proposed estimator with logistic regression, gradient-boosted trees, a recurrent neural network, and a last-state persistence benchmark. The evaluation considers probabilistic accuracy, endpoint discrimination and calibration for severe disability and death, risk concentration, and transition matrix error after aggregation. The proposed estimator improves severe disability discrimination relative to logistic regression and gradient-boosted tree benchmarks, maintains strong calibration, and yields the lowest transition matrix error among the evaluated models in the held-out test analysis. Results show that a structured machine learning estimator can support long-term care transition modeling when judged by calibration and projection fidelity, beyond discrimination.

</details>


### [110] [Gefen: Optimized Stochastic Optimizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13894)
*Nadav Benedek,Tomer Koren,Ohad Fried*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gefen is a memory-efficient optimizer that reduces AdamW's memory footprint by ~8x via learned quantization of first moments and automatic block-wise sharing of second-moment estimates, guided by a theoretical insight about Hessian-aligned parameters, while maintaining AdamW-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AdamW is widely used but incurs high memory overhead — roughly two parameter-sized buffers for first and second moment states. This limits scalability in large-model training. The goal is to drastically reduce this memory footprint without sacrificing performance.

Method: Gefen operates on two fronts: (1) It automatically groups parameters into blocks that share second-moment (variance) estimates, motivated by a theoretical result that large off-diagonal mixed Hessian entries constrain the ratio of squared gradients toward one, making Hessian-aligned parameters natural sharing candidates. Block structure is inferred from initial squared gradients, avoiding Hessian computation. (2) It quantizes first-moment estimates using a learned histogram-based dynamic-programming codebook. The same block partitioning is reused for first-moment scaling. No architecture-specific metadata or extra hyperparameters beyond AdamW defaults are needed.

Result: Gefen achieves the lowest peak optimizer memory among AdamW-like methods while matching AdamW's performance. It reduces memory by ~8x (~6.5 GiB per billion parameters). In FSDP and DDP training, this enables larger microbatches and significantly improves throughput, allowing training of larger models or use of larger batch sizes.

Conclusion: Gefen is a practical drop-in replacement for AdamW that dramatically cuts optimizer memory, increases throughput, and enables scaling to larger models — all without compromising performance or requiring architecture-specific tuning.

Abstract: AdamW is a default optimizer for modern deep learning, but its first and second moment states add roughly two parameter-sized buffers to training memory. We propose Gefen, a memory-efficient optimizer that automatically shares second-moment estimates across parameter blocks and quantizes the first moment using a learned codebook, thereby reducing AdamW's memory footprint by ~8x while maintaining the same performance, corresponding to a reduction of 6.5 GiB per billion parameters. The method is motivated by a theoretical result showing that large mixed Hessian entries constrain the ratio of squared gradients toward one, suggesting that Hessian-aligned parameters are natural candidates for sharing second-moment statistics. Since computing Hessians is impractical at scale, Gefen infers block structure from the initial squared gradients, requiring no architecture-specific metadata or hyperparameters beyond AdamW defaults. Gefen learns an exact histogram-based dynamic-programming quantization codebook and reuses the same blocks for first-moment scaling. Across diverse experiments, Gefen achieves the lowest peak optimizer memory among the compared AdamW-like methods while maintaining AdamW-level performance. In FSDP and DDP training, the reduced memory footprint enables larger microbatches and improves throughput significantly over AdamW, providing a practical drop-in replacement with lower memory usage that can increase throughput and enable training larger models or using larger batch sizes. We provide the complete Python implementation, including fused CUDA kernels at https://github.com/ndvbd/Gefen

</details>


### [111] [SpikF-GO: Spiking Fourier Graph Operators for Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13901)
*Jafar Bakhshaliyev,Niels Landwehr*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SpikF-GO proposes a spiking neural network for multivariate time series forecasting that models inter-variable dependencies via a hypervariate graph with spike-driven Fourier-domain processing, achieving top-ranked SNN performance and outperforming its ANN counterpart at lower energy cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing SNN-based time series forecasting methods process variables independently, lacking mechanisms to capture inter-variable dependencies critical in multivariate settings. Additionally, there is no unified benchmark comparing SNN forecasting architectures.

Method: SpikF-GO introduces: (1) a hypervariate graph formulation converting every scalar observation into a graph node; (2) spike-driven spectral processing via a Hard Concrete frequency gate for learnable sparse frequency selection; (3) a Complex LIF gate applying independent spiking neurons to real and imaginary Fourier components, preserving binary event-driven computation; (4) a variant with Central Pattern Generator-based positional encodings for long-range temporal modeling. Eight benchmarks under a unified protocol are used for evaluation.

Result: SpikF-GO achieves the best average rank among all SNN methods and outperforms its ANN counterpart (FourierGNN) at reduced energy cost. It maintains competitive accuracy even at substantially smaller embedding dimensions, yielding significant energy reductions.

Conclusion: This is among the first works to bring graph-based multivariate modeling into the spiking domain for time series forecasting and the first to provide a unified comparison across SNN forecasting architectures under a common experimental protocol.

Abstract: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) have emerged as an energy-efficient alternative to conventional neural networks, demonstrating strong performance in computer vision and robotics. More recently, SNNs have been applied to time series forecasting (TSF), with methods exploring spiking temporal backbones, spike-compatible positional encodings, Fourier-domain processing, and redesigned neuron dynamics. However, existing SNN forecasting approaches process variables independently, lacking explicit mechanisms for modeling inter-variable dependencies. This is a critical limitation in multivariate settings, where cross-variable correlations carry substantial predictive information. We propose Spiking Fourier Graph Operators (SpikF-GO), which addresses this gap by combining a hypervariate graph formulation in which every scalar observation becomes a graph node with spike-driven spectral processing. SpikF-GO introduces a Hard Concrete frequency gate for learnable sparse frequency selection and a Complex LIF gate that applies independent spiking neurons to real and imaginary Fourier components, preserving binary, event-driven computation throughout the spectral domain. We further present a variant incorporating Central Pattern Generator-based positional encodings for stronger long-range temporal modeling. Evaluated on eight benchmarks under a unified experimental protocol, SpikF-GO achieves the best average rank among all SNN methods and outperforms its ANN counterpart, FourierGNN, at reduced energy cost. SpikF-GO maintains competitive accuracy even at substantially smaller embedding dimensions, thereby achieving significant energy reductions. To our knowledge, this is among the first works to bring graph-based multivariate modeling into the spiking domain for TSF and the first to provide a unified comparison across SNN forecasting architectures under a common experimental protocol.

</details>


### [112] [Smoothing Dark Areas in Molecular Latent Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13955)
*Xi Wang,Jiahan Li,Yuxuan Xia,Yingcheng Wu,Shaoyi Zheng,Shengjie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TopVAE introduces a topology-optimized variational autoencoder that internalizes structural and chemical constraints during training to eliminate "dark areas" in the latent space, enabling robust latent diffusion for 3D molecular generation without test-time corrections.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing molecular VAEs trained with reconstruction objectives produce latent spaces with "dark areas" — regions reachable during diffusion sampling that decode to disconnected or chemically invalid molecules. Unlike image generation, molecular decoding demands strict structural and chemical precision, making even small latent perturbations catastrophic.

Method: TopVAE trains the decoder to internalize structural and chemical constraints during training rather than relying on test-time chemical correction. This topology-optimized VAE explicitly reduces dark areas in the latent space. The resulting latent space is then paired with a standard DiT (Diffusion Transformer) for 3D molecular generation.

Result: When paired with a standard DiT, TopVAE achieves 77% lower FCD-3D on QM9, the highest Validity & Connectivity (V&C), 52% lower FCD-3D on GEOM-Drugs, and 1.29× more stable and connected molecules on zero-shot scaffold inpainting.

Conclusion: TopVAE significantly improves off-posterior robustness in molecular VAE latent spaces, eliminating the need for test-time chemical correction and enabling scalable, high-fidelity 3D molecular generation via latent diffusion.

Abstract: Latent diffusion is a promising framework for scalable 3D molecular generation, but it requires a latent space that remains smooth, valid, and navigable beyond posterior samples. Existing molecular VAEs, however, are typically learned through reconstruction-based objectives, which do not guarantee such a latent space. We show that this leads to dark areas: regions of latent space that are reachable during diffusion sampling but decode to disconnected or chemically invalid molecules. Unlike in image generation, molecular decoding requires strict structural and chemical precision, so even small latent perturbations can produce catastrophic failures. We therefore propose TopVAE, a topology-optimized VAE that reduces dark areas by making the decoder internalize structural and chemical constraints during training, eliminating the need for test-time chemical correction. TopVAE greatly improves off-posterior robustness, and when paired with a standard DiT, achieves $77\%$ lower FCD-3D on QM9, the highest V&C, $52\%$ lower FCD-3D on GEOM-Drugs, and $1.29{\times}$ more stable and connected molecules on zero-shot scaffold inpainting.

</details>


### [113] [Can Machine Learning Forecast Rice Yields in Data-Constrained Settings? Satellite Climate Data, National Crop Statistics, and Lessons from Sierra Leone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13959)
*Ibrahim Denis Fofanah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether rice yields in Sierra Leone can be forecast using existing data, finding that climate-augmented machine learning models (especially XGBoost) significantly outperform persistence baselines, with early-season rainfall as the dominant predictor. However, no model anticipated the 2018 yield collapse, which had institutional rather than climatic origins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sierra Leone's agricultural sector operates with virtually no data-driven decision support, and no published machine learning study has examined crop yields in the country. The authors aim to fill this gap by determining whether actionable yield forecasts can be generated from data Sierra Leone already possesses.

Method: Using 25 years of FAOSTAT production data (2000–2024) for nine major crops, the authors train XGBoost, Gradient Boosting, and Random Forest models under a strict anti-leakage protocol with expanding-window walk-forward validation across seven held-out years. Models are first trained on crop statistics alone, then augmented with free satellite climate data (CHIRPS rainfall, NASA POWER temperature). Performance is benchmarked against a naive persistence forecast. Early-season (May–June) rainfall is identified as the dominant predictor through feature importance analysis.

Result: Models trained solely on crop statistics fail to outperform persistence. However, climate-only XGBoost reduces forecast error by approximately one-third (RMSE 284 vs 428 kg/ha). This improvement holds for a linear model and is robust to excluding the anomalous 2018 season. Early-season rainfall emerges as the dominant predictive feature, indicating that seasonal yield risk is observable months before harvest. No model anticipated the 2018 collapse, attributed to institutional rather than climatic factors.

Conclusion: Freely available satellite climate data can enable meaningful rice yield forecasting in data-sparse environments like Sierra Leone. The findings are translated into policy recommendations for the country's Feed Salone Strategy, with a fully open-source pipeline to facilitate adoption.

Abstract: Sierra Leone's agriculture operates with almost no data-driven decision support, and no published machine learning study has examined the country's crop yields. We ask whether rice yield can be forecast from data Sierra Leone currently has. Using 25 years of FAOSTAT production data (2000-2024) for nine major crops, we train XGBoost, Gradient Boosting, and Random Forest under a strict anti-leakage protocol with expanding-window walk-forward evaluation across seven held-out years, benchmarked against naive persistence. No model trained on crop statistics alone outperforms persistence. Augmenting with free satellite climate data (CHIRPS rainfall, NASA POWER temperature) reverses this result: a climate-only XGBoost reduces forecast error by one third (RMSE 284 vs 428 kg/ha), a gain that holds for a linear model and is robust to excluding the anomalous 2018 season. Early-season (May-June) rainfall is the dominant predictor, implying seasonal yield risk is observable months before harvest. No model anticipated the 2018 collapse, whose origins were institutional rather than climatic. We translate the findings into policy recommendations for Sierra Leone's Feed Salone Strategy, with a fully open-source pipeline.

</details>


### [114] [PostDeg: Placement Beats Parameterization in LayerNorm GNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14022)
*Yash Tomar,Aryav Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies that the post-LayerNorm position within a residual block is the critical slot where per-node topology scalars (like inverse degree) survive to influence downstream scoring. The authors propose PostDeg, a parameter-free inverse-degree scaling inserted after LayerNorm, and demonstrate consistent gains across multiple graph tasks. The key finding is that placement, not learned parameterization, drives the improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LayerNorm-based GNNs erase topological signals (degree, centrality, k-core) that are essential for node selection tasks. While this erasure is known, the literature has not pinpointed exactly where in the residual block it occurs. The authors seek to locate the position where topology information is destroyed and find a surviving slot where such signals can be preserved and leveraged.

Method: The authors analytically show that a positive per-node scalar inserted before LayerNorm is divided out (up to a stabilizer term), whereas the same scalar inserted after LayerNorm survives as representation magnitude and reaches the score head. They instantiate this insight with PostDeg, a parameter-free post-LayerNorm inverse-degree scaling. They pre-register four falsifiers to rigorously test the rule: graphwise scalars, extra LayerNorm, expressive same-slot capacity, and backbone-agnostic source.

Result: PostDeg achieves +3.5%, +2.5%, and +5.6% improvement over the LayerNorm backbone on influence maximization, network dismantling, and maximum independent set, respectively, with 10/10 paired-seed wins per task. None of the four pre-registered falsifiers fired, confirming the robustness of the placement rule.

Conclusion: The central takeaway is that placement, not parameterization, is what carries the performance gain. The finding generalizes: any positive topology scalar inserted post-LayerNorm in any normalized residual stack can preserve and convey useful structural signals to downstream tasks.

Abstract: LayerNorm-based GNNs routinely erase the topology signals (degree, centrality, $k$-core) that node-selection policies should depend on, but the literature has not located where in the residual block the erasure happens. We answer that question: a positive per-node scalar inserted before LayerNorm is divided out up to a stabilizer term, while the same scalar inserted after LayerNorm reaches the score head as representation magnitude. The surviving slot is the post-LayerNorm position. We instantiate it with PostDeg, a parameter-free post-LayerNorm inverse-degree scale, and pre-register four falsifiers (graphwise scalars, extra LayerNorm, expressive same-slot capacity, backbone-agnostic source) that would reject the rule. PostDeg gains $+3.5\%/+2.5\%/+5.6\%$ over the LN backbone on influence maximization, network dismantling, and maximum independent set, with $10/10$ paired-seed wins per task; none of the four falsifiers fires. The takeaway is that placement, not parameterization, carries the gain -- a small invariance check that generalizes to any positive topology scalar in any normalized residual stack.

</details>


### [115] [Utility-Constrained Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14029)
*Mehrdad Moghimi,Bernardo Avila Pires*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a practical methodology for Utility-Constrained MDPs (UCMDPs), extending beyond standard CMDPs by supporting risk-sensitive constraints. The approach does not require fixing constraint limits before training, enabling post-hoc adjustments at no extra cost, and demonstrates strong empirical performance on Safety Gymnasium benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard CMDPs only support risk-neutral constraints, which can lead to policies that mix rare catastrophic failures with overly conservative behavior to meet expected-value constraints.
