Risk-sensitive constraints are needed both for safety and because prior work suggests they improve performance even under risk-neutral evaluation. However, no practical algorithms existed for UCMDPs, the natural framework for risk-sensitive constraints.

Method: The authors propose a simple methodology for solving UCMDPs and constrained RL that incorporates risk-sensitive constraints via utility functions. A key innovation is that constraint limits need not be fixed before training—only a sensible range is required—allowing flexible, post-hoc adjustment of limits without retraining.

Result: The proposed agent consistently matches or outperforms existing baselines across several Safety Gymnasium benchmark tasks, demonstrating both the generality of the framework and strong practical performance.

Conclusion: The UCMDP framework with the proposed methodology provides a practical, flexible, and high-performing approach to risk-sensitive constrained RL, overcoming key limitations of standard CMDPs.

Abstract: Constrained MDPs (CMDPs) are a widely adopted framework for incorporating safety into RL agents; however, the framework does not support risk-sensitive constraints. This can be problematic: For example, CMDPs allow for optimal solutions that, in order to satisfy the risk-neutral constraints, mix infrequent catastrophic behaviors and frequent, overly conservative ones. Moreover, prior empirical results suggest that enforcing stricter, risk-sensitive constraints can improve performance even under risk-neutral evaluation. The natural framework to incorporate risk-sensitive constraints is utility-constrained MDPs (UCMDPs), but no practical solutions for this problem existed. In this work, we introduce a simple yet powerful methodology for UCMDPs and constrained RL. Besides allowing for risk-sensitive constraints, our framework does not require us to fix constraint limits in advance of training the agent, provided that a sensible range is known. This increases policy flexibility and, in practice, allows for adjustments to these limits at no extra training cost. Besides benefiting from the generality of the framework, our agent shows strong performance in practice, consistently matching or outperforming existing baselines in several Safety Gymnasium benchmark tasks.

</details>


### [116] [Decompose Sparsely Where You Should, Absorb Densely Where You Should No](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14040)
*Ruixuan Deng,Zehao Jin,Zekun Wang,Zihan Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAEs struggle to sparsely encode a low-rank, dense "computational scaffold" in residual stream activations. Adding a parallel rank-r bottleneck absorbs this component, reducing dense latents by 84%, improving probing, and revealing the scaffold as structurally identifiable (top PCs), causally necessary (7.5× CE increase when removed), and redundantly encoded by sparse dictionaries. This challenges the universal applicability of sparse decomposition for activations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper questions the implicit assumption in sparse autoencoder training that all residual stream activation content can be sparsely decomposed into monosemantic features. The authors observe persistent dense latents in trained SAEs and hypothesize these arise from a low-rank, dense component that is computationally important but inherently unsuitable for sparse representation.

Method: A small rank-r linear bottleneck (low-rank matrix decomposition) is added in parallel with standard SAE architectures (BatchTopK and Matryoshka) on Gemma-2-2B layer 12. The bottleneck absorbs dense structure before sparse reconstruction. The authors then characterize the absorbed component structurally (via PCA comparison), causally (via ablation experiments measuring next-token cross-entropy), and representationally (by ablating sparse features and measuring MMLU classification accuracy).

Result: A rank-24 bottleneck reduces dense latent count by up to 84% while improving sparse probing and targeted probe perturbation at matched sparsity. The absorbed component is: (i) structurally identifiable as top principal components and outlier dimensions; (ii) causally necessary — removing it raises CE by 7.5× vs. 2.8× for top-24 PCA removal; (iii) redundantly encoded — ablating 787 aligned sparse features raises CE only 2.9×, and ablating 2,048 topic-aligned features leaves MMLU topic classification near ceiling, while removing the scaffold drops it from 98.7% to chance.

Conclusion: Residual stream activations contain a compact, semantically informative, causally important "computational scaffold" that sparse dictionaries represent inefficiently. This suggests the scope of sparsity-based interpretability methods needs careful re-examination, and hybrid dense-sparse approaches may be more appropriate.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are typically trained to reconstruct the \textbf{entire} residual stream through a sparse dictionary, implicitly assuming that all activation content is amenable to sparse, monosemantic decomposition. We question this assumption and hypothesize that activations contain a low-rank, dense component that is computationally important to the model yet inherently unsuitable for sparse representation, which serves as a major source of the persistent dense latents widely observed in trained SAEs. To test this, we add a small rank-$r$ linear bottleneck in parallel with standard SAEs (BatchTopK and Matryoshka), allowing dense structure to be absorbed before sparse reconstruction. On Gemma-2-2B layer 12, a rank-24 bottleneck reduces dense latent count by up to 84\% while improving sparse probing and targeted probe perturbation on both architectures at matched sparsity. The absorbed component is (i) \textbf{structurally identifiable} as the top principal components and outlier dimensions; (ii) \textbf{causally necessary}, with removing it raising next-token cross-entropy by 7.5$\times$, far exceeding the 2.8$\times$ from removing the geometrically near-identical top-24 PCA directions; and (iii) \textbf{redundantly encoded by sparse dictionaries}, with ablating 787 maximally aligned sparse features raising cross-entropy by only 2.9$\times$ and ablating 2,048 topic-aligned features leaving MMLU topic classification virtually unchanged, whereas removing the scaffold drops it from 98.7\% to chance. Together, our findings identify a compact, semantically informative and causally important component of residual stream activations (which we term a \textbf{computational scaffold}) that standard sparse dictionaries represent inefficiently, suggesting that the scope of sparsity-based interpretability methods warrants careful re-examination.

</details>


### [117] [Non-Parametric Machine Text Detection via Multi-View Gaussian Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14060)
*Aleem Khan,Nicholas Andrews*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A multi-view, non-parametric detection framework using Gaussian process ensembles that combines complementary document features (stylistic, likelihood, structural) to robustly detect machine-generated text under adversarial attacks, with calibrated uncertainty and principled abstention on out-of-distribution inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing machine text detectors are brittle under adversarial attacks like paraphrasing and style transfer because they rely on single signal types. Although multi-signal parametric classifiers can be trained, they suffer from overconfidence under distribution shift (novel attacks, unseen LMs). A robust detector must combine multiple complementary signals while providing reliable uncertainty estimates.

Method: A multi-view, non-parametric framework that extracts complementary feature views (stylistic, likelihood/rank-order, structural) from a document. Per-view evidence is aggregated via a Gaussian process ensemble, which fuses independent detection axes. The GP formulation yields calibrated probabilities and supports principled abstention on out-of-distribution samples.

Result: Evaluated on DetectRL, RAID, and the PAN2025 shared task benchmarks covering diverse generators and attacks, the multi-view detector maintains strong performance under considered attacks and outperforms existing approaches against held-out attacks.

Conclusion: Combining multiple complementary feature views through a Gaussian process ensemble raises the evasion cost for adversaries (who must defeat multiple independent detection axes) and provides calibrated, trustworthy predictions suitable for high-stakes deployment.

Abstract: Adversarial conditions such as paraphrasing and targeted style transfer sharply degrade the accuracy of machine text detectors. A document, however, carries multiple complementary signals (e.g., stylistic features, likelihood and rank-order features, and structural features), and an attack that suppresses one may leave others intact. While a parametric classifier can learn to combine these features given sufficient supervision, classifiers are prone to making confidently incorrect predictions when the distribution shifts (e.g., novel attacks or unseen language models). To address this, we propose a multi-view, non-parametric detection framework that extracts complementary feature views from the same document and aggregates per-view evidence through a Gaussian process ensemble. By aggregating evidence across views, an adversary must simultaneously defeat multiple independent axes of detection, substantially raising the cost of evasion. The Gaussian process formulation additionally provides calibrated probabilities and principled abstention on out-of-distribution inputs, supporting reliable deployment in high-stakes settings. We evaluate on three benchmarks spanning diverse generators and attacks: the DetectRL and RAID benchmarks, and the PAN2025 shared task and demonstrate that our multi-view detector maintains strong performance under the considered attacks, outperforming existing approaches against held out attacks.

</details>


### [118] [Rethinking Backdoor Adversarial Unlearning through the Lens of Catastrophic Forgetting in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14078)
*Zhenqian Zhu,Yamin Hu,Yujiang Liu,Luping Wei,Wenbo Hou,Bin Li,Haodong Li,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper frames backdoor unlearning as a continual learning problem, derives necessary conditions for complete backdoor removal via catastrophic forgetting, and proposes BI-BAU — a method using blind inversion and EM-based adversarial training to eliminate backdoor effects across diverse attack types, including untargeted and multi-modal scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current backdoor defenses lack robustness against specific attacks, and safety tuning only provides superficial protection without fully eradicating backdoor effects. There is a need for a principled, theoretically-grounded approach to achieve complete backdoor unlearning.

Method: The authors model backdoor learning/unlearning as a three-stage sequential continual learning process. They derive necessary conditions for complete backdoor unlearning based on catastrophic forgetting. BI-BAU formulates adversarial example generation meeting these conditions as a blind inversion problem, solved by integrating bi-level adversarial training optimization into an EM algorithm framework to optimize a MAP objective. The method is extended to untargeted adversarial settings and multi-modal contrastive learning.

Result: Extensive experiments show BI-BAU exhibits general applicability across a wide spectrum of backdoor attacks and effectively, thoroughly eliminates backdoor effects from compromised models.

Conclusion: BI-BAU provides a theoretically grounded, general-purpose solution for complete backdoor unlearning, applicable to diverse attack types, untargeted scenarios, and multi-modal settings, significantly advancing the robustness of backdoor defenses for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Existing studies reveal that current backdoor defenses exhibit limited robustness and often fail against specific types of attacks. More concerningly, prevailing safety tuning strategies tend to provide only superficial safety protection, as they fall short of completely eliminating the backdoor effects. In this work, we present a novel formulation of backdoor learning and unlearning as a sequential, three-stage process from a continual learning perspective. Within this framework, we formally define complete backdoor unlearning and further derive the necessary conditions for achieving it based on the mechanism of catastrophic forgetting. Guided by these insights, we propose Blind Inversion-Backdoor Adversarial Unlearning (BI-BAU), which formulates the generation of adversarial examples satisfying the unlearning conditions as a blind inversion problem. We solve this by integrating the bi-level optimization process of adversarial training into an Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm framework to optimize the maximum a posteriori (MAP) objective. Furthermore, BI-BAU is extended to untargeted adversarial scenarios with unknown target classes, as well as to multi-modal contrastive learning tasks, enhancing its applicability to real-world deployment scenarios where pre-trained models may be compromised. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method exhibits general applicability across a wide spectrum of backdoor attacks and can effectively and thoroughly eliminate the backdoor effects from a backdoor model.

</details>


### [119] [Deep Spectral Learning of Embedded Latent Transfer Operators for Stochastic Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14079)
*Ryogo Tanaka,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Deep Spectral Encoder (DSE) is a spectral learning method that uses a neural encoder to learn nonlinear feature maps, defines Markovian latent states via transfer operators, and employs functional canonical correlation analysis in a Galerkin-projected space to estimate linear operators in closed form, enabling Bayesian filtering and Koopman mode decomposition in feature space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic nonlinear dynamical systems pose challenges for latent state-space modeling, especially under noise and partial observability. The authors aim to combine the representational power of deep learning with the theoretical guarantees of spectral operator methods to learn stable, interpretable latent dynamics.

Method: A time-invariant neural encoder maps observations to a deep feature space. Functional canonical correlation analysis (fCCA) in a learnable Galerkin-projected feature space yields state coordinates from past/future observation windows. Transfer and observation operators are estimated as ridge-regularized closed-form solutions equivalent to Galerkin projections of covariance operators. Sequential Bayesian filtering and Koopman spectral mode decomposition are then generalized in this feature space.

Result: DSE achieves stable and superior performance compared to sequential Bayesian filtering and dynamic mode decomposition baselines across multiple scenarios, demonstrating robustness to noise and partial observability.

Conclusion: Embedding latent transfer operators in learned deep feature spaces provides an effective framework for modeling stochastic nonlinear dynamics, with DSE offering both strong empirical performance and theoretically grounded operator estimation.

Abstract: We propose a spectral learning method for stochastic nonlinear dynamical systems represented with embedded latent transfer operators in deep feature spaces. We instantiate the method as Deep Spectral Encoder (DSE), an operator-based latent state-space model in which a time-invariant neural encoder implements learnable nonlinear feature maps from observations, and these features define Markovian latent states whose temporal evolution and observation mapping are described by the transfer and observation operators, respectively. Functional canonical correlation analysis in a learnable Galerkin-projected feature space provides state coordinates from past and future observations, and the two linear operators are estimated on the state coordinates as ridge-regularized closed-form solutions that coincide with Galerkin projections of the associated covariance operators. On this representation, we generalize sequential Bayesian filtering and Koopman spectral mode decomposition in feature space. Experiments on several scenarios show stable and superior performance with sequential Bayesian filtering and dynamic mode decomposition baselines even under noise and partial observability.

</details>


### [120] [Lyapunov-Based Sample Complexity Analysis for Weakly-Coupled MDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14095)
*Tianhao Wu,Matthew Zurek,Weina Wang,Qiaomin Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper establishes polynomial sample and computational complexity bounds for learning near-optimal policies in average-reward weakly-coupled MDPs and restless bandits under a generative model, introducing a novel Lyapunov-based analysis framework with a drift transfer technique and a fine-grained LP perturbation analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior approaches for weakly-coupled MDPs and restless bandits suffer from exponential sample complexity when naively reduced to tabular MDPs, since the state-action space grows exponentially with the number of arms N. The authors aim to exploit the weakly-coupled structure to achieve polynomial complexity, addressing the lack of finite-sample PAC guarantees with polynomial dependence on N.

Method: The paper adopts a plug-in approach: estimate an empirical model from generative samples, then apply an efficient planning algorithm (based on LP relaxation of the average-reward criterion). The core technical innovation is a Lyapunov-based analysis framework that replaces classical bias-function arguments. This framework uses an explicitly constructed Lyapunov function together with a drift transfer technique to relate the true and empirical models. A fine-grained perturbation analysis of the underlying LP relaxation is developed as a key sub-component, serving as a general tool for analyzing LP-based policies.

Result: For fully heterogeneous WCMDPs, the authors establish the first finite-sample PAC guarantee with polynomial complexity and an O(1/√N) optimality gap. For homogeneous restless bandits, they prove a smaller optimality gap is achievable under mild structural assumptions.

Conclusion: Weakly-coupled structure can be systematically exploited to achieve polynomial sample and computational efficiency, bypassing the curse of dimensionality. The Lyapunov-based analysis and LP perturbation analysis offer broadly applicable tools for analyzing LP-based policies in weakly-coupled systems beyond the settings studied.

Abstract: We study the sample complexity of learning in average-reward weakly-coupled Markov decision processes (WCMDPs) and Restless Bandits (RBs) under a generative model. Naive reduction to a tabular MDP leads to high complexity bounds as the state-action space is exponentially large in the number of arms $N$. By exploiting the weakly coupled structure, we show that near-optimal policies can be learned with sample and computational complexities that are polynomial in $N$. Specifically, we analyze the plug-in approach, which applies an efficient planning algorithm to an empirical model estimated from data. For fully heterogeneous WCMDPs, we establish the first finite-sample PAC guarantee with polynomial complexity and an $O(1/\sqrt{N})$ optimality gap. For homogeneous RBs, we further prove that a smaller optimality gap is achievable under mild structural assumptions. A primary technical contribution of our work is a novel Lyapunov-based analysis framework. Unlike classical approaches that rely on the difficult-to-control bias function, our framework uses an explicitly constructed Lyapunov function along with a drift transfer technique between the true and empirical models. A key step of independent interest in our framework is a fine-grained perturbation analysis for the underlying linear programming (LP) relaxation, which provides a general tool for analyzing LP-based policies and weakly-coupled systems.

</details>


### [121] [Numbers Already Carry Their Own Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14108)
*Suhyun Bae,Donghun Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AOE introduces training-free, plug-and-play numerical embeddings that encode both real values and p-adic modular signatures, preserving additive and multiplicative structure natively. It achieves state-of-the-art results on algebraic combinatorics tasks, including first-ever perfect accuracy on the Weaving Pattern benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems fundamentally struggle with numerical reasoning — the "number problem." Standard embeddings treat numbers as atomic tokens or rely on learned representations that fail to capture deep arithmetic structure. The authors aim to inject principled mathematical structure directly into embeddings without requiring task-specific retraining.

Method: Adelic Operation-preserved Embeddings (AOE) leverage the adelic framework from number theory, combining Archimedean (real magnitude) information with non-Archimedean (p-adic) information for multiple primes p. The construction is operation-preserving by design: addition and multiplication in embedding space correspond to the same operations on the underlying numbers. Crucially, the method is training-free and plug-and-play, requiring no modification to downstream architectures.

Result: AOE yields consistent performance gains on algebraic combinatorics benchmarks. The standout result is the first-ever perfect accuracy (100%) on the Weaving Pattern task. Improvements are achieved without any task-specific fine-tuning or architectural changes.

Conclusion: Encoding both real and modular number-theoretic structure into embeddings offers a principled solution to the number problem in AI. The plug-and-play nature of AOE makes it broadly applicable, and the results suggest that marrying deep mathematical structure with learned representations is a promising direction for improving numerical reasoning in neural networks.

Abstract: We introduce Adelic operation-preserved embeddings (AOE), a training-free representation that captures both a number's real value and its modular (p-adic) signatures. This construction preserves additive and multiplicative structure by design, turning numerical input into embeddings that "speak in the language of mathematics." Unlike prior approaches that rely on task-specific retraining, AOE is plug-and-play and drops seamlessly into existing architectures. On algebraic combinatorics benchmarks, it delivers consistent gains including the first-ever perfect accuracy on the Weaving Pattern task-while suggesting a principled path forward for overcoming the long-standing "number problem" in AI.

</details>


### [122] [DTVEM-RE: A Hierarchical Random-Effects Extension of the Differential Time-Varying Effect Model for Person-Specific Multi-Lag Estimation in Intensive Longitudinal Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14116)
*Amartya Bhattacharya*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DTVEM-RE extends the Differential Time-Varying Effect Model to allow person-specific lag structures using two complementary approaches: a hierarchical Bayesian VAR in Stan and a continuous-time Ornstein-Uhlenbeck model in ctsem. The method recovers individual differences in optimal time lags, improves predictive performance, and detects multi-lag heterogeneity that single-lag methods miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original DTVEM assumes a homogeneous lag structure across all individuals, which contradicts the person-specific premise of modern clinical research. Jacobson et al. (2019) explicitly identified this limitation as future work. There is a need for a method that detects optimal time lags while accommodating between-person heterogeneity in intensive longitudinal data.

Method: The authors propose DTVEM-RE with two confirmatory variants: (1) a discrete-time hierarchical Bayesian Vector Autoregression (VAR) implemented in Stan, which pools across persons and yields calibrated uncertainty via posterior distributions; (2) a continuous-time per-person Ornstein-Uhlenbeck model in ctsem, which natively handles irregularly spaced measurements. The framework includes a multi-lag extension estimating person-specific lag coefficients across multiple τ_k values simultaneously.

Result: Four key findings: (1) Simulation shows the Bayesian version recovers between-person spread τ_a with bias < 0.01 and 90–93% coverage. (2) On Fisher et al. (2017) EMA data (N=40), person-specific lag-1 effects vary by an order of magnitude; Bayesian and GAMM estimates correlate at r=0.87–0.92; DTVEM-RE achieves best one-step-ahead prediction among four discrete-time methods. (3) Multi-lag version reveals all nine τ_k credible intervals exclude zero, with the lag of maximum between-person variance differing across mood items. (4) The two confirmatory versions yield near-identical person-specific lag-1 estimates (r ≥ 0.995), diverging only as shrinkage predicts.

Conclusion: DTVEM-RE is the first person-specific implementation of DTVEM-style lag detection, subsuming standard DTVEM as a special case. It successfully captures meaningful individual differences in temporal dynamics, outperforms homogeneous alternatives in prediction, and reveals multi-lag heterogeneity inaccessible to single-lag methods like mlVAR.

Abstract: The Differential Time-Varying Effect Model (DTVEM) of Jacobson et al. (2019) is a popular tool for finding the best time lag in intensive longitudinal data, but it assumes everyone shares the same lag structure. The original authors named fixing this as future work, and it clashes with the premise of modern clinical research, which is that people differ. We present DTVEM-RE, an extension that lets each person have their own lag coefficients, with two versions of the confirmatory step: a discrete-time hierarchical Bayesian VAR in Stan, which pools across people and gives calibrated uncertainty, and a continuous-time per-person Ornstein-Uhlenbeck model in ctsem, which handles unevenly spaced beeps directly. We report four results. A simulation shows the Bayesian version recovers the between-person spread tau_a with bias below 0.01 and coverage of 90 to 93 percent. On the Fisher et al. (2017) EMA dataset (N=40), person-specific lag-1 effects vary by an order of magnitude across three mood items, the Bayesian and GAMM estimates agree closely (r=0.87 to 0.92), and DTVEM-RE gives the best one-step-ahead prediction among four discrete-time methods. A multi-lag version shows all nine tau_k values have credible intervals excluding zero, and the lag where people differ most changes across items, something lag-1-only methods like mlVAR cannot detect. Finally, the two versions agree almost exactly on person-specific lag-1 estimates (r >= 0.995), differing only as shrinkage predicts. DTVEM-RE is, to our knowledge, the first person-specific implementation of DTVEM-style lag detection, and it contains standard DTVEM as a special case.

</details>


### [123] [Recovering Stranded Discrimination in Knowledge Tracing: Per-Item Bias Correction via Empirical-Bayes Shrinkage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14123)
*Xiaoran Yan,Cheng Tang,Atsushi Shimada*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SLC (State-space Logit Correction) addresses per-item logit bias in frozen knowledge-tracing models by converting binary observations to Gaussian pseudo-observations, applying empirical-Bayes shrinkage via a Kalman smoother, and fitting an offset-Platt link. Unlike global monotone calibrators, SLC improves both AUC and NLL, particularly for sparse items, and a derived detectability bound explains when temporal tracking provides no benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployed knowledge-tracing models are frozen after training, but systematic per-item logit bias arises from limited per-item expressivity in backbone architectures and post-deployment shifts in item properties. Global post-hoc calibrators (Platt scaling, temperature scaling, isotonic regression) improve probability estimates but leave AUC unchanged because they are monotone score-only transforms. Recovering the stranded discrimination requires conditioning on item identity.

Method: SLC operates in three steps: (1) converts per-item binary observations to Gaussian pseudo-observations using Laplace approximation / IRLS; (2) applies empirical-Bayes shrinkage through a Kalman smoother in a state-space formulation to borrow strength across time and items; (3) fits an offset-Platt link for recalibration. The state-space framework also yields a detectability bound characterizing the Bernoulli information floor.

Result: Across four datasets, five backbones, and three seeds, SLC improves AUC on all four datasets and NLL on three, with gains concentrated on sparse items. Cross-domain controls suggest the same phenomenon (entity-level bias from frozen deployed backbones) generalizes beyond education.

Conclusion: SLC recovers discriminative power stranded by monotone global calibrators by conditioning on item identity within a principled state-space empirical-Bayes framework. The detectability bound explains why temporal tracking yields no benefit at current data densities, and the phenomenon of entity-level bias extends beyond educational domains.

Abstract: Deployed knowledge-tracing models are typically frozen after training, yet systematic per-item logit bias arises, from limited per-item expressivity in backbone architectures and from post-deployment shifts in item properties, degrading prediction quality. Global post-hoc calibrators such as Platt scaling, temperature scaling, and isotonic regression improve probability estimates but leave discriminative ability, as measured by AUC, unchanged. This AUC invariance is a structural consequence of monotone score-only transforms; recovering the stranded discrimination requires conditioning on item identity. We propose SLC (State-space Logit Correction), which converts binary observations to Gaussian pseudo-observations via Laplace/IRLS, applies empirical-Bayes shrinkage through a Kalman smoother, and fits an offset-Platt link. The state-space formulation also yields a detectability bound that characterizes the Bernoulli information floor, explaining why temporal tracking provides no benefit at current data densities. Across four datasets, five backbones, and three seeds, SLC improves AUC on all four datasets and NLL on three, with the advantage concentrating on sparse items. Cross-domain controls suggest that the same phenomenon can arise beyond education when the deployed backbone leaves entity-level bias.

</details>


### [124] [Decoupled Latent Optimization of Diffusion Models for Full Waveform Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14139)
*Chen Min,Zheng Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DLO introduces a decoupled latent optimization framework for full waveform inversion that separates data-fidelity gradient updates (in physical space) from diffusion-prior sampling (in latent space), outperforming both classical regularizers and existing diffusion-based methods across clean, noisy, and missing-trace scenarios, while transferring zero-shot to larger benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical regularizers in FWI stabilize inversion but produce unrealistic geology; recent diffusion-prior methods improve realism but suffer from a fragile trade-off between data fidelity and prior consistency. The goal is to achieve both high data fidelity and geologically realistic reconstructions without the fragile balancing act.

Method: DLO reformulates latent optimization as a quadratic-penalty objective over two decoupled variables: an auxiliary physical variable and a latent variable. The data-fidelity gradient is computed entirely in physical space, while the diffusion model contributes solely through a decoded prior sample — the two paths are connected via a quadratic penalty rather than end-to-end backpropagation through the decoder. Classical smoothed-velocity FWI initialization is preserved. A pretrained diffusion prior on 70×70 OpenFWI models is used.

Result: DLO outperforms classical regularizers and existing diffusion-based FWI methods on the OpenFWI benchmark under clean, noisy, and missing-trace conditions. The prior transfers directly (zero-shot) to the larger Marmousi and Overthrust benchmarks, recovering intricate fault structures while remaining robust to initialization smoothing and measurement noise.

Conclusion: Decoupling the physical variable and latent variable via a quadratic penalty resolves the fidelity–realism trade-off in diffusion-prior FWI. The approach generalizes beyond the training distribution, achieving state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks with strong robustness to noise and missing data.

Abstract: Full waveform inversion (FWI) recovers subsurface velocity from seismic recordings by solving a severely ill-posed, nonconvex PDE-constrained optimization. Classical regularizers stabilize the inversion but fail to reproduce realistic geological structures; recent diffusion-prior methods improve realism at the cost of a fragile trade-off between data fidelity and prior consistency. We propose Decoupled Latent Optimization (DLO), which relaxes the standard latent-optimization formulation into a quadratic-penalty objective over an auxiliary physical variable and a latent variable. The data-fidelity gradient acts in physical space, the diffusion sampler contributes only through a decoded prior sample, and the standard smoothed-velocity initialization of classical FWI is preserved. On the OpenFWI benchmark, DLO outperforms classical regularizers and existing diffusion-based methods under clean, noisy, and missing-trace acquisitions. The prior, trained on 70*70 OpenFWI models, transfers directly to the Marmousi and Overthrust benchmarks, where DLO recovers intricate fault structures and remains robust to initialization smoothing and measurement noise.

</details>


### [125] [Trust but Verify: Mitigating Medical Hallucinations via Post-Hoc Adversarial Auditing and Multi-Agent Feedback Loops](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14149)
*Muhammad Osama,Maheera Amjad,Zartasha Mustansar,Arslan Shaukat,Muhammad U. S. Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs frequently recommend banned/withdrawn drugs in clinical scenarios; a "Trust but Verify" multi-agent system reduces such hallucinations by ~53%, shifting outputs from unsafe recommendations toward appropriate refusals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in healthcare but suffer from hallucination, posing direct risks to patient safety. The authors identify a specific failure mode: regulatory knowledge obsolescence, where models recommend pharmaceuticals that were once correct but are now banned or withdrawn, reflecting outdated training data.

Method: (1) An adversarial dataset of 103 clinical MCQs was constructed where historically correct answers reference currently banned/withdrawn substances, spanning multiple therapeutic classes. (2) A five-agent "Trust but Verify" system was built using a single LLM backbone, where agents cross-check outputs against regulatory data. (3) Three open-access model families (GPT-OSS, Llama-3, Falcon-3) and proprietary models were evaluated under vanilla vs. agentic conditions using pointwise score, label accuracy, Hallucination Error Rate (HER), and Component Fidelity (CF) score.

Result: All models in default configurations exhibited high hallucination rates, consistently selecting banned drugs aligned with training distribution patterns. The agentic architecture reduced HER by ~53% across models. Pointwise scores shifted from approximately -0.25 (unsafe recommendation) toward 0.0 (appropriate refusal). Safety regression was also observed in proprietary models. The safety audit successfully intercepted dangerous outputs even when parametric knowledge favored banned substances.

Conclusion: The multi-agent framework provides a model-agnostic, practical method for enforcing regulatory compliance in clinical LLM deployment, prioritizing patient safety over fluent generation. It demonstrates that real-time regulatory data integration into LLM pipelines can effectively support clinical decision-making in safety-critical settings.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in healthcare settings, yet their tendency to hallucinate poses risks when clinical decisions are involved. This study examine whether LLMs recommend recently banned or withdrawn pharmaceuticals when answering clinical questions and tests an agent-based method for reducing such errors. We developed a five-agent "Trust but Verify" system using a single LLM backbone. To measure regulatory knowledge obsolescence, we created an adversarial dataset of 103 clinical MCQs where historically correct answers now refer to banned substances. This scale ensures statistical significance across various therapeutic classes. We evaluated three open-access model families (GPT-OSS, Llama-3, Falcon-3) under vanilla and agentic conditions. Performance was measured via pointwise score, label accuracy, Hallucination Error Rate (HER), and Component Fidelity (CF) score. We also observed clinical safety regression in proprietary models. In default configurations, all models showed high hallucination rates, consistently selecting banned drugs that matched training data patterns. Our proposed agentic architecture reduced HER by approximately 53% across models. Pointwise scores shifted from -0.25 (unsafe recommendation) toward 0.0 (appropriate refusal). The safety audit intercepted dangerous outputs even when models' parametric knowledge favored the banned substance. The proposed multi-agent framework offers a model-agnostic method for enforcing regulatory compliance that prioritizes patient safety over fluent text generation. Our work demonstrates a practical approach for deploying autonomous AI systems in safety-critical healthcare settings. It shows how real-time regulatory data can be integrated into LLM pipelines to support clinical decision-making.

</details>


### [126] [Small LLMs: Pruning vs. Training from Scratch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14150)
*Yufeng Xu,Taiming Lu,Kunjun Li,Jiachen Zhu,Mingjie Sun,Zhuang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper evaluates whether pruning large language models (specifically Llama-3.1-8B) yields better small models than training from scratch, under token-matched budgets. Findings show pruned initialization is advantageous when training tokens are limited, but training from scratch becomes competitive at coarser granularities when token budgets are abundant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously test the common claim that pruning provides a "shortcut" to strong small language models, by comparing pruned models against models trained from scratch under strictly controlled, token-equivalent training budgets.

Method: Llama-3.1-8B is pruned at ratios of 0.5–0.8 using six methods spanning depth pruning, width pruning, and sparse (unstructured/structured) granularities. Two controlled settings are evaluated: (1) same training token budget for pruned vs. random initialization; (2) training from scratch given the full token budget of the entire prune-and-retrain pipeline.

Result: Under equal token budgets, pruned initialization consistently beats random initialization, but the margin shrinks with more tokens and higher pruning ratios. Under the full pipeline budget, fine-grained pruning retains advantage while coarser structured pruning is matched or surpassed by training from scratch.

Conclusion: If a large pretrained model exists and the training token budget is limited, pruning is recommended. If the training budget is unlimited, training from scratch can be competitive for coarser pruning, meaning a large pretrained parent model is not strictly necessary in that regime.

Abstract: Pruning promises a shortcut to strong small language models. In this work, we examine this promise by pruning Llama-3.1-8B at pruning ratios of 0.5--0.8 with six methods spanning depth, width, and sparse granularities, under two controlled token-matched settings. (1) With the same training token budget, pruned initialization consistently outperforms random initialization. This shows that the parent model provides a strong starting point, although the advantage narrows as the training token budget grows and as the pruning ratio rises, nearly vanishing at the highest pruning ratio we study. (2) When training from scratch is instead given the full token budget consumed by the whole pipeline, pruning at finer granularities still retains an advantage, while coarser structured pruning can be matched or surpassed. This suggests that the parent model transfers knowledge that additional training tokens alone cannot fully recover, but only at fine granularity. Taken together, our results yield a clear recommendation: with a large pretrained model in hand and a limited training token budget, pruning is better than training from scratch; when the training budget is not limited, training from scratch can be competitive for coarser pruning, so a large pretrained parent is not always necessary.

</details>


### [127] [Graph-based Target Back-Propagation for Context Adaptation in Multi-LLM Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14155)
*Tan Zhu,Tong Yao,Kananart Kuwaranancharoen,Amit Singh,Yushang Lai,Deepa Mohan,Shankara Bhargava*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GTBP is a graph-based context adaptation framework for multi-LLM agentic workflows that propagates target outputs backward through DAGs to guide stage-wise prompt updates, offering convergence guarantees and outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing context adaptation (automated prompt engineering) methods fail when extended to multi-LLM agentic systems due to inaccurate credit assignment across agents and lack of convergence guarantees. A principled framework is needed for optimizing prompts in compositional, graph-structured agent workflows.

Method: GTBP models agentic workflows as directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). It performs backward propagation of local target outputs through the workflow graph, then uses the discrepancies between targets and actual outputs to drive a stage-wise prompt update mechanism. Each stage's prompt is revised iteratively based on task feedback without modifying model weights.

Result: Theoretically, GTBP's stage-wise prompt updates provably stabilize over iterations, and a sufficiently capable LLM optimizer guarantees monotonic decrease of the overall objective. Empirically, GTBP consistently outperforms strong baselines on three benchmarks with comparable computational cost.

Conclusion: GTBP provides the first context adaptation framework for multi-agent LLM systems with both theoretical convergence guarantees and strong empirical performance, enabling reliable automated prompt optimization in compositional agentic workflows.

Abstract: Context adaptation automates prompt engineering in LLM-based systems by iteratively revising tunable prompts from task feedback, without modifying model weights. Extending this paradigm to multi-LLM agentic systems is crucial: existing methods suffer from inaccurate credit assignment and lack convergence guarantees. We propose \textbf{G}raph-based \textbf{T}arget \textbf{B}ack-\textbf{P}ropagation (GTBP), a context adaptation framework for agentic workflows modeled as directed acyclic graphs. GTBP propagates local target outputs backward through the workflow graph and uses target--output discrepancies to guide a stage-wise prompt update mechanism. Theoretically, we show that GTBP's stage-wise prompt updates become stable over iterations, and that a sufficiently capable LLM optimizer can decrease the overall objective. Empirically, GTBP consistently outperforms strong baselines across three benchmarks while maintaining comparable computational cost.

</details>


### [128] [Learning High Coverage Discriminative Parsimonious Rulesets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14156)
*Mariamma Antony,Raman Sankaran,Chiranjib Bhattacharyya,Uma Satya Ranjan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces CDPR, a novel approach for learning interpretable IF-THEN rule sets for classification. It employs two submodular maximization algorithms that provide provable coverage guarantees while producing rule sets that are both discriminative and parsimonious. Empirical results show superior accuracy, interpretability, and a 2.5× improvement in average coverage over the next best method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing state-of-the-art algorithms for IF-THEN rule learning implicitly prioritize predictive accuracy but often fail on key interpretability metrics such as coverage and parsimony. There is a gap between achieving high discriminative power and maintaining interpretability, motivating the need for a principled approach that balances both.

Method: The authors propose CDPR, a framework for classification rule set learning grounded in submodular maximization. Two algorithms are introduced that leverage submodularity to obtain provable guarantees on coverage while simultaneously optimizing for discriminative power and parsimony of the resulting rule sets.

Result: The rule sets learned via CDPR achieve higher accuracy and interpretability compared to competing methods. Notably, the approach yields more than a 2.5-fold improvement in average coverage rates over the next best algorithm.

Conclusion: CDPR represents the first attempt to explicitly design rule-learning algorithms that jointly optimize accuracy, coverage, and parsimony using submodular maximization. The strong empirical performance validates the approach as a promising direction for interpretable, rule-based classification.

Abstract: Learning systems based on IF-THEN rule representations readily offer interpretability, making them a crucial focus in contemporary AI research. A key objective for such rule sets is to achieve both high discriminative power and interpretability. While existing state-of-the-art algorithms implicitly prioritize predictive accuracy, they often fall short on one or more quality metrics that ensure interpretability, such as coverage and parsimony of rule sets. Motivated by this, this paper propose the development of CDPR, which aims to create highly accurate and interpretable rule sets for classification problems. To the best of our knowledge, this represents the first attempt to establish such an approach. In this study, we introduce two algorithms rooted in submodular maximization, which not only provide provable guarantees on coverage but also yield rule sets that are both discriminative and parsimonious. We empirically demonstrate that rule sets learned through our approaches achieve higher accuracy and interpretability and has more than a 2.5-fold improvement in average coverage rates when compared to the next best algorithm.

</details>


### [129] [Learning Urban Access Costs from Origin-Destination Flows via Inverse Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14157)
*Paula Joy B. Martinez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper frames urban service access as an optimal transport problem, using school enrollment flows in the Philippines to recover latent choice costs via inverse optimal transport. Two models — a distance-banded interpretable model and a neural cost model — estimate a "subsidy-equivalent distance" metric that quantifies how much perceived travel cost a subsidy offsets, transforming administrative origin-destination data into actionable planning metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Urban planners observe location-based choices (e.g., which school households select) but not the underlying cost function governing trade-offs among distance, price, and institutional access. In the Philippines, a large national education subsidy aims to shift learners from congested public schools to private ones, yet the latent behavioral response to this subsidy is unknown. The authors seek to recover these unobserved choice costs from administrative enrollment flows to inform subsidy design and facility siting.

Method: The authors treat school-to-school enrollment flows as an entropic optimal transport plan and apply two complementary inverse optimal transport (IOT) approaches to recover latent cost matrices: (1) a distance-banded interpretable model with an explicit subsidy term, and (2) a neural cost model trained via a differentiable Sinkhorn forward pass (enabling gradient-based learning of the cost function). The framework is applied to 283,016 learner trips across 23,820 observed flows in the Philippines' most populated region. The key output is a subsidy-equivalent distance metric λ^(k), expressed in kilometers of perceived travel cost offset by the subsidy.

Result: The framework successfully recovers latent choice costs from administrative origin-destination data at scale (283,016 trips, 23,820 flows). The subsidy-equivalent distance λ^(k) provides an interpretable metric quantifying the subsidy's effect in terms of perceived travel cost reduction. The neural IOT model, enabled by the differentiable Sinkhorn forward pass, captures non-linear cost structures beyond the simpler distance-banded model.

Conclusion: Administrative origin-destination data can be transformed into interpretable planning metrics through inverse optimal transport. The subsidy-equivalent distance offers a practical tool for accessibility-aware subsidy design, facility siting, and urban service allocation. The dual-model approach balances interpretability (distance-banded) with flexibility (neural cost), demonstrating that IOT is a viable framework for diagnosing and optimizing mixed public-private service delivery networks in cities.

Abstract: Cities deliver basic services through mixed public-private facility networks, including schools, clinics, transit providers, and subsidized service points. In these systems, planners often observe where households go, but not the latent cost function through which they trade off factors such as distance, price, and institutional access. We study this urban problem through school choice in the Philippines, where the country's largest national education subsidy is intended to redirect learners from congested public schools to participating private schools. Treating school-to-school enrollment flows as an entropic optimal transport plan, we recover latent choice costs using two complementary inverse optimal transport models: an interpretable distance-banded model with a subsidy term, and a neural cost model trained through a differentiable Sinkhorn forward pass. Applied to 283{,}016 learner trips across 23{,}820 observed flows in the most populated region, the framework estimates a subsidy-equivalent distance, $λ^{(k)}$, interpreted as the kilometers of perceived travel cost offset by the subsidy. The case demonstrates how administrative origin-destination data can be transformed into interpretable planning metrics for accessibility-aware subsidy design, facility siting, and urban service allocation.

</details>


### [130] [Curvature-Guided Geometric Representation for Protein-Ligand Binding Affinity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14159)
*Shuai Li,Chuan-Xian Ren,Yuhao Li,Ziqi Huang,Yue Pan,Mingzhe Tang,Hong Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RicciBind is a geometric framework that combines Ricci curvature-guided hierarchical structure learning with optimal transport-based cross-domain alignment to predict protein-ligand binding affinity, outperforming existing methods on benchmarks and virtual screening tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ML-based methods for protein-ligand binding affinity prediction inadequately capture both local geometric organization within individual molecules and globally coordinated cross-molecular interactions, limiting their ability to model complex binding mechanisms.

Method: RicciBind employs two coupled components: (1) Ricci curvature is used to quantify local interaction tightness and organize atomic interactions into curvature-aware hierarchical representations; (2) An optimal transport (OT)-based cluster matching mechanism aligns protein and ligand clusters across heterogeneous domains under geometric constraints, establishing globally consistent correspondences beyond local neighborhoods.

Result: Extensive experiments showed RicciBind achieves superior predictive performance and generalization on PLA benchmarks and virtual screening tasks. Ablation studies confirmed the essential contribution of Ricci curvature to enhancing molecular interaction representations.

Conclusion: By coupling curvature-guided structure encoding with OT-driven cross-domain alignment, RicciBind effectively models complex interaction semantics, substantially improving both accuracy and interpretability of binding affinity prediction.

Abstract: Protein-ligand binding affinity (PLA) prediction is critical in drug discovery. Despite the notable advancements in machine learning-based approaches, existing methods struggle to jointly characterize local geometric organization and globally coordinated cross-molecular interactions, limiting their ability to model complex binding mechanisms. Here, we propose RicciBind, a geometric representation framework that integrates curvature-guided hierarchical structure learning with optimal transport (OT)-based cross-domain alignment to model molecular interactions. Specifically, RicciBind leverages Ricci curvature to capture local interaction tightness within molecular structures, enhancing structural awareness and organizing atomic interactions into curvature-aware hierarchical representations. An OT-based cluster matching mechanism then aligns protein and ligand clusters across heterogeneous domains under geometric constraints, enabling globally consistent correspondences and revealing higher-order interaction patterns beyond local neighborhoods. By coupling curvature-guided structure encoding with OT-driven cross-domain alignment, RicciBind effectively models complex interaction semantics and substantially improves both the accuracy and interpretability of binding affinity prediction. Extensive experiments demonstrate that RicciBind achieved superior predictive performance and generalization across PLA benchmarks and virtual screening tasks. Ablation studies further confirmed the essential role of Ricci curvature in enhancing molecular interaction representations.

</details>


### [131] [Machine Learning for Biomedical Raman Spectroscopy: From Spectral Acquisition to Clinical Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14169)
*Bogdan Oancea,Ana Maria Seciu-Grama,Nicoleta Siminea,Laura Mihaela Stefan,Alice Stoica,Joel Sjoberg,Marian Necula,Ana-Maria Prelipcean,Corneliu Ovidiu Vrancianu,Eduard Milea,Andrei Păun,Ion Petre,Mihaela Păun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This review examines how machine learning can enhance biomedical Raman spectroscopy across the entire analysis pipeline—from preprocessing to multimodal integration—while highlighting translational barriers like data scarcity, lack of standardization, and reproducibility issues that must be addressed for clinical deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical Raman spectra are high-dimensional, noisy, and confounded by fluorescence background, acquisition variability, and biological heterogeneity. While Raman spectroscopy is valuable for cancer diagnosis, molecular subtyping, and microbiological identification, robust computational analysis is essential to unlock its clinical potential. The review is motivated by the need to move beyond mere diagnostic classification toward biologically interpretable and clinically actionable machine learning approaches.

Method: This is a comprehensive review article that surveys machine learning methods applied across the biomedical Raman spectroscopy pipeline, including: preprocessing and signal correction, unsupervised structure discovery, supervised diagnosis and molecular stratification, representation and transfer learning, explainability, biomarker discovery, and multimodal integration with imaging, pathology, and molecular profiling.

Result: The review identifies that ML can significantly enhance Raman-based biomedical analysis, but clinical translation is hindered by limited dataset sizes, inter-instrument variability, inconsistent preprocessing, insufficient external validation, reproducibility concerns, and limited sharing of software, data, and metadata.

Conclusion: Progress toward clinically deployable Raman-AI systems requires not only methodological advances in machine learning but also standardization, robust validation protocols, explainability, and deployment-ready analytical frameworks that integrate methodological, biomedical, and translational perspectives.

Abstract: Raman spectroscopy provides label-free, chemically specific characterization of biological systems and has become an important tool for cancer diagnosis, molecular subtyping, microbiological identification, and intraoperative decision support. Biomedical Raman spectra are, however, high-dimensional, noisy, and affected by fluorescence background, acquisition variability, and biological heterogeneity, making robust computational analysis essential.
  This review examines the role of machine learning across the biomedical Raman spectroscopy pipeline, from preprocessing and signal correction to unsupervised structure discovery, supervised diagnosis and molecular stratification, representation and transfer learning, explainability, biomarker discovery, and multimodal integration with imaging, pathology, and molecular profiling. Emphasis is placed on the use of machine learning not only for diagnostic classification, but also for biologically interpretable and clinically actionable analysis.
  We also discuss the main barriers to clinical translation, including limited dataset sizes, inter-instrument variability, inconsistent preprocessing, insufficient external validation, reproducibility concerns, and limited sharing of software, data, and metadata. We argue that progress will require methodological advances together with standardization, robust validation, explainability, and deployment-ready analytical frameworks. By integrating methodological, biomedical, and translational perspectives, this review outlines key directions for developing reliable and clinically deployable Raman-AI systems.

</details>


### [132] [Context-aware Modality-Topology Co-Alignment for Multimodal Attributed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14172)
*Sirui Zhang,Xu Wang,Zhengyu Wu,Xunkai Li,Hongchao Qin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoMAG proposes a unified multimodal attributed graph backbone that learns task-adaptive reliable contexts via semantic-consistency edge reliability estimation and performs modality-preserving alignment through hop-token matching, achieving SOTA across graph prediction, cross-modal matching, and graph-conditioned generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MAG methods suffer from task-agnostic propagation (fixed graph contexts cannot adapt to diverse tasks) and over-compressed fusion (uniform representations lose modality-specific evidence). There is a need for a unified backbone that simultaneously supports graph-centric tasks requiring structural/class-discriminative representations and modality-centric tasks requiring fine-grained cross-modal correspondence.

Method: CoMAG has two core modules: (1) Reliable Context Learning — estimates edge reliability from multimodal semantic consistency, supplements raw topology with semantic neighbors, and uses a task-aware gate to select relevant context components; (2) Modality-preserving Hop-token Alignment — maintains modality-specific multi-hop trajectories, matches modality-hop tokens across modalities, and decouples shared vs. private representations. Both graph and modality representations are produced in a single forward pass while preserving modality-specific cues.

Result: Experiments on nine OpenMAG datasets against feature-only, graph-only, multimodal, and unified MAG baselines show CoMAG achieves the best reported performance on graph-level prediction, modality matching, and graph-conditioned generation tasks.

Conclusion: Task-adaptive reliable contexts and modality-preserving alignment significantly improve structural prediction, cross-modal matching, and graph-conditioned generation. CoMAG achieves these gains while maintaining sparse edge-linear complexity, with theoretical analysis demonstrating stable propagation, over-smoothing mitigation, and modality-collapse control.

Abstract: Multimodal Attributed Graphs (MAGs) model real-world entities by coupling graph topology with heterogeneous attributes such as text and images. They support graph-centric tasks requiring structural and class-discriminative representations, and modality-centric tasks requiring fine-grained cross-modal correspondence. However, existing MAG methods often rely on fixed graph contexts or uniformly fused representations, causing task-agnostic propagation and over-compressed fusion that hinder diverse task requirements and modality-specific evidence preservation. To address this, we propose CoMAG, a unified MAG backbone that learns task-adaptive reliable contexts and modality-preserving alignment within them. CoMAG first conducts Reliable Context Learning by estimating edge reliability from multimodal semantic consistency, complementing raw topology with semantic neighbors, and selecting context components through a task-aware gate. It then performs Modality-preserving Hop-token Alignment by maintaining modality-specific multi-hop trajectories, matching modality-hop tokens across modalities, and decoupling shared and private representations. Thus, CoMAG produces graph and modality representations from one forward pass while retaining modality-specific cues. We further analyze stable propagation, over-smoothing mitigation, and modality-collapse control. Experiments on nine OpenMAG datasets compare CoMAG with feature-only, graph-only, multimodal, and unified MAG baselines across graph-level prediction, modality matching, and graph-conditioned generation. Results show that CoMAG achieves the best reported performance, demonstrating that task-adaptive reliable contexts and modality-preserving alignment improve structural prediction, cross-modal matching, and graph-conditioned generation while retaining sparse edge-linear complexity.

</details>


### [133] [Zeta: Dual Whitening for Matrix Optimization via Coordinate-Adaptive Preconditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14187)
*Kaiwen Chen,Shuhai Zhang,Qiuwu Chen,Zimo Liu,Linxiao Li,Ying Sun,Yuchen Li,Yifan Zhang,Bo Han,Mingkui Tan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Zeta is a dual whitening optimizer that sequentially applies coordinate whitening followed by spectral whitening to stabilize Newton-Schulz iteration in matrix-aware optimization. It resolves intra-matrix scale heterogeneity in momentum matrices, reduces orthogonalization error, and achieves faster convergence across language modeling, MoE, and vision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing matrix-aware optimizers like Muon rely on Newton-Schulz iteration, which is sensitive to input conditioning. The raw momentum matrices exhibit severe coordinate-wise scale heterogeneity, undermining the reliability of spectral normalization. The authors identify this as an underappreciated vulnerability and seek to correct it systematically.

Method: First, the authors verify scale heterogeneity using a chi-square uniformity test across Transformer layers. They then propose a strictly ordered dual whitening pipeline: coordinate whitening first establishes statistical isotropy, enabling spectral whitening to function reliably. They prove this ordering reduces orthogonalization error by improving the condition number of the input relative to pure spectral methods.

Result: Zeta matches or surpasses strong baselines on language modeling (0.6B–8B parameters), mixture-of-experts architectures, and vision tasks, demonstrating faster convergence and better generalization.

Conclusion: Resolving scale imbalance via ordered dual whitening before orthogonalization yields measurable gains in training efficiency and model quality. The mathematical dependency between coordinate and spectral whitening makes the pipeline order non-negotiable.

Abstract: Large-scale neural network training increasingly relies on matrix-aware optimizers that exploit the structure of weight parameters beyond element-wise adaptation. However, existing matrix-aware methods such as Muon have an underappreciated vulnerability: their core operation, Newton-Schulz iteration, depends critically on input conditioning, yet the raw momentum matrices exhibit severe coordinate-wise scale heterogeneity. In this paper, we first verify this scale heterogeneity through a chi-square uniformity test, showing that intra-matrix scale imbalance is prevalent across Transformer layers and that coordinate whitening effectively corrects it. Motivated by this finding, we propose Zeta, a dual whitening optimizer that applies coordinate whitening and spectral whitening in a strictly ordered pipeline. The ordering is not a tunable choice but follows from a mathematical dependency: coordinate whitening establishes the statistical isotropy that spectral whitening requires to function reliably. We further prove that this dual pipeline strictly reduces orthogonalization error relative to pure spectral methods by improving the condition number of the input. Empirically, Zeta matches or surpasses strong baselines across language modeling (0.6B to 8B parameters), mixture-of-experts architectures, and vision tasks, demonstrating that resolving scale imbalance before orthogonalization leads to faster convergence and better generalization. Code is available at https://gitcode.com/kevin259/MindSpeed.

</details>


### [134] [Simplex-Constrained Sparse Bagging: Transitioning from Uniform Priors to Sparse Posteriors in Ensemble Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13589)
*Meher Sai Preetam,Meher Bhaskar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCSB proposes a mathematically rigorous, post-training framework that jointly optimizes ensemble pruning and probability calibration by solving a simplex-constrained sparse optimization problem over OOB loss, using a concave quadratic penalty to overcome the L1-simplex paradox, achieving up to 96% compression with improved calibration and maintained accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard bagging ensembles assign uniform voting weights to all base estimators, ignoring their varying local competence, which leads to model overconfidence and poor probability calibration. Existing pruning methods lack a principled optimization framework that simultaneously addresses sparsity and calibration over the probability simplex.

Method: SCSB formulates ensemble pruning and calibration as a joint optimization problem over the probability simplex, minimizing OOB loss subject to simplex constraints. To induce sparsity, it introduces a concave quadratic penalty that circumvents the "L1-simplex paradox" — where the L1 norm is constant (equal to 1) on the simplex and thus cannot induce sparsity. The framework is model-agnostic and operates post-training.

Result: SCSB achieves up to 96% ensemble compression, yielding linear speedups in inference time. It lowers Expected Calibration Error (ECE), indicating superior probability calibration, while preserving or even enhancing generalization accuracy compared to standard uniform-weight bagging.

Conclusion: SCSB provides a mathematically rigorous, model-agnostic solution for simultaneous ensemble pruning and calibration, effectively addressing the L1-simplex paradox and delivering substantial compression with improved reliability of probabilistic predictions.

Abstract: We present Simplex-Constrained Sparse Bagging (SCSB), a mathematically rigorous framework for post-training compression and probability calibration of bootstrap-based bagging ensembles. Standard bagging ensembles (such as Random Forests, Bagged SVMs, and Bagged Neural Networks) assign uniform voting power to all constituent estimators. However, this naive uniform prior ignores the varying local competence of base estimators and contributes to model overconfidence. We formulate ensemble pruning and calibration as a joint optimization problem over the probability simplex by minimizing the Out-Of-Bag (OOB) loss. To induce sparsity, we address the theoretical "L1-simplex paradox" -- the mathematical reality that the L1 norm is constant on the simplex and fails to prune -- by introducing a concave quadratic penalty. SCSB is model-agnostic and achieves up to 96% ensemble compression, yielding linear inference speedups and superior probability calibration (lowered Expected Calibration Error) while preserving or enhancing generalization accuracy.

</details>


### [135] [DRIVE: Distributional and Retrieval-Augmented Bidding with Value Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14192)
*Miduo Cui,Haochen Wang,Shangqin Mao,Xun Yang,Qianlong Xie,Xingxing Wang,Xuri Ge,Ying Zhou,Zhiwei Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DRIVE is a Transformer-based offline auto-bidding framework that decouples candidate generation from decision-making via distributional action modeling, retrieval-augmented candidate selection, and value-based evaluation, outperforming unimodal policies on sparse and long-tail traffic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing offline RL and Transformer-based bidding policies suffer from two key limitations: (1) unimodal, purely parametric formulations collapse diverse effective strategies into suboptimal averaged actions, and (2) they perform unreliably under sparse or long-tail traffic conditions. Safe online exploration is infeasible in real-time advertising, necessitating a better offline approach.

Method: DRIVE uses a unified Transformer framework with three components: (1) distributional action modeling that captures the full distribution of viable bids rather than a point estimate; (2) retrieval-augmented candidate generation that retrieves high-quality historical decisions from a logged dataset to augment the candidate pool; and (3) value-based evaluation that scores and selects the most promising bid among candidates at inference time.

Result: Extensive experiments on AuctionNet and additional offline RL benchmarks show that DRIVE consistently improves bidding performance and generalizes well across multiple Transformer-based backbone methods.

Conclusion: Decoupling candidate generation from decision-making, combined with distributional modeling and retrieval augmentation, effectively addresses the mode-collapse and tail-traffic failure modes of prior unimodal Transformer policies for offline auto-bidding.

Abstract: Auto-bidding is a core component of real-time advertising systems, where decisions must optimize long-term performance under budget and cost constraints, while online exploration is prohibitively risky. Offline reinforcement learning and, more recently, Transformer-based sequence modeling have shown promise for learning bidding policies from logged data, but their unimodal and purely parametric formulations often collapse multiple effective bidding strategies into suboptimal averaged actions and perform unreliably under sparse or long-tail traffic. To mitigate these limitations, we propose DRIVE (Distributional and Retrieval-Augmented Bidding with Value Evaluation), a unified Transformer-based framework that decouples candidate action generation from decision making for offline auto-bidding. DRIVE combines distributional action modeling, retrieval-augmented candidate generation from high-quality historical decisions, and value-based evaluation to select the most promising bid at inference time. Extensive experiments on AuctionNet and additional offline reinforcement learning benchmarks demonstrate that DRIVE consistently improves bidding performance and generalizes well across multiple Transformer-based methods.

</details>


### [136] [Structured Noise Adaptation for Sequential Bayesian Filtering with Embedded Latent Transfer Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14195)
*Naichang Ke,Pongpisit Thanasutives,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an ELTO-based Bayesian filtering approach with structured noise adaptation, which combines a learned time-invariant noise model with dynamic parameter updates to improve state estimation in non-stationary environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ELTO-based Kalman filters rely on simplified noise models that cannot dynamically adapt to non-stationary processes, limiting their effectiveness in time-varying settings.

Method: The authors introduce a structured parameterization for the filter's noise model that couples data-driven learning of an optimal time-invariant noise model with dynamic parameter adaptation responsive to changes in non-stationary dynamics.

Result: Empirical results demonstrate that the proposed structured noise adaptation improves dynamic state estimation performance in noisy, time-varying environments.

Conclusion: Structured noise adaptation effectively addresses the limitation of simplified noise models in ELTO-based filters, enabling robust sequential state estimation under non-stationary conditions.

Abstract: Kalman filters based on the Embedded Latent Transfer Operators (ELTO) emerge as novel statistical tools for sequential state estimation. However, a critical limitation stems from their use of simplified noise models, which fail to dynamically adapt to non-stationary processes. To address this limitation, we introduce an ELTO-based Bayesian filtering approach with a new structured parameterization for the filter's noise model. This parameterization enables structured noise adaptation, which couples the data-driven learning of an optimal time-invariant noise model with dynamic parameter adaptation that responds to changes in dynamics within non-stationary processes. Empirical results show that our structured noise adaptation improves the filter's dynamic state estimation performance in noisy, time-varying environments.

</details>


### [137] [LapidaryEngine: Fully Conversational Crystal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14215)
*Yusei Ito,Yuta Suzuki,Tomoya Murata,Masaki Adachi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LapidaryEngine introduces conversational, bidirectional text-to-crystal generation using a pivot representation, enabling free-form natural-language requests with iterative refinement, overcoming the one-directional and format-restricted limitations of prior models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-crystal generative models are limited by requiring highly structured inputs (e.g., chemical formulas) and supporting only one-directional generation (text→crystal). These constraints prevent fully conversational, iterative workflows and fail to accommodate users' ambiguous, evolving design goals.

Method: LapidaryEngine introduces a pivot representation — an intermediate form that decouples text and crystal modalities — enabling bidirectional translation without requiring direct paired text-crystal datasets. This pivot supports free-form natural-language input, iterative refinement, and structural editing in a dialogue-like interaction loop.

Result: The model is demonstrated across diverse tasks: insulator discovery, stability optimization, compositional modification, and structural editing, showing effective alignment of generated crystal materials with user intent through interactive, conversational refinement.

Conclusion: LapidaryEngine advances text-to-crystal generation from rigid, unidirectional pipelines to a fully conversational paradigm, enabling intuitive, iterative materials design aligned with natural human expression.

Abstract: The emergence of Large Language Models (LLMs) has inspired the vision of generating bespoke crystal materials directly from natural-language instructions, enabling users to design materials through intuitive, conversational interaction. Existing text-to-crystal generative models represent important early steps toward this goal, but they suffer from two critical limitations: (i) restricted input formats that require highly structured descriptions (e.g., chemical formulas), and (ii) one-directional generation, where models can map text to crystal but cannot perform the inverse. These limitations prevent fully conversational workflows and hinder alignment with users' inherently ambiguous and evolving desiderata. We address these challenges with LapidaryEngine, the first model to support fully conversational crystal generation. LapidaryEngine accepts free-form natural-language requests and performs iterative refinement and editing in a dialogue-like manner. The key innovation is a pivot representation, a third, intermediate form that enables bidirectional translation between text and crystal structures despite the absence of direct paired datasets. Leveraging this pivot allows robust interpretation of user feedback and precise structural control. We demonstrate LapidaryEngine across diverse tasks, including insulator discovery, stability optimization, compositional modification, and structural editing, showcasing its ability to align generated materials with user intent in an interactive manner.

</details>


### [138] [Curvature-Informed Potential Energy Surface for Protein-Ligand Binding Affinity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14217)
*Peng-Fei Sun,Chuan-Xian Ren,Hong Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CPES proposes a curvature-informed potential energy surface GNN that models conformational flexibility via Hessian-derived curvature spectral descriptors and spectral cross-attention, fusing dynamic and static representations to improve protein-ligand binding affinity prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing geometric deep learning methods for binding affinity prediction rely solely on static interaction geometry from a single bound conformation, neglecting molecular flexibility and binding-induced conformational changes that are critical to accurate affinity estimation.

Method: CPES derives curvature spectral descriptors from the Hessian of the potential energy surface at equilibrium, using eigenvalues to define local principal curvatures. It applies spectral cross-attention between unbound ligand/protein and bound complex states to capture conformational dynamics. In parallel, hierarchical protein-ligand interaction representations are learned via geometry-aware message passing, soft clustering, and bidirectional cross-attention on static structural features. The curvature-informed dynamic and static representations are fused for affinity regression.
