Result: Extensive evaluations on multiple benchmark datasets show that CPES achieves improved predictive performance over existing methods, with additional physical interpretability from the curvature-informed representations.

Conclusion: Incorporating physics-informed curvature representations of the potential energy surface effectively captures conformational flexibility and binding-induced changes, leading to more accurate and interpretable protein-ligand binding affinity predictions.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of protein-ligand binding affinity is essential for structure-based drug discovery. Recent geometric deep learning methods have achieved promising performance by representing protein-ligand complexes as three-dimensional graphs. However, most existing approaches mainly rely on static interaction geometry from a single bound conformation, while neglecting molecular flexibility and binding-induced conformational changes. To address this limitation, we propose a curvature-informed potential energy surface (CPES) graph neural network for protein-ligand binding affinity prediction, which incorporates physics-informed curvature representations to model conformational flexibility. CPES first derives curvature spectral descriptors from the Hessian of the potential energy surface evaluated at equilibrium configurations, whose eigenvalues define the local principal curvatures of the potential energy surface. It then uses spectral cross-attention to compare the unbound ligand and protein with the bound complex, thereby capturing binding-induced changes in conformational dynamics. In parallel, hierarchical protein-ligand interaction representations are learned from static structural features through geometry-aware message passing, soft clustering, and bidirectional cross-attention. Finally, CPES fuses the curvature-informed dynamic representations with static interaction representations for affinity regression. Extensive evaluations on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that CPES achieves improved predictive performance and offers physical interpretability.

</details>


### [139] [Learning the Context of Errors: Black-Box Online Adaptation of Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14222)
*Xilin Dai,Yiding Liu,Hongjie Xia,Yifan Hu,Zewei Dong,Jiang-Ming Yang,Qiang Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ORCA proposes a black-box online adaptation method for Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) that learns from the predictive errors conditioned on both the model's input and output (error context), eliminating the need for white-box access like gradients or parameter fine-tuning. Experiments across 5 TSFMs and 8 datasets validate its effectiveness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Future TSFMs may be deployed as commercial closed-source API services, creating a paradigm mismatch with existing online adaptation methods that require white-box access (gradient backpropagation, parameter fine-tuning). The core question: in black-box online adaptation for TSFMs, what should we learn?

Method: ORCA (Online Residual Contextual Adaptation) leverages the insight that base model predictive errors are conditioned on both the input and output of the base model — termed the "context of errors." It learns a residual adapter in a black-box manner using this contextual conditioning, without requiring gradient access or internal model parameters.

Result: Extensive experiments across 5 state-of-the-art TSFMs and 8 datasets demonstrate ORCA's effectiveness. Ablation studies quantitatively analyze how different adapter learning hypotheses affect final black-box online adaptation performance.

Conclusion: The key insight — that predictive errors depend on the context formed by both model inputs and outputs — provides a principled foundation for black-box online adaptation of TSFMs. ORCA validates this approach, showing that effective adaptation is feasible without white-box access.

Abstract: The rapid evolution of Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) has advanced zero-shot forecasting across diverse domains. Inspired by the current form of Large Language Models, future TSFMs may be offered as commercialized, closed-source API services. However, many existing online adaptation methods still rely on white-box access for parameter fine-tuning or gradient backpropagation. This paradigm mismatch raises a question: In black-box online adaptation for TSFMs, what should we learn? We answer this with an insight: the predictive errors of the base model are conditioned on both the input and output of the base model (i.e., the context of errors). To validate this insight, we propose ORCA (Online Residual Contextual Adaptation). We conduct extensive experiments across 5 state-of-the-art TSFMs and 8 datasets to demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. Furthermore, through ablation studies, we quantitatively analyze the impact of different adapter learning hypotheses on the final adaptation performance in black-box online adaptation. Code available at https://github.com/Fifthky/ORCA.

</details>


### [140] [Implicit Variational Rejection Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14235)
*Jian Xu,Shigui Li,Wei Chen,Jiacheng Li,Zhiqi Lin,Delu Zeng,Xinghao Ding,John Paisley,Qibin Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IVRS combines neural-network-based implicit distributions with rejection sampling and a discriminator network to refine posterior approximations in VI, introducing IR-ELBO as a quality metric and achieving superior performance over traditional VI methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional VI with mean-field factorization often fails to capture complex posterior structures, and even neural-network-based implicit distributions face architectural limitations. There is a need for a more flexible and accurate posterior approximation method.

Method: Proposes Implicit Variational Rejection Sampling (IVRS): (1) neural networks construct implicit proposal distributions; (2) rejection sampling, guided by a discriminator network that estimates the density ratio between the proposal and true posterior, refines the approximation; (3) introduces Implicit Resampling Evidence Lower Bound (IR-ELBO) as a quality metric and derives a tighter variational lower bound.

Result: Experimental results show that IVRS outperforms traditional variational inference techniques in approximating complex posterior distributions.

Conclusion: IVRS effectively combines implicit distributions with rejection sampling to overcome limitations of both mean-field VI and purely neural implicit approaches, providing a tighter variational bound and improved posterior approximation quality.

Abstract: Variational Inference (VI) is a fundamental inference technique in Bayesian machine learning for approximating complex posterior distributions. Traditional VI often relies on the mean-field factorization, which can inadequately capture true posterior complexity. Recent advancements have leveraged neural networks to model implicit distributions, offering increased flexibility. However, the practical constraints of neural network architectures still produces inaccuracies. In this paper, we propose a method called Implicit Variational Rejection Sampling (IVRS), which integrates implicit distributions with rejection sampling to improve the posterior approximation. Our method uses neural networks to construct implicit proposal distributions, and rejection sampling with a discriminator network that estimates the density ratio between the implicit proposal and the true posterior for refining the approximation. Towards this end, we introduce the Implicit Resampling Evidence Lower Bound (IR-ELBO) as a metric to characterize the resampled distribution's quality and derive a tighter variational lower bound. Experimental results demonstrate that our method outperforms traditional variational inference techniques.

</details>


### [141] [Where Black-box Drug-Target Interaction Prediction Models Look: Cross-Method Explainability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14245)
*Ali Vefghi,Zahed Rahmati,Mohammad Akbari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An interpretability audit of the BridgeDPI architecture across three datasets uses multiple gradient-based attribution methods and occlusion ablation with intersection consensus to reveal modality dominance, dataset-dependent effects, and chemistry-consistent motifs, demonstrating that XAI can generate testable hypotheses for computational drug discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DTI/DTA predictors achieve strong benchmark scores but their internal reliance on sequence, fingerprint, and graph features is opaque. The authors aim to scrutinize these black-box mechanisms through rigorous interpretability methods to enable model criticism and generate testable hypotheses.

Method: An interpretability audit of BridgeDPI on Gao, Human, and C. elegans datasets combining gradient-based attributions (integrated gradients, saliency, LRP, SmoothGrad, SmoothGrad-IG) with feature-wise occlusion ablation and strict intersection consensus across methods to reduce single-explainer bias. Analysis spans raw inputs, the bridge similarity scaffold, and graph convolution layers including edge-level sensitivities and targeted edge removals.

Result: Explainability reveals modality dominance, padding and special-token artifacts, dataset-dependent cooperative versus suppressive effects across layers, and chemistry-consistent fragment and composition motifs where methods agree.

Conclusion: These XAI analyses do not substitute for structural or experimental ground truth but can provide testable hypotheses for downstream validation. Applying modern XAI to DTI/DTA models is still nascent, yet even this initial scrutiny helps relate predictions to drug- and target-side representations and prioritize external validation.

Abstract: Drug-target interaction (DTI) and affinity (DTA) predictors increasingly achieve strong benchmark scores, yet their internal use of sequence, fingerprint, and graph features often remains opaque. We present an interpretability audit of BridgeDPI architecture on three different datasets including Gao, Human, and C.elegans. This study combines gradient-based attributions -- integrated gradients, saliency, layer-wise relevance propagation, SmoothGrad, and SmoothGrad-IG -- with feature-wise occlusion ablation and strict intersection consensus across methods to reduce single-explainer bias. We summarize sensitivity and signed effects at raw inputs, at the bridge similarity scaffold, and through the graph convolution, including edge-level sensitivities and targeted edge removals. The results show that explainability is most informative when treated as model criticism: it reveals modality dominance, padding and special-token artifacts, dataset-dependent cooperative versus suppressive effects across layers, and chemistry-consistent fragment and composition motifs where methods agree. These analyses do not substitute for structural or experimental ground truth, yet they can provide testable hypotheses for downstream validation in computational drug discovery pipelines. More broadly, applying modern XAI to contemporary DTI/DTA models is still an early pass over the rich structure implicit in trained weights and data -- yet even this first layer of scrutiny already helps researchers relate predictions to drug- and target-side representations and to prioritize external validation.

</details>


### [142] [Beyond a Single Explanation of the Adam--SGD Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14259)
*Chenxiang Zhang,Rustem Islamov,Enea Monzio Compagnoni,Jun Pang,Aurelien Lucchi,Antonio Orvieto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper investigates the performance gap between Adam and SGD optimizers through controlled experiments across diverse tasks. It finds no single factor consistently explains the gap; instead, it arises from complex data–architecture interactions. A key empirical finding is a "crossover batch size" where the relative advantage shifts from SGD to Adam as batch size increases, which is captured by their theoretical model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work identifies multiple isolated factors (data, architecture, optimization properties) contributing to the Adam–SGD performance gap, but their relative importance remains unclear. This paper aims to systematically revisit these hypotheses in a unified, controlled empirical framework to disentangle their contributions.

Method: The authors conduct controlled empirical studies spanning vision, language, genomics, and graph tasks, covering both modern and classical architectures. They carefully design training setups to isolate individual factors. They also develop a theoretical gap model that predicts a batch-size-dependent crossover in relative optimizer advantage.

Result: No single factor consistently explains the Adam–SGD gap. Adam's advantage persists under uniform vocabulary but nearly vanishes under heavy-tailed distributions; it reverses (SGD favored) in softmax-attention models; and grows under soft architectural changes (e.g., ReLU→GeLU). A robust pattern emerges: a crossover batch size exists where advantage shifts from SGD to Adam as batch size scales. The theoretical model captures this crossover.

Conclusion: The Adam–SGD performance gap stems from nontrivial interactions between data and architecture rather than a single common factor. The proposed batch-size-dependent crossover perspective helps reconcile existing fragmented hypotheses and offers practical guidance for optimizer selection across domains.

Abstract: Prior work has identified several factors that can contribute to the performance gap between Adam and SGD, spanning data aspects, architecture design, and optimization properties. Yet these explanations are often studied in isolation, leaving their relative importance unclear. In this work, we revisit these hypotheses through a controlled empirical study across vision, language, genomics, and graph tasks, spanning modern and classical architectures, and carefully designed training setups. Our results suggest that no single factor consistently explains the Adam--SGD gap. For instance, the Adam advantage can (1) persist under a uniform vocabulary distribution yet nearly disappear under a heavy-tailed one; (2) reverse in favor of SGD in softmax-attention models; and (3) become larger under soft architectural modifications, e.g., when ReLU is replaced by a GeLU nonlinearity. This suggests that the gap arises from nontrivial data and architecture interactions, rather than from a single common factor. Yet, we observe a pattern across our settings: a \emph{crossover batch size} at which the relative advantage shifts from SGD to Adam as the batch size scales. These empirical results are captured by our theoretical gap model, which predicts this batch-size-dependent crossover. Our perspective helps reconcile several existing hypotheses while offering practical insights across domains.

</details>


### [143] [DIFF-ERO: A Conformance-Aware Loss for Deep Learning in Process Mining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14283)
*Johannes De Smedt,Jari Peeperkorn,Artem Polyvyanyy,Jochen De Weerdt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DIFF-ERO introduces a differentiable, entropy-based conformance loss that explicitly encodes control-flow structure into deep learning training for process analytics, yielding models that better internalize process model structure without sacrificing token-level predictive accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard deep learning objectives for predictive process monitoring (e.g., cross-entropy) optimize local next-step likelihoods but only implicitly capture global control-flow structure, leading to models with high token accuracy yet structurally imprecise behaviour. There is a need for a loss function that explicitly integrates conformance to process structure during training.

Method: The authors propose DIFF-ERO, a differentiable formulation of entropy-based stochastic conformance checking. It constructs batch-level stochastic transition matrices with soft edge memberships, enabling structural precision and recall signals to flow via backpropagation. The loss is model-agnostic and applicable whenever the final layer parametrizes stochastic transitions. It is instantiated in transformer encoder-decoder pipelines for next-activity prediction, used jointly with cross-entropy loss.

Result: Across benchmarks comparing various loss functions and targets, DIFF-ERO improves predictive performance in settings where structural fidelity matters most while maintaining parity elsewhere. The learned stochastic automaton converges toward the structural ground truth, demonstrating that the network internalizes process model structure.

Conclusion: DIFF-ERO successfully bridges deep learning and conformance checking by making structural conformance differentiable, enabling end-to-end training that produces both accurate next-activity predictions and structurally faithful learned automata.

Abstract: Deep learning has driven many recent advances in process analytics, especially for predictive and prescriptive monitoring. However, standard objectives such as cross-entropy optimize local next-step likelihoods and only implicitly capture control-flow structure. As a result, models can achieve high token-level accuracy while permitting imprecise global behaviour. We introduce DIFF-ERO, a conformance-aware loss function for deep learning models on process data. DIFF-ERO is a differentiable formulation of entropy-based stochastic conformance that incorporates control-flow information during training. Our approach constructs batch-level stochastic transition matrices with soft edge memberships, allowing structural precision and recall signals to directly inform backpropagation. The loss is model-agnostic and can be applied whenever the final representation parametrizes stochastic transitions. We instantiate DIFF-ERO in transformer encoder-decoder pipelines for next-activity prediction and use it jointly with cross-entropy to analyse its theoretical components with respect to convergence. Across benchmarks comparing other loss functions and targets, DIFF-ERO shows improved predictive performance where structure matters most while maintaining parity elsewhere. At the same time, the learned stochastic automaton converges towards the structural ground truth, indicating that the network internalizes process model structure.

</details>


### [144] [Hierarchical ODE: Learning Continuous-Time Physical Prototypes for Early Link Failure Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14284)
*Jiaen Lv,Leran Qi,Shaowei Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a hierarchical ODE clustering network that uses Neural ODEs to model time series as continuous integral curves, disentangling smooth trends from stochastic noise, while an adaptive mechanism autonomously determines prototype count, enabling robust early link failure detection from irregularly sampled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series prototype learning suffers from observational ambiguity — discrete architectures cannot separate stochastic noise from continuous dynamics, and closed-set assumptions miss unseen diversity. These limitations motivate a continuous, flexible clustering approach.

Method: A hierarchical ordinary differential equation clustering network is proposed. Neural ODEs model latent state evolution as continuous integral curves, enforcing temporal continuity to disentangle smooth features from noise. An adaptive hierarchical mechanism autonomously determines the number of prototypes without rigid priors.

Result: The method was validated on early link failure detection with irregularly sampled time series, demonstrating effective extraction of underlying physical prototypes and robust failure detection performance.

Conclusion: The hierarchical ODE clustering network effectively resolves observational ambiguity in time series prototype learning by combining continuous dynamics modeling with adaptive prototype discovery, enabling robust detection on irregularly sampled real-world tasks.

Abstract: Time series prototype learning is fundamentally challenged by observational ambiguity. Discrete architectures fail to resolve this, as they lack the capacity to decouple stochastic noise from continuous dynamics. Furthermore, rigid closed-set assumptions fail to capture unseen diversity. To address these limitations, we propose a hierarchical ordinary differential equation clustering network, which utilizes neural ordinary differential equation to model latent state evolution as a continuous integral curve. This formulation enforces temporal continuity to effectively disentangle smooth feature trends from stochastic noise, while our adaptive hierarchical mechanism autonomously determines the appropriate number of prototypes without rigid prior constraints. Validated on the early link failure detection task with irregularly sampled time series, the proposed method effectively extracts underlying physical prototypes, thereby enabling robust failure detection. Our code is available at https://github.com/NJ-LNN/Hierarchical-ODE.

</details>


### [145] [Riemannian Metric Matching for Scalable Geometric Modeling of Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14334)
*Jacob Bamberger,Adam Gosztolai,Pierre Vandergheynst,Michael Bronstein,Iolo Jones*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes Riemannian metric matching, a neural network-based framework that learns the Riemannian geometry of data by estimating the carré du champ operator via conditional expectation over random perturbations. This enables amortized, graph-free inference that is up to 400× faster than k-NN diffusion geometry methods and works on high-dimensional data like images where nearest-neighbor approaches fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional data often concentrate near low-dimensional manifolds, but existing methods for estimating their geometry (based on graphs and kernels like k-NN) scale poorly with dataset size and dimension, and break down entirely for very high-dimensional data such as images.

Method: The authors learn the carré du champ operator — a key object in diffusion geometry that unlocks the Riemannian geometry toolkit (Laplacian, Ricci curvature, etc.). They reformulate this operator as a conditional expectation over random perturbations of the data, enabling sample-wise neural network training with constant-cost amortized inference, avoiding explicit kernel or graph construction entirely.

Result: Metric matching rivals or surpasses the accuracy of k-NN-based diffusion geometry estimators, achieves up to 400× faster amortized inference, and supports geometric analysis on high-dimensional image data where nearest-neighbor methods degrade or become infeasible.

Conclusion: Learning the Riemannian geometry of data through the carré du champ operator via neural networks provides a scalable, accurate alternative to traditional graph-based diffusion geometry, enabling geometric analysis in regimes where k-NN methods fail.

Abstract: High-dimensional datasets often concentrate near low-dimensional structures, but estimating their geometry from samples typically relies on graphs and kernels that scale poorly with dataset size and dimension. We propose Riemannian metric matching: a denoising probabilistic framework for learning the Riemannian geometry of data using neural networks. Specifically, we learn the carré du champ operator, which, using diffusion geometry, gives us access to the Riemannian geometry toolkit for downstream machine learning and statistical tasks. Our key observation is that the carré du champ operator can be formulated as a conditional expectation over random perturbations of the data, which can be exploited for sample-wise training and constant cost, amortized inference without explicit kernel construction. Empirically, metric matching rivals or improves the accuracy of $k$-NN-based diffusion geometry estimators, while enabling amortized inference that is up to $400\times$ faster, and supports graph-free geometric analysis on high-dimensional images where nearest neighbors break down.

</details>


### [146] [More with LESS -- Local Scene Representations for Tactile Imaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14344)
*Zohar Rimon,Elisei Shafer,Tal Tepper,Daniel Kozin,Alon Malka,Roy Holland,Aviv Tamar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LESS introduces an object-centric, local-encoder tactile representation using a grid of recurrent encoders with local receptive fields, enabling strong generalization from single- to multi-inclusion imaging, spatial uncertainty estimation, hand-held palpation, and full 3D reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-supervised tactile imaging methods depend on global, unstructured representations and robot-controlled sensing, which limits generalization to varied object configurations and prevents practical, hand-held use.

Method: LESS models the tactile scene as a grid of recurrent encoders with local receptive fields. Each encoder processes local tactile signals; their hidden states are fused to reconstruct 2D or 3D internal structure. The method also incorporates external pose tracking for hand-held operation and supports spatial uncertainty estimation via the local compositional architecture.

Result: Models trained only on single-inclusion phantoms successfully image objects with multiple inclusions and varying sizes, demonstrating strong compositional generalization. The approach also enables hand-held tactile imaging and full 3D reconstruction with spatial uncertainty estimates.

Conclusion: An object-centric, locally structured tactile representation significantly improves generalization, enables hand-held sensing, and extends tactile imaging to practical 3D reconstruction with uncertainty awareness.

Abstract: Tactile imaging seeks to reconstruct the internal structure of soft objects through touch sensing, with applications in medical diagnosis and robotic manipulation. Recent self-supervised learning approaches have shown promising results, but rely on global, unstructured representations and robot-controlled sensing, limiting generalization and practical use. We propose Local Encoder for Spatial Sensing (LESS), an object-centric tactile representation that exploits the local nature of touch. The tactile scene is modeled as a grid of recurrent encoders with local receptive fields, whose states are fused to reconstruct 2D or 3D images of internal structure. This compositional design enables strong generalization: models trained on single-inclusion phantoms accurately image objects with multiple inclusions and varying sizes. The local structure further supports spatial uncertainty estimation. In addition, we enable hand-held tactile imaging via external pose tracking and human-like palpation data, and extend tactile imaging to full 3D reconstruction.

</details>


### [147] [Squeeze-Release: Iterative Pruning with Exact Structural Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14346)
*Roman Denkin,Ida Akerholm,Prashant Singh,Ida-Maria Sintorn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Squeeze-Release is an iterative pruning-minimization cycle that converts sparse masked networks into structurally smaller dense networks via an exact rewrite ("minimization"), with a release step that reintroduces pruned positions as calibrated noise to recover capacity. It achieves 39× compression on FC networks and 14.8× on ConvNeXt-Tiny at comparable accuracy, and extends to transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard unstructured pruning removes weights but retains the original tensor shapes, yielding no actual reduction in deployed model size. The authors aim to convert unstructured sparsity into genuine structural compression—smaller dense networks with identical forward functions—while also enabling iterative discovery of structural redundancy that single-pass pruning cannot find.

Method: The Squeeze-Release cycle has three stages: (1) unstructured pruning to identify near-zero weights; (2) minimization, an exact structural rewrite that compacts the masked network into a smaller dense network with equivalent forward behavior; (3) release, which re-enables the zeroed positions as small calibrated noise, restoring trainable capacity. Additionally, CompensatedLayerNorm is introduced as a function-preserving LayerNorm replacement that enables channel reduction across residual streams with LayerNorm.

Result: On a fully-connected model network, Squeeze-Release compresses to 39× smaller than the unpruned model; on ConvNeXt-Tiny (modern CNN), 14.8× smaller. Both results are achieved at comparable accuracy. The minimization rewrite is also proven extensible to transformer architectures.

Conclusion: The Squeeze-Release cycle, with minimization and CompensatedLayerNorm, effectively converts unstructured sparsity into genuine structural compression, enabling significant model size reduction while maintaining accuracy across diverse architectures including CNNs and transformers.

Abstract: Unstructured pruning produces sparse weight tensors, but the standard implementation keeps tensor shapes unchanged so the deployed model is no smaller than before pruning. We present an exact structural rewrite, which we call minimization, that converts a masked network into a smaller dense network with the same forward function up to floating-point rounding. The Squeeze-Release cycle iterates pruning and minimization with an intermediate release step that re-enables the exact-zero positions inside the compacted tensors as small calibrated noise, turning otherwise wasted capacity back into trainable parameters. Successive cycles use that capacity to find structural redundancy a single pass cannot reach. We additionally introduce CompensatedLayerNorm, a function-preserving replacement for LayerNorm that extends minimization to channel reduction across LayerNorm-equipped residual streams. Squeeze-Release compresses the deployable network to 39x smaller than the unpruned model on a fully-connected model network and 14.8x smaller on modern CNN (ConvNeXt-Tiny), at comparable accuracy. In addition we prove that the rewrite can be extended to transformer architectures.

</details>


### [148] [When Language Representations Interact: Separability and Cross-Lingual Effects in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14347)
*Boris Marinov,Angira Sharma,Christian Schroeder de Witt,Philip Torr,Anisoara Calinescu,Jialin Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Applying causal-geometric analysis to multilingual LLMs, this paper finds that language concepts are encoded as approximately linear and separable directions, with hierarchical simplex-like structures emerging among related language families, extending interpretability frameworks to multilingual settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Causal-geometric structure has explained concept encoding in monolingual models, but whether this framework extends to multilingual LLMs — where language identity involves correlated and hierarchical relationships — remains underexplored. Understanding these representations is critical for reliable multilingual system behavior.

Method: The authors apply causal-geometric analysis to three multilingual LLMs across 28 bilingual contrasts. They examine whether language representations are linearly separable under a covariance-adjusted (causal) inner product and analyze geometric structure among language families.

Result: Language concepts admit stable, largely separable linear representations under the causal inner product. Structured deviations from perfect separability reflect linguistic similarity. Languages within the same family (e.g., Germanic, Romance) exhibit simplex-like geometric organization, indicating hierarchical structure.

Conclusion: The work extends causal-geometric interpretability to multilingual settings, showing that separability and similarity coexist in multilingual LLM representations. Residual cross-lingual structure has practical implications for trustworthy deployment, as monitoring or interventions may trigger unintended cross-lingual effects.

Abstract: Large language models exhibit strong multilingual capabilities, however, their internal representations are difficult to interpret. Understanding these interactions is important for ensuring reliable behavior in multilingual systems. Recent work has shown that causal-geometric structure can explain how certain concepts are encoded as approximately linear and separable directions, but whether this framework extends to multilingual models, where language identity is correlated and hierarchical, is underexplored. We apply causal-geometric analysis to multilingual LLMs, studying 28 bilingual contrasts across three models, allowing us to analyze when languages behave as approximately independent factors and when structured dependencies persist. We find evidence that language concepts admit stable linear representations that are largely separable under a covariance-adjusted (causal) inner product, with structured deviations reflecting linguistic similarity. Moreover, languages within the same family (such as Germanic or Romance) exhibit a simplex-like geometric structure, suggesting hierarchical organization. These results extend causal-geometric interpretability to multilingual settings and provide insight into how separability and similarity may exist in multilingual LLM representations, motivating interpretability analyses that diagnose when and how structured dependencies between concepts can be anticipated. This has implications for trustworthy deployment, as residual structure between languages may lead to unintended cross-lingual effects when models are monitored or intervened upon.

</details>


### [149] [Can Deep Neural Networks Improve Compression of Very Large Scientific Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14353)
*Muhannad Alhumaidi,Guozhong Li,Spiros Skiadopoulos,Panos Kalnis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether modern ML-based weather forecasting models (CRA5, GraphCast, Aurora) can outperform traditional predictors (SZ3.1) in error-bounded lossy compression of scientific data. Using ERA5 climate data (~1.7 TB), ML predictors improve reconstruction quality by up to 91% and variable-level compression ratios by up to 9.6×, yet fail to improve overall dataset-level compression ratios. The key insight: prediction accuracy alone is insufficient — the spatial structure of residuals critically determines entropy coding efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art scientific data compressors rely on the prediction-residual paradigm, where better predictors yield smaller, more compressible residuals. The authors ask whether pretrained ML foundation models (abundant in climate science) can serve as superior predictors. Directly developing compression-specific ML predictors is resource-intensive, so they leverage existing weather forecasting models as a testbed to explore this question systematically.

Method: The authors propose a framework that integrates spatial and temporal deep learning predictors into a conventional error-bounded compression pipeline, supporting auto-regressive forecasting without error accumulation. They evaluate three ML predictors — VAEformer-based CRA5, Graph Neural Network-based GraphCast, and Vision Transformer-based Aurora — against SZ3.1, all using identical quantization and entropy-coding backends. The evaluation uses ~1.7 TB of ERA5 climate reanalysis data.

Result: ML predictors produce more accurate predictions, improving reconstruction quality by up to 91% and achieving up to 9.6× higher compression ratios for highly predictable variables. However, they do not improve overall dataset-level compression ratio compared to SZ3.1. The spatial structure of residuals — not just prediction accuracy — is the decisive factor in entropy coding efficiency, explaining this counterintuitive outcome.

Conclusion: Prediction accuracy alone is an insufficient metric for compressor design; the spatial autocorrelation structure of residuals dominates entropy coding gains. This finding reframes how future ML-based scientific data compressors should be designed and evaluated, emphasizing residual structure optimization over pure prediction error minimization.

Abstract: Error-bounded lossy compression is a fundamental technique for managing the rapidly growing volumes of scientific data produced by modern simulations and observational instruments. Most state-of-the-art-compressors follow a prediction-residual paradigm, where compression effectiveness depends on the quality of the predictor: more accurate predictions generate smaller residuals that are easier to compress. This observation raises a question: can modern machine learning models serve as superior predictors for scientific data compression? Answering this question directly is challenging because developing compression-specific ML predictors requires substantial resources. Instead, we leverage the climate domain where highly accurate pretrained weather forecasting foundation models already exist, making them an ideal testbed. We present a framework that integrates spatial and temporal deep learning models into a conventional error-bounded compression pipeline. The framework supports auto-regressive forecasting models and avoids error accumulation. Using ERA5 climate data as a representative large-scale scientific dataset, we evaluate three distinct ML predictors: a VAEformer-based codec (CRA5), a graph neural network forecaster (GraphCast), and a vision-transformer forecaster (Aurora), against the state-of-the-art compressor SZ3.1 under identical quantization and entropy-coding backends. Our evaluation over approximately 1.7 TB of data reveals a surprising result: although ML predictors generate more accurate predictions and can improve reconstruction quality by up to 91% while achieving up to 9.6x higher compression ratios for highly predictable variables, they do not improve overall dataset-level compression ratio. We show that prediction accuracy alone is insufficient: the spatial structure of the resulting residuals plays a decisive role in entropy coding efficiency.

</details>


### [150] [MUFFLe: Efficient Model Update Compression via Generalized Deduplication for Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14354)
*Xiaobo Zhao,Daniel E. Lucani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MUFFLe applies generalized deduplication (GD) to compress federated learning model updates, achieving significant communication savings over quantization, sparsification, and uncompressed FedAvg on IID MNIST.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning in edge environments suffers from high uplink communication costs when transmitting model updates. Existing compression methods (quantization, sparsification) offer improvements but leave room for more efficient schemes that exploit redundancy in update vectors.

Method: MUFFLe integrates generalized deduplication (GD) into the FedAvg pipeline. It identifies and removes repeated patterns across the model update vector, producing a fixed-rate, variable-count compression scheme — meaning a consistent compression ratio with varying numbers of transmitted elements.

Result: On IID MNIST with 20 clients, MUFFLe reaches 92.93% target accuracy with only 38 MB cumulative uplink communication, versus 75 MB for 8-bit quantization, 86 MB for Top-k sparsification, and 310 MB for uncompressed FedAvg — achieving roughly 8× savings over uncompressed and 2× over quantization.

Conclusion: Generalized deduplication is a viable and effective approach for communication-efficient federated learning, outperforming standard compression baselines in preliminary experiments.

Abstract: Federated learning is well suited to edge environments but is often limited by the uplink cost of transmitting model updates. This Work-in-Progress paper presents MUFFLe, a communication-efficient update compression scheme that integrates generalized deduplication (GD) into the FedAvg pipeline. MUFFLe deduplicates repeated patterns across the update vector, yielding a fixed-rate, variable-count compression scheme. Preliminary experiments on IID MNIST with 20 clients show that MUFFLe reaches the target accuracy of $92.93\%$ with 38~MB cumulative uplink communication, compared with 75~MB for 8-bit quantization, 86~MB for Top-$k$ sparsification, and 310~MB for uncompressed FedAvg. These results demonstrate the feasibility of applying GD to communication-efficient federated learning.

</details>


### [151] [Be My Tutor: On-Policy Co-Distillation for Mutual LLM Improvement via Peer Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14368)
*Woohyeon Byeon,Jiwon Jeon,Jeonghye Kim,Youngchul Sung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OPCoD proposes mutual Pareto improvement for multi-domain LLM co-training via on-policy co-distillation with cognizance-based gating and feedback anchoring, outperforming baselines on Science Q&A.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-model training approaches (e.g., one-way distillation, single-model fine-tuning) fail to achieve mutual improvement across domains without sacrificing each model's original strengths. The authors aim for Pareto improvement where two specialized LLMs co-evolve by tutoring each other.

Method: OPCoD (On-Policy Co-Distillation) has each student perform self-distillation conditioned on its own correct rollouts and peer feedback. Two key mechanisms enable effective feedback exchange: (1) cognizance-based gating — determining when a model should provide feedback based on its confidence/competence; (2) feedback anchoring — grounding the peer's feedback in the original problem context to ensure relevance.

Result: OPCoD consistently outperforms baseline methods and achieves Pareto improvement across all evaluated domain pairs and student models on Science Q&A tasks.

Conclusion: OPCoD successfully enables mutual cross-domain improvement between two specialized LLMs through structured on-policy co-distillation, validating the feasibility of collaborative multi-model training without performance regression in either model's area of expertise.

Abstract: We study multi-domain LLM training in which two models, each stronger in a different domain, co-evolve by tutoring each other through on-policy feedback. Unlike one-way distillation or single-model fine-tuning, our goal is mutual Pareto improvement: each model improves across domains without losing its original strength. To this end, we propose On-Policy Co-Distillation (OPCoD), where each student's self-distillation is conditioned on its own correct rollout and feedback from its peer. To make feedback exchange effective, OPCoD uses cognizance-based gating to decide when to give feedback and feedback anchoring to ground feedback in the problem. On Science Q\&A tasks, OPCoD consistently outperforms baselines and achieves Pareto improvement across all evaluated domain pairs and students.

</details>


### [152] [SemPiper: Interactive Code Synthesis for Semantic Operators in Machine Learning Pipelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14361)
*Olga Ovcharenko,Luciano Duarte,Sebastian Schelter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SemPipes introduces declarative, LLM-powered semantic data operators that integrate natural language instructions into ML pipelines, with a system called SemPiper that synthesizes optimized implementations and visualizes pipeline evolution through an interactive interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML pipeline development is tedious and error-prone due to extensive data preparation and integration needs. While LLMs assist coding, chat interfaces offer limited control over pipeline behavior and produce code that is hard to optimize or productionize.

Method: SemPipes provides a programming model with declarative semantic data operators specified via natural language, combined with arbitrary Python code. Specialized operator implementations are synthesized at pipeline training time, conditioned on dataset characteristics and pipeline context. An evolutionary search procedure optimizes these implementations, all visualized through the SemPiper interactive interface.

Result: The system is demonstrated through three end-to-end scenarios where users can modify pipelines, inspect generated code, and observe how semantic operators are synthesized and iteratively optimized.

Conclusion: Declarative semantic operators enable controllable, optimizable, and practical integration of LLMs into ML pipeline development, bridging the gap between natural language expressiveness and production-ready pipeline engineering.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) pipelines require extensive data preparation, feature engineering, and integration across heterogeneous sources, making them tedious and error-prone to develop. While large language models (LLMs) have recently shown promise for assisting programming tasks, chat-based interfaces provide limited control over pipeline behavior and often produce code that is difficult to optimize or integrate into production systems. We demonstrate SemPipes, a novel programming model that extends ML pipelines with declarative, LLM-powered semantic data operators. SemPipes allows developers to specify high-level natural language instructions for data-centric operations, while seamlessly combining these operators with arbitrary Python code from standard data science libraries. For the semantic operators, it synthesizes specialized implementations at pipeline training time, conditioned on dataset characteristics and pipeline context, enabling the flexible yet controlled integration of LLM capabilities. We demonstrate SemPipes through SemPiper, an interactive interface that visualizes computational graphs of the pipelines, synthesized operator implementations, and optimization trajectories produced by an evolutionary search procedure. Attendees can explore three end-to-end scenarios, modify pipelines, inspect generated code, and observe how semantic operators are synthesized and iteratively optimized. The demonstration highlights how declarative semantic operators enable controllable, optimizable, and practical integration of LLMs into ML pipeline development.

</details>


### [153] [Discovery under Hypothesis Redundancy: A Geometric Theory of Discovery Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14386)
*Li Xia,Baoxun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes the Search Compression Hypothesis, formalizing three geometric conditions (spectral compression, orthogonal escape, residual alignment) that must co-occur for hybrid LLM+local search systems to outperform local-only discovery. Gains concentrate in weakly represented, target-bearing directions and vanish as the hypothesis space approaches full rank.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific discovery saturates as new hypotheses fail to provide independent information. While hybrid systems combining local search with LLM-generated proposals are popular, it remains unclear when non-local exploration actually helps. The paper seeks to move beyond generic novelty search toward a diagnostic framework for deciding when directed non-local exploration is warranted.

Method: The authors formalize three geometric necessary conditions for hybrid advantage: (1) spectral compression — the hypothesis space has low effective rank; (2) orthogonal escape — non-local proposals lie outside the span of explored hypotheses; (3) residual signal alignment — proposals correlate with unexplained target variance. They test the mechanism through controlled synthetic experiments with signal-planting, directed-versus-random jump comparisons, large-scale A-share factor discovery, and LLM-SRBench symbolic regression tasks, plus a public tabular sanity check for budget allocation.

Result: Random orthogonal jumps expand coverage but do not improve yield without predictive alignment — novelty alone is insufficient. Across compression sweeps, real factor archives, and SR benchmarks, hybrid gains concentrate in weakly represented but target-bearing directions and vanish as the hypothesis space approaches full rank. The budget-allocation sanity check validates the practical diagnostic value.

Conclusion: The framework reframes LLM-guided discovery from generic novelty search into a principled diagnostic procedure: measure spectral compression, escape orthogonality, and residual alignment to decide when directed non-local exploration is warranted. When these conditions are absent, allocating budget to local search alone is sufficient.

Abstract: Scientific discovery saturates when new hypotheses cease to provide independent information, even if the nominal hypothesis space remains large. We study hybrid discovery systems that combine structured local search with LLM-generated non-local proposals and pose the Search Compression Hypothesis: non-local exploration helps only when three geometric conditions co-occur: spectral compression, orthogonal escape from the explored span, and residual signal alignment with the target. We formalize these conditions, derive necessary conditions for hybrid advantage, and test the mechanism in controlled synthetic environments, large-scale A-share factor discovery, and symbolic-regression benchmarks; a public tabular operational sanity check tests the associated budget-allocation implication. Signal-planting and directed-versus-random experiments show that novelty alone is insufficient: random orthogonal jumps expand coverage but do not improve yield without predictive alignment. Across compression sweeps, real factor archives, and LLM-SRBench tasks, hybrid gains concentrate in weakly represented but target-bearing directions and vanish as the hypothesis space approaches full rank. The framework turns LLM-guided discovery from generic novelty search into a diagnostic procedure for deciding when directed non-local exploration is warranted.

</details>


### [154] [A Low-Rank Subspace Analysis of LLM Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14388)
*Angira Sharma,Christian Schroeder de Witt,Philip Torr,Anisoara Calinescu,Jialin Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM behaviors (refusal, sycophancy) share internal representations in activation space. Intervening on one behavior produces asymmetric side-effects on others. Two geometric quantities predict these propagation effects: subspace overlap between behaviors, and each behavior's angle to the decision subspace.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety interventions on LLMs (e.g., reducing sycophancy, inducing refusal) frequently cause unintended changes in other behaviors. Understanding why and how these side-effects occur is critical for designing reliable safety controls, yet the phenomenon lacks a systematic diagnostic framework.

Method: The authors model behaviors as low-rank subspaces in activation space and introduce a diagnostic framework to analyze how interventions on one behavior affect others. They measure two geometric quantities: (i) subspace overlap via average squared cosine of principal angles, and (ii) the angle between each behavior subspace and a "decision subspace" that captures the model's final output decision. Experiments span instruction-tuned models from 7B to 70B parameters across refusal, jailbreak, and sycophancy settings.

Result: Behaviors share internal representations and interventions produce asymmetric propagation. Behaviors closer to the decision subspace act as "upstream control points" that broadly affect others, while some behaviors remain isolated. Higher subspace overlap between behavior pairs predicts larger intervention side-effects. Smaller angle to the decision subspace for the source behavior also predicts broader propagation.

Conclusion: Targeted behavior control in LLMs is fundamentally challenging because behaviors are not independently modifiable — they are entangled through shared subspaces and asymmetric interactions, with some behaviors acting as dominant upstream nodes in the representation geometry.

Abstract: Interventions designed to modify a particular behavior in LLMs, such as refusal or sycophancy, often produce unintended changes in other behaviors. This lack of targeted control makes it difficult to design and implement reliable safety controls. To understand these side-effects, we introduce a diagnostic framework for analyzing interacting behaviors in LLMs. We model behaviors as low-rank subspaces in activation space, and study how interventions influence across behaviors. Across multiple instruction-tuned models (7B-70B) and across refusal, jailbreak, and sycophancy settings, we find that different behaviors share internal representations, and intervening on one behavior alters others in asymmetric ways. Some behaviors act as upstream control points whose interventions propagate broadly across other behaviors, while others remain more isolated. We relate these effects to two geometric quantities: (i) the overlap between behavior subspaces, measured as the average squared cosine of principal angles, and (ii) the angle between each behavior subspace and the decision subspace (capturing the model's final decision e.g., refuse vs. comply). Empirically, intervention effects on other behaviors tend to be larger for behavior pairs with higher subspace overlap, and for source behaviors whose subspaces lie closer (smaller angle) to the decision subspace. These findings highlight a challenge for targeted behavior control: behaviors are difficult to modify independently, as interventions can propagate through shared representations and asymmetric interactions.

</details>


### [155] [Running the Gauntlet: Re-evaluating the Capabilities of Agents Beyond Familiar Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14397)
*Mykola Vysotskyi,Runqi Lin,Grzegorz Biziel,Michal Zakrzewski,Sebastian Montagna,Damian Rynczak,Shreyansh Padarha,Kumail Alhamoud,Zihao Fu,William Lugoloobi,Kai Rawal,Hanna Yershova,Xander Davies,Taras Rumezhak,Guohao Li,Fazl Barez,Baoyuan Wu,Arkadiusz Drohomirecki,Yarin Gal,Chris Russell,Christopher Summerfield,Adam Mahdi,Volodymyr Karpiv,Philip Torr,Adel Bibi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GauntletBench is a web-based benchmark that evaluates LLM agents on three underexplored capabilities (temporal perception, graphical understanding, 3D reasoning) across five professional applications, revealing that even the best agent achieves only 19.1% success versus 80%+ for non-expert humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent benchmarks are saturated, focusing on simple tasks and narrow capabilities tied to popular applications. They fail to probe agent limitations in more complex, real-world professional scenarios, leaving critical capability gaps (temporal perception, graphical understanding, 3D reasoning) underexplored.

Method: The authors construct GauntletBench with 100 vision-intensive tasks across five professional web applications: Video Editor, Workflow Builder, 3D Modeller, Flight Analyser, and Circuit Designer. The benchmark features a modular pipeline with (1) an environment compatible with both open- and closed-source agent frameworks, (2) controlled web-based applications, (3) a structured task suite, and (4) an automated evaluation engine with diverse metrics. Tasks target temporal perception, graphical understanding, and 3D reasoning.

Result: The state-of-the-art agent achieves only a 19.1% success rate on GauntletBench, while non-expert human annotators exceed 80% success. This reveals a substantial capability gap in temporal perception, graphical understanding, and 3D reasoning, as well as poor generalization to less-covered professional application domains.

Conclusion: Frontier agentic systems remain far from human-level performance on complex, real-world professional tasks. The overlooked capabilities and domains highlighted by GauntletBench represent critical areas for future research. The benchmark provides a more faithful evaluation framework for measuring agent generalization in challenging scenarios.

Abstract: As agentic systems continue to evolve and are widely deployed in real-world scenarios, there is a growing demand to faithfully evaluate their capabilities. However, current benchmarks are typically built on popular applications with relatively simple tasks and focus on a narrow set of capabilities while overlooking broader dimensions, resulting in saturated performance on modern agents and failing to probe their limitations. To this end, we introduce GauntletBench, a web-based benchmark for evaluating agent generalisation in challenging scenarios, focusing on three underexplored capabilities (temporal perception, graphical understanding, and 3D reasoning), across five less-covered professional applications (Video Editor, Workflow Builder, 3D Modeller, Flight Analyser, and Circuit Designer), each with 20 vision-intensive tasks (100 in total). Our benchmark provides a modular pipeline that comprises an environment compatible with both open- and closed-source agent frameworks, a controlled web-based application, a well-structured task suite, and an automated evaluation engine with diverse metrics. Contrary to widespread expectations, our empirical results reveal that frontier agentic systems remain far from achieving human-level performance. Even the state-of-the-art agent achieves only a 19.1% success rate on our GauntletBench, highlighting the limitations in these overlooked capabilities and generalisation. By comparison, non-expert human annotators achieve over 80% success on our challenging yet feasible tasks, revealing the substantial gap between current agent capabilities and those required for complex real-world scenarios.

</details>


### [156] [Flood and Harvest: The Provable Necessity of Trivia for Generating Valuable Mathematics via the Lens of Language Generation in the Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14688)
*Xiaoyu Li,Andi Han,Dai Shi,Zheng Gao,Jiaojiao Jiang,Junbin Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper formalizes AI-generated mathematics using a nested language model where a verifiable formal language F contains an unknown valuable sublanguage H. A verifier (proof checker) provides a membership oracle for F but cannot distinguish H. The authors prove that verifiers cannot substitute for mathematical taste, showing a sharp dichotomy: finite trivia generation achieves optimal coverage α/2, while any infinite trivia allowance jumps the optimum to 1-α/2. The unbounded stream of correct-but-worthless statements is a provable necessity for covering unrecorded valuable mathematics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems can now generate formal mathematics at scale, but the bottleneck is not formal verification — it is distinguishing valuable mathematics from trivial but correct statements. The paper asks whether access to a perfect proof checker can substitute for mathematical "taste" in generating valuable mathematics, and what fundamental limits exist when an AI system must cover an unknown valuable sublanguage H (the literature) embedded within a verifiable formal language F.

Method: The authors model the problem as nested language generation in the limit: a verifiable formal language F with a membership oracle (proof checker), containing an unknown valuable language H revealed through an adversarial enumeration of a core C ⊆ H with density α. Outputs are classified as valuable (∈ H), trivial (∈ F \ H), or hallucination (∉ F). They analyze this within Angluin's inductive inference framework, characterizing generation with breadth, sound coverage, and proving a sharp dichotomy on the tight family regarding finite vs. infinite trivia generation and its impact on optimal coverage bounds.

Result: Four main results: (1) The verifier is not taste — collections admitting generation with breadth match the oracle-free model characterized by Angluin's condition. (2) The verifier enables sound coverage (covering all unseen valuable statements while asserting only valid ones), which is impossible without it; errors shift from false to trivial. (3) A sharp dichotomy: finite trivia generators achieve optimal coverage α/2; any infinite trivia allowance (even at vanishing rate) jumps the optimum to 1-α/2. The transition depends on trivia count, not rate. (4) Both regimes instantiate in a compression model of mathematics.

Conclusion: A perfect verifier cannot substitute for mathematical taste. The unbounded stream of correct-but-worthless statements is not an engineering artifact but a provable necessity: covering unrecorded valuable mathematics requires an infinite (though asymptotically negligible) stream of certified trivia. The gap 1-α represents the unrecorded mass that fundamentally cannot be covered without trivial output.

Abstract: AI systems coupled to proof assistants now generate formal mathematics at scale, and the gap between what a checker can verify and what a mathematician would value has become the binding constraint. We model the generation of valuable mathematics as nested language generation in the limit: a verifiable formal language $F$, accessed through a membership oracle (the proof checker), contains an unknown valuable language $H \in \mathcal{H}$ revealed only through an adversarial enumeration of a core $C \subseteq H$ of exact density $α$ (the literature). Every output is valuable ($\in H$), trivial ($\in F \setminus H$), or a hallucination ($\notin F$). We settle four questions. First, the verifier is not taste: the collections admitting generation with breadth are exactly those of the oracle-free model, characterized fiber-wise by Angluin's condition. Second, the verifier does buy sound coverage, covering all unseen valuable statements while asserting only valid ones: possible with it, impossible without it; it relocates unavoidable errors from false to trivial. Third, and centrally, a sharp dichotomy on the tight family: generators emitting finitely many trivia achieve optimal coverage $α/2$, while any infinite trivia allowance, even at vanishing rate, jumps the optimum to $1-α/2$ (both tight, for cores presented as the candidate intersection), and one generator attains both ends. The transition is in trivia count, not rate; the gap $1-α$ is the unrecorded mass. Fourth, both regimes instantiate in a compression model of mathematics. A perfect verifier cannot substitute for taste: the unbounded stream of correct-but-worthless statements is not an engineering accident but a provable necessity, since covering unrecorded valuable mathematics requires an infinite, but asymptotically negligible, stream of certified trivia.

</details>


### [157] [A theoretical model for task routing in mixture-of-expert transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14398)
*Yongli Xiang,Vinoth Nandakumar,Yunzhi Yao,Peike Li,Tongliang Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper provides the first theoretical proof that discrete MoE transformers can achieve task-expert specialization by constructing a single-layer model where experts encode structured knowledge (syntactic templates + key-value dictionaries), with expert size scaling only with task complexity, supporting empirical observations of localized knowledge circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing theoretical work on MoE task specialization relies on continuous mixture models that cannot effectively model natural language. There is an open problem of theoretically explaining task-expert specialization using discrete language models, which this work aims to solve.

Method: The authors represent structured knowledge using syntactic templates and finite key-value dictionaries, then formally prove that a single-layer MoE transformer can encode this knowledge via task-specialized experts. The construction demonstrates that queries are routed to unique, task-specific experts whose size depends only on the intrinsic complexity (combined size of templates and dictionaries) of the corresponding task.

Result: Theoretical results show that a single-layer MoE transformer provably achieves task-expert specialization where each expert encodes a distinct task. Expert size is bounded by task complexity alone, independent of total model scale. Experiments evaluating model performance under varying MoE loss functions further support the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The construction provides theoretical grounding for empirically observed localized knowledge circuits in MoE models, bridging the gap between discrete language modeling and theoretical understanding of expert specialization in transformer MoE architectures.

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) layers enable the scaling of transformer models while keeping the inference compute fixed. While task-expert specialization has been observed in empirical studies of frontier MoE transformer models, existing theoretical work analyzes this using continuous mixture models that cannot be used to model natural language effectively. An important open question is to \textit{theoretically explain task-expert specialization in transformer MoE models using discrete models of language}. To address this, we represent structured knowledge via syntactic templates and finite key-value dictionaries, and prove formally that a single-layer MoE transformer can encode knowledge by using experts that specialize in the corresponding tasks. Our construction shows how queries are routed to unique, task-specific experts whose size depends solely on the intrinsic complexity of the given task (i.e. the combined size of its syntactic templates and factual dictionary). Our construction provides a theoretical support for empirical results on localized knowledge circuits in MoE models. We support our theoretical findings with experiments evaluating model performance under varying MoE loss functions.

</details>


### [158] [Persona-Pruner: Sculpting Lightweight Models for Role-Playing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14695)
*Jinsu Kim,Jihoon Tack,Noah Lee,Jongheon Jeong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Persona-Pruner identifies and extracts persona-specific subnetworks from language models, enabling lightweight role-playing with minimal performance degradation compared to dense models, outperforming existing pruning methods by reducing performance drop by up to 93.8%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-scale LMs are computationally inefficient for role-playing applications involving multiple characters (e.g., NPC ecosystems), as dedicating an entire generalist model to a single persona is wasteful. The authors hypothesize that a character identity requires only a fraction of the model's capacity.

Method: Persona-Pruner isolates persona-specific sub-networks from a generalist LM using only a character description, pruning away redundant knowledge while preserving essential character traits. This contrasts with naive pruning, which fails to distinguish between the two.

Result: On RoleBench using LLM-as-a-judge evaluation, Persona-Pruner reduces the performance drop from the dense model by up to 93.8% over the strongest baseline, while retaining general LLM capabilities.

Conclusion: A full generalist LM is unnecessary for role-playing a single persona; persona-specific pruning yields compact, efficient models that maintain role consistency and general language ability.

Abstract: Language Models (LMs) have shown remarkable potential as role-playing chatbots, delivering consistent, stylized interactions when given a specification of a character or user persona. However, applying these capabilities to real-world applications (e.g., ecosystems with numerous NPCs interacting simultaneously) exposes a critical inefficiency due to the excessive computational cost. In this paper, we question the necessity of dedicating a full, generalist model to a single persona, hypothesizing that a specific character identity relies on only a fraction of the model's total capacity. We observe that naively pruning LMs often severely degrades the role-playing performance for a specific persona; it does not distinguish between redundant knowledge and essential character traits. We propose Persona-Pruner, a framework that sculpts a lightweight role-playing model by isolating persona-specific sub-networks from a single description. Our experiments consistently show that Persona-Pruner preserves role-playing performance substantially more effectively than existing state-of-the-art LLM pruning techniques, reducing the performance drop from the dense model by up to 93.8% over the strongest baseline on RoleBench in LLM-as-a-judge score, while still maintaining general LLM capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/jsu-kim/Persona-Pruner.

</details>


### [159] [Federated Learning for Feature Generalization with Convex Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14416)
*Dongwon Kim,Donghee Kim,Sung Kuk Shyn,Kwangsu Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedCONST improves federated learning generalization by adaptively modulating local update magnitudes using global parameter strength and convex constraints, preserving transferable features during aggregation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning suffers from poor generalization due to heterogeneous client data. Local models overfit their own distributions, and even transferable features get distorted during the aggregation step, creating a misalignment between local and global objectives.

Method: FedCONST introduces two key mechanisms: (1) adaptive modulation of local update magnitudes based on the parameter strength of the global model — dampening updates to well-learned parameters while boosting underdeveloped ones; (2) linear convex constraints applied during aggregation to stabilize training and preserve locally learned generalization capabilities. A Gradient Signal-to-Noise Ratio (GSNR) analysis provides theoretical validation.

Result: FedCONST achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse FL environments, with GSNR analysis confirming enhanced feature transferability and robustness against data heterogeneity.

Conclusion: By adaptively constraining updates according to global parameter strength, FedCONST effectively aligns local and global objectives, mitigates client overfitting, and promotes stronger generalization in heterogeneous federated learning settings.

Abstract: Federated learning (FL) often struggles with generalization due to heterogeneous client data. Local models are prone to overfitting their local data distributions, and even transferable features can be distorted during aggregation. To address these challenges, we propose FedCONST, an approach that adaptively modulates update magnitudes based on the parameter strength of the global model. This prevents over-emphasizing well-learned parameters while reinforcing underdeveloped ones. Specifically, FedCONST employs linear convex constraints to ensure training stability and preserve locally learned generalization capabilities during aggregation. A Gradient Signal to Noise Ratio (GSNR) analysis further validates the effectiveness of FedCONST in enhancing feature transferability and robustness. As a result, FedCONST effectively aligns local and global objectives, mitigating overfitting and promoting stronger generalization across diverse FL environments, achieving state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [160] [EM-NeSy: Expectation Maximization for Neurosymbolic Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14463)
*Annegret Seibt,Luc De Raedt,Giuseppe Marra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EM-NeSy reformulates probabilistic neurosymbolic learning as an Expectation-Maximization algorithm, enabling approximate symbolic reasoning without requiring differentiability of the symbolic component.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: State-of-the-art NeSy models require the symbolic component to be differentiable, which complicates approximate inference. The authors aim to decouple neural learning from symbolic reasoning to support flexible, non-differentiable symbolic solvers.

Method: The framework casts NeSy learning as EM: the E-step computes the posterior over neural symbol predictions conditioned on labels via probabilistic inference; the M-step updates neural parameters via gradient descent through only the neural component, using the posterior as soft targets.

Result: EM-NeSy naturally extends to approximate reasoning with no additional modifications, recovers standard end-to-end gradient-based NeSy under exact inference, and demonstrates scalability and computational efficiency experimentally.

Conclusion: The EM formulation unlocks the full potential of probabilistic inference for NeSy, enabling the use of arbitrary (non-differentiable) symbolic reasoners while maintaining compatibility with exact gradient-based learning.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic (NeSy) models integrate neural networks and symbolic reasoning for robust and interpretable AI. State-of-the-art NeSy models require that the symbolic component is expressed in a differentiable way, often complicating the use of approximate inference. We propose EM-NeSy which casts probabilistic NeSy learning as an instance of the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm. In the expectation step, we compute the posterior over the neurally predicted symbols conditioned on the label via probabilistic inference. In the maximization step, we update the neural parameters based on this posterior using gradient descent only through the neural component. This formulation unlocks the full potential of the EM algorithm for NeSy learning. It allows NeSy to extend naturally to approximate reasoning without any additional modifications or differentiability requirements of the symbolic component. Furthermore, it recovers the standard end-to-end gradient-based NeSy setting under exact inference. Our experimental results demonstrate the scalability and computational efficiency of EM-NeSy.

</details>


### [161] [Recipe-Controlled Decoder Audit for Structural Knowledge-Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14492)
*Xihang Shan,Ye Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a recipe-controlled decoder audit (RCDA) for knowledge-graph completion, isolating decoder effects from encoder and training recipe contributions. By swapping decoders (ComplEx vs. DistMult) under identical conditions across seven benchmarks, the authors show modest but consistent decoder effects on standard KGs, substantial decoder sensitivity on small KGs, and interactions between decoder choice and encoder depth. The result is a protocol for rigorous KGC evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior KGC research often attributes performance gains to encoder architectures or training recipes without controlling for decoder choice. This confounds attribution: improvements may stem from the decoder rather than the claimed innovation. The authors aim to disentangle these factors through systematic decoder-swapping under fixed recipes.

Method: The authors conduct a recipe-controlled decoder audit (RCDA): they fix the training recipe (optimizer, negative sampling, regularization, etc.) and swap decoders—primarily ComplEx vs. DistMult, with spot-checks using RotatE and TransE—across seven KGC benchmarks (five standard KGs:WN18RR, FB15k-237, YAGO3-10, CoDEx-S, CoDEx-M; two small KGs: Kinship, UMLS). They evaluate decoder effects in isolation, examine interaction with encoder depth (e.g., CompGCN layers), and assess seed sensitivity and provenance effects through multi-seed reruns.

Result: On five standard KGs, ComplEx-vs-DistMult differences are modest but consistent (+0.005 to +0.012 MRR). CompGCN-style encoder effects vary more by dataset. On small KGs, decoder effects dominate: Kinship shows a stable ComplEx advantage of +0.143 MRR across 6 seeds; UMLS favors ComplEx by +0.022 MRR in clean reruns but reverses under an earlier provenance variant. Decoder choice interacts with encoder depth on WN18RR. Under their recipe, an L=0 ComplEx model on YAGO3-10 achieves 0.6971 ± 0.0048 MRR at d=128.

Conclusion: Decoder choice is a non-trivial confound in KGC evaluation. The authors propose a compact audit protocol: (1) report matched decoder rows to isolate effects, (2) log small-KG provenance details due to sensitivity, and (3) sweep decoder × depth interactions before making encoder-level claims. Small-KG decoder choice should be treated as recipe- and provenance-sensitive rather than a fixed dataset winner.

Abstract: We present a recipe-controlled decoder audit (RCDA) for structural transductive knowledge-graph completion (KGC). The audit asks a simple reporting question: before attributing gains to an encoder or training recipe, what changes when the decoder is swapped under the same recipe? Using ComplEx and DistMult as the primary controlled pair, with targeted RotatE/TransE spot-checks, we evaluate seven benchmarks. On five standard KGs, ComplEx-vs-DistMult differences are modest but consistent under our recipe (+0.005 to +0.012 MRR), whereas CompGCN-style encoder effects vary more by dataset. On small KGs, decoder effects become the main diagnostic: Kinship shows a stable ComplEx advantage of +0.143 MRR (6 seeds), while UMLS favours ComplEx by +0.022 MRR in a clean 6-seed server rerun but reverses in an earlier provenance variant. We therefore treat small-KG decoder choice as recipe- and provenance-sensitive rather than as a fixed dataset winner. We further show that decoder choice interacts with encoder depth on WN18RR, and that under our recipe L=0 ComplEx on YAGO3-10 reaches 0.6971 +/- 0.0048 MRR at d=128. The result is a compact audit protocol: report matched decoder rows, log small-KG provenance, and sweep decoder x depth before making encoder-level claims.

</details>


### [162] [PepALD: Macrocyclic Peptide Generation via Autoregressive Latent Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14510)
*Junming Zhang,Siyu Yi,Wei Ju,Zhonghui Gu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PepALD is an Autoregressive Latent Diffusion foundation model for de novo macrocyclic peptide design that uses structured chemical embeddings of HELM monomers, context-conditioned diffusion in latent space, R-group-aware ring closure prediction, and preference-optimized reward alignment, outperforming existing baselines in generation quality and affinity optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Macrocyclic peptides are promising for targeting intracellular targets, but their design is challenging because it requires simultaneously controlling non-natural monomer chemistry, ring topology, membrane permeability, and target binding. Existing generative models (SMILES/HELM-string based) either operate in inefficient atom-level sequence spaces or treat monomers as symbolic tokens lacking chemical grounding, thus failing to adequately handle the chemical complexity of macrocyclic peptides.
