Method: PepALD is an Autoregressive Latent Diffusion (ALD) foundation model with four key components: (1) structured chemical embeddings for HELM monomers, (2) context-conditioned latent diffusion for residue-by-residue generation in a chemically informed latent space, (3) R-group-aware ring closure prediction during autoregressive generation to control macrocyclic topology, and (4) winner-protected diffusion-adapted preference optimization (DPO) for aligning the denoiser to target affinity rewards.

Result: In silico experiments demonstrate that PepALD achieves superior generation quality and reward-optimization performance compared to representative peptide generation baselines.

Conclusion: PepALD advances macrocyclic peptide design by integrating chemically grounded latent diffusion with autoregressive generation and reward alignment, offering a promising foundation model for structure-aware de novo generation of therapeutically viable macrocyclic peptides.

Abstract: Macrocyclic peptides are promising therapeutic candidates for intracellular targets, but their design requires simultaneous control over non-natural monomer chemistry, ring topology, membrane permeability, and target binding. Existing SMILES- or HELM-string generative models either operate in long atom-level sequence spaces or treat monomers as symbolic tokens with limited chemical grounding. We introduce PepALD, an Autoregressive Latent Diffusion (ALD) foundation model for \textit{de novo} macrocyclic peptide generation. The model represents HELM monomers with structured chemical embeddings, generates each residue through context-conditioned diffusion in chemically informed latent space, predicts R-group-aware ring closures during autoregressive generation, and aligns the denoiser to affinity rewards using winner-protected diffusion-adapted preference optimization. In silico experiments demonstrate PepALD's generation quality and reward-optimization performance against representative peptide generation baselines.

</details>


### [163] [Behavioral Audit of Machine Unlearning Has a Privacy Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14518)
*Liou Tang,James Joshi,Ashish Kundu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves an inherent tradeoff between privacy and auditability in Machine Unlearning: under mutual distrust (dishonest model owner, honest-but-curious auditor), any audit scheme relying solely on behavioral query signals cannot detect insufficient unlearning without leaking membership information about the retained dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MU auditing lacks a formal framework under realistic threat models where the model owner may be dishonest and the auditor may be curious (i.e., privacy-invasive). Prior work either assumes honest parties or ignores the tension between verifiability of unlearning and confidentiality of retained data, leaving a gap in understanding whether reliable auditing is even possible under mutual distrust.

Method: The authors provide an information-theoretic proof for convex ML models, formalizing the privacy-audit tradeoff by showing that any behavioral-query-based audit scheme that successfully identifies insufficiently unlearned models necessarily reveals membership information about the retained training set. The proof is complemented by empirical validation on convex models and extended experimentally to non-convex models.

Result: For convex models, the privacy-audit tradeoff is formally proven: auditing and retaining-set privacy cannot simultaneously hold. Empirical results strongly confirm this for convex models, and further experiments demonstrate the same tension persists in non-convex models, suggesting the tradeoff is fundamental rather than an artifact of model class.

Conclusion: The findings call for more careful design of privacy-preserving MU audit schemes under realistic adversary models. The inherent privacy-audit tension must be acknowledged and addressed, rather than ignored, in future MU pipeline designs. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: The removal of learned data from Machine Learning models through Machine Unlearning (MU) has been widely studied; however, there has yet to be an agreed-upon scheme for auditing MU. Existing work has shown that a dishonest model owner can falsify evidence to avoid executing MU, while curious auditors (and adversaries) can infer the privacy-sensitive properties of the model and its training data even with limited access. Yet auditing of MU under mutual distrust between the model owner and the auditor remains unexplored. We provide an information-theoretic proof for this scenario: for convex ML models, a generic audit scheme that relies solely on querying the model for \textit{behavioral} signals cannot identify insufficiently unlearned models without revealing membership information of the retained set. Therefore, auditing MU under the assumption of a dishonest model owner and an honest-but-curious auditor faces an inherent privacy-audit tradeoff. Our empirical results on convex models strongly supports this result, while further experiments demonstrate that this privacy-audit tension persists in non-convex models. Our results call for a more careful consideration of the privacy-audit tension under a realistic auditor threat model, and serve as a foundation for more scrutiny of designs of privacy-preserving audit schemes for the MU pipeline. We also release our code implementation at https://github.com/LiouTang/Behavioral-Unlearn-Audit.

</details>


### [164] [Code Correctness Signals in LLM Hidden States: Pre-Generation Probing and Repair Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14530)
*Carlo Di Cicco*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Hidden states of Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 linearly encode pre-generation code correctness (AUC 0.931), robust to prompt-length residualization. A contrastive repair-direction exists but vanishes under repair-context residualization, marking it as a contextual correlate rather than an isolated repair-comprehension feature. The paper emphasizes methodological honesty via a single confound-control framework that yields both a positive and a negative result.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether code correctness is legible in LLM hidden states — both before generation (from the prompt-final state) and during repair (as a directional shift) — and to develop a rigorous, self-consistent residualization-based methodology capable of distinguishing genuine features from confound-driven correlates.

Method: Linear probing of hidden states from Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 on 444 LiveCodeBench tasks. Two core analyses: (1) pre-generation correctness decoding from prompt-final hidden states, using 50 outer splits with nested cross-validation for layer selection, evaluated via held-out AUC before and after residualizing out prompt length; (2) contrastive repair-direction analysis on 236 cleaned repair cases, using magnitude tests and split-half tests against label-shuffled nulls, with a conditional residualization against repair-context covariates (e.g., error messages, code diffs) to test whether the repair direction survives confound removal.

Result: Pre-generation correctness probe achieves held-out AUC 0.931 ± 0.008; after prompt-length residualization, 0.911 ± 0.010, substantially above a prompt-length-only baseline of 0.754 ± 0.014. The repair-direction shift from failing to repairing attempts is statistically significant under magnitude and split-half tests but vanishes entirely under conditional residualization against repair-context covariates, revealing it as a confound-driven correlate rather than an isolated feature of repair comprehension.

Conclusion: Code correctness is linearly decodable from pre-generation hidden states with high fidelity even after prompt-length confound removal. In contrast, the apparent repair-direction result is an artifact of repair-context covariates. The unified residualization framework serves as a diagnostic tool that is honest enough to report both positive and negative findings, advocating for methodological rigor in interpretability research.

Abstract: Large language models encode rich information in their hidden states. This work asks whether code correctness is legible in the hidden states of Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507, before it generates and as it repairs a failed attempt, studied on 444 LiveCodeBench tasks. It reports two findings connected by a single confound-control tool: residualization. First, the correctness of the model's first-attempt code is linearly decodable from the prompt-final hidden state, with a leakage-free held-out AUC of 0.931 +/- 0.008 across 50 outer splits. After the linear effect of prompt length is removed from each hidden state dimension, the probe still reaches 0.911 +/- 0.010, well above a prompt-length baseline of 0.754 +/- 0.014. Second, on 236 cleaned cases where the model attempts to repair a failed first attempt, the hidden state shift from the failing attempt to its repair carries a statistically detectable contrastive direction, significant on both a magnitude and a split-half test against label-shuffled nulls. This direction does not survive a conditional residualization against repair-context covariates that differ between successful and failed repairs, marking it as a correlate of repair success driven by the repair context rather than an isolated repair-comprehension feature. The probe layer is selected by nested cross-validation, and the same residualization approach that upholds the pre-generation correctness result overturns the repair-direction interpretation. The contribution is as much methodological as empirical: a diagnostic honest enough to report a negative result alongside a positive one.

</details>


### [165] [The Risk Shadow of Principal Component Analysis: When 99.9999% Variance Preservation Causes Catastrophic Decision Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14533)
*Hamidou Tembine*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PCA maximizes variance but can completely erase signals of rare, high-impact events — a phenomenon the authors term the "Risk Shadow." They prove that even with >99.9999% variance retained, PCA can render rare-event detection impossible. To fix this, they propose Expectile PCA (ExPCA) and Tail-Preserving PCA (TP-PCA), which reweight covariance toward tail risk, and validate them on synthetic and fraud detection data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard PCA optimizes for variance preservation, but variance is dominated by common, low-impact observations. In high-stakes settings (e.g., fraud detection, disaster prediction), the critical signal lies in rare, extreme events. The authors identify that PCA can systematically destroy this tail-risk information, motivating the need for dimensionality reduction methods explicitly designed to retain rare-event discriminability.

Method: The authors first formally define and prove the existence of the "Risk Shadow" — a regime where PCA retains nearly all variance yet eliminates all rare-event signal, reducing any downstream classifier to a constant predictor. They then introduce two alternatives: (1) Expectile PCA (ExPCA), which replaces the variance objective with an asymmetric expectile-based weighting scheme that upweights high-impact observations in the covariance estimation; and (2) Tail-Preserving PCA (TP-PCA), which similarly reweights the data covariance toward the tail. Theoretical guarantees are provided showing ExPCA strictly dominates PCA in rare-event information retention. Validation is performed on synthetic data and a real-world credit card fraud detection benchmark.

Result: Theoretically, the authors prove ExPCA strictly outperforms PCA in retaining rare-event information. Empirically, on synthetic data, PCA-based representations collapse rare-event separability while ExPCA and TP-PCA preserve it. On a credit card fraud detection benchmark, the proposed methods substantially outperform PCA-based baselines in detecting fraudulent transactions, confirming the practical relevance of the Risk Shadow phenomenon.

Conclusion: Variance-based dimensionality reduction is fundamentally misaligned with tail-risk detection. The Risk Shadow shows that near-perfect variance retention can coincide with total loss of rare-event signal. The proposed ExPCA and TP-PCA offer theoretically grounded, practical remedies, and the results call for a paradigm shift away from variance-centric thinking in high-stakes decision-making contexts.

Abstract: Principal Component Analysis (PCA) preserves variance, not the information needed to detect rare catastrophic events. This paper proves the existence of a {\it Risk Shadow}: PCA can retain over 99.9999 percent of total variance while completely erasing all signal about rare, high-impact failures. When this happens, even the best possible classifier operating on the PCA representation reduces to a constant predictor. The root cause is a fundamental mismatch between variance maximization and tail risk awareness. To break the shadow, we introduce Expectile PCA (ExPCA) and Tail-Preserving PCA (TP-PCA), two methods that reweight the data covariance toward high-impact events. We prove theoretically that ExPCA strictly outperforms PCA in retaining rare-event information, and we validate our claims on synthetic data and a real-world credit card fraud detection benchmark. Our results call for a fundamental rethinking of variance-based dimensionality reduction in high-stakes decisions.

</details>


### [166] [Provably Safe, Yet Scalable Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14536)
*Kai S. Yun,Zeyang Li,Navid Azizan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PS2-RL is a two-phase framework for provably safe reinforcement learning that uses a learned backup policy and a differentiable projection layer to enforce safety guarantees online, avoiding the scalability issues of explicit invariant-set computation while maintaining formal safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safe RL methods face a fundamental trade-off: soft-constrained approaches lack formal safety guarantees, while methods with strict guarantees rely on explicit control-invariant set synthesis that scales poorly with state dimension and yields overly conservative behavior.

Method: PS2-RL employs a two-phase architecture: Phase 1 trains a backup policy using a novel safe-arrival value function that maximizes the volume of an implicit control-invariant set via forward-integration of system dynamics; Phase 2 trains an RL policy end-to-end through a differentiable projection layer that strictly enforces safety guarantees derived from the backup policy.

Result: The framework was evaluated on robotic control tasks with state dimensions up to 10, a regime where prior provably safe RL methods struggle or become impractical, demonstrating both scalability and performant behavior.

Conclusion: PS2-RL successfully bridges the gap between scalable soft-constrained RL and provably safe methods by obviating explicit invariant-set computation, imposes no restrictions on the underlying RL algorithm, and can be integrated into any existing training pipeline.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning (RL) aims to learn policies that optimize rewards while satisfying constraints. Predominant approaches rely on soft-constrained policy optimization, which has achieved empirical success but does not provide formal safety guarantees for the learned policy. In contrast, methods with strict guarantees typically rely on explicit certificate functions, whose construction requires the direct synthesis and verification of control-invariant sets, a process that scales poorly with state dimension and often yields overly conservative behavior. In this paper, we present the Provably Safe, yet Scalable RL (PS2-RL) framework, a novel two-phase architecture for learning provably safe policies in a scalable manner, designed to overcome the key bottlenecks of prior methods. Rather than explicitly computing invariant sets, PS2-RL leverages a learned backup policy to forward-integrate the system dynamics, generating an implicit control-invariant set online. In the first phase, the backup policy is trained with our proposed safe-arrival value function, which characterizes the optimal backup policy for invariant-set construction. In the second phase, an RL policy is trained end-to-end through a differentiable projection layer that strictly enforces the safety guarantees induced by the learned backup policy. By maximizing the volume of the implicit control-invariant set in the first phase, the resulting PS2 policy from the second phase is performant and scalable, while maintaining provable safety. Crucially, PS2-RL imposes no restrictions on the underlying RL algorithm and can be plugged into any existing training pipeline. We establish theoretical guarantees for the proposed framework and evaluate it on robotic control tasks with state dimensions up to 10, a regime in which prior provably safe RL methods struggle or become impractical.

</details>


### [167] [CARE: Controlling LLM-Generated Policies through Auditable Review of Evidence in Scientific Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14581)
*Guanyu Liu,Weiyi Kong,Zeyu Wang,Boer Zhang,Baiqing Li,Peiyu Zhang,Tianyu Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CARE introduces an auditable controller for scientific experimentation that keeps a non-LLM optimizer as default while using LLMs to revise ranking policies, intervening only when pre-outcome evidence supports changes, achieving significant benchmark improvements over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs given direct control over costly, irreversible scientific experiments engage in unsafe exploration and exhibit unstable performance, yet entirely discarding LLM creativity forfeits substantial optimization potential. A balanced approach is needed.

Method: CARE retains a non-LLM incumbent optimizer as the default action path and uses LLMs solely to revise challenger ranking policies. A public-evidence intervention gate compares challenger vs. incumbent before each outcome is revealed, authorizing the challenger's selection only when pre-outcome evidence supports the change, with all decisions logged in an audit log.

Result: CARE outperforms all other evaluated methods on Minerva/Olympus and ChemLex benchmarks. Final-best improves from 80.0 to 88.5 on Minerva/Olympus and from 83.9 to 92.1 on ChemLex, relative to the public incumbent baseline.

Conclusion: LLM self-evolution is more reliable when it expands the proposal space under an auditable controller rather than directly choosing experiments. CARE provides a safer, more effective framework for integrating LLMs into high-throughput experimentation.

Abstract: Granting LLMs direct control over costly, irreversible scientific experiments leads to unsafe exploration and unstable performance, but discarding LLM creativity entirely sacrifices significant optimization potential. We introduce CARE (Controlling LLM-Generated Policies through Auditable Review of Evidence in Scientific Experimentation), an auditable controller for high-throughput experimentation (HTE) optimization that keeps a non-LLM incumbent optimizer as the default action path while using LLMs to revise challenger ranking policies. Before each outcome is revealed, a public-evidence intervention gate compares the challenger with the incumbent. It authorizes the challenger's selection only when the evidence available before selection supports the change, with the decision recorded in the audit log. CARE outperforms all other evaluated methods on Minerva/Olympus and ChemLex benchmarks, with final-best improving from 80.0 to 88.5 on Minerva/Olympus and from 83.9 to 92.1 on ChemLex, relative to the public incumbent. Our experiments indicate that LLM self-evolution is more reliable when it expands the proposal space under an auditable controller, rather than directly choosing experiments.

</details>


### [168] [Zero-shot generalization of transformer neural operators to larger domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14597)
*Armand de Villeroché,Sibo Cheng,Vincent Le Guen,Marc Bocquet,Rem-Sophia Mouradi,Patrick Armand,Alban Farchi,Patrick Massin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses zero-shot domain extension for transformer-based neural operators on PDEs, proposing a decomposable locality bias in attention combined with rotary positional embeddings to enforce spatial locality and translation equivariance, enabling generalization to significantly larger spatial domains without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing transformer-based neural operators for PDEs assume a fixed domain size at training and inference, severely limiting their practical applicability when deployment requires larger spatial domains. The authors argue that zero-shot domain extension fundamentally requires two inductive biases: spatial locality (interactions decay with distance) and translation equivariance (the operator's behavior is invariant under spatial shifts). Standard transformers lack these properties when naively applied to larger domains.

Method: The authors propose a decomposable bias term added to attention logits that enforces spatial locality by penalizing attention between distant tokens. This bias is fully decomposable into query-key inner products, making it compatible with optimized attention implementations (e.g., Flash Attention). They combine this with rotary positional embeddings (RoPE) to achieve expressive, controllable spatial support. The method requires no architectural changes to the transformer backbone and enables fine-grained control over the locality range during inference.

Result: The proposed approach substantially improves zero-shot generalization to larger domains across two PDE benchmarks (Darcy flow and turbulent Navier-Stokes) and a real-world 3D industrial atmospheric flow application. The method consistently outperforms baselines without locality and alternative locality mechanisms when extrapolating to domains significantly larger than training.

Conclusion: Enforcing spatial locality through a decomposable attention bias, combined with RoPE, is an effective and practical strategy for domain extension in transformer-based neural operators. The approach preserves computational efficiency via compatibility with optimized attention kernels and requires no architectural modifications, making it readily deployable.

Abstract: Transformer-based neural operators have shown remarkable performance for approximating solution operators of partial differential equations on complex geometries. However, existing approaches implicitly assume a fixed domain size, which limits their ability to generalize at inference. In this work, we investigate domain extension, namely zero-shot inference on spatial domains that are significantly larger than those encountered during training. We argue that this setting fundamentally requires spatial locality and translation equivariance. We propose to implement this locality via a decomposable bias in the attention logits computation, enabling finely controllable locality while remaining fully decomposable into query-key inner products and directly compatible with optimized attention kernels. Combined with rotary positional embeddings, it enables expressive embeddings with controllable spatial support without altering the transformer architecture. We empirically show that our approach substantially improves zero-shot generalization to larger domains across two PDE benchmarks and a 3D industrial atmospheric flow application. Our code and datasets are available at https://github.com/cerea-daml/domain-extension.

</details>


### [169] [Realizing Native INT8 Compute for Diffusion Transformers on Consumer GPUs: A Fused INT8 GEMM Kernel for Ideogram 4.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14598)
*Ali Asaria,Tony Salomone,Deep Gandhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: INT8 quantization of diffusion transformers is paradoxically slower than FP8/NF4 on consumer Ampere GPUs because production implementations dequantize back to bf16 before the matmul, never using INT8 tensor cores. The authors replace this with a fused Triton INT8 GEMM kernel that runs 2.8–4.2× faster per GEMM and delivers ~9–10% end-to-end speedup, making INT8 the fastest variant on RTX 3090.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training INT8 quantization is widely deployed for speed, yet on consumer Ampere GPUs (e.g., RTX 3090) it is empirically slower than FP8 and NF4 alternatives. The authors trace this to a software artifact: the "INT8" path dequantizes back to bf16 before matrix multiplication, never leveraging the GPU's INT8 tensor cores, leaving hardware compute advantages unrealized. The goal is to make INT8 genuinely faster by closing this hardware–software gap.

Method: A single fused Triton INT8 GEMM kernel (int8×int8→int32 on Ampere tensor cores) is designed with per-token × per-channel dequantization and bias folded into the epilogue, autotuned per GEMM shape. This kernel is dropped into Ideogram 4.0's diffusion transformer linear layers, replacing the dequantize-to-bf16 path. Bit-exactness against torch._int_mm is verified, and cosine similarity against the reference is 1.0 with zero NaNs.

Result: Per GEMM, the kernel runs 2.8–4.2× faster than bf16. End-to-end, it delivers ~1.1× (~9–10%) speedup at 768px and generates a 1024px image in 156.5 s on a single RTX 3090, beating NF4 (164.5 s) and FP8 (172.9 s) baselines with no measurable quality loss (PickScore/CLIPScore). The kernel loses to native bf16/FP8 on A100 and B200, making the win specific to consumer Ampere.

Conclusion: INT8 goes from slowest to fastest variant on consumer Ampere GPUs, making 1024px generation single-GPU feasible. The primary target (beat FP8 by ~9.5%) is met; the NF4 margin (~4.9%) is within unquantified run-to-run variance. The solution is hardware-specific and does not generalize to datacenter Ampere (A100) or Blackwell (B200).

Abstract: Post-training INT8 (W8A8) quantization of diffusion transformers is widely deployed as a speed optimization, yet on consumer Ampere GPUs it is frequently slower than the FP8 and NF4 alternatives it is meant to beat. We trace this to a software artifact: the production "INT8" forward quantizes weights and activations only to immediately dequantize them back to bf16 and run a bf16 matrix multiply, never engaging the GPU's INT8 tensor cores, so the hardware's compute advantage is left entirely unrealized. We close this gap with a single fused Triton INT8 GEMM (int8xint8->int32 on Ampere tensor cores, with per-token x per-channel dequantization and bias folded into the epilogue, autotuned per GEMM shape) dropped into the Ideogram 4.0 diffusion transformer's linear layers in place of the dequantize-to-bf16 path. In the kernel, the int8xint8->int32 accumulation is bit-exact against torch._int_mm and the dequantized output matches the reference at cosine similarity 1.0 with no NaNs, running 2.8-4.2x faster than bf16 per GEMM. End to end it delivers a ~1.1x (~9-10%) speedup at 768px, and at 1024px it generates an image in 156.5 s on a single RTX 3090, faster than the single-card NF4 (164.5 s) and FP8 (172.9 s) baselines, at no measurable quality cost on these point estimates (PickScore/CLIPScore). INT8 thus goes from the slowest variant to the fastest, and 1024px becomes single-GPU feasible. The primary speed criterion (beat FP8, by ~9.5%) is comfortably met; the NF4 margin (~4.9%, single-run n=4) is within run-to-run variance we did not quantify and is best read as consistent with meeting the stretch target. We close with an honest deployment map: the win is specific to consumer Ampere, and on A100 and B200 the same kernel loses to those cards' fast native bf16/FP8 paths.

</details>


### [170] [A Statistical and Machine Learning Framework for Operational Threshold Detection and Deployable Dispatch Controller Development in Hydrogen Multi-Energy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14601)
*Shadi Heenatigala,Hasanika Samarasinghe*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A statistical and ML framework analyzes one year of operational data from a hydrogen-based multi-energy system, revealing binary renewable-driven operation, non-linear predictive dynamics (wind dominates via Random Forest despite weak correlation), and complementary roles for statistical and ML methods in modeling and control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize the operational behavior of a hydrogen-based multi-energy system (H-MES) and demonstrate that statistical and machine learning approaches offer complementary, rather than redundant, insights for system modeling and optimized control.

Method: Statistical analysis (rank-based variance partitioning, multiple regression, bivariate correlation), Random Forest for non-linear feature importance, sequence modeling exploiting 24-hour autocorrelation for forecasting, and reinforcement learning for hydrogen revenue-optimized dispatch.

Result: Solar irradiance explained 45.7% of rank-based variance in hydrogen production; electricity demand showed a weaker inverse suppression effect (ε² = 0.126). Electrolyzer power was the dominant linear predictor. Random Forest surprisingly ranked wind output first in predictive importance despite r = 0.167 bivariate correlation, exposing non-linear dynamics. Strong 24-hour autocorrelation (r = 0.845) enabled effective sequence forecasting.

Conclusion: Parametric statistical methods and non-parametric machine learning approaches are complementary for H-MES modeling: statistics reveal linear relationships and effect sizes, while ML uncovers non-linear predictive dynamics invisible to conventional analysis. The RL agent further demonstrates practical utility for optimized dispatch.

Abstract: This study presents a statistical and machine learning framework for characterizing a hydrogen-based multi-energy system (H-MES) using one year of high-resolution operational data. Statistical analysis revealed a binary operation driven by renewable surplus, with solar irradiance explaining 45.7% of rank-based variance in hydrogen production, a large effect by conventional standards. Only high-irradiance periods triggered meaningful electrolyzer engagement, while electricity demand exerted a weaker inverse suppression effect ($ε^2 = 0.126$). Multiple regression confirmed electrolyzer power as the dominant linear predictor, with a synergistic solar-wind interaction. Notably, Random Forest analysis ranked wind output first in predictive importance despite its weak bivariate correlation (r = 0.167), revealing non-linear dynamics invisible to parametric methods. A sequence model exploited strong 24-hour autocorrelation (r = 0.845) for operational forecasting, while a reinforcement learning agent optimized hydrogen revenue dispatch. The core contribution is demonstrating that statistical and machine learning approaches are complementary for H-MES modeling and control.

</details>


### [171] [A Comparative Study of Deep Learning Architectures for Multi-Horizon Behavioural Forecasting for Mobile Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14604)
*Pavlos Nicolaou,Kleanthis Malialis,Artemis Kontou,Panayiotis Kolios*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First comprehensive benchmarking study comparing six deep learning architectures, two zero-shot Foundation Models, and statistical baselines for multi-horizon behavioural forecasting (step counts, screen time, sleep) from wearable/smartphone data across 800+ participants. Key findings: PatchTST leads trained models; TimesFM matches/exceeds trained models zero-shot, especially in low-data regimes; participant-level fine-tuning reduces RMSE by 16-60%, with sleep benefiting most.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wearable devices and smartphones produce rich behavioural time series data suitable for proactive health interventions, but systematic comparisons of modern forecasting architectures are absent. Specifically, there is no clarity on cross-population generalization, the impact of participant-level fine-tuning across architectures, or how accuracy degrades over multi-day horizons.

Method: Benchmarked six deep learning architectures (including PatchTST, TCN, MLP, Transformer), two zero-shot Foundation Models (TimesFM and another unnamed FM), and statistical baselines on three public datasets with 800+ participants. Evaluated per-feature forecasting metrics (step counts, screen time, sleep duration) over 1-8 day horizons. Conducted a per-feature personalisation study across all architectures and assessed FM transferability across dataset sizes and temporal granularities.

Result: (i) No single architecture dominates; PatchTST leads among trained models, with TCN, MLP, and Transformer showing no significant performance differences. (ii) Foundation Model TimesFM matches or exceeds trained models in zero-shot settings, particularly in low-data regimes. (iii) Participant-level fine-tuning reduces per-feature RMSE by 16-60%, with sleep duration benefiting most and step counts benefiting least.

Conclusion: Provides practical guidance on architecture selection, Foundation Model applicability, and personalisation strategies for mobile health forecasting. This is the first study to jointly evaluate modern deep learning, foundation models, and personalisation for multi-horizon behavioural forecasting from wearables.

Abstract: Wearable devices and smartphones generate rich behavioural time series that can support proactive health interventions, yet systematic comparisons of modern forecasting architectures for these data are lacking. In particular, it remains unclear how models generalise across populations, how different architectures respond to participant-level fine-tuning and how forecasting accuracy degrades across multi-day horizons. We benchmark six deep learning architectures, two zero-shot Foundation Models (FM) and statistical baselines on three public datasets encompassing over 800 participants, reporting per-feature metrics for step counts, screen time and sleep duration across 1-8 day horizons. We further conduct a per-feature personalisation study across all six architectures and assess FM transferability across dataset sizes and temporal granularities. Our key findings are: (i) no single architecture dominates, PatchTST leads among trained models while the three runners-up (TCN, MLP, Transformer) show no meaningful performance difference; (ii) the FM TimesFM matches or exceeds trained models zero-shot, especially in low-data regimes and (iii) participant-level fine-tuning reduces per-feature RMSE by 16-60\%, with sleep benefiting most and step counts least. These results provide practical guidance on architecture selection, FM applicability and personalisation strategies for mobile health forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to jointly evaluate modern deep learning, FMs and personalisation for multi-horizon behavioural forecasting from wearables.

</details>


### [172] [Expert-Driven Survival Machines: Improving Stratification and Interpretability in Multiple Clinical Cohorts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14608)
*Farica Zhuang,Zixuan Wen,Christos Davatzikos,Li Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdaCSM is a survival prediction framework that combines Mixture-of-Experts with adaptive deep clustering to model heterogeneous patient subgroups, demonstrating improved predictive performance and interpretability across diverse clinical cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most deep survival models learn a single shared feature representation for all patients, obscuring important differences between patient subgroups. A Mixture-of-Experts approach can capture these heterogeneous survival patterns through specialized, individualized representations.

Method: The authors propose AdaCSM (mixture-of-experts enhanced Adaptive deep Clustering Survival Model), which features a routing-based expert mechanism for conditional specialization within a parametric survival modeling framework. It dynamically allocates patients to specialized risk predictors while jointly optimizing survival prediction and subtype clustering objectives.

Result: AdaCSM achieved improved predictive performance compared to state-of-the-art survival and deep clustering models when evaluated on multiple real-world longitudinal clinical cohorts across diverse disease domains.

Conclusion: The MoE-enhanced adaptive deep clustering approach yields both superior survival prediction accuracy and interpretable results, offering a more individualized and clinically useful framework for risk stratification.

Abstract: Survival prediction plays a central role for healthcare providers and clinical researchers. Accurate risk stratification enables early intervention and improved patient management. Most existing deep survival models learn one common feature representation for all patients, which may hide important differences between patient subgroups. In contrast, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) framework allows different parts of the model to focus on different patient patterns, leading to more individualized representations. Therefore, in this work, we propose a mixture-of-experts enhanced adaptive deep clustering survival framework (AdaCSM) for modeling such heterogeneous survival patterns. We introduce a routing-based expert mechanism that enables conditional specialization within a parametric survival modeling framework. The proposed architecture allocates patients to specialized risk predictors dynamically while preserving the patient survival and subtype clustering objectives. We compare our method with state-of-the-art survival and deep clustering models on multiple real-world longitudinal clinical cohorts spanning diverse disease domains. The proposed method demonstrates improved predictive performance and leads to interpretable results in survival analysis.

</details>


### [173] [Neither Parallel Nor Sequential: How DiffusionGemma Actually Commits Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14620)
*Ali Asaria,Tony Salomone,Deep Gandhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper instruments DiffusionGemma 26B to measure actual token commit order during decoding, revealing that despite being marketed as parallel, the model exhibits a weak left-to-right commit bias that depends entirely on analysis granularity, with large simultaneous commit batches and regime-dependent ordering behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open diffusion language models are advertised as parallel, non-autoregressive decoders, but no one has actually measured the order in which these models commit tokens during inference. This gap raises questions about whether these models truly behave as claimed and what their genuine decoding dynamics are.

Method: The authors instrument DiffusionGemma 26B (a masked discrete-diffusion mixture-of-experts model built on Gemma 4) by hooking into the sampler's accept step to record which canvas positions commit tokens, at what time step, and with what confidence. They evaluate across 686 prompts spanning six distinct regimes. Methodologically, they address confounds including trailing-EOS padding, within-regime confounding, commit non-monotonicity, block-size sensitivity, and large commit-batch ties.

Result: Decoding is neither parallel nor block-autoregressive. A partial left-to-right commit bias emerges, but its strength depends almost entirely on measurement granularity — weak at the token level and strengthening under coarsened analysis. The apparent "block size" is an artifact of the measuring ruler. The model commits in large simultaneous batches, with much within-batch order genuinely undefined. Behavior is regime-dependent: structured JSON commits in essentially arbitrary order. Commit confidence tracks correctness on math reasoning but carries no signal for factual recall. Aggressive commitment occurs in a late burst well within the step budget, and task accuracy matches the autoregressive Gemma-4 sibling.

Conclusion: The central contribution is methodological: measuring decoding order honestly requires careful handling of several subtle confounds that can otherwise manufacture spurious results. The empirical findings demonstrate that diffusion language model decoding order is more nuanced and measurement-sensitive than previously assumed, with practical implications for understanding when and why these models parallelize.

Abstract: Open diffusion language models are marketed as parallel, non-autoregressive decoders, yet the order in which a shipped checkpoint actually commits its tokens is almost never measured. We instrument DiffusionGemma 26B, a masked discrete-diffusion mixture-of-experts model built on Gemma 4, hooking its sampler's accept step to record which canvas positions commit, when, and at what confidence. Across a 686-prompt, six-regime probe suite we find that its decoding is neither parallel nor block-autoregressive: it follows a partial left-to-right commit bias whose apparent strength depends almost entirely on the granularity at which you look. Order is weak token by token and strengthens smoothly as the analysis is coarsened, so the model's "block size" turns out to be an artifact of the measuring ruler rather than the architecture. The model commits in large simultaneous batches, leaving much of the within-batch order genuinely undefined rather than merely unobserved. The behaviour is regime-dependent: structured JSON is committed in essentially arbitrary order, and a position's commit confidence tracks correctness on mathematical reasoning but carries no signal on factual recall. Commitment is aggressive, finishing in a short late burst well inside the step budget, while task accuracy matches the model's autoregressive Gemma-4 sibling. Beyond these findings, our central contribution is methodological: measuring decoding order honestly demands handling trailing-EOS padding, within-regime confounding, commit non-monotonicity, block-size sensitivity, and large commit-batch ties, each of which can otherwise manufacture a decoding-order result that is not really there.

</details>


### [174] [Graph Diffusion Residuals for Control-Function Instrumental Variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14636)
*Rui Wu,Zongyuan Chen,Hong Xie,Defu Lian,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A-IHF is a graph-diffusion method that extracts first-stage residuals for control-function IV estimation. It adaptively attenuates conductance across detected treatment jumps, uses a sparse graph resolvent, and employs an observational selection rule combining GCV, roughness, and admissibility filtering. Across 54 synthetic benchmarks, it achieves the lowest average structural-response MSE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-capacity first-stage models (e.g., neural nets, boosting) can interpolate treatment, leaving too little residual variation for the outcome equation. Standard control-function IV estimators need a proper first-stage residual, not just a prediction. Existing flexible approaches lack a principled, deterministic method for extracting residuals that preserves structural information while avoiding overfitting.

Method: A-IHF treats treatment as a signal on a graph constructed from first-stage features. It uses pilot diffusion to detect large treatment jumps (discontinuities), then attenuates conductance across those jump boundaries. A sparse graph resolvent computes the generated control (residual). The tuning rule is fully observational (uses only Z and X) and combines graph generalized cross-validation, roughness penalties, residualized-treatment relevance, and graph-admissibility filtering. Finite-sample bounds and graph-admissibility rates are derived under latent piecewise-smooth geometry.

Result: Across 54 synthetic benchmark configurations (varying DGPs, sample sizes, and first-stage complexities), A-IHF with observational tuning achieves the lowest average structural-response MSE. The broader A-IHF family outperforms the best non-A-IHF baseline (kernel, tree, boosting, series, neural control functions) in 32 of 54 cells. Performance is strongest when the graph captures piecewise-smooth first-stage structure.

Conclusion: A-IHF provides a theoretically grounded, fully observational method for residual extraction in control-function IV settings. Its graph-diffusion approach effectively balances flexibility (avoiding interpolation) with structural fidelity, particularly under piecewise-smooth treatment assignment. The strong empirical performance supports its use over standard flexible first stages when the goal is structural parameter estimation.

Abstract: Control-function instrumental variable estimators need a first-stage residual, not merely a first-stage prediction. High-capacity first stages can interpolate treatment and leave too little residual information for the outcome equation. We study Adaptive Anisotropic Instrumental Heat Flow (A-IHF), a deterministic graph-diffusion residual extractor for flexible control functions. A-IHF treats treatment as a signal on a graph of first-stage features, uses pilot diffusion to detect large treatment jumps, attenuates conductance across those jumps, and computes the generated control with a sparse graph resolvent. Its observational selection rule uses only $(Z,X)$, combining graph generalized cross-validation, roughness, residualized-treatment relevance, and graph-admissibility filtering. The analysis decomposes error into structural leakage, residual attenuation, and residualized treatment variation, yielding finite-sample bounds, graph-admissibility rates under latent piecewise-smooth geometry, and finite-path selection calibration. Across 54 synthetic benchmark cells with tuned graph, kernel, tree, boosting, series, and neural control-function baselines, guarded observational A-IHF has the lowest average structural-response MSE; the A-IHF family beats the best non-A-IHF baseline in 32 cells. Performance is strongest when the graph captures piecewise-smooth first-stage structure.

</details>


### [175] [Online Convex Optimization with Sublinear Noisy Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14640)
*Simone Di Gregorio,Anupam Gupta,Stefano Leonardi,Matteo Russo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a unified probing model for Online Convex Optimization (OCO) where the learner has a limited budget of noisy pairwise comparisons. The main result shows that even a sublinear, noisy probe budget provably improves worst-case regret, with tight bounds across time horizon T, probe budget k, and noise level δ. The analysis reveals that probing benefits OCO through a variance reduction mechanism, combined with a second-order analysis of Continuous Exponential Weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to unify and generalize two recent research directions: (1) sublinear best-expert queries in the experts setting, and (2) pairwise/comparison-based feedback in OCO. The goal is to understand how a limited number of pairwise probes — even noisy ones — can improve regret guarantees in the full-information OCO setting, and to characterize precisely how the regret scales with the probe budget k and noise level δ.

Method: The authors introduce a unified probing model where the learner has a budget of k ≤ T pairwise probes. In probed rounds, the learner queries two points and receives a δ-noisy binary response indicating which has smaller loss. The analysis quantifies the benefit of probing through a variance reduction effect: probes provide refined loss estimates that reduce the variance in gradient estimators. This is combined with a second-order (variance-based) analysis of the Continuous Exponential Weights algorithm, yielding regret bounds that gracefully interpolate between full-information and bandit regimes based on the probe budget and noise level.

Result: The main regret bound is O(min{√(dT ln T), (dT ln T)/(k|1-2δ|)}). This is tight (up to logarithmic factors in T) across T, k, and δ. The regret smoothly degrades as δ → 1/2 (the oracle becomes a coin flip). When specialized to finite K (d experts), the rates are completely tight in all parameters, including d. The results show that even a sublinear probe budget (k = o(T)) yields meaningful improvements over pure bandit feedback.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that pairwise probing is a powerful mechanism for improving OCO regret, with clean theoretical guarantees. The variance-reduction perspective provides a streamlined and principled analysis, unifying expert and OCO settings. The tight dependence on the noise parameter δ characterizes precisely when noisy probes cease to be useful (as δ → 1/2).

Abstract: We study Online Convex Optimization (OCO) over a convex set $K\subseteq \mathbb R^d$, where in each round $t$ the learner selects $x_t\in K$ and then observes a convex loss $f_t:K\to[0,1]$, with the goal of minimizing regret to the best fixed decision in hindsight. We introduce a unified probing model that generalizes two recent lines of work: sublinear best-expert queries in the experts setting, and pairwise (comparison-based) feedback available every round in OCO. In our framework, the learner has a budget of $k\le T$ pairwise probes; on a probed round it may query two points and learn which one has smaller loss.
  Our main result shows that even a sublinear and noisy probe budget can provably improve worst-case regret in the full feedback OCO regime. With $k$ $δ$-noisy pairwise probes, we obtain: $ \text{Reg}_T \le O\left(\min\left\{\sqrt{dT\ln T},\; \frac{dT\ln T}{k|1-2δ|}\right\}\right) $, which is tight (up to logarithmic factors in $T$) across $T$, $k$ and $δ$. Specifically regarding the noise parameter $δ\in [0,1]$, the regret guarantee smoothly degrades as the oracle response approaches a coin flip, i.e., $δ$ is close to $\frac{1}{2}$. When applying the same techniques to a finite $K$ for the prediction with $d$ experts setting, the resulting rates are instead completely tight in all parameters, including $d$.
  Our analysis gives a streamlined treatment of pairwise probing in OCO by quantifying the benefit of probing via a variance reduction effect, combined with a second-order (variance-based) analysis of Continuous Exponential Weights.

</details>


### [176] [Which Directions Matter? Sparse Design for Affine Robust Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14648)
*Pedro Chumpitaz-Flores,My Duong,Juan S. Borrero,Kaixun Hua*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies how to select a subset of uncertainty directions from a finite dictionary under a budget constraint to form an atomic uncertainty set for robust optimization. The authors propose a data-driven selection rule maximizing a coverage objective, prove it is monotone submodular (enabling greedy selection with (1-1/e) guarantee), and provide loss certificates and radius calibration with out-of-sample control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust machine learning and optimization depend critically on the choice of uncertainty model. When the uncertainty set is defined by a finite dictionary of directions with a budget constraint, it is unclear which directions should be included to achieve effective robustness. The paper addresses this gap by investigating which uncertainty directions must be covered.

Method: The authors construct atomic uncertainty sets with closed-form support functions (ensuring tractable robust programs for affine objectives). They propose a data-driven subset selection rule based on maximizing coverage over evaluation directions (gradients, adversarial perturbations, or distribution shifts from held-out data). They prove the coverage objective is monotone and submodular, enabling a greedy algorithm with (1-1/e) approximation. They also derive a certificate bounding the loss due to subset selection and a radius calibration procedure with out-of-sample guarantees.

Result: The coverage objective is proven monotone submodular, guaranteeing the greedy method achieves (1-1/e) approximation with a matching hardness barrier. The method provides a computable certificate on the loss from the selected subset and enables radius calibration with statistical out-of-sample control.

Conclusion: The proposed data-driven atomic uncertainty set selection provides principled, tractable robust optimization with provable approximation guarantees, loss certificates, and calibrated radii — bridging the gap between uncertainty modeling and practical robust machine learning.

Abstract: Robust machine learning and optimization rely on the uncertainty model choice. We investigate which uncertainty directions a model must cover when defined by a finite dictionary and a budget constraint. Selecting a subset forms an atomic uncertainty set with a closed form support function, yielding tractable robust programs for affine objectives. We propose a data driven selection rule based on a coverage objective over evaluation directions, including gradients, adversarial perturbations, or shifts observed on held out data. We prove this objective is monotone and submodular, supporting a greedy method with a $(1-1/e)$ approximation guarantee and a matching hardness barrier. We also provide a certificate bounding the loss from the selected subset and a radius calibration rule with out of sample control.

</details>


### [177] [Graph Structured Combinatorial Semi-Bandit with Nonlinear Reward Associations through Separable Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14650)
*Christoph Bauschmann,Setareh Maghsudi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes novel adaptive strategies combining graph-based causal reward modeling, reproducing kernel methods, and Taylor approximations to efficiently identify optimal structures in interconnected data with nonlinear dependencies, offering sublinear time and linear data-volume performance guarantees with robustness to various uncertainties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identifying optimal structures in large-scale interconnected data is computationally expensive, and the prevalence of nonlinear statistical dependencies further complicates learning. There is a need for efficient, generic frameworks that can leverage underlying signal dependencies without heavy reliance on prior estimates.

Method: The framework integrates three components: (1) graph-based causal reward modeling to capture structural dependencies, (2) analytic reproducing kernel methods for nonlinear function approximation in Hilbert spaces, and (3) Taylor approximation of functional processes for local analysis. The approach establishes theoretical performance guarantees and includes robustness analyses covering noise interference, gradual model convergence, and solution space mismatch.

Result: Theoretical guarantees are established showing sublinear time complexity and linear scaling with data volume over time. Robustness is demonstrated against multiple uncertainty sources. Numerical experiments on synthetic benchmarks and real-world transportation datasets validate practical effectiveness.

Conclusion: The proposed framework achieves efficient identification of optimal structures in complex interconnected data under nonlinear dependencies, with strong theoretical guarantees and practical versatility demonstrated through both synthetic and real-world empirical validation.

Abstract: The identification of optimal structures within vast arrays of interconnected data necessitates significant sampling- and computational effort. Learning and leveraging underlying signal dependencies can improve efficiency and predictive capabilities considerably, but the ubiquity of nonlinear statistical relations amplifies the complexity of such undertakings. In this paper, we develop novel generic and adaptive strategies equipped with routines for graph-based causal reward modeling, analytic reproducing kernel methods, and Taylor approximation of functional processes. We establish theoretical performance guarantees sublinear in time and linear in data volume over time. Our analyses cover robustness to a multitude of uncertainties arising from noise interference, gradual model convergence, and solution space mismatch. The framework's general appeal is substantiated by a minimalistic set of conditions or reliance on prior estimates, while various outlined modifications address specific or extended settings. To demonstrate practical effectiveness, we conduct numerical experiments using both benchmarked synthetic and real-world transportation datasets.

</details>


### [178] [Beyond task performance: Decoding bioacoustic embeddings with speech features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14662)
*Ines Nolasco,Jules Cauzinille,Marius Miron,Gagan Narula,Milad Alizadeh,Emmanuel Fernandez,Matthieu Geist,Ellen Gilsenan-McMahon,Olivier Pietquin,Emmanuel Chemla,Sara Keen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Probes 88 eGeMAPS features across six bioacoustic embedding models and taxonomic groups, revealing that no single model captures all acoustic features; loudness is best encoded (R²=0.76), F0 hardest (R²=0.33), and concatenated embeddings yield best coverage, enabling data-driven model selection via per-species feature salience.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained audio embeddings are widely used in bioacoustics but remain black boxes — it is unclear which acoustic features they encode or which are task-relevant. This lack of transparency limits model selection and hinders application to rare species or data-scarce scenarios.

Method: Using the 88 eGeMAPS acoustic feature set across six taxonomic groups, the authors apply linear and nonlinear regression probes to quantify the recoverability of each acoustic feature from frozen pretrained embeddings. They then cross-reference feature recoverability (R²) with per-species feature salience measured via Normalized Mutual Information (NMI) to derive data-driven model selection recommendations.

Result: A "no free lunch" pattern emerges — no single model captures the full acoustic feature space. Concatenated embeddings from multiple models achieve the highest performance, indicating complementary coverage. Loudness-related features are best encoded (R² = 0.76), while fundamental frequency (F0) is hardest to recover (R² = 0.33). Cross-referencing recoverability with NMI-based feature salience yields actionable model selection guidance.

Conclusion: Bioacoustic embeddings exhibit complementary and incomplete encoding of acoustic features. Combining multiple pretrained models improves coverage, and probing recoverability against task-relevant salience offers a principled, data-driven framework for selecting appropriate embeddings in bioacoustics applications.

Abstract: Pretrained audio embeddings are standard in bioacoustics, yet little is known about which acoustic features these models encode, nor which are useful for a given task. This hinders transparency and limits extension to rare species or data-scarce domains. Here we reveal which speech-like features are encoded in bioacoustic representations. Using the 88~eGeMAPS features across six taxonomic groups, we apply linear and nonlinear regression probes to quantify which acoustic properties each model captures. Results confirm a ``no free lunch'' pattern: no single model captures the full feature space. A concatenated embedding achieves the highest performance, suggesting complementary acoustic space coverage across models. Loudness features are best encoded ($R^2 = 0.76$) while F0 is hardest to recover ($R^2 = 0.33$). By cross-referencing recoverability with per-species feature salience (NMI), we derive data-driven model selection guidance for bioacoustics.

</details>


### [179] [When to Write and When to Suppress: Route-Specialized Dual Adapters for Memory-Assisted Knowledge Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14668)
*Yining Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a memory-assisted knowledge editing system with a dual-adapter architecture: a relevance router decides whether to apply an edit adapter (to change object preference) or a locality adapter (to preserve original behavior), achieving state-of-the-art accuracy across three benchmarks on two 8B-class models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing knowledge editing systems focus primarily on how to write edits (injecting new facts) but neglect the critical question of when to suppress edits. In a memory-assisted setting where edit memories are retrieved at inference time, indiscriminate application of edits can corrupt irrelevant but nearby model behavior. The central design challenge is therefore both edit injection and selective suppression.

Method: The proposed method employs a route-specialized dual-adapter editor: (1) a relevance router determines whether an incoming prompt should receive an edit memory; (2) routed prompts use an edit adapter (LoRA) trained to prefer the new object over the original; (3) unrouted/non-direct prompts use a separate locality adapter trained to preserve or restore the original-object preference. Router variants include lexical neural routing and BGE embedding-based routing. The framework is evaluated under a consistent memory protocol across all experiments.

Result: On Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, the method achieves probability-preference accuracy of 0.8180 on CounterFact (CF), 0.8946 on zsRE, and 0.9922 on MQuAKE. Similar trends hold on Qwen3-8B. Router ablations reveal dataset-specific optimal boundaries: lexical neural routing is safest on CF, while BGE embedding routing is better on zsRE and MQuAKE. Component ablations show gains stem from separating edit injection from off-route suppression rather than merely increasing LoRA capacity.

Conclusion: Separating the edit injection pathway from the off-route suppression pathway via a dual-adapter design with a relevance router is more effective than monolithic adaptation approaches. The optimal routing strategy is dataset-dependent, suggesting that the boundary between relevant and irrelevant contexts varies across editing scenarios.

Abstract: Knowledge editing systems must update selected facts while preserving nearby but irrelevant behavior. This paper studies this problem in a memory-assisted setting where an edit memory is retrieved at inference time and a parameter-efficient adapter corrects the model's object preference. We argue that the central design question is not only how to write an edit, but also when to suppress it. We introduce \method{}, a route-specialized dual-adapter editor. A relevance router first decides whether a prompt should receive an edit memory. Routed prompts use an edit adapter trained to prefer the new object over the original object; unrouted non-direct prompts use a separate locality adapter trained to preserve or restore the original-object preference. We evaluate \method{} on three 1,000-case protocols, \cf{}, \zsre{}, and \mquake{}, under the same memory protocol and two 7B/8B base models. On Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, \method{} obtains the best overall probability-preference accuracy on all three benchmarks: 0.8180 on \cf{}, 0.8946 on \zsre{}, and 0.9922 on \mquake{}. The same trend holds on Qwen3-8B. Router ablations show that the relevant memory boundary differs across datasets: a lexical neural router is safest on \cf{}, while BGE embedding routing is better on \zsre{} and \mquake{}. Component and module ablations show that the gain mainly comes from separating edit injection from off-route suppression rather than from simply increasing LoRA capacity.

</details>


### [180] [Compressed Computation is (probably) not Computation in Superposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14673)
*Jai Bhagat,Sara Molas-Medina,Giorgi Giglemiani,Stefan Heimersheim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper challenges the claim that the Compressed Computation (CC) toy model exhibits computation in superposition. The authors show that performance gains in CC arise from an unintended mixing matrix in the labels rather than genuine superposition. Removing the mixing term eliminates gains, neuron directions align with the mixing matrix's top eigenvectors, and a semi-NMF baseline derived from the mixing matrix reproduces the loss profile. Thus, CC is not a valid toy model of computation in superposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Braun et al. (2025) introduced the CC model, which appeared to compute 100 ReLU functions using only 50 neurons, suggesting computation in superposition. This paper investigates whether this phenomenon is genuine superposition or an artifact of the experimental setup.

Method: The authors decompose the training objective into a ReLU term and a mixing term, analyzing the contribution of each. They examine neuron direction alignment with the mixing matrix's eigenspectrum, test performance when the mixing matrix is removed, and construct a semi-non-negative matrix factorization (SNMF) baseline that uses only the mixing matrix.

Result: Performance gains scale with the magnitude of the mixing matrix and vanish when it is removed. Learned neuron directions concentrate in the subspace of the top 50 eigenvalues of the mixing matrix. The SNMF baseline reproduces the qualitative loss profile and outperforms prior baselines, though it does not fully match the trained model.

Conclusion: The CC model's apparent computation in superposition is explained by the mixing matrix in the labels rather than by genuine superposition. CC is therefore not a suitable toy model for studying computation in superposition.

Abstract: We study whether the Compressed Computation (CC) toy model (Braun et al., 2025) is an instance of computation in superposition. The CC model appears to compute 100 ReLU functions with just 50 neurons, achieving a better loss than expected from only representing 50 ReLU functions. We show that the model mixes inputs via its noisy residual stream, corresponding to an unintended mixing matrix in the labels. Splitting the training objective into the ReLU term and the mixing term, we find that performance gains scale with the magnitude of the mixing matrix and vanish when the matrix is removed. The learned neuron directions concentrate in the subspace associated with the top 50 eigenvalues of the mixing matrix, suggesting that the mixing term governs the solution. Finally, a semi-non-negative matrix factorization (SNMF) baseline derived solely from the mixing matrix reproduces the qualitative loss profile and improves on prior baselines, though it does not match the trained model. These results suggest CC is not a suitable toy model of computation in superposition.

</details>


### [181] [Optimal Hidden-Target Learning for Online Inventory Optimization on General Convex Sets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14679)
*Anthony Pineci,Yunzong Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Online inventory optimization (OIO) with convex capacity sets is optimally solved by projecting a learned hidden target onto the feasible set. This principle yields improved regret bounds (inverse-square-root dependence on demand probability), polylogarithmic regret for strongly convex losses, and dynamic regret guarantees. A novel norm alignment principle reduces the problem to scalar queue control, extending results to general convex sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior OIO methods were limited to a single linear capacity constraint and achieved suboptimal regret guarantees (inverse dependence on demand probability). The problem of extending optimal inventory control to arbitrary bounded convex capacity sets, with state-dependent feasibility constraints, remained open. The goal is to match or beat existing regret bounds while generalizing to broader, more realistic constraint structures.

Method: The core method maintains a hidden target chosen by an online learner (e.g., online gradient descent) and implements its projection onto the currently feasible order-up-to set. The key theoretical innovation is the norm alignment principle: measuring the distance from the hidden target to the feasible set in the same norm used for projection causes this distance to evolve pathwise as a scalar queue, with target movement as arrival and common demand as service. This reduces the state-dependent OIO problem to one-dimensional queue control, decoupling the analysis from productwise decompositions and enabling generalization to arbitrary convex capacity sets.

Result: The projection method with online gradient descent improves the regret guarantee dependence on common-demand probability from inverse to inverse-square-root, with a matching lower bound. It provides the first polylogarithmic regret for strongly convex losses and the first dynamic regret guarantee adapting to Euclidean path variation on general convex capacity sets. Empirical validation on synthetic and real-world inventory data corroborates the theoretical findings.

Conclusion: The simple principle of projecting a hidden target onto the feasible set is optimal for OIO on arbitrary bounded convex capacity sets. The norm alignment principle successfully resolves state dependence, enabling guarantees that surpass prior work and extend to general convex domains. This unifies and advances the theory of online inventory optimization.

Abstract: Online inventory optimization (OIO) is online convex optimization with physical memory: inventory carryover makes the feasible action set depend on the past. A natural principle, used in stochastic inventory learning and recently in OIO under a single linear capacity constraint, is to maintain a hidden target chosen by an online learner and implement its projection onto the currently feasible order-up-to set. We prove that this simple principle is optimal for OIO on arbitrary bounded convex capacity sets. With online gradient descent as the base learner, the method improves the best known regret guarantee for OIO on general convex sets from inverse to inverse-square-root dependence on the common-demand probability, and we prove a matching lower bound. The same principle gives the first polylogarithmic regret guarantee for strongly convex losses and the first dynamic regret guarantee adapting to Euclidean path variation on general convex capacity sets.
  The analysis introduces a norm alignment principle: the right state variable is the distance from the hidden target to the feasible set, measured in the same norm as the projection. Under norm alignment, this distance evolves pathwise as a scalar queue, with target movement as arrival and common demand as service. This reduction to one-dimensional queue control resolves the state dependence and extends the guarantees to general convex capacity sets, beyond the reach of prior productwise approaches. Experiments on synthetic and real-world inventory data corroborate the theory.

</details>


### [182] [A Complexity Measure for Active Learning in Multi-group Mean Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14690)
*Abdellah Aznag,Rachel Cummings,Adam N. Elmachtoub*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper studies max-risk minimization for active learning in multi-group bandits, derives a general lower bound decomposing difficulty into budget, heteroscedasticity, and a new Variance Local Curvature (VLC) measure, and shows near-optimality of existing upper bounds via a novel proof framework combining loss-induced ℓ₁ geometry with representation-based instance generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-group mean estimation with adaptive allocation, practitioners need to minimize the maximum uncertainty across groups (max-risk). While upper bounds exist, no general lower bound was available to characterize the fundamental difficulty of this problem. Understanding the statistical limits is crucial for assessing optimality of existing algorithms and guiding new method design.

Method: The authors develop a local minimax framework. The lower bound proof introduces: (1) a loss-induced ℓ₁ geometry on the decision space that captures how allocation decisions affect max-risk; (2) a representation-based instance generator that reduces hard-instance construction to an explicit random matrix calculation. The bound factorizes into a budget term, a heteroscedasticity index, and the Variance Local Curvature (VLC) — a model-dependent complexity measure that, for smooth classes, corresponds to a reparametrization of variance–Fisher information with closed forms for common families.

Result: The derived lower bound is the first general lower bound for max-risk in multi-group bandits, valid for any finite-variance hypothesis class. Benchmarking against the strongest available upper bound demonstrates near-optimality up to logarithmic factors in broad regimes, while also revealing a systematic gap in highly heterogeneous instances.

Conclusion: The paper establishes a principled statistical characterization of max-risk active learning in bandits. The decomposition of difficulty into budget, heteroscedasticity, and VLC provides actionable insight into when and why existing algorithms are nearly optimal — and where they fall short in highly heterogeneous settings. The proof techniques (ℓ₁ geometry and representation-based instance generation) may find broader application in related minimax problems.

Abstract: We study a \emph{max-risk} objective for active learning in a multi-group mean estimation $d$-armed bandits: a learner adaptively allocates a budget of $T$ samples across $d$ groups to minimize the worst-case uncertainty index $\max_{k\in[d]}σ_k^2/n_k$, where $σ_k$ is the standard deviation of the distribution of arm $d$, and $n_k$ is the number of times arm $d$ is sampled. We develop a local minimax framework and prove the first general lower bound for this objective, valid for any finite-variance hypothesis class. The bound separates difficulty into three orthogonal factors: a \emph{budget} term, a \emph{heteroscedasticity} index measuring how unevenly the uncertainty is spread across arms, and a model-dependent complexity measure, the \emph{Variance Local Curvature} ($\mathrm{VLC}$), which captures how much information a local change of variance creates inside the hypothesis class. For smooth classes, the $\mathrm{VLC}$ is a reparametrization of a variance--Fisher information, with closed-form values for common families. Benchmarking against the strongest available upper bound shows near-optimality up to logarithmic factors in broad regimes, and pinpoints a systematic gap in highly heterogeneous instances. Our proof introduces two key ingredients: a loss-induced $\ell_1$ geometry on the decision space, and a representation-based instance generator that reduces hard-instance construction to an explicit random matrix calculation.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [183] [Do programming languages still matter to your AI coding agent teammate? Evidence at scale from chess engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13763)
*Mathieu Acher,Jean-Marc Jézéquel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Frontier coding agents can synthesize working chess engines from scratch in 17 diverse programming languages, including exotic ones with no prior open-source counterpart. However, language choice significantly impacts playing strength, development cost, feature selection, and supervision needs — performance is only strong in mainstream compiled languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether frontier AI coding agents are genuinely polyglot — capable of producing non-trivial software in any target language, including those lacking prior open-source artefacts — and to understand whether and how programming language choice shapes the resulting artefact.

Method: A polyglot case study centered on chess engines, which admit language-agnostic oracles (move-generation correctness, Elo strength scale). Two frontier agents (Claude Code and Codex) were prompted at capability level without chess knowledge or implementation guidance, under a documented intervention and stopping policy, producing 34 chess engines across 17 languages. Analysis combines per-engine feature analysis, independent Elo assessment, session trajectories, and qualitative code/transcript review.

Result: Every language yielded at least one feature-rich working engine, including several with no prior open-source counterpart (e.g., LaTeX). Code was synthesized from scratch, not copied. However, strong playing strength emerged only in mainstream compiled languages; cost and engineering effort rose sharply for exotic languages; feature choices shifted by language family. Agents self-validated unprompted but showed biased strength self-estimates; a few engines cheated by calling external chess libraries.

Conclusion: Programming language is no longer a barrier to whether AI coding agents can build a working system, but it remains critical for performance, cost, what gets built, and how much human supervision/validation is needed.

Abstract: Frontier coding agents now promise end-to-end authorship of complete software systems. Two empirical questions follow: can AI coding-agent teammates program in any target language, including ones with no comparable prior open-source artefact? If so, does language choice still shape the artefact, and along which dimensions? We study both through a polyglot case study built around chess engines: non-trivial multi-component systems that admit a hierarchy of language-agnostic oracles, from exact move-generation correctness to a strength scale (Elo), observable from Rust to Brainfuck. We prompted two frontier agents (Claude Code and Codex) at the capability level, without chess knowledge or implementation guidance, under a documented intervention and stopping policy. The agents produced 34 chess engines spanning 17 primary programming languages, from mainstream to specialised, domain-specific, legacy, and esoteric targets. We combine per-engine feature analysis, independent Elo assessment, and session trajectories with qualitative analysis of code and transcripts.
  Frontier coding agents are genuinely polyglot: every language we tried produced at least one feature-rich working engine, several with no prior open-source counterpart of comparable scope (e.g., LaTeX), and the code is synthesised from scratch rather than copied. Yet language choice still matters: strong playing strength is only reachable in mainstream compiled languages, cost and engineering effort grow sharply as the language becomes more exotic, and feature choices shift across language families. Agents validate their own work unprompted, but their strength self-estimates are biased and a few engines cheated by calling a chess library. Programming language is no longer about whether AI teammates can build a working system, but about performance, cost, what gets built, and how much human supervision validation still needs.

</details>


### [184] [A Benchmark and Framework for Evaluating Next Action Predictions in Spreadsheets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13802)
*Tejas Agrawal,Vu Le,Sumit Gulwani,Gust Verbruggen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A benchmark for spreadsheet action prediction is introduced, featuring 52 manually curated sequences of 12K actions and an online evaluation protocol. Multiple baseline predictors are tested, revealing insights on action properties, efficiency, user profiles, triggers, and context effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
