Motivation: Predictive code completion accelerates programming, yet analogous auto-completion for spreadsheets is virtually absent despite their ubiquity. The gap stems from two challenges: lack of edit histories in public spreadsheet corpora and the complex space of spreadsheet actions (spatial, temporal, composite).

Method: (1) Dataset creation: 52 sequences of 12K actions manually curated by recreating spreadsheets from public corpora, seeded by parametrized heuristics and LLM refinement. (2) Online evaluation protocol: after each user action, a prediction is made, accepted/rejected; upon acceptance, future actions are updated, repeating until the target spreadsheet is achieved. Baselines include zero-shot LLMs, fine-tuned SLMs, and classical models.

Result: The benchmark yields empirical findings on: properties of saved actions and false positives, prediction efficiency, effect of user profiles, effect of triggers, and effect of context, across multiple baseline predictor types.

Conclusion: The proposed benchmark provides a structured framework for evaluating spreadsheet action prediction, revealing actionable insights for building effective auto-completion systems in spreadsheet environments.

Abstract: Predictive code completion greatly accelerates how quickly developers work. In spreadsheets, despite being much more common, such auto-completion features are virtually non-existent. To address this gap, we introduce a benchmark for systems that observe a sequence of user actions in a spreadsheet and predict future actions. Two challenges are (1) the absence of edit histories in public spreadsheet corpora and (2) the complex space of spreadsheet actions (spatial, temporal, composite). To address (1), we manually curate 52 sequences of 12K actions that recreate spreadsheets from public corpora, seeded by parametrized heuristics and LLM refinement. To address (2), we propose an online evaluation that expects a prediction after each user action, accepts or rejects that prediction, updates the future actions upon acceptance, and repeats this until the target spreadsheet is obtained. We use multiple baseline predictors (including zero-shot LLMs, fine-tuned SLMs, and classical models) and analyze different properties that our benchmark teaches us, including but not limited to: properties of saved actions and false positives, efficiency, effect of user profiles, effect of triggers, and effect of context.

</details>


### [185] [An Empirical Study of Gemini 3 for Detecting Natural Language Test Smells in Manual Test Cases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13804)
*Keila Lucas,Rohit Gheyi,Márcio Ribeiro,Fabio Palomba,Luana Martins,Elvys Soares*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates using a large language model (GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW) with a whole-test-case prompt-based strategy to detect seven types of test smells in natural language manual test cases, finding it outperforms previously evaluated small language models (GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, PHI-4-14B) on 100 Ubuntu test cases, while also revealing test smells are pervasive (~1 smell/step).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual testing is essential but manual test cases often contain test smells (ambiguity, redundancy, missing checks) that degrade reliability, maintainability, and reproducibility. Existing detection methods rely on manually engineered rules that fail to generalize or scale across heterogeneous test suites, motivating the exploration of LLM-based approaches for automated, scalable smell detection.

Method: Employed a prompt-based, whole-test-case analysis strategy using GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW (an LLM) to detect seven smell types across 100 real-world Ubuntu manual test cases. Unlike prior SLM approaches that analyze individual steps in isolation, the whole-test-case strategy allows the model to consider inter-step relationships and dependencies. Performance was compared against three SLMs: GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B.

Result: GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW outperformed all three SLMs in test smell detection. The LLM also produced actionable explanations to help practitioners revise test cases. Additionally, the study found test smells are pervasive, averaging nearly one smell per test step, underscoring the need for automated quality support for manual testing artifacts.

Conclusion: Contemporary LLMs employing whole-test-case analysis can effectively detect test smells in manual test cases, surpassing SLMs. The pervasiveness of test smells (~1 per step) highlights a strong need for scalable, automated quality assurance tools for manual testing. Future work should build on these findings to integrate such detection into testing workflows.

Abstract: Manual testing, in which testers follow natural language instructions to validate system behavior, remains essential for uncovering issues that are difficult to capture with automation. However, manual test cases often contain test smells, quality issues such as ambiguity, redundancy, or missing checks that reduce reliability, maintainability, and reproducibility. Existing detection approaches largely depend on manually engineered rules and thus struggle to generalize and scale across heterogeneous test suites. In our previous work, we assessed the feasibility of using Small Language Models (SLMs) for test smell detection by evaluating GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B on test steps from 143 real-world Ubuntu test cases, covering seven smell types. PHI-4-14B achieved the best performance. In this article, we investigate whether a contemporary Large Language Model (GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW) available at the time of the study can identify test smells in natural language manual test cases using a prompt-based, whole-test-case analysis strategy. Unlike approaches that analyze individual test steps in isolation, our approach evaluates complete test cases, enabling the model to consider relationships and dependencies among test steps. We evaluate the approach on 100 Ubuntu test cases covering seven test smell types and compare its performance against previously evaluated SLMs, including GEMMA-3-4B, LLAMA-3.2-3B, and PHI-4-14B. Our results show that GEMINI-3-PRO-PREVIEW outperforms the SLMs, while producing actionable explanations that can help practitioners revise manual test cases for greater clarity and consistency. We also find that test smells are pervasive in practice, with nearly one detected test smell per step on average, highlighting the need for scalable and automated quality support for manual testing artifacts.

</details>


### [186] [A Principled Framework for Safe Algorithm Updates in Automated Insulin Delivery Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13882)
*Thomas Screven,Ziqiang "Joe" Zhu,Deniz Cengiz,Rayhan A. Lal,Korey K. Hood,Samuel T. King*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a two-part framework for evaluating the safety of software updates in automated insulin delivery (AID) systems, classifying bugs (factual, heuristic, computational) and assessing clinical equivalence using shadow execution, mechanistic in silico simulation, and data-driven replay. Validated on Trio's oref algorithm port from JavaScript to Swift, the framework demonstrated near-identical glycemic outcomes and low mismatch rates, meeting clinical equivalence thresholds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AID algorithms require continuous software updates and bug fixes, but in co-adapted systems—where users fine-tune settings around existing algorithmic behavior—even beneficial bug fixes can paradoxically worsen glycemic control. No systematic, principled framework exists for evaluating the safety of AID algorithm updates before deployment.

Method: A two-part framework: (1) Bug classification taxonomy (factual, heuristic, computational) with distinct management strategies, validated by porting Trio's oref algorithm from JavaScript to a bug-fixed Swift implementation; (2) Clinical equivalence assessment using error analysis on paired glucose values via three complementary methods: mechanistic in silico simulation, data-driven replay simulation, and shadow execution on 736,480 invocations from eight Trio users.

Result: Mechanistic simulation showed near-identical outcomes: Time in Range (84.9% vs. 84.9%), Glycemia Risk Index (23.5% vs. 23.9%), with >99% of paired glucose in Parkes Error Grid Zones A+B. Shadow execution mismatch rates were low across oref components (iob: 0.43%, autosens: 1.22%, determineBasal: 0.07%, meal: 0.01%), with clinically meaningful differences in only 0.03% of iob invocations. Data-driven replay also met clinical equivalence thresholds (>99% Zones A+B).

Conclusion: The framework integrates bug-fixing principles with multi-method clinical evaluation to systematically assess AID update safety. It is system-agnostic, applicable to all widely used open-source AID systems, and case studies underscore the necessity of systematic remediation for factual and computational bugs.

Abstract: Background: AID algorithms require ongoing software updates and bug fixes. In co-adapted systems, where users tune settings around existing algorithmic behavior, bug fixes can paradoxically disrupt glycemic control. No principled framework evaluates the safety of AID algorithm updates.
  Methods: Our two-part framework classifies bugs and evaluates the clinical equivalence of AID system software updates. Bugs are classified as factual, heuristic, or computational, each with distinct management strategies. Classifications were validated from porting Trio's oref algorithm from Javascript to a bug-fixed Swift implementation. We compared implementations using shadow execution on 736,480 invocations from eight Trio users. The second component assesses clinical equivalence with error analysis on paired glucose values, applied to both Trio implementations using mechanistic in silico and data-driven replay simulation.
  Results: In mechanistic in silico simulation, the Swift and Javascript implementations produced nearly identical Time in Range (84.9% vs. 84.9%) and Glycemia Risk Index (23.5% vs. 23.9%), with more than 99% of paired glucose in Parkes Error Grid Zones A and B, meeting our clinical equivalence threshold. Shadow execution showed low mismatch rates in oref components (iob 0.43%, autosens 1.22%, determineBasal 0.07%, meal 0.01%), with clinically meaningful differences in 0.03% of iob invocations. Data-driven replay simulations of bugs revealed more than 99% of downstream paired glucose in Parkes Error Grid Zones A and B, also meeting our clinical equivalence threshold.
  Conclusions: Our framework integrates bug-fixing principles with multi-method clinical evaluation to assess AID algorithm update safety. It is system-agnostic and applicable to all widely used OS-AID systems, with case studies highlighting the need for systematic remediation of factual and computational bugs.

</details>


### [187] [Bayesian-Calibrated Detection of Hallucinated Package Imports in AI-Assisted Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13918)
*Lom M. Hillah,Jean-Marc Richard,Ryan Hasnaoui*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Bayesian calibration layer for detecting hallucinated package imports in LLM-generated code. Unlike binary detectors, it outputs Beta-posterior probabilities using a 3-category epistemic taxonomy for priors. It augments standard registry checks with PyPI metadata signals to catch registered-but-suspicious packages. Evaluated on 1,734 Python snippets across six models, the method reproduces binary baseline detections while adding well-calibrated metadata-channel flags, validated via McNemar test, Expected Calibration Error, and Brier score.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing slopsquat detectors emit binary flag/no-flag decisions, lacking calibrated uncertainty quantification. Binary registry-only checks miss registered-but-suspicious packages, which represents a realistic post-LLM-emission attacker regime where malicious packages are actually registered on PyPI. A risk-aware, probabilistically principled detection primitive is needed for downstream CI gate integration with configurable thresholds.

Method: The authors propose a Bayesian calibration layer that replaces binary decisions with Beta-posterior probabilities. Priors are assigned via a 3-category epistemic taxonomy: empirically calibrated, constructively argued, or engineering-judgement-traced. Detection combines two channels: (1) a standard 200/404 registry lookup and (2) PyPI metadata signals (package age, release count, author descriptor, summary) to surface suspicious-but-registered packages. The posterior output is designed for direct consumption by CI gates with principled thresholding.

Result: Evaluated on a merged corpus of 1,734 Python snippets (189-prompt BigCodeBench slice + 100-prompt niche-library stress-test set) across six models (Claude-Sonnet-4.6, Mistral-Large, DeepSeek-v4-pro, DeepSeek-R1, Mistral Codestral, Meta CodeLlama). Against a re-implemented binary baseline (Mahmud et al. inspired), the calibrated layer reproduces strict-registry detections and adds well-calibrated metadata-channel flags. Calibration assessed via McNemar paired test (asymmetry), flagged-subset Expected Calibration Error, and full-corpus Brier score.

Conclusion: The Bayesian calibration layer provides a principled upgrade from binary to probabilistic slopsquat detection, capturing the realistic threat of registered-but-suspicious packages that binary registry detectors miss. The risk-aware primitive enables configurable CI-gate thresholds with well-calibrated uncertainty.

Abstract: We present a Bayesian calibration layer for slopsquat detectors -- those that flag hallucinated package imports in code produced by large language models (LLMs). Where existing pipelines emit binary decisions (flag / do-not-flag), our layer emits a Beta-posterior probability per detection, derived from a 3-category epistemic taxonomy that explicitly classifies each prior as empirically calibrated, constructively argued, or engineering-judgement-traced. Beyond the primary 200/404 registry channel, the calibrated layer exploits PyPI metadata signals -- package age, release count, author descriptor, summary -- to surface registered-but-suspicious packages that a binary registry detector misses, which is the realistic post-LLM-emission attacker regime. The resulting risk-aware primitive is directly consumable by downstream CI gates and supports principled threshold decisions across detection rules. We evaluate the calibration on a merged corpus of 1,734 Python snippets -- a stratified 189-prompt BigCodeBench slice plus a 100-prompt niche-library stress-test set, generated across a six-model panel spanning four cloud models (Claude-Sonnet-4.6, Mistral-Large, DeepSeek-v4-pro, DeepSeek-R1) and two local open-weight code models (Mistral Codestral, Meta CodeLlama). Against a re-implemented binary baseline inspired by Mahmud et al. -- which shares its registry oracle with our ground truth and therefore serves as a degenerate upper bound rather than a genuine competitor -- the calibrated layer reproduces the strict-registry detections and introduces well-calibrated additional flags on the metadata channel. We assess detector asymmetry with a McNemar paired test and calibration with both a flagged-subset Expected Calibration Error and a strictly proper full-corpus Brier score.

</details>


### [188] [LLM Agents Can See Code Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14061)
*Dongjian Ma,Silin Chen,Yufei Yang,Yulin Shi,Yanfu yan,Xiaodong Gu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic study on whether visual repository representations (folder hierarchies, dependency graphs) improve LLM-based coding agents for issue resolution. A vision-only approach hurts accuracy and increases cost, but supplementing text with visual graphs reduces token consumption by up to 26% while maintaining or improving accuracy, especially during fault localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human developers leverage visual structures like folder hierarchies and dependency graphs to navigate large codebases, yet LLM-based coding agents typically consume repositories as flat text. With the emergence of multimodal LLMs (MLLMs), the authors ask whether agents can benefit from visual repository representations — a question not previously studied systematically.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic empirical study evaluating four recent MLLMs on repository-level issue resolution. They compare three setups: (1) strictly vision-only (repository as images), (2) standard text-only, and (3) a hybrid approach integrating visual structure graphs as a supplementary modality alongside text interfaces. Metrics include issue-resolution accuracy and token consumption.

Result: Vision-only degrades accuracy and increases token cost because agents lack symbolic detail and compensate with repeated visual queries. The hybrid text-and-vision approach reduces input token consumption by up to 26% while maintaining or improving accuracy. Visualization proves most beneficial during fault localization and when agents autonomously control exploration depth.

Conclusion: A practical hybrid design combining text and visual repository representations is the most effective path forward for next-generation coding agents, rather than purely visual or purely textual interfaces.

Abstract: Coding agents powered by large language models have demonstrated strong performance on software engineering tasks. Yet most agents consume repositories almost entirely as text, which differs from how human developers use visual structure such as folder hierarchies and dependency relationships to orient themselves in large codebases. With multimodal large language models (MLLMs), it is an open question whether agents can effectively benefit from visual representations of repositories. This paper presents the first systematic empirical study of visual repository representations for LLM-based agents on repository-level issue resolution. We evaluate four recent multimodal models. Our results show that a strictly vision-only setup degrades accuracy and increases token cost, because agents lack sufficient symbolic detail and compensate with repeated visual queries. In contrast, integrating visual graphs of repository structure as a supplementary modality alongside standard text interfaces helps agents understand structure more efficiently: input token consumption decreases by up to 26% while issue-resolution accuracy is maintained or improved. Visualization is most useful during fault localization and when the agent autonomously controls exploration depth. These findings point to a practical hybrid text-and-vision design for next-generation coding agents.

</details>


### [189] [FastContext: Training Efficient Repository Explorer for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14066)
*Shaoqiu Zhang,Maoquan Wang,Yuling Shi,Yuhang Wang,Xiaodong Gu,Yongqiang Yao,Rao Fu,Shengyu Fu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: FastContext introduces a specialized exploration subagent that decouples repository exploration from task solving in LLM coding agents, using dedicated 4B–30B models trained via bootstrapping and task-grounded rewards. Integrated into Mini-SWE-Agent, it boosts resolution rates up to 5.5% while cutting token consumption by up to 60%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Repository exploration is a major bottleneck in LLM coding agents: locating relevant code consumes large token budgets and pollutes the solver's context with irrelevant snippets. Existing agents use the same model for both exploration and solving, leaving noisy exploratory traces in the history.

Method: FastContext is a dedicated exploration subagent invoked on demand. It issues parallel tool calls and returns focused context (concise file paths and line ranges). The underlying exploration models (4B–30B parameters) are bootstrapped from strong reference-model trajectories and refined using task-grounded rewards targeting three objectives: broad first-turn search, multi-turn evidence gathering, and precise citation generation.

Result: Across SWE-bench Multilingual, SWE-bench Pro, and SWE-QA benchmarks, integrating FastContext into Mini-SWE-Agent improved end-to-end resolution rates by up to 5.5% while reducing coding-agent token consumption by up to 60%, with only marginal overhead.

Conclusion: Repository exploration can be effectively separated from solving and handled by specialized, smaller models, yielding both higher accuracy and dramatically lower token costs.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) coding agents have achieved strong results on software engineering tasks, yet repository exploration remains a major bottleneck: locating relevant code consumes substantial token budget and pollutes the agent's context with irrelevant snippets. In most agents, the same model explores the repository and solves the task, leaving exploratory reads and searches in the solver's history. We present FastContext, a dedicated exploration subagent that separates repository exploration from solving. Invoked on demand, FastContext issues parallel tool calls and returns concise file paths and line ranges as focused context. FastContext is powered by specialized exploration models spanning 4B--30B parameters. We bootstrap them from strong reference-model trajectories and refine them with task-grounded rewards for broad first-turn search, multi-turn evidence gathering, and precise citation generation. Across SWE-bench Multilingual, SWE-bench Pro, and SWE-QA, integrating FastContext into Mini-SWE-Agent improves end-to-end resolution rates up to 5.5\% while reducing coding-agent token consumption up to 60\%, with marginal overhead. These results show that repository exploration can be separated from solving and handled effectively by specialized models. Code and data: https://github.com/microsoft/fastcontext

</details>


### [190] [tap: A File-Based Protocol for Heterogeneous LLM Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14445)
*Minseo Kim*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: tap is a file-based collaboration protocol enabling heterogeneous LLM agents (Claude/Codex) to collaborate on a shared codebase without shared memory or common runtime, using git worktrees for isolation and file-based message persistence paired with real-time notifications. In a 27-day self-applied operation, heterogeneous model pairs detected defects in 69.8% of reviews vs. 53.1% for homogeneous pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-agent software development systems assume a common runtime, central conversation server, or same API family, preventing LLM agents from different vendors from directly exchanging messages from their own execution environments while collaborating on shared code. This motivates a protocol that decouples agent collaboration from any specific runtime or vendor assumption.

Method: tap introduces a two-tier file-based collaboration protocol: (1) Tier 1 — file communication via markdown files with metadata that preserve original messages and are inspectable along a file path; (2) Tier 2 — real-time notification paths for Claude and Codex. Work isolation is achieved through separate git worktrees. The file-first design ensures message availability even if real-time notification fails or a receiver restarts. The protocol was self-applied over 27 days across 37 generations, yielding 209 pull requests and 717 operational artifacts.

Result: Analysis of 375 review artifacts showed heterogeneous model pairs recorded at least one defect or requested change in 69.8% of reviews, compared to 53.1% for homogeneous pairs — a 16.7 percentage point improvement, suggesting that combining heterogeneous models broadens review perspectives.

Conclusion: tap demonstrates that file-based message preservation combined with real-time notification is viable in a real production repository, and that heterogeneous model/execution environment combinations can improve defect detection in multi-agent code review. The protocol is released as open-source npm package @hua-labs/tap (v0.5.2).

Abstract: Existing multi-agent software development systems have proposed many forms of agent collaboration, including role-based collaboration and automated code review. However, many systems assume a common runtime, a central conversation server, or the same API family. Under these assumptions, LLM agents from different vendors cannot easily exchange messages directly from their own execution environments while dividing development and review work on a shared codebase. This paper presents tap, a file-based collaboration protocol that allows Claude (Anthropic) and Codex (OpenAI) to collaborate on one codebase without shared memory or an identical runtime. The core of tap is a file-first design that preserves markdown files with metadata as original messages, combines a file inspection path (file communication, Tier 1) with real-time notification paths for Claude and Codex (real-time communication, Tier 2), and isolates work through separate git worktrees. Even if real-time notification fails or a receiver restarts, the message file remains available and the same content can be inspected again. In a 27-day, 37-generation self-applied operation where tap was used to develop and review itself, we collected 209 tap-related pull requests and 717 operational artifacts. An analysis of 375 review artifacts showed that the share of reviews recording at least one defect or requested change was 69.8% for heterogeneous model pairs and 53.1% for homogeneous model pairs. These results show that tap, which combines file-based message preservation with real-time notification, operates in a real production repository, and that combining heterogeneous models and execution environments can broaden review perspectives. tap is distributed as the open-source npm package @hua-labs/tap (v0.5.2).

</details>


### [191] [Simulating Students' Java Programming Errors with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14113)
*Ali Keramati,Jie Cao,Iman Mohammadi,Mark Warschauer,Yang Shi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs can simulate realistic student programming errors, evaluating five models across three prompting strategies on 74K+ Java submissions. LLMs generate diverse errors functionally indistinguishable from real student mistakes, but alignment varies by model and problem difficulty, with higher-struggle problems yielding less student-like errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Obtaining representative student errors for newly designed programming tasks is slow and costly, requiring extensive classroom deployment. The authors explore whether LLMs can serve as scalable, low-cost proxies for generating authentic-looking student mistakes to accelerate programming education research and tool development.

Method: Using the CodeWorkout dataset (74,000+ unique Java submissions across 37 problems), the authors evaluate five LLMs under three prompting strategies: Input-Output (IO), Chain-of-Thought (CoT), and iterative Self-Refine. Performance is assessed quantitatively along diversity (range of distinct error patterns) and alignment (similarity to authentic student mistakes), stratified by problem struggling level. A blinded expert annotation study (N=401) qualitatively compares synthetic vs. authentic errors.

Result: All models generate diverse errors, but alignment to human submissions diverges significantly across models — Claude Sonnet 4 achieves the most balanced performance. The blinded expert study (N=401) confirms synthetic errors are functionally indistinguishable from authentic ones. Higher-struggling-level problems elicit more diverse but less student-like errors, revealing a diversity-alignment trade-off.

Conclusion: LLMs can serve as scalable proxies for simulating student programming errors, but with important trade-offs between diversity and alignment that vary by problem difficulty. Design considerations are needed when integrating synthetic errors into teachable agents, intelligent tutoring systems, and learning analytics pipelines.

Abstract: Understanding student errors in the programming is a cornerstone of programming education, yet obtaining a representative set of student errors for any newly designed task remains slow and costly, since authentic submissions only accumulate after extensive classroom deployment. This paper explores whether large language models (LLMs) can serve as scalable proxies for students by simulating realistic logical errors in code submissions. Using the CodeWorkout dataset of 74,000+ unique student Java submissions across 37 problems, we evaluate five LLMs under three mainstream prompting strategies: Input-Output (IO), Chain-of-Thought (CoT), and iterative Self-Refine. We assess performance along two key dimensions: diversity (the range of distinct error patterns) and alignment (alignment with authentic student mistakes), and examine how these vary by struggling level of programming tasks. Our quantitative findings reveal that while all models generate diverse errors, their alignment to human submissions diverges: Claude Sonnet 4 achieves the most balanced performance. In addition, we conducted a blinded expert annotation study (N = 401) comparing synthetic and authentic errors. This qualitative analysis confirms that the generated errors are functionally indistinguishable from authentic student errors. Moreover, higher-struggling-level problems elicit more diverse but less student-like errors. These results highlight trade-offs in using LLMs to simulate human learners and suggest design considerations for integrating synthetic errors into teachable agents, intelligent tutoring systems, and large-scale learning analytics.

</details>


### [192] [Investigating Metamorphic Fuzz Oracle Enhancement via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14164)
*Ruixiang Qian,Ding Yang,Zengxu Chen,Yuxuan Gao,Chunrong Fang,Chao Zhang,Zhenyu Chen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents MetaFOE, an LLM-based framework that automatically generates and integrates metamorphic-based oracles into existing fuzz drivers. Evaluated on OSS-Fuzz drivers, it generated 3,475 metamorphic relations (77.3% applicable), implemented 12,351 meta drivers (6,228 valid), achieved 18.7% average edge coverage improvement, and triggered 1,528 unique crashes, demonstrating the viability of LLM-driven metamorphic oracle enhancement for greybox fuzzing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing greybox fuzz drivers rely predominantly on crash-based oracles for security testing, which overlooks library functionality (e.g., logical errors, semantic bugs) and limits overall bug detection capability. There is a need for enhanced oracles that can detect non-crashing functional defects, but constructing metamorphic-based oracles requires substantial domain knowledge, making manual effort prohibitive.

Method: The authors propose MetaFOE, an LLM-based framework for Metamorphic-based Fuzz Oracle Enhancement (MFOE). It automatically: (1) derives metamorphic relations (MRs) from target library interfaces using LLMs with various prompt strategies; (2) generates meta drivers that integrate these MRs as oracles into existing fuzz drivers; and (3) validates and executes the augmented drivers. Three modern LLMs and five prompt strategies were explored.

Result: MetaFOE generated 3,475 MRs (77.3% applicable) and implemented 12,351 meta drivers (6,228 valid, ~50.4% validity rate). Valid meta drivers improved edge coverage by an average of 18.7% after three hours of fuzzing and triggered 1,528 unique crashes, substantially outperforming crash-only oracles.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that metamorphic-based oracle enhancement is effective for improving greybox fuzzing outcomes and that LLMs can feasibly automate the otherwise knowledge-intensive MFOE process, offering valuable insights for advancing fuzzing methodologies.

Abstract: Fuzz drivers are essential components of greybox fuzzing, as they encapsulate target interfaces, define test spaces, and largely determine fuzzing effectiveness. Existing fuzz drivers typically rely on crash-based oracles for security testing, overlooking library functionality and limiting bug detection capability.
  In this paper, we present the first study on metamorphic-based fuzz oracle enhancement (MFOE), which augments existing fuzz drivers with metamorphic-based oracles derived from metamorphic relations (MRs). Since constructing and integrating such oracles requires substantial domain knowledge, automating MFOE is challenging. To address this challenge, we propose MetaFOE, an LLM-based framework that automatically generates and integrates metamorphic-based oracles.
  We evaluate MetaFOE on OSS-Fuzz drivers using three modern LLMs and five prompt strategies. MetaFOE generates 3,475 MRs, of which 77.3% are applicable, and implements 12,351 meta drivers, with 6,228 being valid. After three hours of fuzzing, the valid meta drivers improve edge coverage by an average of 18.7% and trigger 1,528 unique crashes. Our results demonstrate both the effectiveness of metamorphic-based oracle enhancement and the feasibility of using LLMs to automate MFOE, providing valuable insights for advancing greybox fuzzing.

</details>


### [193] [Evaluating LLMs for Obfuscation Detection and Classification in Android Apps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14233)
*Luca Ferrari,Marco Alecci,Jordan Samhi,Tegawende' F. Bissyande',Jacques Klein,Mariano Ceccato,Luca Verderame*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can detect obfuscation in Android apps through semantic reasoning, without relying on handcrafted rules, signatures, or dedicated training. Evaluations on both a controlled benchmark and real-world Google Play apps examine prompt design, model selection, and decision thresholds across open-weight and proprietary LLMs, comparing results with existing SAST-based detection approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Android developers increasingly use code obfuscation to protect intellectual property, but this simultaneously hinders static analysis and vulnerability detection tools. Existing obfuscation detection methods depend on handcrafted heuristics, engineered features, or task-specific pipelines that struggle to generalize across evolving obfuscation strategies. This motivates exploring whether LLMs' semantic reasoning capabilities can offer a more generalizable, training-free alternative.

Method: The authors conduct a large-scale empirical study evaluating off-the-shelf LLMs (both open-weight and proprietary) on obfuscation detection. The methodology involves: (1) a controlled benchmark with an app obfuscated using multiple techniques, (2) a real-world dataset of Android apps from Google Play, (3) systematic variation of prompt design, model selection, and decision thresholds, and (4) comparative analysis against existing SAST-based obfuscation-detection approaches.

Result: The paper presents empirical findings on how different LLMs perform on obfuscation detection tasks under varying prompt designs and decision thresholds, compared against traditional SAST-based methods. Specific quantitative results are not detailed in the abstract, but the study examines broader implications and limitations of LLM-based reasoning for Android security analysis.

Conclusion: The study discusses the broader implications and limitations of applying LLMs to Android security analysis, particularly for obfuscation detection. It highlights that while LLMs offer a promising, flexible alternative to heuristic-based methods, there remain important constraints and open challenges when deploying them in practical security contexts.

Abstract: Android applications (apps) developers increasingly rely on code obfuscation techniques to hinder reverse engineering and protect intellectual property. However, obfuscation also reduces the effectiveness of static analysis and vulnerability detection tools, creating challenges for Android security analysis. Existing approaches for detecting obfuscation in Android apps predominantly rely on handcrafted heuristics, engineered features, or task-specific learning pipelines, which may struggle to generalize across evolving obfuscation strategies.
  This paper presents a large-scale empirical study investigating the capability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to detect obfuscation in Android apps through semantic reasoning. Our study evaluates whether off-the-shelf LLMs can identify obfuscated code without relying on handcrafted rules, predefined signatures, or dedicated model training. The empirical evaluation is conducted on both a controlled benchmark containing an app obfuscated with multiple techniques and a real-world dataset of Android apps collected from Google Play. The study further examines the impact of prompt design, model selection, and decision thresholds across several open-weight and proprietary LLMs. Finally, the analysis compares LLM-based reasoning with existing SAST-based obfuscation-detection approaches and discusses the broader implications and limitations of applying LLMs to Android security analysis.

</details>


### [194] [I'm Sorry Driver, I'm Afraid I Can't Do That: Appraising the Safety of LLMs within Automotive Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14327)
*Shaun Feakins,Ibrahim Habli,Kim Littler,Robert Palin*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper evaluates the safety assurance challenges of integrating LLMs into automotive control tasks, identifies conceptual and standards-based obstacles (ISO 21448, ISO/PAS 8800), grounds findings in a Talk2Drive case study, presents a safety argument exposing current limitations, and proposes forward-looking assurance mechanisms for LLM-related hazardous events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are rapidly being integrated into automotive settings, but their use in real-time safety-critical control tasks raises unresolved safety assurance concerns. The authors aim to systematically identify and articulate these challenges to guide responsible deployment.

Method: The paper conducts a structured appraisal combining: (1) conceptual analysis of the upstream-downstream assurance dilemma (general-purpose LLMs developed by large AI labs deployed into specific vehicle architectures); (2) mapping of concrete engineering and LLM-specific challenges against existing standards (ISO 21448 SOTIF and ISO/PAS 8800); and (3) an experimental case study using the open-source Talk2Drive repository to ground findings and construct an explicit safety argument.

Result: Current frameworks face significant efficacy limitations for safety-critical contexts. Conceptual challenges include the dual assurance burden across upstream development and downstream deployment. Concrete challenges span fundamental engineering constraints (e.g., latency per ISO 21448) and LLM-specific issues (e.g., alignment failures per ISO/PAS 8800). The Talk2Drive case study demonstrates these limitations in practice, with the safety argument making explicit where existing solutions fall short.

Conclusion: Despite technical exploration and operationalization of LLMs in automotive contexts, significant assurance gaps remain. The authors propose potential assurance mechanisms for LLM-related hazardous events as a path forward, emphasizing the need to bridge the gap between general-purpose LLM development and safety-critical automotive deployment.

Abstract: This paper appraises recent frameworks within AI development to integrate LLMs into control tasks in automotive contexts from the perspective of safety assurance. This work has built upon the rapid integration of LLMs across automotive settings. However, we find that at present, these frameworks face significant challenges, limiting their efficacy in real-time safety-critical contexts. Firstly, we consider conceptual challenges, including the fact that deployers are faced with a dual challenge, wherein they must assure a model which has been developed upstream, i.e. as general-purpose tools by the large AI labs, in a downstream context, i.e. into specific vehicle architectures. Secondly, we consider concrete challenges from across existing standards. We show that there are currently both fundamental engineering constraints covered in ISO21448, such as latency, and novel LLM-specific issues, such as alignment-related issues covered in ISO/PAS8800. We ground both examples in a concrete introductory, experimental case study exploring an existing open-source repository, Talk2Drive. We present a safety argument in order to make explicit the limitations of existing solutions. Nonetheless, given that the use of LLMs in automotive contexts is being explored at a technical level and operationalised, we propose potential assurance mechanisms for LLM-related hazardous events going forward.

</details>


### [195] [No Accidental Software Agent First Canonical Code for Human Code Entropy Reduction and 30 to 500 times Lower Frontier Model Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14357)
*Jepson Taylor*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes "agent-first canonical code," a proof-carrying framework that rewrites software into behavior-equivalent canonical forms. The core idea is quotienting code by behavioral equivalence under a declared oracle, aiming for a ~100-fold reduction in the all-in cost of verified correct changes. Preliminary experiments with Qwen2.5-Coder-14B show that canonical trajectories are learnable and suppress forbidden-language markers, though behavior preservation and scaling economics remain unproven.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier coding models waste capacity learning accidental entropy from human repositories — framework churn, naming drift, generated-source ambiguity, dependency rituals, CI dialects, and human-oriented review customs — rather than focusing on program behavior. Valuable operational signals (tests, incidents, edge cases) are entangled with this noise. The goal is to separate behavioral essence from accidental complexity to enable more efficient, verifiable software development by AI agents.

Method: The proposed substrate — agent-first canonical code — employs several constructs: canonical behavior profiles, typed change algebra, proof lanes, constrained edit grammars, semantic patch cells, runtime negative memory, and proof-carrying change objects. The central mechanism quotients software by behavioral equivalence under a declared oracle, collapsing equivalent encodings into governed representatives with explicit evidence and proof obligations. Verification targets amortized cost per verified correct change across all dimensions (source, context, reasoning, tools, security, provenance, review, defects, etc.). Preliminary validation uses QLoRA fine-tuning on Qwen2.5-Coder-14B with 64,088 canonical trajectories.

Result: Preliminary QLoRA experiments demonstrate that 64,088 canonical trajectories are learnable by Qwen2.5-Coder-14B and that the fine-tuned model suppresses tested forbidden-language markers. However, these experiments do not establish behavior preservation, scaling economics, or verified-change cost reduction. The reported reduction bands (~100-fold all-in cost reduction) are presented as hypotheses and planning targets for supported routine-product distributions, not as measured results.

Conclusion: The contribution is a falsifiable research program centered on minimum functional description length and verified-change cost. The proposed "No-Accident Horizon" represents a limit where removable accident entropy decreases until only residual novelty, evidence, governance, risk, and future optionality dominate. For supported routine-product distributions, this yields a defensible planning target of ~100-fold all-in cost reduction — explicitly not a guarantee for all software. The framework is positioned as testable rather than proven.

Abstract: Frontier coding models may spend substantial capacity learning not only program behavior, but also accidental entropy in human repositories. Such repositories contain valuable signals: tests, incidents, migrations, edge cases, product judgment, and operational history. These signals are entangled with framework churn, naming drift, generated-source ambiguity, dependency rituals, CI dialects, weak proof routes, and human-oriented review customs. We propose agent-first canonical code, a proof-carrying substrate that rewrites routine product software into canonical behavior profiles, typed change algebra, proof lanes, constrained edit grammars, semantic patch cells, runtime negative memory, and proof-carrying change objects.
  The core hypothesis is that quotienting software by behavior equivalence under a declared oracle can collapse equivalent encodings into governed representatives with explicit evidence and proof obligations. The endpoint is amortized cost per verified correct change, including source, context, reasoning, tools, verification, security, provenance, review, failed loops, defects, and foundry cost under a common oracle. Reported reduction bands are hypotheses, not measured frontier results. The proposed limit is a No-Accident Horizon: removable accident decreases until residual novelty, evidence, governance, risk, and future optionality dominate. For supported routine-product distributions, this gives a defensible planning target near 100-fold all-in cost reduction, not a guarantee for all software. Preliminary QLoRA experiments on Qwen2.5-Coder-14B show that 64,088 canonical trajectories are learnable and suppress tested forbidden-language markers, but do not establish behavior preservation, scaling economics, or verified-change cost. The contribution is a falsifiable program centered on minimum functional description length and verified-change cost.

</details>


### [196] [Security Threats and Their Impact on Blockchain Interoperability: Identification and Countermeasures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14554)
*Shawn M. Reynolds,Hassan Reza*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper systematically identifies, classifies, and proposes countermeasures for security threats in blockchain interoperability systems, organizing them into five categories: core blockchain attacks, network attacks, interoperability-specific attacks, social engineering, and code vulnerabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The lack of comprehensive security mechanisms in blockchain interoperability has already led to asset losses exceeding hundreds of millions of dollars, yet no systematic threat taxonomy exists to guide secure design and evaluation.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic threat identification and classification study, analyzing attack surfaces for each identified threat across five defined categories and proposing corresponding defensive strategies, resulting in a structured taxonomy.

Result: A comprehensive threat taxonomy covering five categories: (1) core blockchain attacks, (2) network attacks, (3) interoperability-specific attacks, (4) social engineering, and (5) code vulnerabilities including smart contract weaknesses. Each threat is paired with its attack surface analysis and proposed countermeasures.

Conclusion: The resulting taxonomy provides a structured foundation for designing and evaluating secure blockchain interoperability solutions, addressing a critical gap in the field.

Abstract: Blockchain interoperability enables independent blockchain systems to communicate and exchange assets across heterogeneous networks. However, the lack of comprehensive security mechanisms remains a critical weakness -- one that attackers have already exploited to cause hundreds of millions of dollars in asset losses. This paper presents a systematic identification and classification of security threats facing interoperable blockchain systems, along with corresponding countermeasures for each. We organize threats into five categories: (1) core blockchain attacks, (2) network attacks, (3) interoperability-specific attacks, (4) social engineering, and (5) code vulnerabilities, with particular attention to smart contract weaknesses. For each identified threat, we analyze its attack surface and propose effective defensive strategies. The resulting taxonomy provides a structured foundation for designing and evaluating secure blockchain interoperability solutions.

</details>


### [197] [When Errors Become Narratives: A Longitudinal Taxonomy of Silent Failures in a Production LLM Agent Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14589)
*Wei Wu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A longitudinal study of an LLM agent system in production reveals a taxonomy of silent failures, with the most dangerous being "fail-plausible" errors where the LLM transforms failures into convincing, fluent narratives. Most silent failures are caught by human observation, not automated tests/audits, and audits function as regression engines rather than prediction engines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM agent systems increasingly operate as long-lived autonomous runtimes, understanding how they fail silently — without actionable error signals reaching humans — is critical for building reliable, trustworthy systems. The authors aim to systematically characterize these silent failure modes through empirical study of a real production system.

Method: An eight-week longitudinal observational study of a personal-assistant agent runtime in continuous production since March 2026, with ~40 scheduled jobs, 8 LLM providers, a tool-governance proxy, and a knowledge-base memory plane. 22 incidents were documented with full root-cause postmortems. The system was defended by 4,286 unit tests and 827 governance checks.

Result: A five-class mechanism-oriented taxonomy of silent failures: (A) environment/platform quirks, (B) design-assumption mismatches, (C) error swallowing/dilution, (D) chained hallucination and fabrication, (E) operational omission/forensic blind spots. Class D ("fail-plausible") is unique to LLM systems — the LLM transforms errors into fluent, plausible narratives. Key findings: ~70% of silent failures caught by human user-view observation, not tests/audits; retrospective audit of 15 incidents found 0% ex-ante prevention but 87% regression blocking; incident latency (13 hours to 60 days) correlates with failure mechanism, not code complexity, with longest-lived failures residing in seams between components.

Conclusion: The authors propose a defense framework and design principles for building agent systems whose failures are "loud, attributable, and boring." Audits serve as regression engines, not prediction engines. The "fail-plausible" failure mode — where the LLM convincingly lies to the observer — represents a novel and uniquely dangerous failure class requiring specific mitigation strategies.

Abstract: LLM agent systems increasingly run as long-lived autonomous runtimes: scheduling jobs, calling tools, maintaining memory, and pushing results to humans. We present a longitudinal study of silent failures in one such system: a personal-assistant agent runtime in continuous production since March 2026, with roughly 40 scheduled jobs, 8 LLM providers, a tool-governance proxy, and a knowledge-base memory plane, defended by 4,286 unit tests and 827 governance checks. Over eight weeks we documented 22 incidents with full root-cause postmortems, in which one meta-pattern -- a failure whose error signal never reaches a human in actionable form -- manifested at least 28 times. We derive a five-class, mechanism-oriented taxonomy: (A) environment and platform quirks, (B) design-assumption mismatches, (C) error swallowing and dilution, (D) chained hallucination and fabrication, (E) operational omission and forensic blind spots. Class D is unique to LLM systems and the most dangerous: the system does not merely fail to report an error -- the LLM transforms it into fluent, plausible narrative delivered to the user. We term this fail-plausible: gray failure's differential observability escalated -- the observer is not just blind, it is convincingly lied to by the failure itself. Three findings: about 70% of silent failures were caught by human user-view observation, not tests or audits; a retrospective audit of 15 incidents found 0% ex-ante prevention but 87% regression blocking -- audits are regression engines, not prediction engines; incident latency (13 hours to 60 days) tracks failure mechanism, not code complexity -- the longest-lived failures lived in the seams between components, where no test runs. We describe the resulting defense framework and distill design principles for agent systems whose failures are loud, attributable, and boring. All postmortems and artifacts are public.

</details>


### [198] [Regulating the Machine Contributor: Governance and Policy Alignment in Open Source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14594)
*Jassem Manita,Aziz Amari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper examines how autonomous AI coding agents strain the human-centric governance assumptions of open-source software, compares AI contribution policies across six major OSS organizations, derives a six-dimensional taxonomy and Policy Maturity Score, maps documented agent incidents onto governance gaps, and aligns findings with emerging AI regulatory frameworks (EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC standards) to propose a harmonized tiered framework for AI contributions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-source software governance assumes legally accountable human contributors, but the rise of autonomous/semi-autonomous AI coding agents (capable of planning, editing, and submitting pull requests) undermines these assumptions. Documented agent-driven incidents, nuisance volume, and platform shutdowns in 2025–2026 demonstrate that this gap is operationally consequential, yet existing organizational responses are fragmented and unmapped against emerging AI governance frameworks.

Method: The authors employ Most-Similar Systems Design to compare AI contribution policies across six organizations: SymPy, LLVM, matplotlib, OpenInfra, the Apache Software Foundation, and the Linux Foundation. They use indicator-based coding and process tracing (for SymPy and LLVM specifically) to derive a six-dimensional taxonomy, an ordinal Policy Maturity Score, and a mapping of documented agent incidents onto policy dimensions.

Result: The analysis produces (1) a six-dimensional taxonomy covering disclosure, responsibility, human oversight, licensing, enforcement, and maintainer workload; (2) an ordinal Policy Maturity Score across the six organizations; (3) a mapping of real agent incidents onto dimensions each policy fails to govern; and (4) identification of overlapping regulatory-policy gaps when aligning these dimensions with the EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF (with UC Berkeley Agentic AI Profile), ISO/IEC 42001, and ISO/IEC 23894.

Conclusion: Neither current OSS contribution policies nor emerging AI regulatory frameworks independently close the governance gaps for AI agent contributions. The paper sketches the shape of a harmonized tiered framework to bridge both sides and calls for empirical evaluation to calibrate it.

Abstract: AI-assisted software development has moved from line-level autocomplete to agents that can plan changes, edit files, and submit pull requests with limited human supervision. Open-source software, however, evolves through a process designed for humans: contributor agreements, codes of conduct, and review norms all assume a legally accountable person who can attest to provenance and answer reviewer questions. Autonomous and semi-autonomous AI contributors strain those assumptions, and the 2025-2026 record of agent-driven incidents, AI-generated nuisance volume, and platform-level shutdowns shows that the gap is operationally consequential. Several open-source organisations have responded with contribution policies, but the result is fragmented, and its alignment with emerging AI governance frameworks (EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF with the UC Berkeley Agentic AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001 and 23894) is unmapped at the contribution level. We compare policies across six organisations (SymPy, LLVM, matplotlib, OpenInfra, the Apache Software Foundation, and the Linux Foundation) using Most-Similar Systems Design with indicator-based coding and process tracing for SymPy and LLVM. From this we derive a six-dimensional taxonomy (disclosure, responsibility, human oversight, licensing, enforcement, maintainer workload), an ordinal Policy Maturity Score, and a mapping of documented agent incidents onto the dimensions each policy fails to govern. Aligning the dimensions with the regulatory frameworks above identifies overlapping gaps neither side currently closes, and we close by sketching the shape of a harmonised tiered framework and the empirical evaluation needed to calibrate it.

</details>


### [199] [Empowering Student Debugging in Parallel Programming with Execution Traces and Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14607)
*God'salvation F. Oguibe,Vinodh Kumaran Jayakumar,Tongping Liu,Andrew Lan,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ParaView is an educational tool that records and visualizes parallel program execution to help students debug concurrency issues in C/C++. Evaluated with 17 students, it significantly improved debugging and implementation success. The authors also explored LLMs for concurrency bug analysis, finding them effective at identification but inconsistent at generating correct fixes for complex synchronization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Concurrent programming is a core yet notoriously difficult subject in CS curricula due to nondeterministic bugs like deadlocks and race conditions. There is a need for tools that make parallel execution observable and comprehensible, along with AI-assisted support for learning outside the classroom.

Method: The authors developed ParaView, a tool providing transparent execution recording and visualization for C/C++ parallel programs. They conducted a user evaluation with 17 students performing debugging and implementation tasks, comparing results against previous course iterations. Additionally, they assessed LLMs' ability to identify concurrency bugs, explain execution traces, and generate bug fixes. A post-study survey captured student perceptions.

Result: Students using ParaView showed significant improvement in debugging and implementation success rates compared to prior course cohorts. The survey indicated most participants found the tool helpful. LLMs proved highly effective at bug identification and trace explanation, but fix correctness was variable, particularly for complex synchronization patterns.

Conclusion: Recording-visualization tools like ParaView, when complemented by AI (LLMs), can enhance the teaching and learning of concurrent programming. However, LLM-generated fixes require caution due to reliability issues with complex concurrency scenarios.

Abstract: Concurrent programming is a core component of Computer Science curricula, yet remains notoriously difficult for students to master due to its inherent complexity and the nondeterministic nature of concurrency bugs such as deadlocks and race conditions. In this work, we present ParaView, an educational tool designed to help students understand, debug, and correct concurrency issues in parallel programs written in C/C++. ParaView provides transparent execution recording and visualization to make parallel execution observable and comprehensible. We evaluated ParaView through a series of debugging and implementation tasks, with 17 students participating. Results showed a significant improvement in debugging and implementation successes compared to previous course iterations. A student survey confirmed that most participants found ParaView helpful. To further support learning outside the classroom, we explored using Large Language Models (LLMs) to analyze concurrency bugs and suggest fixes. While LLMs were highly effective in identifying bugs and explaining execution traces, the correctness of their bug fixes varied, especially for more complex synchronization patterns. Our findings suggest that recording-visualization tools like ParaView, complemented by artificial intelligence (AI), can improve teaching and learning of concurrent programming.

</details>


### [200] [GitHub Template Repositories: Served Domains, Maintenance, and Practitioner Guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14616)
*Leuson Da Silva,Altaf Allah Abbassi,Imen Trabelsi,Paulo Borba,Foutse Khomh*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a large-scale empirical study of GitHub template repositories across five major programming languages. It categorizes template domains (with Web Development dominating), analyzes associations between repository characteristics and quality issues (code smells, vulnerabilities), and derives practical design guidelines through qualitative analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite GitHub template repositories being available since 2019 as a mechanism for project scaffolding, there is little empirical understanding of what domains they serve, how well they are maintained, their quality characteristics, or what constitutes effective template design practices.

Method: The authors conduct a mixed-methods empirical study: (1) mining and categorizing templates across the top five programming languages using an LLM-as-a-judge strategy for domain classification; (2) statistical analysis of associations between repository characteristics (activity, maintenance) and quality indicators (code smells, vulnerabilities, security hotspots); and (3) qualitative analysis of a representative template subset to extract design guidelines and common pitfalls.

Result: Web Development is the dominant template domain across all ecosystems studied. Maintenance patterns and quality issues vary significantly by programming language. High-quality templates consistently adopt established software engineering practices, provide comprehensive documentation, and offer clear usage guidance.

Conclusion: The study provides empirical insights and actionable guidance for practitioners to design and adopt high-quality template repositories, filling a gap in understanding this understudied GitHub feature.

Abstract: Over time, GitHub has introduced different strategies for sharing reusable code artifacts. In addition to fork-based reuse, template repositories provide a distinct feature for generating new projects from scaffolding. Although this feature has been available since 2019, little is known about the domains it supports, its maintenance characteristics, or the practices that guide practitioners for effective template design. To address this gap, we conduct a large-scale empirical study of GitHub template repositories across the five most used programming languages. First, we mine and categorize templates to analyze the domains they serve, exploring the LLM-as-a-judge strategy. Next, we explore the reliability of templates by evaluating the associations between repository characteristics and activity, and quality-related issues (e.g., code smells, vulnerabilities, and security hotspots) through statistical analysis. Finally, we qualitatively analyze a representative subset of templates to derive practical guidelines and recurring pitfalls for template design and management. Our results show that Web Development is the predominant domain across ecosystems, while maintenance and quality issues vary by programming language. We further find that high-quality templates tend to adopt established software engineering practices, while providing comprehensive documentation and clear guidance for use. Overall, our findings offer empirical insights and actionable guidance to support practitioners in designing and adopting high-quality template repositories.

</details>


### [201] [Teaching Machine Learning to Software Engineers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14618)
*Nafiseh Kahani,Jason Jaskolka*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper identifies gaps in how undergraduate SE curricula address AI/ML-based software development, surveys instructors on topic importance and integration feasibility, and proposes evidence-based guidelines for embedding high-priority AI/ML topics into existing SE courses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI/ML components are increasingly prevalent in production software, yet undergraduate SE programs lack systematic coverage of the skills needed to build, test, deploy, and maintain such systems. There is a need for structured, evidence-based guidance on what topics to integrate and where.

Method: The authors (1) compiled a structured inventory of SE-relevant topics for AI/ML-based software, (2) mapped these topics against required courses in representative SE curricula to find coverage gaps, (3) surveyed SE instructors to assess topic importance and integration constraints, and (4) derived placement guidelines via a crosswalk of topic definitions, curriculum gaps, and instructor priorities.

Result: The study reveals significant coverage gaps in current SE curricula regarding AI/ML engineering topics. Instructor survey data provides ranked topic importance and identifies practical constraints on integration. The resulting guideline specifies where and how high-priority topics can be embedded into existing SE courses.

Conclusion: Integrating AI/ML-relevant content into core SE education is both necessary and feasible. The proposed guideline offers a pragmatic, evidence-based roadmap for curriculum designers to address identified gaps without requiring entirely new courses.

Abstract: Machine learning (ML) and Artificial Intelligence (AI) components are increasingly embedded in software products, yet undergraduate software engineering (SE) curricula rarely provide systematic preparation for building, testing, deploying, and maintaining AI/ML-based software systems. This paper aims to provide evidence-based guidance for integrating AI/MLrelevant content into core SE education. We compile and define a structured inventory of topics relevant to SE practice in AI/MLbased software, then map these topics against required courses in a set of representative SE curricula to identify coverage gaps. To assess educational priorities and feasibility, we survey SE instructors on topic importance and integration constraints. Based on the crosswalk between topic definitions, curriculum coverage, and instructor prioritization, we derive a guideline that recommends where and how high-priority topics can be embedded within existing SE courses.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [202] [Large Language Models as Supervised Extraction Assistants: Lowering the Barrier to Documentation Standard Adoption in Agent-Based Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13749)
*Peer-Olaf Siebers,Christopher Frantz*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper explores using Large Language Models (LLMs) to semi-automate ABM documentation, specifically testing four LLMs on the RAT-RS reporting standard. LLMs perform well on descriptive tasks but struggle with explanatory/evaluative ones, offering practical heuristics for when LLM assistance is reliable versus when human oversight is required.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite existing documentation standards (ODD, TRACE, EABSS, RAT-RS), adoption remains limited due to the high effort required to produce documentation that is often relegated to supplementary material. The authors aim to investigate whether LLMs can lower this barrier and improve documentation uptake.

Method: A feasibility study using four LLMs to extract RAT-RS reports from a single published ABM paper. The authors assess inter-model consistency and performance across different question types (descriptive, explanatory, evaluative), analyzing where LLMs succeed and fail.

Result: LLMs generate coherent outputs and perform reliably on descriptive questions but show notable limitations on explanatory and evaluative tasks. The study identifies practical heuristics for determining when LLM-assisted documentation is trustworthy and when human intervention is necessary.

Conclusion: LLMs can improve ABM reporting quality and consistency but cannot fully replace human judgment. The authors call for systematic, community-level exploration to develop robust LLM-assisted documentation pipelines that enhance rigour and adoption in ABM reporting.

Abstract: Agent-Based Modelling (ABM) relies on clear documentation to ensure credibility and transparency. Although standards exist for documenting models (e.g. ODD), processes (e.g. TRACE, EABSS), and data use (e.g. RAT-RS), their adoption remains limited due to the effort required to produce documentation that is often treated as supplementary. This paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to facilitate and partially automate such processes. We conduct a feasibility study focusing on the underused Rigour and Transparency Reporting Standard (RAT-RS), using four LLMs to extract reports from a published ABM paper. We assess consistency and performance across question types, finding that LLMs generate coherent outputs and perform more reliably on descriptive than on explanatory or evaluative tasks. While LLMs can improve reporting quality and consistency, they also exhibit notable limitations. We identify practical heuristics for when LLM-assisted documentation is reliable and when human oversight is needed and call for systematic community-level exploration to enhance rigour and adoption in ABM reporting.

</details>


### [203] [Safety-Contract Graph Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Network Security Response](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13832)
*Jose Luis Lima de Jesus Silva*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper presents ACD³-GAT, a safety-contract graph MARL framework for autonomous network security response that enforces operational budgets (MTTR, false positives, firewall changes). It demonstrates that unconstrained methods violate downtime budgets in 100% of episodes, while the constrained approach reduces violations to 0.3–13.8% with dramatically lower costs, establishing a safety-contract Pareto frontier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward-only multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) for autonomous network security response can improve security metrics but remains non-deployable because it systematically violates operational budgets—specifically SOC downtime constraints. Prior CAGE Challenge 4 findings indicated a gap between reward optimization and operational discipline, motivating the need for safety-constrained MARL that respects real-world resource budgets while maintaining defensive efficacy.

Method: The authors propose a safety-contract graph MARL framework instantiated as ACD³-GAT (Adaptive Constrained Counterfactual Decisioning with Graph Attention Network encoder). Key architectural components include: (1) separation of simulator observations from reusable operational budgets; (2) constrained optimization via Lagrangian methods; (3) graph state encoding with GAT; (4) counterfactual action screening; (5) budget context and CVaR tail-risk estimation; (6) opponent-belief state; and (7) Graph Counterfactual Risk Propagation (G-CRP). A simpler variant, C-MAPPO-GAT, isolates Lagrangian operational-cost control with budget-aware screening. Evaluation uses CAGE Challenge 4 with three 200-episode seeds comparing IPPO, MAPPO-GAT, C-MAPPO-GAT, and ACD³-GAT, plus stress tests with topology-seed variation and coupled adaptive Red-process attacks.

Result: Every unconstrained method (IPPO, MAPPO-GAT) violates the SOC downtime budget (budget=50) in 100% of episodes, with mean downtime proxy costs of 311–430. C-MAPPO-GAT reduces downtime violation rate to 0.3% and mean downtime cost from 355.4 to 15.5 relative to MAPPO-GAT. ACD³-GAT achieves mean downtime cost of 48.2 with a 13.8% violation rate, placing it on the safety-contract frontier—balancing security performance with budget compliance rather than being maximally conservative. Under topology-seed and adaptive Red-process stress tests, safety-constrained policies show lower worst-case adaptive degradation than reward-only MAPPO-GAT.

Conclusion: Safety-contract graph MARL is essential for deployable autonomous network defense. Unconstrained reward-only methods catastrophically violate operational budgets. Lagrangian-constrained approaches like C-MAPPO-GAT and ACD³-GAT successfully enforce budget compliance while maintaining defensive performance, with ACD³-GAT offering a tunable trade-off on the safety-contract frontier. The framework's robustness under distribution shift (topology/adaptive stress tests) further supports real-world applicability in SOC environments.

Abstract: Autonomous network-security response systems promise to reduce Security Operations Centre (SOC) reaction latency, but reward-only multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) can improve security reward while remaining non-deployable. We present a safety-contract graph MARL framework and instantiate it as ACD$^3$-GAT (Adaptive Constrained Counterfactual Decisioning with a Graph Attention Network encoder), an architecture that separates simulator observations from reusable operational budgets, constrained optimization, graph state encoding, and counterfactual action screening. We evaluate the method in CAGE Challenge 4, where agents operate under budgets for Mean Time to Recover (MTTR), false-positive response, and firewall change-management disruption. Across the benchmark, every unconstrained method violates the SOC downtime budget in 100% of evaluated episodes, with mean downtime proxy costs of 311-430 against a budget of 50. This complements prior CAGE Challenge 4 findings by showing that reward-only learning lacks operational discipline. Constrained MAPPO-GAT (C-MAPPO-GAT) isolates Lagrangian operational-cost control and budget-aware screening, while ACD$^3$-GAT adds budget context, CVaR tail-risk estimation, opponent-belief state, and Graph Counterfactual Risk Propagation (G-CRP). The replicated comparison includes three 200-episode seeds for IPPO, MAPPO-GAT, C-MAPPO-GAT, and ACD$^3$-GAT. C-MAPPO-GAT reduces downtime violation from 100% to 0.3% and mean downtime cost from 355.4 to 15.5 relative to MAPPO-GAT. ACD$^3$-GAT reduces mean downtime cost to 48.2 with a 13.8% violation rate, placing it on the safety-contract frontier rather than at the most conservative compliance point. Topology-seed and coupled adaptive Red-process stress tests preserve this contrast and show lower worst adaptive degradation for safety-constrained policies than reward-only MAPPO-GAT.

</details>


### [204] [Naive Visual Memory is Not Enough: A Failure-Mode Study of GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14106)
*Seoyoung Choi,Minseok Ko,Hyunseok Lee,Kunwoong Kim,Woomin Song,Chanseok Jeon,Jinwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: The paper systematically analyzes how visual memory affects GUI agents, proposing a four-category failure taxonomy. It finds full-image memory reduces state-level failures but worsens action-level ones, particularly hidden operation blindness and grounding error. The authors propose Action-Grounded Visual Memory (AGMem), which stores localized image crops around successful actions, achieving a 33.3% task success rate improvement over full-image memory on OSWorld.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The effect of visual memory in GUI agents is insufficiently understood — it is unclear which failures it mitigates and which it exacerbates. Prior work introduced experiential memory (text-based) and visual memory (screenshot-based) but lacked systematic analysis of their failure modes.

Method: (1) A taxonomy of four GUI agent failures mapped to the perception-reasoning-action pipeline: cognitive failure, visual state misunderstanding, hidden operation blindness, and grounding error. (2) Systematic analysis revealing divergent effects of full-image memory. (3) AGMem framework: stores image crops capturing local GUI regions tied to successful actions or recoveries, rather than full screenshots.

Result: Full-image memory reduces state-level failures (cognitive failure, visual state misunderstanding) but worsens action-level failures (hidden operation blindness, grounding error). AGMem improves task success rates by 33.3% over full-image memory on the OSWorld benchmark.

Conclusion: AGMem provides an effective, action-grounded representation for visual memory in GUI agents, outperforming full-image memory by focusing on localized, action-relevant visual context.

Abstract: Graphical User Interface (GUI) agents are increasingly used to automate complex computer tasks across applications, websites, and operating systems. To improve their reliability, recent work has introduced experiential memory, where agents retrieve prior trajectories to guide decision-making in similar states. More recent approaches further extend this idea to visual memory by storing and retrieving screenshots from past interactions, providing agents with richer contextual information than text-only memories. However, the effect of visual memory in GUI agents remains insufficiently understood: it is unclear which failures visual memory mitigates, or which failures it exacerbates. To systematically analyze the effect of visual memory, we introduce a taxonomy of four GUI agent failures (i.e., cognitive failure, visual state misunderstanding, hidden operation blindness, and grounding error) that map to distinct stages of the perception-reasoning-action pipeline. We find that prepending full-image memory has a divergent effect on the failure distribution: it reduces state-level failures but worsens action-level ones, and increases hidden operation blindness and grounding error. Motivated by this finding, we propose Action-Grounded Visual Memory (AGMem), an action-grounded memory framework for GUI agents. The core idea of AGMem is to store image crops that capture the local GUI region closely related to a successful action or a recovery, rather than storing full screenshots. Experiments on OSWorld show that AGMem improves task success rates by 33.3 % over full-image memory. These results demonstrate that AGMem is an effective representation for visual memory in GUI agents.

</details>


### [205] [Learning Coordinated Preference for Multi-Objective Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14693)
*Pengxin Wang,Lihao Guo,Yi Xie,Bo Liu,Siyang Cao,Jingdi Chen*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: PCMA proposes learning coordinated, agent-specific preferences in cooperative multi-objective multi-agent RL to enable complementary trade-offs, theoretically proving preference diversity can improve team performance via first-order decomposition, and demonstrating gains in benchmarks and traffic control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In cooperative multi-objective multi-agent RL (MOMARL), conflicts arise both across objectives and across agents with heterogeneous observations, roles, and contributions.
