Existing methods typically assume uniform preferences across agents, failing to exploit agent diversity for complementary trade-offs that could improve overall team performance.

Method: The authors propose Preference Coordinated Multi-agent Policy Optimization (PCMA), which learns agent-specific preference vectors in a coordinated manner. Theoretically, cooperative MOMARL is formulated as a team-optimal game, and a first-order improvement decomposition is derived showing that preference diversity across agents can induce team-wide policy improvement under suitable conditions.

Result: Experiments on multiple cooperative MOMA environments and a practical traffic-control scenario demonstrate that PCMA improves both overall team performance and the quality of trade-off coordination among agents.

Conclusion: Preference diversity, when properly coordinated through PCMA, enables agents to specialize in complementary trade-offs, yielding better team outcomes than uniform-preference approaches in cooperative MOMARL settings.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-objective multi-agent reinforcement learning (MOMARL) models team decision making under multiple, potentially conflicting objectives. In this setting, conflicts arise not only across objectives but also across agents with different observations, roles, and contributions. We propose Preference Coordinated Multi-agent Policy Optimization (PCMA), which learns coordinated agent-specific preferences to enable complementary trade-offs among agents. Theoretically, we formulate cooperative MOMARL as a team-optimal game and show that, under suitable conditions, preference diversity can induce team improvement through a first-order improvement decomposition. Experiments on multiple cooperative MOMA environments and a practical traffic-control scenario show that PCMA improves both performance and trade-off coordination.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [206] [A Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL)-Based Transformer Method for Solving the Open Shop Scheduling Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13682)
*Faezeh Ardali,Mwembezi A. Nyelele,Gerald M. Knapp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A Transformer-based scheduling policy trained on small Open Shop Scheduling Problem (OSSP) instances generalizes to large-scale problems, achieving makespans within 12.89–15.12% of a lower bound and outperforming classical dispatching rules like SPT and LPT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Open Shop Scheduling Problem is computationally challenging at scale; exact methods become intractable and classical heuristics require substantial tuning. The authors seek a learning-based policy that can generalize from small training instances to much larger problems without retuning.

Method: A Transformer encoder-decoder architecture with multi-head attention is trained on Taillard benchmark instances (4×4, 5×5, 7×7, 10×10) using only the processing-time matrix as input. The trained policy is applied without retraining to randomly generated instances ranging from 40×40 to 100×100 and compared against SPT, LPT, MWKR, and EST dispatching rules.

Result: The Transformer achieved makespans within 15–30% of best-known values on training-scale benchmarks and 12.89–15.12% average gaps relative to a standard lower bound on large instances. It remained competitive with EST and substantially outperformed SPT and LPT.

Conclusion: A Transformer policy trained on small OSSP instances generalizes effectively to substantially larger problems, offering a feature-light, learning-based alternative to classical dispatching rules without requiring problem-specific retuning.

Abstract: The open shop scheduling problem (OSSP) arises in many industrial and service settings but remains computationally challenging as the number of jobs and machines increases. While exact methods quickly become intractable, classical dispatching rules and metaheuristics may require substantial tuning to maintain solution quality at large scales. This study develops a Transformer-based scheduling policy for OSSP using an encoder-decoder architecture with multi-head attention. The model is trained on Taillard benchmark instances (4x4, 5x5, 7x7, and 10x10) using only the processing-time matrix as input and produces feasible schedules with makespans typically within 15-30% of best-known values. To evaluate scalability, the trained policy is applied without retraining to randomly generated instances from 40x40 to 100x100 and compared against classical dispatching heuristics, including SPT, LPT, MWKR, and EST. Across these large instances, the Transformer achieved average gaps of 12.89-15.12% relative to a standard lower bound. Compared with EST, the Transformer remained competitive, typically within a modest margin, while substantially outperforming SPT and LPT. These results indicate that a Transformer policy trained on small OSSP instances can generalize to substantially larger problems and provide a feature-light, learning-based alternative to classical dispatching rules.

</details>


### [207] [UP-NRPA: User Portrait based Nested Rollout Policy Adaptation for Planning with Large Language Models in Goal-oriented Dialogue Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13683)
*Hui Wang,Fafa Zhang,Meng Liu,Xiangyu Chen,Chaoxu Mu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UP-NRPA is an LLM-based online dialogue policy adaptation framework that uses real-time user portraits (personality, preferences, objectives) and nested rollout mechanisms to dynamically customize dialogue strategies without offline RL training, achieving 100% success rates on multiple tasks and a 56.41% sale-to-list ratio improvement in negotiations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current dialogue policy planning methods rely on offline reinforcement learning over static user groups and cannot dynamically adapt to diverse, real-time user characteristics. There is a need for an online adaptation mechanism that responds to individual user feedback without requiring model retraining.

Method: The paper proposes UP-NRPA (User Portrait based Nested Rollout Policy Adaptation), an online framework integrated with Large Language Models. It constructs a real-time user portrait mapping personality, preferences, and objectives from ongoing interactions, then uses a nested rollout mechanism to dynamically adapt dialogue strategies based on this portrait and live user feedback — eliminating the need for offline RL training.

Result: UP-NRPA achieved a 100% success rate across multiple collaborative and non-collaborative dialogue tasks. In negotiation tasks, the sale-to-list ratio (SL) improved by 56.41%, demonstrating substantial benefits over conventional approaches.

Conclusion: UP-NRPA effectively adapts dialogue policies to diverse user characteristics in an online manner without any training mechanism, enabling dialogue systems to dynamically customize strategies based on real-time user portraits and feedback.

Abstract: To address the challenge that current dialogue policy planning methods struggle to dynamically adapt to diverse user characteristics, this paper proposes a User Portrait based Nested Rollout Policy Adaptation (UP-NRPA) online framework with Large Language Models. In contrast to conventional approaches dependent on model training and require offline reinforcement learning policy models for user groups, UP-NRPA enables dynamic customization of dialogue strategies through an adaptive mechanism. This is achieved by leveraging real-time user feedback alongside personality, preferences, and objectives mapped from the current user portrait, thereby adapting to user characteristics without offline reinforcement learning. In collaborative and non-collaborative dialogue benchmarks, UP-NRPA demonstrated considerable benefits, achieving an impressive 100% success rate in multiple dialogue tasks. Particularly in negotiation tasks, the sale-to-list ratio (SL) increased by 56.41%. This demonstrates that UP-NRPA can adapt to diverse user needs without requiring a training mechanism, enabling the dialogue system to adapt to user characteristics.

</details>


### [208] [History of the Muddy Children Puzzle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13703)
*Hans van Ditmarsch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper traces the historical origins of the Muddy Children Puzzle across two centuries of logical and literary publications, surveys its variations (numeric, coloured hats), and introduces a novel self-referential hats puzzle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Muddy Children Puzzle has been foundational for epistemic logic, yet its precise origin is unclear. The authors aim to resolve this uncertainty by tracing its first appearances and evolution.

Method: Historical-literary analysis: the authors survey logical and literary publications spanning the past two centuries to identify early formulations and trace the puzzle's genealogical development. They also present a conceptual analysis of known variations and construct a new self-referential variant.

Result: The paper documents the puzzle's trajectory through 19th- and 20th-century sources, catalogues its numerous adaptations (e.g., numeric versions, coloured-hat variants), and contributes a novel hats puzzle that incorporates self-reference.

Conclusion: The Muddy Children Puzzle's origins, while murky, can be traced through a rich interweaving of logical and literary sources. Its enduring generative power is evidenced by the many variations it has spawned, including the new self-referential hats puzzle proposed by the authors.

Abstract: The Muddy Children Puzzle is a puzzle about knowledge and ignorance that has been inspiring for the development of epistemic logic. Who came up with it first? This is unclear. We trace the origin of the Muddy Children Puzzle through logical and literary publications over the past two centuries. The puzzle inspired a numerous variations such as involving numbers or coloured hats. We also present a novel hats puzzle involving self-reference.

</details>


### [209] [Orchestra-o1: Omnimodal Agent Orchestration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13707)
*Fan Zhang,Vireo Zhang,Shengju Qian,Haoxuan Li,Hao Wu,Jinyang Wu,Donghao Zhou,Zhihong Zhu,Zheng Lian,Xin Wang,Pheng-Ann Heng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Orchestra-o1 is an omnimodal agent orchestration framework that enables modality-aware task decomposition and parallel sub-task execution for LLM-based multi-agent systems, achieving SOTA on OmniGAIA (+10.3% accuracy) and introducing DA-GRPO for efficient RL training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM orchestration frameworks are limited to narrow modalities and fail in omnimodal scenarios where text, image, audio, and video coexist. There is a need for a unified orchestration mechanism that can handle task decomposition and agent collaboration across heterogeneous modalities.

Method: Orchestra-o1 proposes: (1) a unified orchestration mechanism with modality-aware task decomposition, online sub-agent specialization, and parallel sub-task execution; (2) Decision-Aligned Group Relative Policy Optimization (DA-GRPO), an agentic RL approach for training the Orchestra-o1-8B model.

Result: Orchestra-o1 surpasses the second-best approach by 10.3% accuracy on the OmniGAIA benchmark. The DA-GRPO-trained Orchestra-o1-8B achieves state-of-the-art performance against all existing open-source omnimodal agents.

Conclusion: A unified, modality-aware orchestration framework with parallel execution and specialized RL training (DA-GRPO) significantly advances multi-agent LLM systems for complex, real-world omnimodal tasks.

Abstract: The recent success of agent swarms has shifted the paradigm of large language model (LLM)-based agents from single-agent workflows to multi-agent systems, highlighting the importance of agent orchestration for task decomposition and collaboration. However, existing orchestration frameworks are limited to a narrow set of modalities and struggle to generalize to more complex settings where heterogeneous modalities coexist and interact. This limitation becomes particularly pronounced in omnimodal scenarios, where tasks require the unified understanding and coordination of diverse inputs such as text, image, audio, and video. In this work, we propose Orchestra-o1, an omnimodal agent orchestration framework designed to support efficient agent collaboration across multiple modalities. Orchestra-o1 introduces a unified orchestration mechanism that enables modality-aware task decomposition, online sub-agent specialization, and parallel sub-task execution. This scalable design allows agent systems to effectively tackle complex real-world tasks involving heterogeneous information sources, surpassing the second-best approach by 10.3% accuracy on the OmniGAIA benchmark. Furthermore, we introduce decision-aligned group relative policy optimization (DA-GRPO), an efficient agentic reinforcement learning approach for training Orchestra-o1-8B, which also achieves state-of-the-art performance against all existing open-source omnimodal agents.

</details>


### [210] [WorkBench Revisited: Workplace Agents Two Years On](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13715)
*Olly Styles*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: From 2024 to 2026, the best agent on WorkBench improved from 43% task completion (GPT-4) to 89% (Claude Opus 4.8), while harmful actions dropped from 26% to 2.5%. Capability and safety improved together, some error classes vanished, but basic mistakes causing irreversible harm persist. Open-weight models now deliver frontier-level performance at drastically lower cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To track and characterize progress in AI agent performance and safety on the WorkBench benchmark over a two-year period, identifying whether capability and safety trade off or improve together, and to document shifts in the cost landscape driven by open-weight models.

Method: Longitudinal benchmark re-evaluation: the authors re-ran WorkBench in June 2026 with updated data and code quality improvements, scoring the latest frontier models (notably Claude Opus 4.8) and comparing results against the March 2024 baseline (GPT-4). They analyzed task completion rates, unintended harmful action rates, error class distributions, and cost-performance relationships across proprietary and open-weight models.

Result: Claude Opus 4.8 achieved 89% task completion (up from 43%) with only 2.5% unintended harmful actions (down from 26%). Capability and safety were positively correlated, not traded off. Several error classes were fully eliminated, but basic mistakes like emailing the wrong person persisted. Open-weight models now match previously proprietary-level performance at much lower cost, while frontier proprietary costs remained roughly stable.

Conclusion: Considerable progress has been made in both agent capability and safety on WorkBench, with the two improving in tandem. However, residual basic errors causing irreversible harm remain a concern. The democratization of performance via open-weight models has reshaped the cost landscape without inflating frontier costs.

Abstract: The best agent on WorkBench in March 2024, GPT-4, completed 43% of tasks and took an unintended harmful action, such as emailing the wrong person, on 26% of them. We re-visit the benchmark in June 2026 and find that the best agent to date, Claude Opus 4.8, completes 89% and takes an unintended harmful action on 2.5%. Aside from this considerable progress in frontier agent performance, three things stand out. First, capability and safety go together on WorkBench rather than trade off, so the models that finish the most tasks also do the least unintended damage. Second, while several classes of error have been totally eliminated, frontier models still make some basic mistakes that occasionally result in irreversible harm, such as sending an email to the wrong person. Third, the rise of open-weight models has drastically lowered costs for a performance level that was previously only accessible to proprietary models, while frontier costs have stayed relatively stable. We release an updated version of the benchmark with data and code quality improvements, new model scores, and analysis of agent progress on WorkBench since 2024.

</details>


### [211] [Hybrid Open-Ended Tri-Evolution Makes Better Deep Researcher](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13710)
*Hongming Piao,Chi Liu,Mengzhuo Chen,Yan Shu,Derek Li,Ying Wei,Bryan Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes HOTE, a Hybrid Open-Ended Tri-Evolution framework that uses hybrid-mode reinforcement learning to enable collaborative co-evolution of three agents (proposer, solver, judge) for open-ended research tasks. An 8B model trained with HOTE outperforms static 8-32B models and SOTA deep research training methods on three long-form benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Two key gaps exist in current AI agent research: (1) deep research systems are limited by static parametric capabilities, unable to improve through experience; (2) agent evolution methods (e.g., RL-based self-improvement) have only been validated on verifiable tasks with standard answers, not open-ended research tasks. The authors aim to bridge these two paradigms by enabling autonomous agent evolution in open-ended environments.

Method: The HOTE framework consists of three collaborative modules — a proposer (generates research questions/hypotheses), a solver (conducts information retrieval and synthesis), and a judge (evaluates output quality) — that co-evolve through hybrid-mode reinforcement learning. "Hybrid-mode" RL combines outcome-based rewards with process-based feedback, leveraging web-scale knowledge. The three modules are trained to improve each other iteratively in open-ended tasks without requiring ground-truth answers.

Result: An 8B-parameter model trained via HOTE outperforms the strongest static open-source models ranging from 8B to 32B parameters, as well as models trained with state-of-the-art deep research training methods, across three long-form deep research benchmarks. The training also requires less time overhead. Ablation studies confirm that the evolution of all three modules (proposer, solver, judge) is indispensable for the performance gains.

Conclusion: HOTE successfully bridges deep research and agent evolution for open-ended tasks, demonstrating that collaborative multi-agent co-evolution via hybrid RL is both effective and efficient. The framework moves toward the goal of autonomous, self-improving agents capable of handling open-ended real-world research problems.

Abstract: Deep research and agent evolution serve as de-facto tasks for AI agents in real-world applications toward artificial general intelligence. The former enables autonomous retrieval and integration of information in open-ended environments to tackle open-ended research tasks, yet it is constrained by the static parametric deep research capabilities of agent systems. The latter allows agents to autonomously interact with the environment to gain experiences that evolve model capabilities. However, its effectiveness has been widely validated only on verifiable tasks with standard answers, leaving a gap with open-ended research tasks. To bridge these two critical tasks, we propose the Hybrid Open-Ended Tri-Evolution (HOTE) framework, which leverages hybrid-mode reinforcement learning to facilitate the collaborative evolution of a proposer, solver and judge based on web-scale knowledge, moving toward autonomous evolving agents in open-ended tasks and environments. Extensive experiments on three long-form deep research benchmarks demonstrate that the 8B model trained via HOTE surpasses the strongest static open 8-32B models as well as those trained by state-of-the-art deep research training methods with less time overhead, and further verify that the evolution of all three modules in HOTE is indispensable.

</details>


### [212] [YeasierAgent: Agentic Social Sandbox as a Canvas for Intent-Driven Creation of Platform-Agnostic Symbiotic Agent-Native Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13722)
*Jory He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: YeasierAgent proposes a new application paradigm that replaces device-coupled software with collaborative spaces where users, agents, and narrative worlds interact, enabling cross-platform agent-native applications through platform-agnostic interactive units while unifying emotional companionship and practical tool execution in a single sandbox.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the limitations of conventional device-coupled software models and isolated, tool-specific chatbots. It seeks to move beyond fixed graphical layouts and single-purpose agents toward cohesive, socially embedded computational environments where emotional companionship and practical utility coexist.

Method: The authors present a system architecture centered on three platform-agnostic interactive units: agents, scenes, and dialogue, replacing fixed graphical layouts. The approach integrates automated generation, user-created narrative worlds, and spatial multi-agent collaboration within a unified experiential sandbox that supports rapid, cross-platform construction of agent-native applications.

Result: The work formalizes the category of "Symbiotic Agent-Native Applications" and demonstrates a paradigm shift from isolated chatbots to cohesive, collaborative computational spaces. The system achieves both rapid cross-platform application construction and the unification of emotional and instrumental agent attributes.

Conclusion: YeasierAgent establishes symbiotic agent-native applications as a viable new category, showing that decoupling applications from specific devices and merging emotional and practical agent capabilities within narrative, scene-aware environments can redefine human-agent-computer interaction.

Abstract: This paper introduces YeasierAgent, an application-building paradigm based on symbiotic agents, narrative worlds, and scene-aware interaction. It challenges the conventional device-coupled model of software by redefining applications as collaborative spaces among users, agents, and worlds. We present a system architecture that achieves two primary contributions: (1) enabling the rapid, cross-platform construction of agent-native applications by utilizing platform-agnostic interactive units (agents, scenes, dialogue) rather than fixed graphical layouts; and (2) unifying the emotional companionship and practical tool execution attributes of intelligent agents within a single experiential sandbox. By integrating automated generation, user-created worlds, and spatial multi-agent collaboration, YeasierAgent formalizes the category of Symbiotic Agent-Native Applications, demonstrating a shift from isolated, tool-specific chatbots toward cohesive, socially embedded computational environments.

</details>


### [213] [TwinBI: An Agentic Digital Twin for Efficient Augmented Interactions with Business Intelligence Dashboards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13731)
*Jisoo Jang Wen-Syan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TwinBI proposes an agentic digital-twin framework that synchronizes LLM-based conversational assistance with executable BI dashboard state, unifying multi-step analysis through a shared interaction log. It significantly boosts benchmark accuracy and reduces timeout rates while receiving positive usability ratings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In modern BI workflows, users frequently alternate between direct dashboard manipulation and LLM-based natural-language queries, but these two modalities easily drift out of sync during multi-step analysis. Preserving consistent analytical state — across filters, hierarchies, metrics, and chart context — is difficult, leading to broken provenance, semantic misalignment, and degraded analytical reliability.

Method: TwinBI introduces an agentic digital-twin framework that couples an LLM-based agent with an executable BI dashboard state. It unifies conversational interaction, dashboard manipulation, semantic grounding, and provenance tracking through a shared analytical state reconstructed from a unified interaction log. The system exposes artifacts such as schema views, SQL, logs, and an /insights command for state-grounded summaries. Evaluation comprises (1) a controlled A/B benchmark comparing TwinBI against a Dashboard-only baseline using the same backbone agent, and (2) a usability study with human participants.

Result: In the A/B benchmark, TwinBI improves exact-match accuracy from 43.3% to 63.3%, partial-credit accuracy from 48.3% to 70.8%, and reduces timeout rate from 40.0% to 10.0% relative to Dashboard alone. In the usability study, participants achieved high task accuracy with moderate workload and gave favorable ratings to the state-aware interaction mechanisms.

Conclusion: TwinBI demonstrates that making visible dashboard state available as richer, actionable context improves both agent-level analytical reliability and user-facing analytical support. The framework effectively bridges the gap between GUI-based BI and conversational AI, offering a practical path toward more coherent multi-step analytical workflows.

Abstract: Business intelligence (BI) increasingly combines dashboard interaction with LLM-based assistance, but these two modes often fall out of sync during multi-step analysis. As users switch between direct dashboard manipulation and natural-language queries, it becomes difficult to preserve a consistent analytical state across filters, hierarchies, metrics, and chart context. We present TwinBI, an agentic digital-twin framework that couples an LLM-based agent system with an executable BI dashboard state. TwinBI unifies conversational interaction, dashboard manipulation, semantic grounding, and provenance tracking through a shared analytical state reconstructed from a unified interaction log. It also exposes artifacts such as schema views, SQL, logs, and an /insights command for state-grounded analytical summaries. We evaluate TwinBI in two complementary ways. In a controlled A/B benchmark with the same backbone agent, TwinBI improves exact-match accuracy from 43.3% to 63.3%, partial-credit accuracy from 48.3% to 70.8%, and substantially reduces timeout rate from 40.0% to 10.0% relative to Dashboard alone. In a usability study, participants benefited from the integrated dashboard-and-chat workflow, with high task accuracy, moderate workload, and favorable ratings for state-aware interaction mechanisms. These results suggest that TwinBI improves both agent-level analytical reliability and user-facing analytical support by turning visible dashboard state into richer actionable context. Our dataset and source code are available at: https://github.com/simonjisu/TwinBI

</details>


### [214] [Refusal Beyond a Single Direction: A Preliminary Comparison of Diff-in-Means and INLP](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13720)
*Elisabetta Rocchetti,Alfio Ferrara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares difference-in-means (DiM) based interventions with Iterative Nullspace Projection (INLP) methods for steering refusal behavior in safety-tuned chat models. INLP counterfactual flipping proves competitive with DiM directional ablation for refusal suppression, while offering tunable control via subspace rank restriction, and reveals a geometric distinction between encoding the absence vs. opposite of a concept.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work (Arditi et al., 2024) demonstrated that refusal is mediated by a single linear direction recoverable via DiM. The authors investigate whether INLP — with its richer parameterization — can match or exceed DiM for steering refusal, and whether this richer parameterization enables more fine-grained, tweakable interventions.

Method: The authors compare four interventions across five open-weight chat models: (1) DiM activation addition, (2) DiM directional ablation, (3) INLP nullspace projection, and (4) INLP counterfactual flipping. They evaluate refusal suppression performance, perplexity impact, and analyze the geometric positioning of transformed activations relative to harmful/harmless clusters. They also experiment with restricting INLP to leading subspace directions to trade off suppression strength against language modeling quality.

Result: INLP counterfactual flipping is competitive with DiM directional ablation on refusal suppression, while INLP nullspace projection is consistently weaker. Restricting INLP to leading subspace directions preserves most suppression effect with near-baseline perplexity, providing a tunable steering capability. Geometrically, nullspace projection places activations between harmful and harmless clusters, while counterfactual flipping moves them into the opposite cluster — suggesting the model encodes "absence of a concept" differently from "opposite of a concept."

Conclusion: INLP provides a viable, more parameter-rich alternative to DiM for refusal steering, with the added benefit of tunability via subspace rank restriction. The geometric distinction between how the two INLP interventions transform activations raises fundamental questions about how language models represent negation vs. opposition, meriting further study.

Abstract: Arditi et al. (2024) has shown that refusal in safety fine-tuned chat models is mediated by a single linear direction in the residual stream, recoverable by a difference-in-means (DiM) of harmful and harmless activations. We compare DiM-based interventions (activation addition and directional ablation) with two interventions derived from Iterative Nullspace Projection (INLP) -- nullspace projection and counterfactual flipping -- on five open-weight chat models, asking whether INLP can match DiM at steering refusal and whether its richer parameterisation yields more tweakable interventions. INLP counterfactual flipping is competitive with DiM directional ablation on refusal suppression, while nullspace projection is consistently weaker. Restricting INLP to the leading directions of the extracted subspace preserves most of the suppression effect at near-baseline perplexity, giving a tunable capability. Geometrically, the two INLP interventions land in qualitatively different regions of activation space: nullspace projection collapses transformed activations \emph{between} the harmful and harmless clusters, while counterfactual flipping moves them into the opposite cluster, suggesting that the model encodes the absence of a concept differently from its opposite -- an intriguing distinction that warrants further investigation in future work.

</details>


### [215] [When Sample Selection Bias Precipitates Model Collapse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13732)
*Xinbao Qiao,Xianglong Du,Wei Liu,Jingqi Zhang,Peihua Mai,Meng Zhang,Yan Pang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Recursive training on synthetic data can cause model collapse, and while data selection is a proposed remedy, it becomes biased when verifiers rely on fragmented local reference distributions. This paper proves that siloed selection accelerates collapse, and proposes Wasserstein proxy references as a mitigation that preserves diversity without sharing raw data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In low-resource, privacy-constrained settings (e.g., healthcare consortia, proprietary finance), raw data cannot be pooled, forcing data selection verifiers to rely on incomplete local reference distributions. The authors investigate whether data selection — widely regarded as a safeguard against model collapse in recursive synthetic-data training — remains reliable under such fragmented verification regimes, or whether it paradoxically precipitates collapse.

Method: The authors provide a theoretical analysis proving that siloed (local-reference) data selection accelerates model collapse and induces power-law diversity decay in recursive training pipelines. They then construct Wasserstein proxy references by aggregating distributional information from multiple silos without sharing raw data, using Wasserstein barycenters or similar optimal transport techniques to form a more globally representative reference for the verifier.

Result: Theoretical proofs demonstrate that local-reference selection preferentially retains samples aligned with the local manifold while pruning globally relevant tail modes, turning selection into a collapse-accelerating mechanism. Empirical results confirm that local-reference selection fails on skewed distributions, while collaborative Wasserstein proxy references successfully mitigate diversity degradation.

Conclusion: Recursive synthetic-data pipelines require particular caution when real-data coverage is fragmented or scarce. Data selection is not an unqualified safeguard — its reliability hinges on the quality and coverage of the verifier's reference distribution. Collaborative proxy references offer a promising privacy-compatible mitigation strategy.

Abstract: The proliferation of recursive training on synthetic data can alleviate data scarcity but risks model collapse, where repeated training erodes distributional tails and homogenizes outputs. Data selection is widely viewed as a remedy, yet its reliability depends critically on the reference distribution used by the verifier. We show that in low-resource verification regimes, where each verifier observes only a small, fragmented, and biased slice of the target manifold, selection itself becomes biased. This situation naturally arises in low-resource data silos such as healthcare consortia or proprietary financial institutions, where raw data cannot be pooled and local references are inherently incomplete. As a result, selection preferentially retains samples aligned with the local manifold while pruning globally relevant tail modes, turning from a safeguard against collapse into a mechanism that precipitates it. We theoretically prove that such siloed selection accelerates collapse and induces power-law diversity decay. As an initial mitigation, we construct Wasserstein proxy references from multiple silos without sharing raw data. Empirical results confirm that local-reference selection fails on skewed distributions, whereas collaborative proxy references mitigate diversity degradation, suggesting that recursive synthetic-data pipelines require particular caution when real-data coverage is fragmented or scarce.

</details>


### [216] [AI Receptivity or AI Adoption Breadth? A Tool-Specific Reanalysis of the Lower-Literacy/Higher-Usage Link](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13734)
*Hristo Inouzhe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper reanalyzes Tully et al. (2025) public data and finds that the negative association between AI literacy and AI receptivity is driven entirely by non-text AI tools; AI literacy does not significantly predict text AI usage. The relationship for non-text tools reflects adoption/non-adoption rather than intensity of use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tully et al. (2025) claimed that lower AI literacy predicts greater receptivity toward AI. The authors suspect this aggregate finding may mask heterogeneity across different AI tool types and aim to test whether the relationship holds uniformly or is an artifact of pooling distinct tool categories.

Method: The authors reanalyze public data from Tully et al. Study 3, which measures self-reported past usage frequency of five AI tool categories. They first reproduce the original negative association using OLS on participant-level averages, binary logit, ordered logit, and multinomial logit. They then disaggregate by tool type (text vs. non-text AI) and estimate demographic-adjusted specifications, including the original Study 3 control specification, to assess heterogeneity in the AI literacy–usage relationship.

Result: In the aggregate, AI literacy negatively predicts AI usage, replicating Tully et al. However, disaggregation reveals stark heterogeneity: AI literacy does not significantly predict text AI usage (ordered-logit β = -0.090, p = .387) but strongly predicts non-text AI adoption (β = -0.377, p < .001; robust under original controls: β = -0.502, p < .001). The non-text effect reflects an adoption/non-adoption pattern (demographic-adjusted OR of ever-use = 0.68) rather than intensity of use.

Conclusion: The evidence does not support a general claim that lower AI literacy predicts greater AI receptivity. Instead, the relationship is confined to broader adoption of lower-penetration, non-text AI tools. The finding underscores the importance of disaggregating AI usage by tool type when studying literacy–receptivity links.

Abstract: Recent evidence reported by Tully, Longoni, and Appel (2025) suggests that lower artificial intelligence (AI) literacy predicts greater receptivity toward AI. We revisit this claim using the public data from Study 3 of that article, which measures past usage of five AI tool categories on a five-point frequency scale. We first reproduce the negative association between AI literacy and aggregate AI usage using OLS on participant-level averages, binary logit, ordered logit, and multinomial logit specifications. We then show that the aggregate relationship masks substantial heterogeneity by tool type. In our demographic-adjusted primary specification, AI literacy does not significantly predict text AI usage (ordered-logit $β$ = -0.090, p = .387), whereas it remains a strong predictor of non-text AI adoption ($β$ = -0.377, p < .001). The non-text effect is also robust under Tully et al.'s original Study 3 control specification ($β$ = -0.502, p < .001). Binary, ordered-logit, and multinomial specifications suggest that the non-text relationship is primarily an adoption/non-adoption pattern rather than evidence of intensive use: the demographic-adjusted odds ratio of ever having used a non-text AI tool is 0.68. Thus, in the study that measures self-reported past usage rather than stated preferences, the evidence does not support a simple claim that lower AI literacy predicts greater receptivity to AI in general. It points instead to a narrower pattern of broader adoption across lower-penetration, non-text AI tools.

</details>


### [217] [MA-ProofBench: A Two-Tiered Evaluation of LLMs for Theorem Proving in Mathematical Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13782)
*Lushi Pu,Weiming Zhang,Xinheng Xie,Zixuan Fu,Bingxiang He,Hongya Lyu,Xin Li,Jie Zhou,Yudong Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MA-ProofBench is the first formal theorem-proving benchmark for Mathematical Analysis, featuring 200 theorems across 6 core topics at two difficulty levels. Even the best model (GPT-5.5) achieves only 16% Pass@8 on undergraduate-level problems and 5% on PhD-level problems, revealing a significant gap between informal and formal mathematical reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing formal theorem-proving benchmarks are limited to algebra and elementary number theory, neglecting advanced fields like mathematical analysis that require deeper reasoning. This gap motivates a dedicated benchmark spanning measure theory, complex analysis, and functional analysis.

Method: The authors construct 200 formalized theorems across 6 core topics and 27 subcategories, divided into Level I (undergraduate, 100 problems) and Level II (PhD qualifying, 100 problems). A human-led, LLM-assisted formalization pipeline followed by independent expert review ensures fidelity to the original mathematics. Evaluation uses Pass@8 on recent reasoning models and theorem provers, with failure analysis categorizing errors into Mathlib hallucinations and incomplete proofs.

Result: GPT-5.5 achieves the best performance at 16% Pass@8 on Level I and 5% on Level II, while most models remain near 0% on Level II. The dominant failure modes are Mathlib hallucinations and incomplete proofs. A natural-language version evaluation reveals a clear gap between informal and formal reasoning capabilities.

Conclusion: MA-ProofBench provides a reliable reference for tracking progress in formal mathematical reasoning within advanced domains. Current LLMs perform poorly, particularly on PhD-level problems, highlighting the need for substantial advances in formal reasoning capabilities for deep mathematical analysis.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have made notable progress in automated theorem proving, yet existing formal benchmarks remain limited in both mathematical coverage and difficulty. Most are concentrated in areas that are easier to formalize, such as algebra and elementary number theory, and provide limited coverage of subfields that require deeper reasoning, including mathematical analysis. To address this gap, we introduce MA-ProofBench, to the best of our knowledge, the first formal theorem-proving benchmark dedicated to Mathematical Analysis. The benchmark contains 200 formalized theorems covering 6 core topics and 27 subcategories, including measure and integration theory, complex analysis, and functional analysis. The problems are divided into two difficulty levels, an undergraduate level (Level I, 100 problems) and a Ph.D. qualifying level (Level II, 100 problems), to evaluate how well LLMs perform formal reasoning at different mathematical depths. Each problem is constructed through a human-led, LLM-assisted formalization pipeline followed by independent expert review, ensuring that the formal statements remain faithful to the original mathematics. We evaluate a range of recent general-purpose reasoning models and formal theorem provers on MA-ProofBench. However, most models perform poorly: even the best-performing model, GPT-5.5, achieves only 16% Pass@8 on Level I and 5% on Level II, while most models stay close to 0% on Level II. Further analysis identifies Mathlib hallucinations and incomplete proofs as the two dominant failure modes, while an evaluation on the natural-language version of the benchmark exposes a clear gap between informal and formal reasoning. MA-ProofBench is intended to serve as a reliable reference for tracking progress in formal mathematical reasoning in advanced domains.

</details>


### [218] [Poker Arena: Multi-Axis Profiling of Strategic Reasoning and Memory in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13815)
*Pratham Singla,Shivank Garg,Vihan Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Poker Arena introduces a multi-axis evaluation framework for LLMs in no-limit Texas Hold'em, revealing that scalar leaderboards misrank models by obscuring the underlying structure of strategic reasoning capabilities — persistent memory effects are heterogeneous, and cross-dimensional consistency proves more important than peak single-axis performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing game-play benchmarks collapse heterogeneous strategic reasoning dimensions (e.g., bet-sizing, positional awareness, bluffing) into a single scalar score, masking the capability profiles of frontier LLMs. The authors aim to decompose strategic reasoning into interpretable axes to surface how different models actually reason under uncertainty.

Method: Poker Arena, a tournament platform for no-limit Texas Hold'em, features a three-layer memory architecture (within-hand, session-level, and cross-session). Seven frontier LLMs are evaluated across 50 sessions of 1,000 hands each, with a nine-axis cognitive profile measuring dimensions such as bet-sizing calibration and positional awareness. A controlled memory ablation study is also conducted to isolate memory effects.

Result: Tournament chip rankings and aggregate axis scores produce divergent orderings. Claude Opus 4.6 leads in chips (+$15,730, 14 first-place finishes) but ranks only fifth of seven on mean axis score. Persistent memory yields heterogeneous effects — benefiting some models while degrading others. Cross-dimensional consistency emerges as more predictive of overall quality than peak performance on any individual axis.

Conclusion: Multi-axis evaluation surfaces capability structures that scalar leaderboards systematically misrank, demonstrating that robust strategic reasoning assessment requires decomposing performance into interpretable, independent cognitive dimensions rather than relying on aggregate win/loss metrics.

Abstract: Strategic reasoning under uncertainty underpins consequential decisions in negotiation, finance, and policy, but prevailing game-play benchmarks collapse heterogeneous reasoning dimensions into a single scalar, leaving the capability structure of frontier LLMs unexamined. We introduce Poker Arena, a no-limit Texas Hold'em tournament platform that couples a three-layer memory architecture (within-hand, session, and cross-session) with a nine-axis cognitive profile decomposing strategic reasoning into interpretable dimensions such as bet-sizing calibration and positional awareness. We evaluate seven frontier models across 50 sessions of 1,000 hands and a controlled memory ablation; tournament chips and aggregate axis score order the field differently: Claude Opus 4.6 wins +$15,730 chips with 14 first-place finishes, yet ranks only fifth of seven on mean axis score, while persistent memory helps some models and hurts others. These findings show that multi-axis evaluation surfaces capability structure that scalar leaderboards systematically misrank, with cross-dimensional consistency outweighing peak performance on any single axis.

</details>


### [219] [Hyperdimensional computing for structured querying on tabular data embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13871)
*Sebastián Bugedo,Stijn Vansummeren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Current tabular embedding methods lack interpretable similarity scores, making it impossible to determine if a retrieved neighbor is a true match. The authors propose using Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR) from HyperDimensional Computing, which yield closed-form expected similarity values with principled thresholds, enabling reliable zero-match detection while matching or outperforming graph-based baselines on row retrieval tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tabular embedding approaches (e.g., for entity resolution, schema matching, table search) produce uninterpretable similarity scores — the cosine distance to a nearest neighbor carries no intrinsic meaning. This makes it impossible to set principled retrieval thresholds or detect when no valid match exists (zero-match detection), severely limiting practical deployment in data integration pipelines.

Method: The authors adopt HyperDimensional Computing (HDC), specifically Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR), to embed tabular rows for answering structured select-project queries in vector space. Leveraging HRR's algebraic properties (binding, bundling), they derive closed-form expected similarity values for equality and non-equality retrieval predicates, which converge to interpretable values as dimensionality increases. These theoretical expectations provide principled thresholds for retrieval. Evaluation is conducted against EmbDI (a graph-based baseline) on two real-world datasets across varying table sizes and predicate lengths.

Result: HDC matches or outperforms EmbDI on row retrieval across all configurations, handles non-equality predicates more robustly, and achieves perfect attribute projection accuracy at sufficient dimensionality. Crucially, the principled thresholds uniquely enable reliable identification of zero-match predicates, a capability absent in existing embedding methods.

Conclusion: HRR-based HDC embeddings provide a theoretically grounded alternative to current tabular embedding methods, offering interpretable similarity scores and principled retrieval thresholds. This enables practical, threshold-based deployment for select-project query answering and, uniquely, zero-match detection. The approach demonstrates strong empirical performance on real-world datasets.

Abstract: Tabular data embeddings have become a cornerstone of data profiling and data integration pipelines, enabling tasks such as entity annotation and resolution; schema matching; column type detection; and table search, among others. Existing approaches embed rows, columns, or entire tables into a vector space and rely on nearest-neighbor search to retrieve candidate matches. A fundamental limitation of current embedding methods is the lack of interpretable similarity scores: the concrete similarity value between a query and its nearest neighbour carries no intrinsic meaning, making it impossible to determine whether that neighbour is a true match or simply the least-dissimilar item in a corpus that contains no valid answer. This inability to set principled thresholds for retrieval undermines practical deployment, particularly for zero-match detection.
  We investigate the use of HyperDimensional Computing (HDC), specifically the Holographic Reduced Representations (HRR) model, as a framework for tabular row embeddings when the retrieval task corresponds to answering structured select-project queries in vector space. Exploiting the algebraic properties of HDC operations, we derive closed-form expected similarity values for both equality and non-equality retrieval predicates, which converge to interpretable values as dimensionality increases, and use these to identify suitable retrieval thresholds. We evaluate HDC against EmbDI, a graph-based baseline, on two real-world datasets across varying table sizes and predicate lengths. Our results show that HDC matches or outperforms EmbDI for row retrieval across all configurations, handles non-equality predicates more robustly, and achieves perfect attribute projection accuracy at sufficient dimensionality -- while uniquely enabling reliable identification of zero-match predicates through its principled thresholds.

</details>


### [220] [Capability Minimization as a Safety Primitive: Risk-Aware Causal Gating for Least-Privilege LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13884)
*Laxmipriya Ganesh Iyer,Rahul Suresh Babu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RACG is a framework that uses causal effect estimation and calibrated risk control to gate model predictions—deciding when to act, defer, or abstain—yielding safer, more transparent decision systems than confidence-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern decision systems rely on learned models that can be confidently wrong, leading to costly downstream errors. Existing confidence-based gating fails to distinguish between predictive uncertainty and true causal risk, motivating a framework that explicitly separates the two.

Method: RACG models the causal pathway from candidate actions to outcomes, gating each decision based on estimated counterfactual risk rather than raw predictive confidence. It derives distribution-free bounds on the probability of acting under high-risk conditions, translating these into operating thresholds satisfying user-specified safety constraints. An adaptive gating policy further adjusts to distribution shift by monitoring discrepancies between predicted and realized outcomes, tightening the gate when causal assumptions appear violated.

Result: Across simulated interventions and real-world benchmarks, RACG substantially reduces high-cost errors while preserving most of the utility of an ungated policy, and outperforms confidence-based and selective-prediction baselines at matched abstention rates.

Conclusion: Explicitly separating causal risk from predictive uncertainty yields decision systems that are both safer and more transparent, providing a principled mechanism for trustworthy automation in high-stakes settings.

Abstract: Modern decision systems increasingly rely on learned components whose outputs may be confident yet wrong, exposing downstream actions to costly errors. We introduce Risk-Aware Causal Gating (RACG), a framework that decides whether to act on, defer, or abstain from a model's prediction by combining causal effect estimation with calibrated risk control. RACG models the causal pathway from candidate actions to outcomes and gates each decision according to an estimated counterfactual risk rather than raw predictive confidence. To make gating reliable, we derive distribution-free bounds on the probability of acting under high-risk conditions and show how these bounds translate into operating thresholds that satisfy user-specified safety constraints. We further propose an adaptive gating policy that adjusts to distribution shift by monitoring discrepancies between predicted and realized outcomes, tightening the gate when causal assumptions appear violated. Across simulated interventions and real-world decision benchmarks, RACG reduces high-cost errors substantially while preserving most of the utility of an ungated policy, and it outperforms confidence-based and selective-prediction baselines at matched abstention rates. Our results indicate that explicitly separating causal risk from predictive uncertainty yields decision systems that are both safer and more transparent, offering a principled mechanism for trustworthy automation in high-stakes settings.

</details>


### [221] [A Multi-Agent AI System for Automated High School Transcript Processing: Collaborative Document Analysis at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13916)
*Ben Torkian,Jun Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent AI system with specialized agents (Pattern Recognition, Semantic Analysis, Vision Intelligence) coordinated by an Orchestration Agent achieves 96.7% accuracy in automatically processing diverse high school transcripts, reducing manual bottlenecks in college admissions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: College admissions offices are overwhelmed by millions of uniquely formatted high school transcripts with varying grading systems and layouts, creating operational bottlenecks that delay admissions decisions and consume substantial resources. Manual processing is the pain point demanding automation.

Method: A multi-agent architecture with three specialized agents: (1) Pattern Recognition Agent for format-specific parsing, (2) Semantic Analysis Agent for natural language understanding, and (3) Vision Intelligence Agent for multimodal document analysis. An Orchestration Agent coordinates inter-agent communication and result reconciliation. The key innovation is agent-based quality control using GPA extraction as a coordination signal to ensure reliability and prevent information loss.

Result: Evaluated on 40 real-world transcripts from high schools across 13 U.S. states: 100% document processing success rate, 96.7% accuracy compared to expert manual review, and processing speed of 45 seconds per transcript.

Conclusion: Multi-agent coordination effectively solves complex heterogeneous document processing challenges, offering institutions a scalable solution that preserves high accuracy while dramatically reducing processing time compared to manual methods.

Abstract: Each year, college admissions offices face an overwhelming challenge: processing millions of high school transcripts, each with unique formats, grading systems, and layouts. This manual process creates operational bottlenecks that delay admissions decisions and consume valuable resources. We present a transformative solution through a multi-agent AI system where specialized agents collaborate to automatically process diverse transcript formats through intelligent coordination and communication. Our multi-agent architecture consists of three specialized agents-a Pattern Recognition Agent for format-specific parsing, a Semantic Analysis Agent for natural language understanding, and a Vision Intelligence Agent for multimodal document analysis-coordinated by an Orchestration Agent that manages agent communication and result reconciliation. Our key innovation lies in agent-based quality control using GPA extraction as a coordination signal, ensuring reliable agent collaboration and preventing critical information loss. When evaluated on 40 real world transcripts from high schools across 13 U.S. states, our agent system successfully processed every document, achieving 96.7% accuracy compared to expert manual review while maintaining practical processing speeds of 45 seconds per transcript. This work demonstrates how multi-agent coordination can solve complex document processing challenges, offering institutions a scalable, collaborative AI solution that preserves accuracy while dramatically reducing processing time.

</details>


### [222] [Sorries Are Not the Hard Part: An Expert-Review Case Study of a Semi-Autonomous Formalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13925)
*Vasily Ilin,Brian Nugent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a case study on semi-autonomously formalizing Grothendieck's vanishing theorem in an interactive theorem prover. While the initial LLM-generated formalization compiled without errors, expert review revealed fundamental flaws in definitions, generality, organization, and API design. After a review-driven refactoring, a second review showed that LLMs handle local, mechanical feedback well but struggle with high-level design choices like definitions and APIs. The authors argue that autoformalization should be evaluated by expert review survival, not just by closure of proof gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of autoformalization (LLM-assisted theorem proving) focuses narrowly on closing "sorry" gaps — i.e., whether the proof compiles. However, a verified theorem is not equivalent to a reusable, well-designed library contribution. The authors aim to investigate this gap systematically: can LLM-generated formalizations survive expert scrutiny as library-quality artifacts, or do they merely patch proofs while neglecting structural quality?

Method: The authors conducted a detailed case study involving the semi-autonomous formalization of Grothendieck's vanishing theorem using LLMs. The process had two phases: (1) an initial formalization that compiled with no sorries, followed by an expert review; (2) a review-driven refactor and compression process, followed by a second expert review. The before-and-after comparison assessed how well LLM-based agents responded to two categories of feedback: local, mechanically checkable issues versus global design choices (definitions, theorem generality, file organization, API design).

Result: The initial version compiled without sorries but the expert review identified serious problems across definitions, theorem generality, file organization, and API design. In the refactoring phase, agents adapted well to local, mechanically checkable feedback. However, they remained weak at making high-level design decisions such as choosing appropriate definitions, structuring APIs, and ensuring the right level of generality. The before-and-after comparison revealed a sharp split between these two types of capabilities.

Conclusion: Autoformalization should be evaluated not merely by whether proofs compile (closed sorries), but by whether the resulting formalization survives expert review as a reusable library contribution. LLMs currently show competence in mechanical, local reasoning but lack the design intuition needed for library-quality mathematics formalization.

Abstract: Large language models can often close proof gaps in interactive theorem provers, but a verified theorem is not the same thing as a reusable library contribution. We study this distinction through a detailed case study: a semi-autonomous formalization of Grothendieck's vanishing theorem. The initial version compiles with no sorries, but an expert review found serious problems in definitions, theorem generality, file organization, and the API. We then ran a review-driven refactor and compression process and obtained a second expert review. The before-and-after comparison shows a sharp split: agents adapted well to local, mechanically checkable feedback, but remained weak at choosing definitions and designing APIs. We argue that autoformalization should be evaluated not only by closed sorries, but by whether the resulting formalization survives expert review.

</details>


### [223] [Adversarial Concept Search: Predicting Compositional Errors From Feature Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13934)
*Jennifer Meng Lu,Ruochen Zhang,Isabelle Lee,David Alvarez-Melis,Ellie Pavlick,Naomi Saphra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs fail to compose concepts when their linear encodings are similar (causing interference) rather than near-orthogonal. This representational geometry approach reliably predicts failure modes across various compositional tasks without evaluating specific inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developers currently rely on human intuition or exhaustive benchmarking to find challenging edge cases for LLMs. The paper proposes a principled alternative: using the model's internal representational geometry to anticipate failures before they occur, bypassing the need for trial-and-error input evaluation.

Method: The authors analyze the geometry of concept representations in LLM embedding spaces. For compositional tasks (programmatic reasoning, multihop QA, multilingual recall), they measure whether concept pairs are encoded near-orthogonally or with high similarity. Interference arises when salient feature encodings are close in representational space, disrupting compositional binding. The method predicts failures based purely on the geometric relationship between concept encodings, without running forward passes on specific inputs.

Result: Near-orthogonal concept encodings enable reliable composition; similar (close) encodings produce interference and systematic failure. This holds across diverse compositional tasks — toy programmatic settings, multihop reasoning, and multilingual factual recall — demonstrating the method's generality.

Conclusion: Representational geometry provides a scalable, input-agnostic foundation for identifying high-risk examples, constructing targeted stress tests, and enabling active learning in deployment. The approach shifts failure analysis from behavioral testing to structural properties of the model's internal representations.

Abstract: Humans cannot always intuit what scenarios are most challenging to LLMs. Hoping to capture challenging edge cases, developers either design problems to be difficult for humans or curate extensive benchmarks. What if we could instead anticipate which scenarios a model will fail on? In this paper, we use an LLM's representational geometry to predict which concept combinations it will fail on. We attribute this compositional failure to interference between salient features. In tasks that require systematic composition - toy programmatic settings, multihop reasoning, multilingual factual recall - we find that when a pair of concepts is encoded near-orthogonally, the model reliably composes them. When their linear encodings are close, producing interference, the model fails to compose them. Our method reliably anticipates failure modes across different compositional tasks, without evaluating specific inputs. These results lay the groundwork to use representational geometry to identify high-risk examples, construct targeted stress tests, and provide a scalable foundation for active learning in real-world deployment.

</details>


### [224] [Minim: Privacy-Aware Minimal View for Agents via Trusted Local Sanitization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13949)
*Hexuan Yu,Chaoyu Zhang,Heng Jin,Shanghao Shi,Ning Zhang,Y. Thomas Hou,Wenjing Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MINIM is a client-side privacy broker that minimizes sensitive UI information before sending observations to remote LLM agents. Using a Contextual Integrity framework, it learns sensitivity and task-necessity scores for each UI element, then applies a ternary disclosure policy (keep/abstract/remove) to reduce sensitive leakage while preserving task-critical affordances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-powered autonomous agents often require full UI state observations to ground actions in digital environments. Transmitting the entire UI state to remote inference servers leaks sensitive information (authentication codes, private notifications, background app states) that is irrelevant to the current task. Existing solutions lack a principled, task-aware mechanism to minimize this privacy leakage without degrading agent performance.

Method: MINIM operates as a trusted local broker on the client device. It is grounded in Contextual Integrity (CI) theory and learns a dual-score representation: (1) an inherent sensitivity score (s) for each UI element, and (2) a task-conditioned necessity score (n). These scores drive a ternary disclosure policy with three actions: keep essential elements intact, abstract sensitive attributes when both necessary and sensitive, and remove task-irrelevant content entirely. The training objective is CI-aware, penalizing necessity prediction errors more heavily on high-risk content to enable aggressive pruning while preserving task-critical information.

Result: Experiments on real-world UI observations from WebArena demonstrate that MINIM substantially reduces task-irrelevant sensitive leakage while preserving the task-critical semantic context and interactive affordances required for reliable agent actions.

Conclusion: MINIM provides an effective privacy-preserving layer for LLM-powered agents by intelligently filtering UI observations before they leave the device, balancing privacy protection with agent functionality through a principled CI-based scoring and disclosure framework.

Abstract: Modern LLM-powered autonomous agents increasingly rely on rich user interface (UI) state observations to achieve reliable action grounding in complex digital environments. However, many deployments transmit the full UI state to remote inference servers even when most elements are irrelevant to the current task, which can leak sensitive but unnecessary context such as authentication codes, private notifications, and background application states. We propose MINIM, a trusted local broker that performs privacy-aware minimization on the client side before any observation leaves the device. Grounded in Contextual Integrity (CI), MINIM learns a dual-score representation for each UI element by predicting an inherent sensitivity score (s) and a task-conditioned necessity score (n). These scores drive a ternary disclosure policy that keeps essential elements, abstracts sensitive attributes when needed, and removes task-irrelevant content. We optimize a CI-aware objective that penalizes necessity errors more strongly on high-risk content, enabling aggressive pruning while preserving task-critical information. Experiments on real-world UI observations derived from WebArena show that MINIM substantially reduces task-irrelevant sensitive leakage while preserving task-critical semantic context and the interactive affordances required for reliable agent actions.

</details>


### [225] [Formalizing Numerical Analysis: An Agent Pipeline and Quality Audit Beyond Kernel Acceptance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14000)
*Theodore Meek,Siyuan Ge,Di Qiu Xiang,Simon Chess,Vasily Ilin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper applies a coding agent to formalize numerical ODE theory (absent from mathlib) in Lean 4 and introduces a 3D evaluation framework (semantic correctness, Mathlib reuse, cross-file reuse) using LLM-as-judge, revealing that compilation-based metrics mask unfaithful formalization patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing autoformalization efforts focus on math domains already well-covered in mathlib and evaluate quality solely by kernel acceptance (compilation). This ignores (1) whether agents can generate novel theory from scratch and (2) whether formalizations are semantically faithful to the source text.

Method: The authors deploy a coding agent to formalize a numerical analysis textbook (ODEs) largely absent from mathlib. They propose a three-dimensional evaluation framework assessing: (1) semantic correctness via LLM-as-judge, (2) Mathlib reuse rate, and (3) cross-file reuse. The framework is applied to their own formalization and publicly released outputs from RepoProver and M2F.

Result: The evaluation uncovers recurring unfaithful formalization patterns: incomplete multi-part statements, added weakening hypotheses, and parameter restrictions — all hidden by kernel acceptance. Compilation-based metrics substantially overstate actual formalization quality across all systems examined.

Conclusion: Compilation alone is an insufficient quality signal for autoformalization. The proposed reproducible audit methodology provides a more rigorous evaluation standard and reveals systematic failure modes that future autoformalization systems must address.

Abstract: Recent work has demonstrated that coding agents can formalize entire advanced mathematics textbooks in Lean 4, yet existing efforts concentrate on branches of mathematics already well-represented in mathlib and measure success solely through kernel acceptance. We address both limitations by applying a coding agent to formalize Numerical Methods for Ordinary Differential Equations, a textbook in numerical analysis that is largely absent from mathlib, stressing the agent's capacity to develop new theory from scratch. We further introduce a systematic, reproducible three-dimensional framework for evaluating the quality of agent-produced formalizations beyond compilation: semantic correctness, Mathlib reuse, and cross-file reuse via LLM-as-judge methods. Applying this framework to our own formalization and to the released outputs of RepoProver and M2F, we uncover recurring unfaithful formalization patterns, including incomplete multi-part statements, added weakening hypotheses, and parameter restrictions, that kernel acceptance entirely obscures. Our results suggest that compilation-based metrics substantially overstate formalization quality, and we provide a reproducible audit methodology to support more rigorous evaluation of future autoformalization systems.

</details>


### [226] [Applicability Condition Extraction for Therapeutic Drug-Disease Relations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14031)
*Guanting Luo,Noriki Nishida,Yuji Matsumoto,Yuki Arase*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces a new task — extracting applicability conditions for drug-disease therapeutic relations — along with a manually annotated dataset of 1,119 drug-disease-condition triples, and a novel LoRA-enhanced method that outperforms existing baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing biomedical relation extraction focuses only on whether a drug treats a disease, ignoring the context-specific conditions (e.g., patient subgroups, co-treatments) under which that relation holds, limiting clinical utility.

Method: Created the Drug-ACE dataset with manually annotated triples (drug, disease, applicability condition) from 1,119 biomedical abstracts. Proposed a method that enhances Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to explicitly model drug–disease relations during condition extraction, and benchmarked against a range of existing approaches.

Result: The proposed LoRA-enhanced method consistently outperformed strong baselines across multiple evaluation settings on the newly introduced Drug-ACE dataset.

Conclusion: Applicability condition extraction is a meaningful and challenging task; the Drug-ACE dataset and the proposed relation-aware LoRA method establish strong foundations for future work in context-specific biomedical relation extraction.

Abstract: Identifying conditions that a certain drug takes therapeutic effect on a target disease is crucial for clinical decision-making support. However, most existing biomedical information extraction methods have focused on identifying only relations between drugs and diseases, while largely overlooking the context-specific conditions where such relations can apply. To address this problem, we introduce the task of applicability condition extraction for therapeutic drug--disease relations from biomedical research literature. We create the first dataset that has manually annotated triples of drugs, diseases, and applicability conditions on biomedical paper abstracts with 1,119 drug-disease pairs. Using this dataset, we systematically evaluate the performance of a range of existing methods. In addition, we propose a new method that enhances LoRA to consider relations between drugs and diseases. Our method consistently outperforms strong baselines across different evaluation settings. The source code and dataset of this paper can be obtained from: https://github.com/guantingluo98/Drug-ACE

</details>


### [227] [FactoryLLM: A Safe and Open-Source AI Playground for Evaluating LLMs in Smart Factories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14119)
*Yash Pulse,Yong-Bin Kang,Abhik Banerjee,Abdur Forkan,Prem Prakash Jayaraman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FactoryLLM is an open-source AI playground for evaluating LLM-based RAG models on cross-machine diagnostic reasoning in smart factories, validated through a case study with 30 maintenance queries across ~600 pages of documentation, where all tested models achieved groundedness scores above 0.88.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fault diagnostics and recovery in smart factories is challenging because critical information is dispersed across manuals of multiple interconnected machines. Existing solutions lack a safe, configurable environment for evaluating how well LLMs can reason across multi-machine documentation without exposing sensitive industrial data.

Method: The authors propose FactoryLLM, a configurable playground supporting retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) with user-selectable local or open-source LLMs. It employs a dual evaluation framework combining RAGAS metrics and NVIDIA's LLM-as-a-Judge. The system was validated through a case study involving an Autonomous Intelligent Vehicle and its Mobile Planner software, with 30 maintenance queries derived from ~600 pages of cross-machine documentation, testing three different LLMs.

Result: All three evaluated LLMs achieved groundedness scores above 0.88, demonstrating effective cross-machine document reasoning capability within the FactoryLLM framework.

Conclusion: FactoryLLM provides an effective, safe, and open-source environment for evaluating LLM-based RAG models on cross-machine diagnostic tasks in smart manufacturing. The code and documentation are publicly available for the community to test with their own manufacturing scenarios.

Abstract: Fault diagnostics and recovery in smart factories is challenging because critical information is dispersed across manuals of multiple machines which are interconnected through the manufacturing process. Large Language Models (LLMs) can provide a promising approach. In this paper, we propose FactoryLLM, a safe and open-source AI playground designed for evaluating different LLM-based retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) models by analysing documents from multiple machines across the manufacturing process. FactoryLLM enables the user to configure the LLM, and assess performance when reasoning over multiple documents, through a dual evaluation setup using both RAGAS and NVIDIA's LLM-as-a-Judge metrics. FactoryLLM is safe because it allows users to run local or open-source LLMs without sharing sensitive industrial data, providing a controlled environment for experimentation. We demonstrate the efficacy of FactoryLLM through a case study which involves an Autonomous Intelligent Vehicle and its Mobile Planner software, evaluating three LLMs across 30 maintenance queries derived from approximately 600 pages of cross-machine documentation. The results suggest that FactoryLLM is effective in cross-machine document reasoning: every model achieved a groundedness score above 0.88. The full code and documentation for community to test FactoryLLM with their manufacturing specific scenarios are publicly available.

</details>


### [228] [VeriGeo: Controllable Geometry Question Generation with Numerical and Analytical Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14176)
*Xiaoxian Duan,Zequn Liu,Yingce Xia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VeriGeo is a controllable geometry problem generation framework that uses executable reasoning traces and a three-stage verification pipeline to ensure mutual consistency among problem statements, diagrams, constraints, and solutions. It repairs invalid generations via verification-guided reflection and produces high-quality synthetic data that significantly boosts multimodal geometry reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing geometry generation methods face a trade-off: seed-based rewriting is flexible but hard to verify, while diagram-first construction is valid but less controllable by user constraints. There is a need for a framework that simultaneously offers controllability (user-specified target concepts and difficulty) and reliability (mutual consistency of text, diagram, constraints, and solution).

Method: VeriGeo employs two LLM agents — an Author that generates the problem and diagram, and a Solver that produces a proof-aligned solution — both using a shared action sequence linking natural language, diagrams, geometric constraints, and proof steps into a unified verifiable representation. A three-stage verification pipeline checks: (1) numerical consistency, (2) analytical realizability, and (3) global consistency. Verification-guided reflection repairs recoverable failures and rejects unrecoverable ones. Supervised fine-tuning on 8.7k VeriGeo-generated examples is used to train multimodal solvers.

Result: Raw generations across five LLM backbones frequently fail the consistency checks, but VeriGeo repairs a substantial fraction of invalid attempts. Fine-tuning on 8.7k VeriGeo examples achieves state-of-the-art GeoQA performance among end-to-end multimodal LLM-based solvers and yields strong results on PGPS9K and MathVista-GPS.

Conclusion: VeriGeo demonstrates that verification-grounded generation with executable reasoning traces effectively produces consistent geometry problems at scale. The resulting verified synthetic data significantly improves multimodal geometry reasoning, establishing a new state-of-the-art for end-to-end multimodal LLM-based solvers on GeoQA.

Abstract: Geometry problem generation is useful for AI-assisted education and multimodal mathematical reasoning, but reliable synthesis remains difficult because the problem statement, diagram, constraints, and solution should be mutually consistent. Existing methods often trade off controllability and reliability: seed-based rewriting is flexible but weakly verifiable, whereas diagram-first construction improves validity but is less suited to arbitrary user-specified constraints. We introduce VeriGeo, a controllable geometry generation framework grounded in executable reasoning traces. Given user constraints such as target concepts and difficulty, an Author agent generates a problem and diagram, and a Solver agent produces a proof-aligned solution. Both agents use a shared action sequence that connects natural language, diagrams, geometric constraints, and proof steps into a verifiable representation.
