A three-stage pipeline checks numerical consistency, analytical realizability, and global consistency, using verification-guided reflection to repair recoverable failures and reject unrecoverable ones. Across five LLM backbones, raw generations frequently fail these checks, while VeriGeo repairs a substantial fraction of the invalid attempts. Supervised fine-tuning on 8.7k examples generated by VeriGeo achieves the best reported GeoQA performance among end-to-end multimodal LLM-based solvers, and obtains strong results on PGPS9K and MathVista-GPS, demonstrating the effectiveness of verified synthetic data for improving multimodal geometry reasoning.

</details>


### [229] [When Should Agent Trust Be Conditional? Characterizing and Attacking Skill-Conditional Reputation in Agent Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14200)
*Yihan Xia,Taotao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that using a single global trust score per agent is suboptimal for routing tasks to heterogeneous LLM agents. It introduces skill-conditional trust, shows conditional routing wins only under specific conditions (high heterogeneity, sparse evidence, correlated skills), and demonstrates an attack where cheap evidence in one skill contaminates trust in another via cross-skill borrowing, quantifying the trade-off rather than claiming Sybil-resistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open platforms increasingly route tasks among heterogeneous LLM agents with sharply varying competence across skills. The standard global trust score approach fails to capture specialization value. The paper investigates whether skill-conditional trust improves routing, how much cross-skill evidence should be borrowed, and whether such borrowing is safe.

Method: The authors formalize skill-conditional trust R(i | k) and use a controlled phase-diagram analysis to identify the regime where conditioning helps (high agent heterogeneity, sparse per-skill evidence, correlated skills). They validate on a public benchmark of 14 heterogeneous AppWorld agents. They then introduce an attacker model using cheap evidence in one skill to hijack conditional routing, evaluate with a Conditional Information Value Test (CIVT), and deploy a zero-evidence gate to bound the attack, characterizing residual cost under an explicit budget.

Result: Phase-diagram analysis shows conditional trust wins only in a specific regime. On the AppWorld benchmark, real pools fall inside this beneficial regime with small but genuine gains. However, cross-skill borrowing creates a dual-use laundering channel: an attacker can drive routing regret from 0 to 0.94. A zero-evidence gate bounds but does not eliminate the attack; the ungated trust verdict shifts from honest +0.19 to -0.06.

Conclusion: Skill-conditional trust offers real but modest routing gains under the right conditions, but cross-skill borrowing is a dual-use mechanism that enables attack laundering. The paper does not claim Sybil-resistance; instead it quantifies the trade-off between data efficiency (borrowing strength β) and vulnerability to adversarial manipulation.

Abstract: Open platforms increasingly route tasks among heterogeneous LLM agents--differing in base model, scaffold, and tool stack--whose competence varies sharply by skill: an agent excellent at one skill may be useless at another. The standard reputation approach summarizes each agent by a single global trust score, but that scalar is the wrong object here, because routing every task to the globally most-trusted agent leaves the value of specialization unclaimed. We study skill-conditional trust R(i | k)--the trust to place in agent i for a task requiring skill k, rather than one score per agent--and pose three falsifiable questions: when is conditioning worth it, how much cross-skill evidence should be borrowed, and whether that borrowing is safe. A controlled phase-diagram analysis answers the first two: conditional trust wins only in a specific regime--high agent heterogeneity, sparse per-skill evidence, and correlated skills--and the coupling strength beta that buys this data efficiency is dual-use, because the same cross-skill borrowing is also a laundering channel. On a public benchmark of 14 genuinely heterogeneous AppWorld agents, real pools land inside the beneficial regime--a small but genuine gain, with the per-skill best agent genuinely changing across skills. We then show that an attacker with cheap evidence in one skill and none in a target skill hijacks the conditional router, driving routing regret from 0 to 0.94 on a pool our zero-cost Conditional Information Value Test (CIVT) rates GREEN--while the ungated trust verdict it contaminates reads -0.06 instead of the honest +0.19. A zero-evidence gate bounds the attack but does not eliminate it; we characterize the residual cost under an explicit budget. We do not claim Sybil-resistance--we quantify the trade-off.

</details>


### [230] [Closing the Reflection Gap: A Free Calibration Bonus for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14211)
*Yinglun Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents suffer from a "reflection gap" where they mis-assess their own performance even after observing environment feedback. The authors propose RefGRPO, which adds a calibration bonus (no extra models needed) and a dynamic coefficient schedule to standard RL, improving both reflection calibration and task accuracy on text-to-SQL benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents deployed in interactive environments receive concrete feedback (execution results, errors, tool outputs) but fail to accurately self-assess their performance — a phenomenon the authors call the "reflection gap." Standard RL fails to fix this due to a credit-assignment mismatch between actions and reflective judgments. There is a need for a method that aligns an agent's self-assessment with actual outcomes without requiring additional reward models or human annotation.

Method: RefGRPO augments standard RL (specifically GRPO) with two components: (1) a free calibration bonus computed by contrasting the agent's own reflection (binary self-assessment) with the actual task outcome — no extra reward model, LLM judge, or external annotation required; and (2) a dynamic schedule that controls the coefficient of this bonus over training. The calibration bonus directly rewards alignment between predicted and actual correctness.

Result: RefGRPO simultaneously improves reflection calibration and task accuracy on text-to-SQL across five benchmarks. Underconfidence rate drops from 44.4% to 7.7%, and task accuracy improves from 75.1% to 76.5% compared to standard RL baselines. The calibrated reflection further enables (i) self-improvement using reflections as pseudo-rewards without outcome supervision, and (ii) effective test-time selective prediction by only committing to rollouts flagged as correct by the agent itself.

Conclusion: Closing the reflection gap via RefGRPO yields a dual benefit: better-calibrated self-assessment and improved task performance. The resulting agent can serve as its own verifier grounded in environment feedback, unlocking downstream capabilities like autonomous self-improvement and selective prediction — all without additional model infrastructure or human annotation.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly deployed as agents that interact with external environments and observe feedback such as execution results, error messages, and tool outputs. A well-functioning agent should be able to leverage this feedback to accurately assess its own performance. Yet we find a persistent reflection gap: LLM agents tend to mis-assess their own outputs after observing concrete environment feedback -- even for questions they correctly answered -- and standard RL barely helps due to a credit-assignment mismatch. To close this gap, we propose RefGRPO, a simple yet effective fix that augments standard RL algorithms with two key ingredients: a free calibration bonus computed by contrasting the agent's own reflection with the actual outcome (requiring no additional reward model, LLM judge, or external annotation), and a dynamic schedule on its coefficient. Compared to standard RL baselines, our method simultaneously improves reflection calibration (e.g., reduces underconfidence rate $44.4\% \to 7.7\%$) and task accuracy (e.g., $75.1\% \to 76.5\%$) on text-to-SQL across five benchmarks. The resulting calibrated reflection turns the agent into its own verifier grounded in environment feedback, which further enables (i) better self-improvement that uses reflections as pseudo-rewards without outcome supervision, and (ii) more effective test-time selective prediction by committing only to rollouts flagged as correct.

</details>


### [231] [SkillAudit: Ground-Truth-Free Skill Evolution via Paired Trajectory Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14239)
*Haowen Gao,Haoran Chen,Can Wang,Shasha Guo,Liang Pang,Zhaoyang Liu,Huawei Shen,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillAudit is a framework that evolves LLM agent skills using only task descriptions and workspace data, without ground-truth feedback. It employs paired trajectory auditing and Process-Aligned Contrastive Evaluation (PACE) to diagnose how skills affect agent behavior, then routes edits through Refine or Repair pipelines. Across 89 tasks in 8 domains, it achieves 73.9% average reward vs. 40.9% (no skills) and 56.7% (static skills).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent skills degrade post-deployment due to edge cases, API changes, and deployment constraints. Existing evolution methods rely on privileged feedback (validation scores, hidden tests, environment rewards) that practitioners often lack. There is a need for skill evolution using only task descriptions and workspace data, without ground-truth signals.

Method: SkillAudit employs paired trajectory auditing — executing the same task with and without a candidate skill each iteration to isolate behavioral differences. PACE (Process-Aligned Contrastive Evaluation) maps trajectory divergences to diagnostic signals tied to specific skill document passages. A structural verifier (compiled once from the task specification) enforces constraints and rolls back harmful updates. Edits are routed through two pipelines: Refine (removes noisy/irrelevant guidance) and Repair (replaces conflicting passages).

Result: Across 89 containerized tasks spanning 8 professional domains, SkillAudit achieves 73.9% average task reward, outperforming the no-skill baseline (40.9%) and static expert skills (56.7%), without access to hidden tests, reference solutions, or external scoring functions during evolution.

Conclusion: SkillAudit demonstrates that effective skill evolution is feasible without ground-truth feedback, using only task descriptions and workspace data. The paired auditing approach combined with PACE enables targeted, diagnostically-informed edits that yield substantial gains over both unskilled agents and static skills.

Abstract: Agent skills are structured procedural packages that guide frozen LLM agents in specialized workflows. Skills rarely remain sufficient after deployment: edge cases, API changes, and deployment constraints become visible only through use, making skill evolution a practical necessity. Existing methods depend on privileged feedback such as held-out validation scores, hidden test outcomes, or environment rewards -- signals often unavailable when a practitioner has only a task description and workspace data. We introduce SkillAudit, a framework for evolving agent skills without ground-truth feedback. The key idea is paired trajectory auditing: at each iteration, the same task is executed with and without the candidate skill, isolating how the skill changes agent behavior without external labels. To turn behavioral differences into edit guidance, SkillAudit uses Process-Aligned Contrastive Evaluation (PACE), a cluster of evaluators that maps trajectory divergences to diagnostic signals linked to specific passages in the skill document. A structural verifier, compiled once from the task specification and then fixed, checks task constraints and rolls back harmful updates. SkillAudit routes edits through two pipelines: Refine removes noisy or irrelevant guidance from broadly useful skills, while Repair replaces passages that conflict with the task. Across 89 containerized tasks spanning 8 professional domains, SkillAudit achieves 73.9% average task reward, outperforming an agent without skills (40.9%) and the static expert skill (56.7%). These gains are obtained without accessing hidden tests, reference solutions, or external scoring functions during evolution.

</details>


### [232] [AFFORDANCE20Q: Evaluating Affordance Reasoning from Physical Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14240)
*Yifan Jiang,Meige Yang,Zitong Li,Jay Pujara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Affordance20Q is a benchmark that tests LLMs' affordance reasoning through a 20-Questions game where object identities are hidden, forcing models to reason from physical properties. Testing 15 LLMs reveals a ~20-point gap versus humans, and models fail to ask discriminating questions. The proposed KARI pipeline, which induces affordance rules from KBs, improves open-source LLMs by up to 15.2 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing affordance benchmarks expose object identities, enabling models to cheat via memorized object-affordance associations rather than engaging in genuine physical property reasoning. The authors aim to isolate and evaluate true affordance reasoning ability.

Method: The benchmark is a 20-Questions game: models must identify a hidden object's affordance by asking yes/no questions about physical properties, without ever knowing the object's identity. It comprises 1,009 games across 454 objects and 59 affordances with manual annotation. The KARI pipeline uses LLMs to generate affordance rules anchored in knowledge base evidence. A KL-divergence-based information-gain analysis evaluates question quality.

Result: 15 SOTA LLMs tested show a ~20-point performance gap compared to humans. The IG analysis reveals models fail to ask discriminating questions as games progress. KARI improves open-source LLMs by up to 15.2 points, though limited KB coverage constrains further gains.

Conclusion: Current LLMs struggle with genuine affordance reasoning when object identity is concealed, relying heavily on memorized mappings. Grounding reasoning in structured KB knowledge via rule induction offers a promising path forward, but broader KB coverage is needed for further improvement.

Abstract: Affordance reasoning, the inference of an object's action possibilities from its physical properties (e.g., shape and material), is fundamental to human physical understanding and increasingly critical for Large Language Models (LLMs). However, existing affordance benchmarks largely expose explicit object identities in the evaluation setup, allowing models to rely on memorized object-affordance mappings rather than reasoning over physical properties. To address this gap, we introduce Affordance20Q, a novel affordance reasoning benchmark formulated as a 20-Questions game without exposing the object's identity. In each game, the model identifies a hidden object's affordance from a candidate set by asking yes/no questions about its physical properties. Affordance20Q comprises 1,009 games over 454 objects and 59 affordances, all manually filtered, refined, and annotated. We conduct comprehensive experiments with 15 state-of-the-art LLMs and find a substantial gap (~20 points) compared to human performance. A KL-based information-gain (IG) analysis further shows that models fail to ask discriminating questions as the game progresses. To close the gap, we develop KB-Anchored Rule Induction (KARI), a pipeline based on LLMs that generates affordance rules grounded in evidence from knowledge bases (KBs). KARI improves open-source LLMs by up to 15.2 points, while the limited coverage of KBs hinders further gains. We release all our code and data at https://github.com/1171-jpg/Affordance20Q.git

</details>


### [233] [HarnessX: A Composable, Adaptive, and Evolvable Agent Harness Foundry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14249)
*Tingyang Chen,Shuo Lu,Kang Zhao,Weicheng Meng,Hanlin Teng,Tianhao Li,Chao Li,Xule Liu,Jian Liang,Zhizhong Zhang,Yuan Xie,Heng Qu,Kun Shao,Jian Luan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarnessX introduces a framework for composable, adaptive agent harnesses that evolve through execution traces. Using a substitution algebra for harness construction and AEGIS, a trace-driven multi-agent evolution engine, it achieves an average +14.5% gain across five benchmarks, demonstrating that runtime optimization rivals model scaling as a performance lever.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agent harnesses (prompts, tools, memory, control flow) are hand-crafted, static, and task-specific. Execution traces contain valuable feedback that is rarely systematically exploited. There is no principled mechanism to compose, adapt, and evolve harnesses across models and tasks, nor to close the loop between runtime execution and model improvement.

Method: HarnessX provides: (1) typed harness primitives with a substitution algebra enabling composable assembly; (2) AEGIS, a trace-driven multi-agent evolution engine that mirrors symbolic adaptation and reinforcement learning through an operational mirror; and (3) a closed-loop mechanism that converts execution trajectories into both harness updates and model training signals.

Result: Across five benchmarks — ALFWorld, GAIA, WebShop, tau^3-Bench, and SWE-bench Verified — HarnessX achieves an average performance gain of +14.5%, with improvements up to +44.0%. Gains are largest where baseline performance is lowest.

Conclusion: Agent performance can be substantially improved not only through model scaling but also by composing and evolving runtime harnesses from execution feedback. This represents an actionable, complementary path to advancing AI agent capabilities.

Abstract: AI agent performance depends critically on the runtime harness, comprising the prompts, tools, memory, and control flow that mediate how a model observes, reasons, and acts. Yet today's harnesses remain largely hand-crafted and static: each new model or task still demands bespoke scaffolding, and the rich traces produced during execution are rarely distilled back into systematic improvement. We introduce HarnessX, a foundry for composable, adaptive, and evolvable agent harnesses. HarnessX assembles typed harness primitives via a substitution algebra, adapts them through AEGIS, a trace-driven multi-agent evolution engine grounded in an operational mirror between symbolic adaptation and reinforcement learning, and closes the harness-model loop by turning trajectories into both harness updates and model training signal. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, GAIA, WebShop, tau^3-Bench, and SWE-bench Verified), HarnessX yields an average gain of +14.5% (up to +44.0%), with gains largest where baselines are lowest. These results suggest that agent progress need not come from model scaling alone: composing and evolving runtime interfaces from execution feedback is an actionable and complementary lever. The complete codebase will be open-sourced in a future release.

</details>


### [234] [Communication Policy Evolution for Proactive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14314)
*Xinbei Ma,Jiyang Qiu,Yao Yao,Zheng Wu,Yijie Lu,Xiangmou Qu,Jiaxin Yin,Xingyu Lou,Jun Wang,Weiwen Liu,Weinan Zhang,Zhuosheng Zhang,Hai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper formalizes "Communication Policy" for LLM agents, comparing text-based and UI-based interaction channels, finding complementary strengths, proposing a hybrid method, and introducing Communication Policy Evolution (CPE) — a self-evolution framework that optimizes communication strategies through prompt refinement without model modification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There exists a persistent information gap between users and LLM agents: communication is costly, and users' identical preferences constrain information exchange. The authors aim to systematically investigate how agents should communicate across modalities to bridge this gap.

Method: The paper formalizes the concept of Communication Policy (textual vs. UI-based), constructs two complementary experimental settings (User-Agent and Planner-Executor) with information asymmetry for proactive agents, evaluates policies across diverse environments/personas/model combinations, and proposes CPE — a self-evolution framework using rollout-based evaluation and prompt-level evolution to refine communication policies without model modification.

Result: Text-based interaction facilitates task performance, while structured UI improves response quality and persona compliance. The hybrid method combines both advantages. CPE achieves the best task success across multiple settings using only prompt refinement, without any model modification.

Conclusion: Communication behavior is identified as a critical yet underexplored design dimension for LLM agents. The complementary nature of different communication modalities and the effectiveness of self-evolution via CPE suggest that optimizing communication policy is a promising direction for improving autonomous agent systems.

Abstract: LLM agents have rapidly evolved into autonomous systems, yet a persistent information gap remains between users and agents: communication is costly, while users' identical preferences further limit information exchange. To investigate how agents should communicate across modalities, this paper formalizes Communication Policy, establishes textual and UI-based policies, and then evaluates communication policies across diverse environments, personas, and model combinations. Building information asymmetry for proactive agents, we set up two complementary settings, User-Agent and Planner-Executor. Experimental results reveal complementary strengths between interaction channels: text-based interaction often facilitates task performance, while structured UI improves agents' response quality and persona compliance. Motivated by that, a hybrid method combines these advantages. We further propose Communication Policy Evolution (CPE), a self-evolution framework for refining communication policies through rollout and prompt-level evolving. Without model modification, CPE achieves the best task success across multiple settings using prompt refinement alone. Our findings identify communication behavior as a critical yet underexplored design dimension for LLM agents.

</details>


### [235] [CSPO: Constraint-Sensitive Policy Optimization for Safe Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14415)
*Ayoub Belouadah,Sylvain Kubler,Yves Le Traon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CSPO introduces a constraint-sensitive correction into primal-dual Safe RL to reduce oscillations and speed up safety recovery, achieving higher constrained returns than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Primal-dual methods for Safe RL scale well but suffer from delayed constraint correction via Lagrange multipliers, causing oscillations and prolonged safety violations near the constraint boundary.

Method: CSPO augments the primal objective with a constraint-sensitive correction term based on the shortest signed distance to the safety boundary. This first-order correction enables proactive safety recovery without waiting for multiplier updates, while preserving KKT optimality conditions of the original CMDP.

Result: On navigation and locomotion benchmarks, CSPO achieves faster safety recovery and higher constrained returns compared to state-of-the-art primal-dual and penalty-based baselines.

Conclusion: Incorporating local constraint sensitivity into policy updates effectively mitigates the oscillatory behavior of primal-dual methods, yielding safer and more rewarding policies.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning (Safe RL) aims to maximize expected return while satisfying safety constraints, typically modeled as Constrained Markov Decision Processes (CMDPs). While primal-dual methods scale well to deep RL, they often suffer from delayed constraint correction, leading to oscillatory behavior and prolonged safety violations. In this paper, we propose Constraint-Sensitive Policy Optimization (CSPO), a first-order primal-dual method that incorporates local constraint sensitivity into policy updates. CSPO augments the primal objective with a constraint-sensitive correction derived from the shortest signed distance to the safety boundary, enabling smarter recovery steps back to safety, compensating for delayed Lagrange multiplier updates, reducing oscillations near the boundary, and preserving the KKT solutions of the original constrained problem. Experiments on navigation and locomotion benchmarks demonstrate that CSPO achieves faster safety recovery and high reward preservation, resulting in higher constrained returns compared to state-of-the-art primal-dual and penalty-based methods

</details>


### [236] [Causal Object-Centric Models for Planning with Monte Carlo Tree Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14418)
*Rodion Vakhitov,Leonid Ugadiarov,Alexey Skrynnik,Aleksandr Panov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COMET introduces object-centric model-based RL with a slot-structured latent space, combining a frozen object-centric encoder, a transformer world model with action-slot fusion, and object-causal attention for task-relevant decision-making in Monte Carlo Tree Search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard model-based RL agents lack explicit object-level inductive biases, making them sample-inefficient and slow to focus on task-relevant entities in visually and dynamically diverse environments.

Method: COMET uses a frozen unsupervised object-centric encoder to produce slot-structured latents, a transformer world model with action-slot fusion for slot transition prediction, and policy/value heads with object-causal attention that uses learned per-slot relevance scores. Planning is performed via Monte Carlo Tree Search in the slot-structured latent space, extending MuZero-style latent planning with explicit object-level structure.

Result: Across eight tasks from Object-Centric Visual RL, ManiSkill, Robosuite, and VizDoom benchmarks, COMET achieves higher mean normalized scores during early training compared to both object-centric and monolithic baselines.

Conclusion: Explicit object-level inductive biases through slot-structured latent spaces, action-slot fusion, and object-causal attention significantly improve sample efficiency in model-based RL, particularly during early training stages.

Abstract: We introduce COMET (Causal Object-centric Model for Efficient Tree search), a model-based reinforcement learning algorithm that performs Monte Carlo Tree Search in a slot-structured latent space. COMET pairs a frozen unsupervised object-centric encoder with a transformer-based world model, in which actions are bound to objects through a novel action-slot fusion mechanism that is used in slot transition prediction. Policy and value heads use object-causal attention, modulating token interactions by learned per-slot relevance scores so that decision-making concentrates on task-relevant entities. COMET adds an explicit object-level inductive bias to MuZero-style latent planning. Across eight visually and dynamically diverse tasks from the Object-Centric Visual RL benchmark, ManiSkill, Robosuite, and VizDoom, COMET achieves a higher mean normalized score during the early stages of training compared to object-centric and monolithic baselines.

</details>


### [237] [GitOfThoughts: Version-Controlled Reasoning and Agent Memory You Can Replay, Diff, and Merge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14470)
*Pavan C Shekar,Abhishek H S,Aswanth Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GitOfThoughts stores LLM reasoning trees as git repositories for auditability and mergeability. However, systematic experiments show that memory substrates (including git) do not improve accuracy on novel problems — gains only appear when retrieved cases are near-duplicates (similarity > 0.8), where the benefit is answer retrieval, not method transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM reasoning is ephemeral — chain-of-thought, pruned search branches, and memory buffers cannot be diffed, merged, or audited, unlike every other complex software artifact. The authors aim to (1) provide version control for reasoning and (2) rigorously test whether memory substrates actually improve reasoning accuracy.

Method: GitOfThoughts represents an agent's reasoning tree as a git repository: thoughts become commits, scores become git notes, outcomes become tags, and retrieval uses git log. The evaluation spans five memory substrates (none, markdown, vector, graph, git), two benchmarks, two model scales, with pre-registered replications to test whether any memory format improves accuracy on novel problems.

Result: No memory substrate reliably improves accuracy on novel problems. Gains appear only above the "copyability threshold" (similarity > ~0.8), where accuracy jumps sharply — but this is answer retrieval, not method transfer. A 4.5× larger model doubles the near-duplicate payoff yet cannot extract transferable methods from worked examples. The only consistently effective lever is test-time sampling.

Conclusion: Git-of-thoughts is justified on grounds of auditability, provenance, and mergeability at accuracy parity — not accuracy improvement. The authors document a retracted result and refuted hypothesis, modeling rigorous evaluation standards.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) reasoning is ephemeral: chains of thought vanish with the context window, pruned search branches leave no record, and memory buffers cannot be diffed, merged, or audited. Every other complex software process (code, infrastructure, data, experiments) is version-controlled; reasoning is not. We introduce GitOfThoughts, which stores an agent's reasoning tree as a git repository: every scored thought is a commit, scores are notes, outcomes are tags, and retrieval is "git log" over the agent's own history. This makes reasoning replayable, auditable, and mergeable across agents at near-zero engineering cost.
  We then ask the harder question: does memory, in any substrate, actually improve accuracy? Across five substrates (none, markdown, vector, graph, git), two benchmarks, two model scales, and pre-registered replications, the answer for novel problems is no. No memory format reliably helps, and a promising early result collapsed under its own pre-registered replication. Memory pays only above what we call the copyability threshold: when the retrieved case is a near-duplicate of the current problem (similarity >~ 0.8), accuracy jumps sharply; below it, nothing. The gain is answer retrieval, not method transfer: a 4.5x larger model doubles the near-duplicate payoff yet still cannot extract a transferable method from a worked example. The only general lever we find is test-time sampling. The case for git-as-substrate is therefore auditability, provenance, and mergeability at accuracy parity. We document a retracted result and a refuted hypothesis to model the evaluation standard we hold ourselves to.

</details>


### [238] [When the Tool Decides: LLM Agents Defer Blindly to Graph Neural Network Tools, and Stronger Backbones Defer More](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14476)
*Zhongyuan Wang,Pratyusha Vemuri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM agents equipped with GNN tools fail to exercise independent judgment, blindly adopting the GNN's predictions 97.6–99.2% of the time instead of reasoning when to rely on the tool. This "GNN parrot" effect worsens with model scale, and selective invocation must be explicitly engineered rather than expected to emerge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work assumes LLM agents can autonomously decide when and how much to rely on callable GNN tools, but this assumption has not been directly tested. The authors aim to measure whether agents actually exercise judgment over tool use in node classification on text-attributed graphs.

Method: A frozen GNN is exposed as an explicit callable tool to a ReAct-style LLM agent. Node classification is performed on ogbn-arxiv and WikiCS. The agent's predictions are compared against the raw GNN output to measure agreement rates. A per-node oracle over available actions establishes headroom, and a selective-invocation gate is tested as an intervention. Backbone LLMs range from Qwen2.5 0.5B to 7B.

Result: Agent predictions agree with the GNN 97.6–99.2% of the time. Agreement rises with model capability (0.60 at 1.5B to 0.98 at 7B). A per-node oracle beats the parrot by 0.09–0.18 at 3B and 0.12–0.22 at 7B, with the gap roughly doubling at high homophily. At 7B, a simpler neighbour-label tool outperforms the GNN at high homophily (0.81 vs 0.71) yet the agent still defers. A selective-invocation gate recovers about half the high-homophily gap (0.71→0.83) but yields no net global gain. Held-out estimates bound the best achievable gate using standard test-time features to at most one-third of the oracle headroom.

Conclusion: LLM agents do not emerge with the ability to judge when to trust tools; they become "GNN parrots." Evaluations of agent+tool systems cannot assume the agent adds judgment, and selective invocation must be deliberately designed rather than expected from scaling alone.

Abstract: A growing line of work equips large language model (LLM) agents with graph neural networks (GNNs) as callable tools, assuming the agent exercises judgment over when and how much to rely on such a tool. We test this directly. We expose a frozen GNN to a ReAct-style LLM agent as an explicit tool and measure, on node classification over a text-attributed graph (ogbn-arxiv, replicated on WikiCS), whether the agent uses the tool or merely obeys it. We find the agent does not exercise judgment: its predictions agree with the raw GNN's 97.6-99.2% of the time (5 seeds), collapsing into a GNN parrot that adopts the tool's output wholesale and bypasses its own reasoning. Sweeping backbone capability (Qwen2.5 0.5B-7B), the deference is not a weak-model artifact: among models able to invoke the tool, agreement rises with capability (0.60 to 0.98 from 1.5B to 7B). Crucially, the cost of deference does not shrink as capability grows and grows where alternatives emerge: a per-node oracle over the available actions beats the parrot by 0.09-0.18 at 3B and 0.12-0.22 at 7B, roughly doubling at high homophily, because the parrot is pinned to the frozen GNN while the agent's alternatives improve; at 7B a simple neighbour-label tool overtakes the GNN at high homophily (0.81 vs 0.71) yet the agent still defers. A simple selective-invocation gate recovers about half of that high-homophily gap (0.71 to 0.83) but yields no net global gain, and held-out estimates bound the best achievable gate over standard test-time features to at most a third of the oracle headroom: reliable selective invocation looks limited by available information, not merely router design. Our results are a cautionary measurement: evaluations of agent+tool systems cannot assume the agent adds judgment on top of the tool, and selective invocation must be designed in rather than expected to emerge from scale.

</details>


### [239] [From Chatbot to Digital Colleague: The Paradigm Shift Toward Persistent Autonomous AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14502)
*Yongheng Zhang,Ziang Liu,Jiaxuan Zhu,Shuai Wang,Xiangqi Chen,Haojing Huang,Jiayi Kuang,Siyu Chen,Ao Shen,Hao Wu,Qiufeng Wang,Qian-Wen Zhang,Junnan Dong,Wenhao Jiang,Ying Shen,Hai-Tao Zheng,Yinghui Li,Di Yin,Xing Sun,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper conceptualizes the evolution of LLMs from conversational chatbots into "Digital Colleagues" — persistent, integrated AI systems with deliberate reasoning (Thinking LLMs) and workspace-based task execution (OpenClaw). The transition is organized along cognitive advancement (fast thinking → deliberate reasoning) and tool-use advancement (ad hoc tool calling → persistent Workspace + Skill paradigms), with corresponding shifts in data construction and evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are moving beyond simple conversational generation toward integrated AI systems capable of reasoning, action, memory, and self-improvement. The current paradigm of chatbots yields ephemeral, stateless interactions that are inadequate for sustained, reliable collaborative work. The authors aim to formalize this shift and provide a structured framework for understanding the emerging capabilities and infrastructure needed.

Method: The authors organize the transition along two tightly coupled dimensions: (1) Cognitive core — from "fast thinking" next-token prediction to "Thinking LLMs" employing inference-time computation, Chain-of-Thought reasoning, reflection, process supervision, and reinforcement learning for deliberate cognition; (2) Tool-augmented task execution — from ad hoc tool-calling Agents to "OpenClaw" workstation systems with persistent Workspaces, reusable skills, verification loops, and governance. The "Workspace + Skill" paradigm introduces state persistence, reusable procedures, task closure, and experience reuse. They also examine shifts in data construction (from instruction-response pairs to State-Action-Observation trajectories) and evaluation (from static benchmarks to sandboxed, auditable, self-evolving AI ecosystems).

Result: The paper presents a conceptual framework rather than empirical results. It maps the trajectory from Chatbot to Digital Colleague, articulating how inference-time reasoning and persistent workspaces transform LLMs from stateless responders into reliable, colleague-like systems. The framework highlights that deliberate cognition and governed tool-use are mutually reinforcing dimensions of this transition.

Conclusion: The transition from Chatbot to Digital Colleague represents a fundamental re-architecture of AI systems, where Thinking LLMs and OpenClaw-style workspaces together enable persistent, auditable, and self-improving AI collaborators. This shift demands new data paradigms (trajectories) and evaluation methods (sandboxed ecosystems) to match the complexity of sustained, multi-step work.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are undergoing a fundamental transformation from conversational generators into integrated AI systems capable of reasoning, action, memory, and self-improvement. We conceptualize this transition as a shift from Chatbot to Digital Colleague: from conversational answers to persistent work. We organize this transition along two tightly coupled dimensions. First, at the cognitive core level, LLMs are advancing from Chatbot-era "fast thinking" systems driven by next-token prediction toward Thinking LLMs that leverage inference-time computation, Chain-of-Thought reasoning, reflection, process supervision, and reinforcement learning to support more deliberate and reliable cognition. Second, at the tool-augmented task execution level, LLMs are progressing from tool-calling Agents that invoke external resources in an ad hoc manner toward OpenClaw-style workstation systems (OpenClaw) equipped with persistent Workspaces, skills, verification loops, and governance. The "Workspace + Skill" paradigm makes episodic tool use colleague-like via state persistence, reusable procedures, task closure, and experience reuse. We examine data construction shifts from instruction-response pairs to State-Action-Observation trajectories and evaluation from static benchmarks to sandboxed, auditable, self-evolving AI ecosystems.

</details>


### [240] [Dense Coordinate-List Fine-Tuning Induces a Controllable Interference Surface in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14507)
*Chenyu Zhou,Qiliang Jiang,Boguang Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fine-tuning VLMs for dense coordinate outputs induces structured repetition artifacts (duplicate bounding boxes). The authors identify this as a controllable "interference surface" — separable from task performance via targeted repeat-stop mechanisms, structure-bound to bbox-coordinate lists, and reproducible across models (Gemma 4, Qwen3-VL) and datasets (COCO).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fine-tuning vision-language models to emit dense coordinate lists improves visual grounding but introduces undesirable changes in serialization behavior — specifically, how models repeat and terminate structured outputs. Understanding and controlling this generation behavior is critical for deploying reliable structured-output VLMs.

Method: The authors study this phenomenon using Gemma 4 12B with LoRA adapters of varying rank configurations (q/k/v/o vs. q/v only) across ranks 4–64. They measure duplicate rate, max repeat count, and F1@0.3/F1@0.5. They introduce object-level repeat-stop (removing exact repeated records) and use structure-axis probes to test whether the effect localizes to bbox-coordinate object lists versus other structured outputs (dense non-bbox, spatial/count JSON). Reproduction is tested on Qwen3-VL-8B and COCO 2017.

Result: High-capacity q/k/v/o LoRA raises F1@0.3 from 0.007 to 0.448 but induces a duplicate rate of 0.080 and max repeat of 23. A q/v rank sweep maintains max repeat at 21–22 across ranks 4–64, indicating capacity persistence. Object-level repeat-stop eliminates exact duplicates (duplicate rate 0.000, max repeat 1) while preserving F1 (0.494→0.490) and improving stricter F1@0.5 (0.381→0.385). The effect is structure-bound: only bbox-coordinate object lists exhibit repeats; dense non-bbox and spatial/count JSON remain clean. Qwen3-VL-8B reproduces a clean controlled endpoint (F1@0.3 0.318, duplicate rate 0.000), and COCO 2017 reproduces both acquisition gains and duplicate pressure.

Conclusion: Dense coordinate-list adaptation creates a structure-bound, cross-family interference surface — repeated-tail pressure is an inherent byproduct of adapting VLMs for dense coordinate outputs, but it is measurable, separable from task accuracy, and controllable via lightweight post-hoc mechanisms like repeat-stop, without architectural changes.

Abstract: Fine-tuning vision-language models to emit dense coordinate lists improves visual grounding but also changes how models serialize, repeat, and terminate structured outputs. We study this behavior as a generation and control surface. In Gemma 4 12B, high-capacity q/k/v/o LoRA raises class-aware F1@0.3 from 0.007 to 0.448 while inducing repeated-tail pressure (duplicate rate 0.080, max repeat 23). A q/v rank sweep keeps max repeat at 21-22 across ranks 4-64, showing capacity persistence. The target signal is separable: object-level repeat-stop removes exact repeated records (duplicate rate 0.000, max repeat 1) while preserving F1 (0.494 to 0.490) and stricter F1@0.5 (0.381 to 0.385). Structure-axis probes localize the effect to bbox-coordinate object lists; dense non-bbox and spatial/count JSON remain repeat-clean, including under high-capacity adapters. Qwen3-VL-8B reproduces a clean controlled endpoint (F1@0.3 0.318, duplicate rate 0.000), and COCO 2017 reproduces acquisition plus duplicate pressure. Dense coordinate-list adaptation therefore creates a structure-bound, cross-family interference surface that can be measured and controlled.

</details>


### [241] [Every Eval Ever: A Unifying Schema and Community Repository for AI Evaluation Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14516)
*Jan Batzner,Sree Harsha Nelaturu,Anastassia Kornilova,Jon Crall,Tommaso Cerruti,Yanan Long,Yifan Mai,Sanchit Ahuja,Asaf Yehudai,Marek Šuppa,John P. Lalor,Oluwagbemike Olowe,Jatin Ganhotra,Brian H. Hu,Eliya Habba,Andrew M. Bean,Chang Liu,Sander Land,Steven Dillmann,Aniketh Garikaparthi,Elron Bandel,Saki Imai,James Edgell,Wm. Matthew Kennedy,Jenny Chim,Patrick Meusling,Asteria Kaeberlein,Venkata Ramachandra Karthik Chundi,Manasi Patwardhan,Martin Ku,Austin Meek,Leon Knauer,Brian Wingenroth,Srishti Yadav,Usman Gohar,Felix Friedrich,Michelle Lin,Jennifer Mickel,Arman Cohan,Stella Biderman,Irene Solaiman,Zeerak Talat,Anka Reuel,Mubashara Akhtar,Gjergji Kasneci,Avijit Ghosh,Leshem Choshen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Every Eval Ever (E3), a standardized JSON schema and crowdsourced repository for AI evaluation results, addressing fragmentation across incompatible formats, frameworks, and metadata practices. The effort spans 22,235 models, 2,273 benchmarks, and 31 evaluation formats.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The AI evaluation ecosystem suffers from deep fragmentation: results are stored in incompatible formats across leaderboards, papers, blog posts, harness logs, and custom repositories. Different evaluation frameworks produce divergent scores for the same nominal evaluations and record metadata inconsistently, impeding cross-community comparison, evaluation science, cost reduction, and reuse.

Method: The authors designed a source-agnostic, unified JSON schema that standardizes evaluation representation. It ingests results from diverse sources (evaluation harnesses, papers, leaderboards) and optionally stores per-instance outputs for fine-grained analysis. Three contributions operationalize this: (i) a community-governed metadata schema with a companion instance-level schema; (ii) automatic converters from popular evaluation formats, harnesses, and leaderboards; (iii) a crowdsourced community database hosted on Hugging Face.

Result: The resulting repository aggregates 22,235 models, 2,273 unique benchmarks, and 31 evaluation formats as of reporting, demonstrating broad adoption and successful normalization of heterogeneous evaluation data into a single queryable resource.

Conclusion: E3 provides the first standardization effort of its kind for AI evaluation results, enabling unified analysis, comparison, and reuse across the fragmented evaluation landscape through a community-driven, schema-governed infrastructure.

Abstract: AI evaluations are widely used for testing and understanding progress. However, the diverse evaluators bring with them inconsistencies that challenge analysis and comparison. First, results are saved in incompatible formats, scattered across leaderboards, papers, blog posts, evaluation harness logs, and custom repositories. Second, results are created by different evaluation frameworks, which produce divergent scores for nominally identical evaluations and record metadata inconsistently, hindering comparison, cross-community evaluation science, cost reduction, and reuse. We introduce Every Eval Ever, the first shared schema and community-crowdsourced repository for AI evaluation results. The schema standardizes how evaluations are represented in a unified, single JSON document. It is source-agnostic by design, ingesting results from evaluation harnesses and papers alike, and optionally stores per-instance outputs for fine-grained analysis. We contribute: (i) a community-governed metadata schema with a companion instance-level schema, the first standardization effort of its kind; (ii) automatic converters from popular formats, evaluation harnesses, and leaderboards to the unified schema; and (iii) a crowdsourced community database hosted on Hugging Face, currently spanning to date 22,235 models, 2,273 unique benchmarks, and 31 evaluation formats.

</details>


### [242] [StreamMemBench: Streaming Evaluation of Agent Memory for Future-Oriented Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14571)
*Guanming Liu,Yuqi Ren,Hansu Gu,Peng Zhang,Weihang Wang,Jiahao Liu,Ning Gu,Tun Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StreamMemBench is a new benchmark for evaluating personal-agent memory that tests whether agents can carry observed evidence and user feedback from an initial task forward into future, similar tasks—an ability existing benchmarks overlook. Experiments across eight memory systems reveal that current models struggle to effectively reuse evidence and feedback even when stored.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory benchmarks for personal agents evaluate dialogue recall or task improvement in isolation, failing to test the real-world trajectory where an agent observes streaming information, uses it in a current request, and then carries that experience forward to similar future tasks. There is a gap in assessing whether agents can reuse both observed evidence and interaction feedback over time.

Method: The authors propose StreamMemBench, built on EgoLife egocentric streams. For each evidence anchor, a two-step task sequence is constructed: (1) an initial task testing whether the agent uses observed evidence, and (2) a follow-up task testing whether feedback and interaction experience from the first task are reused. Four metrics are defined—evidence recall, initial evidence use, feedback incorporation, and follow-up reuse—to provide fine-grained diagnosis. Eight memory systems across two backbones are evaluated.

Result: Experiments show that current memory systems frequently fail to use observed evidence or to convert feedback into reliable follow-up behavior, even in cases where evidence is successfully stored or feedback is incorporated locally. Performance degrades significantly in the follow-up reuse stage.

Conclusion: StreamMemBench exposes a critical weakness in current personal-agent memory systems: the inability to bridge streaming observations and interaction feedback into future-oriented assistance. The benchmark serves as a diagnostic tool to drive progress toward memory systems that support longitudinal, experience-driven assistance.

Abstract: A central role of personal-agent memory is to turn stored information and prior interactions into future-oriented assistance. In daily use, useful cues come from what the agent observes and how the user interacts with the agent, and the agent must carry them forward from the current request to similar future tasks. Existing memory benchmarks usually test dialogue recall or task improvement in isolation, leaving the trajectory from streaming observations to later assistance largely untested. We introduce StreamMemBench, a streaming benchmark that constructs a two-step task sequence around each evidence anchor from EgoLife egocentric streams. The initial task tests evidence use, while the follow-up task tests whether feedback and interaction experience are reused. Four metrics diagnose evidence recall, initial evidence use, feedback incorporation, and follow-up reuse. Experiments with eight memory systems across two backbones show that current systems often fail to use observed evidence or turn feedback into reliable follow-up behavior, even when evidence is stored or feedback is incorporated locally. StreamMemBench is publicly available at https://github.com/landian60/StreamMemBench.

</details>


### [243] [VISTA: View-Consistent Self-Verified Training for GUI Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14579)
*Xinyu Qiu,Yunzhu Zhang,Heng Jia,Shuheng Shen,Changhua Meng,Linchao Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VISTA addresses a core failure mode in GRPO for GUI grounding — uninformative all-win or all-lose rollout groups — by constructing comparison groups from multiple target-preserving views (crops) of the same GUI, and adds a self-verified cross-view anchor loss to stabilize training without collapsing to imitation. The method yields consistent accuracy gains across five benchmarks and multiple Qwen backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In GRPO for GUI grounding, rollouts are sampled from a single screenshot. This causes groups to be entirely successes (easy instances) or entirely failures (hard instances), providing no relative advantage signal and breaking the optimization. The authors aim to create informative comparison groups and stabilize the RL training for short coordinate generation.

Method: VISTA generates multiple target-preserving views of the same GUI instance via cropping that keeps the target element visible and remaps its bounding box exactly. Rollouts across these semantically equivalent but geometrically varied views form each comparison group, ensuring within-group variance. Additionally, a self-verified cross-view anchor is introduced: an oracle answer trained with advantage-weighted loss, excluded from the group baseline computation, and activated only when the model achieves maximum reward on a rollout, preventing RL from degrading into unconditional imitation.

Result: On ScreenSpot-Pro, Qwen3-VL 4B/8B/30B-A3B improve from 55.5/52.7/53.7 to 63.4/65.8/67.0. Consistent gains are reported across five GUI-grounding benchmarks. Robustness analyses show higher worst-view accuracy and lower prediction flip rates.

Conclusion: VISTA effectively stabilizes GRPO for GUI grounding by injecting meaningful variance into comparison groups through multi-view construction, and the self-verified anchor prevents RL drift. The approach is model-agnostic and benefits multiple backbone sizes.

Abstract: When applying Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) for GUI Grounding, rollouts are sampled from a single screenshot view; groups often become either all failures on difficult instances or all successes on easy ones, yielding no useful relative advantage. We propose VISTA (View-Consistent Self-Verified Training), a GRPO-based training framework that constructs each comparison group from multiple target-preserving views of the same GUI instance.Each view is generated by a crop that keeps the target element visible and remaps its box exactly, so model rollouts are compared across semantically equivalent but geometrically different inputs. To stabilize short coordinate generation without turning reinforcement learning into unconditional imitation, VISTA further adds a self-verified cross-view anchor: an oracle answer optimized with an advantage-weighted loss, excluded from the group baseline and activated only when the model has produced a maximum-reward rollout. Across five GUI-grounding benchmarks and multiple Qwen backbones, VISTA consistently improves grounding accuracy.On ScreenSpot-Pro, it raises Qwen3-VL 4B/8B/30B-A3B from 55.5/52.7/53.7 to 63.4/65.8/67.0. Robustness analyses further show higher worst-view accuracy and lower prediction flip rates.

</details>


### [244] [A Temporal Planning Framework for Disruption Aware Dynamic Route Optimization in Heterogeneous Railway Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14582)
*Pollob Chandra Ray,Sabah Binte Noor,Fazlul Hasan Siddiqui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a temporal planning framework using PDDL 2.1 for dynamic route optimization and disruption management in heterogeneous multi-gauge single-track railway networks, demonstrating effective plan generation across 200 benchmark instances with up to 1,000 track points and 120 trains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing railway optimization research focuses on high-level timetabling while neglecting operational details like track switching coordination and gauge compatibility, forcing human operators to handle these manually—increasing safety risks. The problem is exacerbated in heterogeneous multi-gauge single-track networks with stochastic disruptions (blocked tracks, engine failures, speed slowdowns), where coordination complexity is intrinsically high.

Method: The authors formulate railway operations as a temporal planning problem using PDDL 2.1, explicitly modeling gauge compatibility constraints and diverse disruption scenarios. The framework produces conflict-free timestamped operational plans with both optimized schedules and executable action sequences. A benchmark of 200 problem instances (up to 1,000 track points, 120 trains) was created, and two state-of-the-art temporal planners plus a plan validator were used for evaluation.

Result: Experimental results show the framework successfully generates temporal operational plans for heterogeneous railway systems, handles multi-gauge constraints and disruptions, and reduces reliance on manual decision-making.

Conclusion: The temporal planning approach effectively addresses operational-level route optimization with gauge compatibility and disruption management, offering a viable alternative to human-operator-dependent decision-making in complex heterogeneous railway networks.

Abstract: Efficient route optimization play a vital role in ensuring both safety and punctuality in railway operations. It is very crucial particularly in heterogeneous multi-gauge railway networks with varying train speed, stopping pattern, infrastructure compatibility constraints increase coordination complexity. In single-track systems these challenges are further intensify due to all trains to share the same track and requires frequent track switching.Stochastic disruptions events including blocked tracks, blocked trains, engine failure and speed slowdowns introduces additional unpredictability in operations and deviate the timetable. However, existing studies predominantly focuses on high-level timetabling, omitting operational details such as track switching coordination. As a result leaving decision to human operators, increasing safety risks into railway operations. This study proposes a framework based on temporal planning for dynamic route optimization and disruption management in heterogeneous railway systems. The framework formulates railway operations as a temporal planning problem using PDDL 2.1 with explicitly modeling gauge compatibility constraints and diverse disruption scenarios. It generates conflict-free timestamped operational plans specifying both optimized schedules and executable action sequences. To evaluate the proposed framework, we developed a benchmark problem set with 200 instances using up to 1,000 track points and 120 trains. Two state-of-the-art temporal planners and a plan validator were employed to assessed the framework. The experimental results demonstrate that the framework effectively generates temporal operational plans for heterogeneous railway systems and handles multi-gauge constraints, disruptions, and reduces dependence on manual decision making.

</details>


### [245] [Abstracting Cross-Domain Action Sequences into Interpretable Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14654)
*Gaurav Verma,Scott Counts*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WorkflowView leverages LLMs to abstract noisy, low-level action sequences into interpretable high-level activities, demonstrating strong performance across three diverse tasks: browser log reconstruction, MOOC dropout prediction, and privacy-preserving analysis of AI tool usage in Microsoft Word.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sequential interaction logs from digital applications are rich but noisy and granular, making it difficult to extract meaningful, actionable insights. Prior deep learning clustering approaches are brittle, sensitive to noise, and fail to generalize across different applications and domains.

Method: The authors propose WorkflowView, an LLM-based framework that abstracts low-level action sequences into high-level activities. The approach is evaluated on three tasks: (a) zero-shot task description reconstruction from browser logs using semantic similarity; (b) few-shot student dropout prediction from MOOC logs; (c) anonymized, privacy-preserving analysis of AI tool integration in Microsoft Word document workflows.

Result: WorkflowView achieves: (a) mean semantic similarity of 0.91 for zero-shot task reconstruction; (b) weighted F1 of 0.90 for few-shot dropout prediction with only five examples; (c) successful privacy-preserving analysis of AI integration patterns in Word workflows.

Conclusion: LLM-based abstraction is a robust, efficient, and generalizable method for transforming low-level behavioral logs into high-level, interpretable insights. The paper also addresses practical deployment concerns including computational cost and user privacy in logging infrastructures.

Abstract: Sequential or time-stamped interaction logs provide objective records of digital application usage, yet their granularity and noise often obscure meaningful insights into people's work. Such insights are essential for improving digital products in ways grounded in real-world user interactions. Prior research has applied deep learning models to cluster user actions into high-level activities, but these approaches are highly sensitive to noise and struggle to generalize across applications. To address this limitation, we introduce WorkflowView, a framework that uses large language models (LLMs) to abstract low-level action sequences into high-level activities. We establish the effectiveness and generality of our approach across three distinct, challenging sequential tasks and diverse domains: (a) zero-shot task description reconstruction from browser logs (achieving high semantic similarity, $μ_{sim} = 0.91$), (b) few-shot student dropout prediction using MOOC interaction logs (reaching weighted $F_1 = 0.90$ with only five few-shot examples), and (c) anonymized, privacy-preserving analysis of AI tool integration within document workflows in Microsoft Word. Our work demonstrates that LLM-based abstraction is a robust and efficient path forward for transforming low-level behavioral data into high-level, interpretable, and actionable insights. We also discuss practical considerations for deploying LLM-based inferences within logging infrastructures, including computational efficiency and user privacy.

</details>


### [246] [Towards Direct Latent-Space Synthesis for Parallel Branches in LLM-Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14672)
*Shikun Liu,Mufei Li,Dongqi Fu,Haoyu Wang,Yinglong Xia,Hong Li,Hong Yan,Pan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Parallel-Synthesis, a framework that enables a synthesizer LLM to directly consume KV caches from parallel worker agents instead of concatenating their text outputs, achieving 2.5x-11x reduction in time-to-first-token while matching or outperforming text-based synthesis on 7 of 9 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern structured agent workflows use parallel branches for subtask exploration, but existing systems merge outputs via text concatenation, discarding the parallel structure and incurring redundant prefill computation. There is a mismatch between the inherently parallel agent execution topology and the sequential text interface of LLMs.

Method: Parallel-Synthesis comprises two components: (1) a cache mapper that calibrates independently generated KV caches from parallel branches into a unified non-sequential cache structure, and (2) a fine-tuned synthesizer adapter that enables the model to generate from this non-sequential cache interface. Training uses data that exposes the synthesizer to parallel cache contexts, teaches aggregation across cached branches, and distills reasoning behavior from standard text-concatenation-based synthesis.

Result: Parallel-Synthesis matches or outperforms text-based synthesis on 7 of 9 downstream datasets (spanning math, science QA, code generation, GAIA, and multi-agent database diagnosis) and remains close on the remaining two. It achieves 2.5x-11x reduction in time-to-first-token.

Conclusion: Direct cache-based synthesis is a promising interface for more native and efficient synthesis over parallel agent branches, suggesting a path toward LLM interfaces better aligned with structured, parallel agent workflows.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly serve as execution engines for agentic systems, yet they still consume context through a sequential text interface. This creates a mismatch with modern structured agent workflows, in which independent branches explore subtasks, retrieve evidence, or generate candidate solutions before a final synthesis step. Existing systems typically merge these branches by concatenating their textual outputs, which discards the parallel structure and incurs redundant prefill computation. In this work, we introduce Parallel-Synthesis, a plug-and-play framework that enables a synthesizer to directly consume the KV caches produced by parallel worker agents. Parallel-Synthesis combines a cache mapper that calibrates independently generated branch caches with a fine-tuned synthesizer adapter that enables generation from this non-sequential cache interface. We train Parallel-Synthesis using data that exposes the synthesizer to parallel cache contexts, teaches aggregation across cached branches, and distills reasoning behavior from standard text-concatenation-based synthesis. Across nine downstream datasets spanning math, science QA, code generation, GAIA, and multi-agent database diagnosis, Parallel-Synthesis matches or outperforms text-based synthesis on seven datasets and remains close on the other two. It also reduces time-to-first-token by 2.5x-11x, suggesting that direct cache-based synthesis is a promising interface for more native and efficient synthesis over parallel agent branches.

</details>
